# Ideas, decisions and open questions

Everything that keeps track of where Strata is going: open questions, ideas, what has been decided (and whether it is built), known gaps and the roadmap. The [README](README.md) and [docs](docs/) describe what is built.

## Open questions (to discuss before building)

- **A field as a `mut` argument** (decided with the user 2026-11-21: allowed, as now). `shift(mut r.lo)` is the same as `mut t := r.lo; shift(mut t); r.lo = t`, and the record's own invariant is checked the same way after the call as after that assignment, so forbidding it would protect nothing. The check after a `mut` call is left out where the checker proves the parameter's type fits the variable's; undecided cases could become proof obligations for `verify`, with the runtime check kept only for what isn't proven.
- **Generating code from a type's shape** (decided 2026-11-21): see the decision "Derived traits, `comptime` and special methods" below.
- **Marking a `mut self` call** (built 2026-11-20, see docs/language.md "Mutation"). `mut queue.claim(2)`, as Hylo's `&x.append(1)`: required when the method takes `mut self`, an error otherwise. The tree is `Expr::Mut(Method(..))`; a `Mut` around anything else is an argument for a `mut` parameter. The parser's `marked` reads a place after `mut`, then marks the first method call on it, and later calls apply to its result (rendered `(mut q.claim(2)).len()`); as an argument the marked call is rendered in parentheses, `call((mut o.pop()), x, y)` (the user's form). Results are not places (user's point): `mut f(x).m()` is a parse error, and a result can't be passed to a `mut` parameter; name it first. Rejected: `(mut queue).claim(2)`, `claim(mut queue, 2)`, and a separate call operator.
- **What iterating a string yields** (decided by the user 2026-11-21; built: the three units, `len` / indexing / slicing on them, and positions as byte offsets; see "Iterators and `FiniteIterator`"). Nothing implicitly: a `String` is not a list and can't be the subject of a `for` (already an error today: "`for` needs a List, Set or Dict"). The unit is always named: `s.bytes()`, `s.code_points()`, `s.graphemes()` (names to settle), each giving an iterator a `for` can run over. `chars` (code points, as a `List[String]`) stays. The same goes for length, indexing and slicing (decided 2026-11-21, built): `len(s)`, `s[i]` and `s[1..3]` are refused on a `String`, naming the units; they are written on the unit, `len(s.bytes())`, `s.graphemes()[1..3]`. Indexing, slicing and `len` need a `FiniteIterator` iterator (see "Iterators and `FiniteIterator`" below); a string's units are finite, so they can be indexed. Settled when building it (user, 2026-11-22): positions are byte offsets (`index_of`, `byte_slice`, `byte_len`).
- **Iterators and `FiniteIterator`** (user, 2026-11-21; built in four steps, below). Two traits: `Iterator` (what a `for` runs over, and what `map` / `filter` / `fold` take as receiver) and `FiniteIterator` (it ends). `List`, `Set`, `Dict`, ranges and a string's units implement both; an unbounded generator implements only `Iterator`. `FiniteIterator` is what indexing, slicing and `len` require, and what lets termination checking say a `for` loop ends (today it knows this only for lists: "only `for` loops"); a `for` over an `Iterator` that isn't `FiniteIterator` is treated like a `while`. Named `FiniteIterator` (user, 2026-11-21: clearer than `Finite`, which means nothing apart from an iterator). Decided (user, 2026-11-21): `FiniteIterator` carries its length, known ahead of time (that is what shows it ends); the length may change where the value is mutable (a `mut` list). `map` keeps the length; `filter` keeps finiteness but returns something with a new length (on a list, a new list). Proposed (Claude, following Rust's `trait ExactSizeIterator: Iterator { fn len(&self) -> usize }`): a supertrait, `trait FiniteIterator: Iterator { fn len(self) -> Natural }`, which Strata already has (`trait Summary: Describe + Priced`). A supertrait is a requirement, not inheritance: implementing `FiniteIterator` requires an `Iterator` impl, but nothing is inherited or overridden and there is no subtyping of values. The user's concern: it looks like a kind of iterator, i.e. inheritance. To settle: that, whether a lazy `filter` returns an eager value (exact length) or a lazy one (only a bound: Rust's `size_hint` upper bound, which is enough for termination but not for indexing), and how `verify` uses `len`. **Step 1 built** (branch `iterators`): `trait Iterator[T] { fn next(mut self) -> Option[T] }` in the prelude (the element type is a trait parameter, as Strata has no associated types), and `for` over any value implementing it. The checker accepts the subject when its type satisfies `Iterator[el]` (`satisfies`, which also infers `el`), and then the loop is rewritten as `{ mut $iter_N := xs; loop { match mut $iter_N.next() { Some(p) => body, None => break } } }` (`types::lower_iterator_fors`) and the program checked again, so the compiler, the prover and termination see an ordinary loop; the second check only happens when a program has such a loop. Lists, sets and dicts keep their built-in loops (they give an iterator through `iter()`, step 4), and `verify` says a `for` over an `Iterator` that isn't a `FiniteIterator` may not end. Decided (user, 2026-11-21): `FiniteIterator` is a supertrait of `Iterator` (`trait FiniteIterator[T]: Iterator[T]`; the inheritance reading is fine). `map` / `filter` are lazy, so a comprehension over an unbounded iterator is itself an iterator (an infinite comprehension). `verify` uses the length for termination first: a `for` over a `FiniteIterator` ends. Invariants over `len` come later. Next: `FiniteIterator` and termination through it, lazy `map` / `filter` / `fold` on any iterator, the built-in collections and ranges as iterators, comprehensions over iterators, a string's units. To settle when building: what a comprehension over a finite iterator gives (a `List`, as today for lists, or a lazy iterator), and where a lazy `filter`'s length comes from (its bound, the source's length, is enough for termination). **Step 2 built** (branch `iterators-2`): `trait FiniteIterator[T]: Iterator[T] { fn len(self) -> Natural }`, and `map` / `filter` / `fold` as default methods of `Iterator` (`map` / `filter` lazy, through the prelude's `Mapped` / `Filtered`). How:
- **The prelude carries code** (`types::PRELUDE_CODE`): definitions with bodies are added to every program when it is resolved (`with_prelude_code`), in the module `std.prelude`, so they are checked and compiled as the program's own and skipped where std is (`verify`, the views, the viewer's type map). Their traits' methods are named `std.prelude.len` and so on, as std's methods are named in their modules, and a program's impls of those traits name their methods the same way: apart from the builtins `len`, `map`, `filter`, `fold`, which lists keep. Calls (`it.len()`) find them through the receiver's traits (`implemented_method`, now for type parameters' bounds and trait-typed values too). Messages show them short (`ListIter.next`, `FiniteIterator::len`).
- **Termination:** a `for` over a `FiniteIterator` holds it in a `$finite_iter_N`; that `loop`, and the `next` call it makes, count as ending. This rests on `len`, which nothing checks yet (with invariants, later).
- **Build tests** (user, 2026-11-21): they no longer compare compiled output with `--interpret`, but with outputs recorded in `tests/data/build/expected` (`STRATA_BLESS=1` records them), recorded from step 1, where both agreed. The trait methods named like builtins had made the interpreter call `Iterator`'s `map` on lists.
**Step 3 built** (branch `iterators-3`; the user's idea: what is passed to `filter` must end, then `filter` and `fold` do):
- `fold` and `to_list` are `FiniteIterator`'s (on an unbounded iterator `fold` never returns: refused, "`fold` needs a `FiniteIterator`"), written as a `for` over `self`, which ends. `Mapped` / `Filtered` are `FiniteIterator`s when their source is (a filtered one's `len` counts, on a copy).
- Lambdas: termination already follows a lambda's calls from the function it is written in, so a call of a function-typed parameter (`f(acc, x)` in `fold`) counts as ending. An impl's call of its own trait method on a field of `self` (`self.inner.len()`) is recursion on a smaller value (values are finite trees), left out of the call graph.
- Comprehensions over iterators: the checker gives `map(it, f)` / `filter` / `fold` on an iterator to its methods, and `set(it)` / `dict(it)` on a finite one to the prelude's `set_of` / `dict_of`. So a comprehension over an iterator is a lazy iterator (finite when its source is), over a list a list as before.
- `verify`'s cross-check: a fault in a function the checked one calls is that function's, not "a bug in the prover".
**Step 4 built** (branch `iterators-4`; user, 2026-11-21: `xs.iter()`, not lists as iterators, which would clash with the list builtins `map` / `filter`): the prelude's `ListIter[T] = { items: List[T], at: Natural }` (an `Iterator` and a `FiniteIterator`), and `trait Iterable[T] { fn iter(self) -> ListIter[T] }` for `List`, `Set` (in order, `to_list`) and `Dict` (entries by key, `items`); ranges are lists. Calls find a built-in type's impls by its receiver too (`implemented_method`). The prelude's code's names (`ListIter`, `Mapped`, ...) are the prelude's: defining one again says so. **A string's units built** (branch `string-units`; user, 2026-11-21: the names `bytes` / `code_points` / `graphemes`, written in Strata over native byte access): `s.bytes()` and `s.code_points()` are the prelude's (`Bytes`, `CodePoints`, trait `TextUnits` for `String`; code points decoded from the UTF-8 bytes, moving past a continuation byte to the next start), over two native builtins, `byte_len(s)` and `byte_at(s, i)` (C runtime and compiler). `s.graphemes()` is `std.unicode`'s (`Graphemes`, trait `GraphemeText`; std is loaded for a program that calls it, as for `matches`): UAX #29's rules GB3 to GB13 with GB9c (Indic_Conjunct_Break, now generated into std.unicode_tables from the UCD's DerivedCoreProperties.txt, `tools/unicode_tables.py`'s second input), its tables decoded at compile time (`comptime`), checked against all 1093 cases of GraphemeBreakTest.txt (`tests/data/grapheme_break_test.st`). Found on the way: precompiling std deadlocked once std had a `comptime` (resolving std ran it, and compiling that waited for the std library being made: `llvm::building_std` now covers the resolve); a lambda's parameters didn't get types from an earlier argument's trait obligation (`it.map(x -> x.m())`: `settle_pending`). `len`, indexing and slicing on a unit **built** (branch `unit-index`): `len` / `at` / `get` / `slice` / `take` / `drop` called on an iterator are its methods (`FiniteIterator`'s `get`, `at` with its precondition, lazy `slice`; `Iterator`'s lazy `take` / `drop`, the prelude's `Taken` / `Dropped`), `join` collects a finite iterator of strings; messages show the prelude's names as written (`types::shown_name`). **Strings by byte offsets, built** (branch `string-bytes`; user, 2026-11-22: positions are byte offsets, Rust's way): `len(s)`, `s[i]`, `get(s, i)` and `s[a..b]` on a `String` are refused, naming the units (`types::string_position`); `index_of` gives a byte offset, `byte_slice(s, a, b)` cuts in constant time, `byte_len(s)`. Decided while building (Claude; the user's option said it faults): `byte_slice` is total, an offset inside a character moving back to its start, so it never faults and needs no proof obligation (a boundary obligation would be unprovable on most strings, and std's `verify --require proven` relies on it). The prover models bytes exactly on ASCII strings and by bounds elsewhere: `utf8_len` (declared in every query) is between `str.len` and 4×, a slice has at most b - a bytes (exactly on ASCII), a match fits in the string; `len(s.code_points())` is `str.len(s)` and `len(s.bytes())` is `utf8_len(s)`, so refinements on them stay provable. Exhaustiveness reads `len(it.code_points())`, `it != ""` and `it == ""` as length conditions on strings; `NonEmptyString` is `it != ""`. Migrated: std (regex, cli, json, http, fs), the compiled prelude's `lines`, examples, test programs, docs. Found on the way: refinements written in types (`zip: String where …`) and `where` conditions in patterns weren't given the checker's method decisions (`ast::decl_exprs_mut_post_typed`); messages printed elaborated names (`printer::shown`). **String builtins in Strata, first batch built** (branch `str-builtins`): `contains`, `starts_with`, `ends_with`, `index_of`, `chars` and `reverse` (on strings) are the compiled prelude's (`llvm/prelude.strata`, `str_*`, over `byte_len` / `byte_at` / `byte_slice`), called in place of the C runtime's; the checker's signatures and the prover's models are unchanged. `index_of` is a naive search (O(n·m), as the C one). `split`, `replace` and `repeat` **are total and in Strata too** (user, 2026-11-22: errors are `Result`s, and these have no error case: an empty separator splits at every character, an empty pattern is found around every character, as Rust's; a count below 1 gives `""`); the prover no longer has obligations for them (`replace` with an empty pattern is left unknown: SMT's `replace_all` differs there). Still in C: `trim`, `to_upper` / `to_lower`, `is_digit` / `is_alpha` / `is_space` (Unicode tables: `std.unicode` has them, but the compiled prelude can't use std), `parse_int` / `parse_float`. Not yet: a nested comprehension whose inner source is an iterator (needs a lazy `flat_map`); a user's own `it.next()` loop isn't shown to end (a call of `next` through the trait may reach `Filtered.next`); `trim`, case mappings, the `is_` tests and number parsing are still C.
- **Regex verification scope** (decided with the user 2026-11-20; not built). By default, regex refinements are proven over ASCII strings only, and the result always says so ("proven for ASCII strings"). Such a proof counts as proven in an ordinary `verify` run. Under a `[verify] proven = [...]` rule it does not, unless the project opts in to accepting ASCII proofs; it is still reported as proven for ASCII either way. Full Unicode proofs are asked for explicitly where they matter (per module in `[verify]`, or per function). Running code stays fully Unicode either way. Pairs with "two solvers": ASCII classes are small enough for Z3 to decide exactly; full-Unicode proofs can go to cvc5, which decides the large classes.
- **Two solvers, each for its strengths** (user, 2026-11-20; direction). Z3 and cvc5 are both wired in (`STRATA_SOLVER`). Use both: route a query by what it contains (cvc5 for strings and large Unicode regex classes, which it decides exactly where Z3 times out; Z3 elsewhere), or run both and take the first definite answer.
- **Complexity / cost.** Separate from termination: a function can terminate and still take exponential time (naive `fib`, Ackermann). The user raised it as "another issue"; nothing is designed yet.
- **Transpilation / interop** with Python (Pydantic, JSON as the boundary), TypeScript and Rust. Later.
- **JSON as a boundary format: a derived `Json` trait** (user, 2026-11-21; built: see "`std.json`: a derived `Json` trait" under Decisions, which supersedes the details below where they differ; kept for the reasoning). Before it, every value had one JSON form (the interpreter's `to_json` / `from_json`, now gone; `to_json` keeps it): variants externally tagged (`"None"`, `{"Some": 3}`, `{"Queued": {...}}`), records as plain objects, integers beyond 64 bits as strings of digits, untagged unions read by trying each member in written order. That is useful for debugging but not for the main use the user sees: JSON from a web front end read straight into a record. Measured problems: with `Int | String`, `"5"` reads back as the `Int` 5; with `String | Int`, a big integer (written `"9223372036854775808"`) reads back as a `String`. The direction:
  - **a derivable trait** (`derivable(Record)` / `derivable(Sum)`, see "Derived traits" below) whose derive succeeds only for types with an unambiguous JSON form, so being `Json` *means* being serializable. Base types implement it built in. Full round-tripping of every value is not the goal; reading any JSON that fits the type is.
  - **`Option[T]` is the value or `null`** (or a missing key, for a field), not `{"Some": x}`. `Option[Option[T]]` can't tell `None` from `Some(None)`, so it isn't `Json`.
  - **a `"type"` key holding the type's name**, as TypeScript's discriminated unions do (user). Proposed: always on a sum's variants (`{"type": "Queued", "name": "a"}`, a variant without data either that or the bare string `"Queued"`); on a plain record written, but optional on input when the expected type is a single record (web forms won't send it). A variant with positional data needs a key for it (`"value"`?, or only record and no-data variants are `Json`). A field named `type` in a `Json` type is an error (or the tag key is configurable).
  - **records and `Dict`s**: reading is driven by the expected type, so they only need telling apart inside a union, where the `"type"` key does it; a `Dict[String, V]` in such a union could have a `"type"` key itself, so that union isn't `Json`. A `Dict` with non-string keys isn't `Json` either (or keeps the `[[k, v], ...]` form).
  - **unions**: `Json` only when the members' JSON kinds can't overlap (number / string / bool / null / array / object with a distinct `"type"`). `Int | String` qualifies once integers are never strings.
  - **integers**: always JSON numbers; a value beyond 64 bits is a fault when written and an error when read (user: "big integers are banned"). Open: JavaScript's numbers are exact only to 2^53, so a web client silently rounds larger ones. Options weighed with the user: write larger ones as strings (any `Int` works, but the JSON's shape then depends on the value: `number | string` for a TypeScript client), or refuse them (a fault when written, an error when read; the shape stays fixed, and with a `SafeInt` alias and `verify` covering derived code the risk is visible). The user leans towards refusing; Claude agrees.
  - **a JavaScript-like `Number`** (user's suggestion): `Float` is already an IEEE double that is never NaN or infinite (docs/language.md), which is exactly what JSON can hold: JSON has no NaN or Infinity (`JSON.stringify(NaN)` is `"null"`). So a `Number` with NaN/Infinity wouldn't go through JSON any better; reading `3` into a `Float` already works. Open: whether `Number` is still wanted for JS interop beyond JSON.
  - **decided since**: values are shown as code everywhere the tools show them (`strata run`, verdicts, traces, messages: "No internal JSON", #191); `data-on-click` became numbered handlers; only the builtin `to_json` keeps the old tagged form.
  - **`Debug` and `Display`, as Python's `repr` and `str`** (user, 2026-11-21; built, branch `runtime-types`: `Display`'s method is `to_string`, `Debug`'s is `debug`, and an `impl` replaces either; see the `std.json` entry under Decisions). `show` (used by `print`, `to_string` and f-strings) was the compact JSON form, except that a top-level string, big integer or variant without data dropped the quotes. Decided instead: two derived traits that every type has by default, overridable like any other trait (`impl Display for T { fn to_string ... }`). `Debug` is the value as written in code: a record with its type name (`Queued { name: "a" }`), strings always quoted (`"hi"`), `Some("hi")`, `[Red, Blue]`, `Dict { "a": 1 }`, `(1, "x")`. `Display` is the readable form: by default the `Debug` form; `String`'s is the one built-in difference (the text without quotes). Collections display their elements with `Debug`, as Python does (`["a b"]` vs `["a", "b"]`). `print`, f-strings and `to_string` use `Display`.
- **Changes are git commits** (built, `git_commit` in main.rs, asked for by the user). After a logged change (`record`: edit / rename / move / add) and at `strata done`, the package's files are committed with the change's message (`git add -A -- .` and `git commit -- .` in the package folder, so other staged work stays out). Nothing during a pending change. Off with `auto_commit = false` in strata.toml or `--no-commit`; skipped outside git and during a merge in progress; a failed commit leaves the change made and says why. Tests that commit by hand after strata commands no longer need to; the log-splitting test uses `--no-commit` to keep several entries in one commit.
- **Editing in an editor** (built, `edit_in_editor` in main.rs). `strata edit [MODULE | SELECTOR]` at a terminal with nothing piped in (or with `--editor`) opens, in `$VISUAL` / `$EDITOR` / git's editor / `vi` (through `sh -c`), the whole package with no argument (one file per module, all given to the editor), a module (`shop`, `main`, or its file `shop/orders.strata`, which may be new; a module's name wins over a definition's, and a definition is named with its module: `shop.total`, `main.main` for the root module's `main`), or the definitions a selector picks (one file per module they are in). Piped, `strata edit shop` puts the definitions into `shop`; there is no `--module` for `edit`. What is saved goes through `apply_edit` like a piped edit; definitions deleted from the buffer are removed. If it doesn't parse or check, the editor reopens with the errors on top (`// ✗ ` lines, dropped when read back); saving unchanged gives up. Errors go on top of the file of the definition they are in.
- **Removing or adding variants in a pending change** (built, `fix_matches` in pending.rs). Patterns can't be holes, so `match`es are fixed first: arms of removed variants are dropped (or only their alternative in an `a | b` pattern; nested ones like `Some(Pending)` too), a `match` on the changed type without a catch-all gets an arm `NewVariant => ?pending_N` (`Lost(_)`, `Lost { .. }`), and a `match` with no arm left becomes a hole. The edit prints what was dropped. Then expression holes as before (the expression walk skips patterns). Not yet: `let` / `for` patterns naming a removed variant still reject the edit.
- **Pending changes** (built, `src/pending.rs`). An `edit` whose own definitions check but that breaks other code isn't rejected: each broken use in the erroring definitions (a call, reference, constructor, method call or `is` test naming a definition whose interface changed, outermost first) becomes `?pending_N`, recorded with what it was and why, and the package enters a pending change (`.strata/pending.json`: the files before, the holes, the renames). Edits, renames and moves during it only write files (`record` in main.rs); `strata status` lists the holes; `strata done` logs the whole change as one entry (from the files before to now, with its renames), refusing while its holes are open unless `--keep-holes` (they become `?todo_N`); `strata abort` restores the files. (`strata ci` used to refuse `?pending_*` holes; it is gone, and every command reports holes.) Not yet: one pending change per package; a use the edit broke compatibly in an erroring definition is also turned into a hole.
- **Storage: hash references, and propagation** (discussed 2026-11-20; direction agreed, not built). The user prefers storing definitions by hash with references by hash and names in a separate table (Unison's model: renames become free, merges of new code can't conflict, several agents can add code at once), over a binary encoding of the same files (the encoding itself is now decided: see the next item). On an edit, dependents follow automatically when they still type-check ("option 1"), and otherwise get a to-do. The user's addition: while a to-do is open, the references to the old version are replaced with typed holes, so the agent sees exactly what to fill in. Before building: settle how a hole keeps what it replaced (the old call, for the agent), and that code with holes still checks but can't run. Status (2026-11-22): the propagation half is built as pending changes (`src/pending.rs`: broken uses become `?pending_N`). The storage half is not: module files hold self-contained, hashed definitions, but references are stored by qualified name, not by hash, there is no names table, and no object per hash (renames still rewrite every reference; two agents adding to one module still touch one file).
- **Storage: the tree, in binary** (decided with the user 2026-11-20; built: packages are stored in it, see below). Code is stored as the syntax tree, not as text: the AST is the language, and the current syntax is one front end among possible others (Nim-, Python-, C-, Haskell-like). The encoding is a compact binary format, not JSON (no serialization round-trips; big integers stored exactly). Ideally a module's `.strata` file *is* that binary, and text is rendered only when needed: `strata edit` already opens a module or definitions as text in the editor and parses what is saved, `render` / `view` print it. Rendered text files, if kept at all, are a read-only view that git ignores. Every change then goes through the tools (`edit`, `rename`, `move`), which check it and answer immediately; with a cache, several agents (the user guesses 3–4) could work in one codebase at once. Before building, settle:
  - ~~the format~~ **built**: our own, not a crate such as `postcard`, because Strata should be able to read and write it itself once bootstrapped (user). `src/binary.rs`, specified in `docs/binary-format.md`. LEB128 integers (zigzag for signed), one tag byte per enum variant, fields in order, strings by index into a table; canonical; corrupt input is an error, never a crash; no compatibility across format versions.
  - **Module files with a header** (built, branch `binary-header`, discussed with the user 2026-11-20: "header files from first principles, but inside the file"). One module file per module (now the `.strata` files themselves), or a bundle of them (`strata pack app.strata`). The header lists each definition's name, kind, `pub`, hash, interface hash, code hash, dependencies and size; each definition is self-contained (its own strings) and stores its interface (bodies as `()`) before its bodies. `store`'s hashes are now those of the binary encoding (no more printing to hash). Measured on `examples/tasks`: all headers 14 µs, all interfaces 82 µs, everything 166 µs (with each definition's hash checked), against 735 µs to parse the text. Files are larger than the first single-file format (27 KB vs 14 KB; 20 KB of source): three 32-byte hashes per definition, and strings repeated per definition. Not done yet: the tools don't use the header (they decode everything, then recompute hashes and the graph); see the next steps.
  - **Next steps, agreed with the user:** (5) read module files in parallel; (6) cache derived results (inferred types, check results) in `.strata/` by hash, and check a module against the interfaces of the modules it uses, re-checking dependents only when an interface hash changes; (7) a long-running process that loads once and reloads only files whose hash changed. Also: interned names in the AST (`Symbol(u32)` instead of `String`), only if profiling shows string handling is the cost.
  - **Binary is the format, `.strata` its extension** (user, 2026-11-20; built, branch `binary-main`). Packages and scripts store only binary module files, named `.strata`; nothing reads text as code any more. Text comes in only through edits: `strata edit` (stdin or the editor; outside a package, `strata --file F edit` writes a script or a folder's module without a log; since branch `script-edit-check` it then checks it, decided with the user: what doesn't check is still written, its errors printed, exit code 1) and `add` of a text file; `render` / `view` / the viewer print it, `render --split` writes `.st` text files that are never read back. `strata upgrade` rewrites each file as the bytes strata writes for it (it replaced `ci --fix`, user 2026-11-22). Git: `.gitattributes` marks `*.strata binary diff=strata merge=strata`; `setup-git` sets `strata render --file` as the textconv, so `git diff` shows text; the merge driver decodes the three sides, merges their text definition by definition and writes a module file, and a conflict leaves the file as text with markers until `strata resolve FILE`. The repository's `std/`, `examples/`, test data and the compiled prelude are binary (decided by the user: GitHub shows no diff for them). The log still carries each change's text (it is history, replayed by parsing). Open:
    - **changing the format** (decided by the user 2026-11-21): no backwards compatibility while the format isn't settled. `VERSION` stays `1`; a change to the tree converts every file in the repository in the same change (render with the old strata, read back with the new: docs/binary-format.md). Version numbers, and perhaps reading older versions, start once the format is settled. Each such change still touches every file, and other branches then conflict on binary files;
    - branches that edited text `.strata` files before this landed conflict with the binary files: re-apply the edit with `strata edit` (or render, merge, read back).
  - **Interned names** (built, branch `interned-names`): every identifier in the AST is a `Symbol` (`src/symbol.rs`), a `u32` into one process-wide table of texts; equal names are equal numbers. Reading a symbol's text takes no lock (append-only chunks); interning takes a mutex. The lexer interns identifiers as it reads them, the decoder once per string of a definition's table, the encoder looks names up by number. The resolver's scopes and tables, the checker's types (`Ty::Con`, `Ty::Rigid`), substitutions and `Env` are keyed by symbols; `x.sym()` converts (free on a symbol). Symbols order by their text, so sorted output doesn't change. Measured on `examples/tasks` (`src/bench.rs`, quiet machine): check 1.6 ms → 0.95 ms, resolve 200 → 137 µs, clone 68 → 38 µs, parse 570 → 520 µs; decoding unchanged (about 170 µs: the per-definition string tables, and checking each definition's hash). Not done: the prover's values (`Val`) still name records, variants and fields with `String`; `Decl::name()` / `qname()` still return `String` (they are map keys in many places).
  - **Faster decoding, after a fast-lexer article** (user sent https://xnacly.me/posts/2025/fast-lexer-strategies/, 2026-11-20; ideas, not built). Decoding runs at about 160 MB/s today, against that lexer's 580–850 MB/s; the time goes into allocation (a `Box` per node, a `String` copy per name) and checking each definition's hash. What carries over: (a) **interned names** (built, see above): the AST holds `Symbol(u32)` instead of `String`, so decoding a name reads an integer, names compare and hash as integers, and the file's string table maps to symbols once per distinct string (the article's zero-copy string windows and per-atom hashing); (b) **a built-in string table** shared by every file (`Int`, `String`, `List`, `Option`, `self`, `it`, …) with fixed indices, like its prehashed keywords: shorter files, and those names are known without a lookup; (c) **an arena / flat AST** (nodes in arrays, children as `u32` indices): one allocation per module, free in one go (the article: parsing 2.1x faster, freeing 26x); the most invasive change; (d) **mmap** the module files, for large codebases only (ours are kilobytes). Not applicable: jump tables / computed goto (a one-byte tag in a `match` already compiles to one), character classification and SIMD scanning (there is no text to scan).
  - **Resolved references (step 4)** (user decided 2026-11-21: store resolved trees, print short names; built, branch `resolved-trees`). Every reference to a definition is stored by its qualified name; text shows each as short as the module's `use`s allow, so it is normalised (`orders.Line` written where `Line` is imported shows as `Line`). `resolve::stored_form` qualifies on write, `resolve::written_form` shortens on read: the resolver's walk in two new modes, which rewrite names only (not the desugaring or static calls `resolve` does). In memory, every tool still works on the written form. Consequences: a `move` reports every definition whose stored references changed, not only those whose text did; the merge driver merges each side shown alone, so another module's names stay in full in a conflict's text. Not done:
    - ~~the checker still resolves on load~~ (branch `stored-load`): programs are now worked on as stored, and shortened only to be shown (render, view, the viewer, the editor, diffs). Renames and moves can't capture names any more. The checker still runs the resolver over the stored form (about 150 µs on `examples/tasks`, mostly a pass that changes nothing but method names and declared names); a lighter path for stored code is possible, but `strata check` spent far more elsewhere: it resolved and type-checked the program three times (`types::check`, `Env::new`, `verify::static_refinements`). Now once (`types::check_and_elaborate`, branch `check-once`): `full_check` on `examples/tasks` went from 3.8 to 1.3 ms, `strata check` from 8 to 5 ms (2.5 ms of that is starting the process);
    - hashes: the header hashes the stored bytes, while the log and verdicts hash the written form (`store::self_hash`); they should be one;
    - a module file shown alone (`git diff`) shortens from its own `use` lines; two modules imported with `*` that both define a name can't be told apart without loading them.
  - how it relates to the hash store above: a binary file per module, or one object per definition under its hash plus a names table;
- **Type checker speed** (user asked to look at it, 2026-11-21; first pass built, branch `checker-speed`). Profiled with macOS `sample` on a loop (`src/bench.rs`: `profile_check`; `allocations` counts allocations, which machine load doesn't blur). Done: declarations borrowed from the environment instead of cloned, error labels and hole obligations written only when shown (`Label`, `Lazy`), FxHash for every map (`src/fx.rs`), expression types recorded only for the compiler, and the environment for checking built from signatures. `examples/tasks`: a check 1.35 → 0.70 ms, 28,300 → 17,700 allocations; `strata check` 5.0 → 4.4 ms end to end (2.3 ms of it is starting the process). Left for later (user, 2026-11-21: good enough for now), by size:
  - types (`Ty`) cloned and dropped everywhere (about 20% of a check): sharing them (`Rc`) or interning them (a type is a number into a table, like symbols) would remove most of it;
  - resolution (13%), which also resolves the standard library on every check of a program that uses it: it could be resolved once;
  - the environment still copies every declaration (and the elaborated one, bodies included): it could borrow the program;
  - interning strings during the check (`Symbol::new`, 5%): call sites that turn a symbol into a string and back.
- **Verification fast enough to run with every check** (user, 2026-11-20; direction, partly built: see "`verify`'s speed"). If agents wait on verification it isn't worth having: the goal is a few milliseconds for a short function, so verifying can run alongside type checking. Measured on `examples/tasks` (67 definitions) on 2026-11-20: `check` 10 ms; `verify` 13 s, of which 108 solver runs took 11 s. Since then functions and queries run on all cores, the fuzzer races the solver, and std's termination is cached (all examples: 142 s → about 29 s). Verdicts are already cached by content hash in a package, so an edit only re-verifies what changed and what depends on it. Ways to get there:
  - ~~keep the solver running~~: Z3 processes kept running, one per core, a script after another (built, branch `speed-verify`; see "`verify`'s speed"); cvc5 still a process per script; linking Z3 as a library not tried;
  - two tiers: a fast pass next to the type check with a small budget per query, where anything that doesn't finish is reported as still being checked; and a slow pass in the background (full limits, fuzzing, the cross-check);
  - a verification server, like a language server: it keeps the syntax tree, the checker's environment, the call graph and the verdicts in memory, re-verifies on each change, and answers commands, the viewer and agents (through the Strata MCP server). `strata ui` is already a server; this pairs with storing the tree in binary (load once) and the Strata IDE;
  - ~~make the cache as modular as the proofs~~ (built for proofs and termination's loops, branch `speed-verify`): a proof of a caller uses each callee's contract, not its body, so proofs are kept by the callees' interfaces (`interface_keys`), except helpers the prover sees through (`transparent`: by their code); a kept proof is cross-checked again (the cross-check runs the callees). Fuzzed verdicts and counterexamples stay keyed by the closure (fuzzing runs the callees): their solver answers could be kept by interface too, the fuzzing alone run again;
  - ~~run independent queries in parallel~~ (built, #193).
  To decide: what an agent sees while the slow pass runs, and whether `edit` waits for the fast pass.
- **Strata IDE**: the viewer as a standalone desktop app (Tauri; the backend is already Rust), speaking ACP (Agent Client Protocol) so agents such as Claude Code or Codex run inside it, with a Strata MCP server giving them structural tools (slices, check, edit, rename). The viewer would show live where each agent is working (log entries tagged with the agent's session). Idea stage. (The browser viewer now runs agents over ACP and shows where they work: "Viewer as a workspace"; no MCP server: it reads `strata` commands from the agent's shell tool.)

## Ideas

### Language

#### Ranges, slices and comprehensions (built 2026-11-20)

Built on the binary-format branch (format version 3: `Expr::Range`, `Expr::Slice`, `Expr::Comp`); see docs/language.md. Decided along the way:

- `a..b` excludes `b`, `a..=b` includes it; a range is an eager `List[Int]` (iterators remain their own feature). Both ends are required, except in a slice.
- Slicing uses ranges, not Python's `:` (one syntax for both): `xs[a..b]`, `xs[..b]`, `xs[a..]`, `xs[..=b]`, on lists and strings, clamped as `slice` was. No negative indices: in a range `-3..0` already means the integers from -3, so `xs[..-1]` meaning "all but the last" would read two ways.
- `xs[i]` is the element, not an `Option`: `at(xs, i)` requires `0 <= i < len(xs)`, a runtime fault otherwise, and an obligation for `verify` like division by zero ("index in range"). `get(xs, i)` stays for the `Option`. Not yet: `d[k]` on dicts (the same, with "key present"). Assigning one, `xs[i] = v`, is `xs = set_at(xs, i, v)` with the same obligation (see "Assigning a list's element").
- Comprehensions: `[e for p in xs where c for q in ys where d]`, with `where` as in refinements; patterns allowed (`for (a, b) in ps`).
- The tree keeps them as written (they render back as written); `resolve` rewrites them into `range`, `slice`, `map`, `filter` and `fold` (`src/desugar.rs`), so the checker, prover and LLVM never see them. Error messages show the builtin calls.

Decided by the user 2026-11-21 (not built):
- The builtins behind the range and slice syntax (`range`, `slice`, the step) become special methods (`@range`, `@slice`, `@step`; see "Derived traits, `comptime` and special methods"): overloadable, implemented through std traits, but not called by name. Code uses the syntax.
- `map`, `filter` and `fold` are not special: they are ordinary methods on any iterator (the receiver), taking a lambda, and stay callable by name; comprehensions keep desugaring to them.
- `take` and `drop` stay ordinary functions (they have no syntax of their own; `xs[..n]` / `xs[n..]` remain the slice forms).
- Steps: `0..n by 2` (and `0..=n by 2`), desugared to `@step`. `by` becomes a keyword.

Still open:
- `xs where it > 0` as a filter on a value (it reads like a refinement type).

#### Other syntax nits (noticed 2026-11-20; not decided)

- **Dict and set literals** (built): `Dict { "a": 1 }`, `Set { 1, 2 }`, `Dict[String, Int] {}`, and comprehensions `Dict { w: len(w) for w in words }`, `Set { x % 3 for x in xs }` (`Expr::Dict`, `Expr::Set`, `Comp` with a `CompKind`; desugared to `dict` / `set`). In a `for` / `if` / `match` header they need parentheses, like record literals.
- **`except` inside a `for` header** reads badly: `for _ in 0..opts.times except { 1 } {`. Proposed: the renderer parenthesizes any header expression (`for`, `while`, `if`, `match`) that ends in a `}`: `for _ in 0..(opts.times except { 1 }) {`. The parser accepts both. Done instead (user's choice, 2026-11-21): `or { … }` / `??` for an `Option` (`Expr::Or`) and `except { … }` / `!!` for a `Result` (`Expr::Except`), each only on its own type, so `Result[Option[T], E]` isn't ambiguous. The short forms are what the renderer writes for a single-value, one-line fallback. So `for _ in 0..opts.times ?? 1 {`.
- **`len(xs)` and `xs.len()`** are both accepted, as for every builtin; the renderer could pick one form (method form for chains, say) so code reads the same everywhere.

#### Proven `ensures` narrow the result at the call site (raised by the user 2026-11-22; not decided, not built)

A function's proven `ensures` that ties the result's member to its inputs becomes the result's type where it is called, so a caller that knows something about the arguments gets the narrowed result without a `match` or an unwrap. `verify` already uses a callee's contract at each call site; what is new is the type checker narrowing with it. Since types are sets and refinements are types, `ensures o is Paid ==> it is Shipped` is the result's type at a call with a `Paid`: a return type that depends on the arguments, used by ordinary narrowing, `match` coverage and codegen (no tag check). Dafny, F* and Liquid Haskell let postconditions reach callers as proof facts; TypeScript's overloads and conditional types do the type half without proof; the two together, without type-level programming, is Strata's.

Examples:
- **Lookup after a membership test.** `fn get(self: Dict[K, V], k: K) -> Option[V] ensures it is Some <==> self.contains(k)`; inside `if users.contains(id) { … }`, `users.get(id)` is a `Some(User)`. One total function would do for both `get` and `at` (the `requires` that faults otherwise): callers that can show the key is there get the value, the others match.
- **State transitions (typestate in one type).** `fn ship(o: Order) -> Order ensures o is Paid ==> it is Shipped ensures o is Pending ==> it is Pending`; in a `Paid` arm, `ship(o).tracking` reads without matching again. No `ship(o: Paid) -> Shipped` beside a version for an `Order`.
- **Combinators that keep the variant.** `map_err(r, f) ensures it is Ok <==> r is Ok` (the user's first example, wrapping an error's message): after `r` is matched as `Ok`, a chain `r.map_err(wrap).map(normalize)` is still `Ok`.
- **Validation.** `parse_port(s) -> Result[Port, PortError] ensures all_digits(s) && 1 <= len(s) && len(s) <= 4 ==> it is Ok`: a literal or a checked string needs no error handling.

Proposed shape (Claude):
- Only a **proven** `ensures` narrows ("proven ⇒ no runtime check"): an unproven one would be unsound, or need a runtime check, i.e. a fault.
- **Two levels.** (a) Conditions the checker already decides: `param is T`, refinements, facts from `match` arms; the checker substitutes the arguments into the `ensures` (transitions, combinators). (b) Any condition (`self.contains(k)`, `all_digits(s)`): decided by the SMT backend at the call site, so the narrowing comes from `verify` or a cached proof, not the plain checker (lookup, validation).

Open questions:
- Does `if cond { … }` narrow on a proven condition (needed for the lookup), not just `match` and `is`?
- With `==>` only the `then` side narrows; does an `else` branch need `<==>`, and should the checker suggest it?
- How the narrowed type shows in the viewer and in `render` (hover on the call: `Shipped`, from which `ensures`).
- What a caller's narrowing depends on when the callee's body changes: a callee whose `ensures` stops being proven breaks its callers' types, so the proof is part of its interface (and of the interface hash).

#### Default values for record fields (built 2026-11-20)

`field: T = value` in a record (or a variant's record payload); see docs/language.md, "Records and defaults". Decided: any expression with no variables in scope (not the other fields), checked against the field's type where the type is defined, and evaluated each time it is used. It applies in constructors (`desugar::defaults` fills them in after resolution), JSON input (`std.json`'s derived `from_json`), and the command line (a scalar field with a default is an option, `--times 3`; a `List` field's default is used when no arguments are left). Mocks and fuzzing still generate every field. Not done: defaults for other types (`type Port = Int where … = 8080`), which the user set aside; a flag whose default is `true` can't be turned off from the command line yet (`--no-x`).

### Running while verifying, then swapping in proven code (user, 2026-11-24; not built)
- **Run first, verify alongside** (direction chosen by the user): in JIT mode (`strata run`), a program whose verdicts aren't cached starts at once, compiled **with all its runtime checks**, while `verify` runs concurrently; a note says it is being verified while it runs. A check that would fail still fails as a fault at run time, so nothing is unsafe in the meantime: unproven checks only cost speed. Verify's verdicts are cached as now, so the next run compiles with the proven checks left out (as `build` does). A counterexample is reported when found (stopping the program is a policy choice, not needed for safety).
- **Not chosen**: compiling as if every proof will succeed and stopping the program if one fails. Until the proof comes back the program runs without checks nobody has proven unneeded (wrong values, crashes on a removed bounds check), and what it did (files written, output) can't be undone. Only a program asking for no capabilities could afford it, and a removed bounds check can still crash before verify finishes.
- **Later: hot swapping.** As proofs arrive, the JIT replaces a function's checked version with its faster one while the program runs.
- **Later: live edits of a running program** (user): the same swapping applied to edits. An edit whose definitions check and verify (its contracts, refinements, the callers' obligations) is a safe change to a running program, so it can replace the running code; an edit that breaks other definitions or leaves holes isn't applied until they're fixed and the edit is done (`strata done`), and then the change happens at run time. Strata's guarantees are what make this safe, unlike hot reloading elsewhere. Open: what happens to state whose type changed (a record gaining a field), to running loops and tasks inside replaced code, and how an edit reaches a running process (the viewer, a socket, the JIT's own session).

### Speed: strata compiles itself in under a second (goal set by the user 2026-11-24; not built)
The next goal once Strata builds itself without Rust: `strata` compiling `compiler/` in under a second. Baseline (2026-11-24, the self-built `target/strata`): `strata --file compiler check` 48 s, `strata build compiler --no-verify` 25 s (unoptimized, 1.2 GB). A `sample` profile of that build (time at the top of the stack; compiled Strata frames don't unwind yet, there is no `"frame-pointer"="all"`): allocation, freeing and reference counting 31%; LLVM 21% (a third of it parsing the IR text); comparing strings and trees (`str_cmp`, `memcmp`, `eq:ast.Expr`) 10%; the call stack's frames (`strata_enter` / `strata_leave` through thread-local storage) 9%; searching dictionaries and sets kept as sorted lists 5% (now hash tables, below); the compiler's own logic under 10%. Causes found: every name is a `String`; no node has an identity, so passes tag calls by their path as text (`f#0.1.3`) and find rewritten children again by comparing whole trees (`check.tag` / `check.replaced`, quadratic); `expanded` desugars and derives the whole program again (up to 8 times) each time more of std is loaded; everything is redone for the whole program on every build although each definition already has its hashes. Directions agreed with the user:
- **Hash on edit, never again.** A definition's hashes are computed when it is stored (edits are small, so this costs nothing), with an existing SHA-256 called natively, not ours written in Strata. Everything after that compares the stored hashes: reading a module file trusts them (today `decode_module` hashes every definition again), cache keys are built from them (a Merkle tree), never by hashing large text. Hashes stay cryptographic: they leave the machine (module files, git merges, the shipped cache), where a collision would be wrong code; cheap hashes are for inside the compiler (hash maps, interning).
- **Names as symbols.** Each module file's string table is read once into a single table, and names in the tree are symbols (integers), compared as integers; strings are copied from the table into the IR when needed. The strings themselves are immortal (count 0, as literals already are in compiled code), so nothing counts them. Names the compiler makes (qualified names, instances `f[Int]`, path tags, `$lam…`) become structured keys (a pair of symbols, an instance's number), not text joined and interned again. Tables keyed by symbol can be arrays indexed by it; dictionaries as hash maps, for what remains, are a smaller gain (5%).
  - *Measured first (2026-11-24, measure-c's `--profile`; built, branch `speed-symbols`)*: most of the 10% was not comparing names but two quadratic loops over them. `check.atoms` filtered every constructor of the program by its owner for each sum it met (`dict_get[String, Shape]`, 10% of `check` alone), as did `codegen.ctors_of` in `build`: a sum's constructors are now kept beside the table (`check.Types.variants`). `check.expression_errors` split every function's `"module name"` for each function (4%); `main.closure_keys` walked each of `verify`'s closures over sets of names (5% of `build`), `main.call_refs` copied a set held in a dictionary per use (3%), and `build` cut the whole IR into lines to find two of them (2%). After these: `str_cmp` 7.5% → 1.4% of `check`'s samples (`memcmp` 4.3% → 2.3%), 6.2% → 1.9% of `build`'s; the functions changed lost about 2,700 of `check`'s 16,000 samples (17%; wall times are too noisy with six streams building at once to say more).
  - *What symbols would still gain*: every `String`-keyed dictionary and set operation together is 12% of `check` (inclusive, with the copies they make of values), of which comparing the strings is about 4%: the rest goes with `dict-hash` and `rc`. So the whole-tree change (every name field of `ast`, every module of `compiler/`, ~3000 sites) is worth about 3–4% now: to do after `dict-hash` lands (hashing a string key, not comparing, is then the cost) and when the tree is stable, not before. Its first step is written (branch `speed-symbols-table`): `compiler/c/symbols.c`, one table for the process, and `ast.Sym` (an `Int`) with `ast.sym` / `ast.text_of`. Decisions there: a symbol is a 61-bit hash of its text, not the order it was first met (first-met numbers would order tables differently from one run to the next, with the cache warm or cold, or threads: the bootstrap needs the same bytes twice); a collision is refused, never silent; `Int` and not `distinct` (a `distinct` value is a boxed block in compiled code), so an interpolation `f"{name}"` of a symbol type-checks and prints a number: each conversion step must look for those; an `extern c` `String` is copied each way, so the text is fetched only where it is printed (the IR, messages).
- **`static` values** (user): a value built at run time that lives until the program ends, declared `static`: allocated once and immortal (its count 0), so it is never counted nor freed. Most programs won't need it; it is for performance. Literals already are constants this way. Better still, codegen emits no `dup` / `drop` at all for a type known to be immortal or not counted (a symbol is an `Int`).
- **Less counting**: what a phase builds and never changes (trees, types, tables) allocated in an arena, its objects immortal, freed together; parameters borrowed where the callee doesn't keep them. Today a `get` on the constructors' table copies a `Shape` out and drops it (about 3% alone).
- **Stored desugared** (user): the stored tree is the program without its sugar, and only the printer knows the sugar: `render` (and the viewer, and `edit`'s text) puts it back. Fewer kinds of node for every pass, and nothing desugared twice. Conditions: (1) each piece of sugar becomes a shape of its own, so the printer gives back exactly what was written (a comprehension becomes its own built-ins, not `map` / `filter` calls the user could have written; `range(a, b)` shown as `a..b` is normalizing, which is fine, as text is canonical already); (2) only sugar that depends on nothing else: `derive`, `comptime e`, refinement aliases (`$where_0`) and split unions (`Shape$own`) depend on other definitions, so they stay as written and their expansions are cached by hash, expanded once (not once per round of loading std). A format change: the repository's files are re-encoded once (the version stays 1).
- **Cached per definition**: each stage's result (desugared and expanded form, inferred types, LLVM IR or object code) cached by the definition's `code_hash` and the `interface_hash`es of what it refers to, so changing a body doesn't redo its callers; a generic function's instances by its code hash and the type arguments. Per-definition IR with stable symbol names is also what the JIT needs to swap one function in a running program (see "Running while verifying, then swapping in proven code").
- **Concurrent**: the program's dependency graph drives the work, each part sent on a channel to worker threads as soon as what it needs is done (decoding, inference of each body once every signature is known, code generation); LLVM with one context and module per worker (a module, or a part of the call graph), objects made in parallel and linked by lld. Output stays deterministic (assembled in the graph's order, never in the order threads finish), since the bootstrap needs the same bytes twice. Later, building IR through LLVM's C API instead of writing and parsing text.
- **Built (streams cache-parallel and parallel-llvm, 2026-11-24): LLVM in parallel parts, objects, IR and executables cached.** `build` hands the IR to `toolchain_objects` (c/toolchain.cpp, now c/toolchain.c), which cuts the text into parts: runs of whole functions, a part ending at a function whose name hashes to 0 mod 8 once it holds 128 KB (512 KB at -O2), so where parts end depends only on the functions around them and an edit changes only its own part. Each part is a module of its own: its functions, the private constants and internal functions they reach (copied, renamed `@.lN` in the order the part first names them, so the program-wide numbering of `.str.N` doesn't reach a part's text), a `declare` for every other function it calls, and at -O2 a copy of each small function (up to 2 KB of IR) it calls, `available_externally`, to inline (as ThinLTO imports). The parts compile on one thread a core, each with its own LLVM context and target machine, largest first; each part's object is kept in the cache (`objects/`) under the SHA-256 of its text and of what it is compiled for (triple, CPU, level, LLVM's version, `--profile`; not the compiler's identity, so bootstrap stages share objects), and the list of an IR's objects under the hash of the whole text (`objects/lists/`), so an IR built before is neither cut nor hashed part by part again; the objects are linked in the parts' order, the same bytes whichever thread finishes first. Kept too: core.rt's object (key: the compiler's identity, core's files, `--profile`), runtime.c's and a `[native]` package's C objects (key: the C compiler, the flags, the file's bytes; a header it includes is not in the key), the program's IR (`main.built_ir`, one entry a program, under the bytes of every file it is made of; it returns codegen's errors with it, so a kept IR, which has none, isn't searched for them again: 0.34 s, the prelude's `str_find` going byte by byte over 55 MB), what `verify` proved as codegen reads it (`main.proofs_kept`: `proofs_lines` under the bytes of the module files and of verify's record; 0.43 s of every build was `keys_over` and `call_refs` working it out again), and a `[native]` package's linked executable (one for each output path, under `toolchain.link_key`: the link's arguments with each named file's or folder's size and time of change, so an LLVM library changed outside the cache relinks; cloned on APFS). `STRATA_LLVM_PARTS=0` compiles the whole module as one part, to compare. **Measured split vs whole** (the user's rule: drop the splitting if -O2 split builds slower or a one-body edit doesn't improve; `build` of a copy of `compiler/`, `--no-verify`, a machine shared with five other streams, load 8–13 on 11 cores, so instructions are steadier than wall): -O0 cold, LLVM 1.0 s split / 6.1 s whole (whole build 28.5 / 28.6 s, 338 / 333 G instructions); -O0 after a one-body edit 12.5 s / 148 G split (LLVM 0.02 s, 1 part of ~200) vs 16.3 s / 217 G whole (LLVM 6.4 s); -O2 cold 30.6 s / 836 G split (LLVM 9.8 s wall on 11 threads) vs 72.4 s / 789 G whole (LLVM 51.3 s on one: splitting costs 6% more instructions, as small callees are compiled in each part that imports them, and is 5x faster); -O2 after the edit 10.2 s / 154 G split (LLVM 0.5 s) vs 62.1 s / 673 G whole; the -O2 compiler built in parts runs `check compiler` in 243.53 G instructions / 63.8 G cycles, built whole in 243.54 G / 63.8 G: the same. So the splitting stays. **With the caches** (this branch's compiler, -O0): cold 25.8 s / 331 G; warm, nothing changed: 12.1 s (main) → 0.15 s / 1.4 G instructions (0.5 s when `-o` names another file: the C compiler links it, 0.36 s); after a one-body edit 17 s / 141 G, of which LLVM 0.03 s and the link 0.4 s: the rest is the front end (comptime 9.5 s, codegen IR 4.1 s), the other streams' work. Left: std's library and core.rt are still compiled as one module each at -O2 on one thread (cold: LLVM machine code 4.8 s + optimize 2.1 s): parts would make std's object several, and `StdLibrary` (a release ships it) is one `.o` and one `.dylib`; with the nothing-cold stream they are made once and carried, so this matters only the first time. lld in place of the C compiler's linker for `[native]` packages: measured the same (0.25 vs 0.33 s), not worth an SDK lookup. When the llvm-api stream builds modules through the C API, parts would be cut from the module (or built as one module a part from the start), each still keyed by its printed text or by its inputs.
  - *Built (stream parallel-front, branch `speed2-parallel-front`)*: `compiler/parallel`'s `map(xs, f)` shares a list out to `STRATA_JOBS` threads (default: the machine's cores; `STRATA_JOBS=1` none), each taking every n-th element, and puts the results back in `xs`' order, so it gives `xs.map(f)` however the threads ran. On it: check (`typed`, `type_errors_with`: each definition's inference), codegen (the queue of instances, in rounds: each instance of a round compiled alone, in its own arena, from the state the round began with, which it only reads; what it adds kept in small tables beside the shared ones, `new_strings`, `new_helpers`, ..., then merged in queue order, a global or helper made twice kept once; names a function makes start with its own symbol, `lambda.<owner>N`, and string constants are named by a hash of their bytes), and decoding the module files a command reads. The IR is byte-identical with any number of threads, and the bootstrap reaches its fixed point. **What tasks cost** (measured first, with 24 tasks building and walking trees): 29 s against 0.6 s on one thread, 776G instructions. (1) core.rt's small-object free lists sat behind one spinlock taken on every allocation and free while any task ran: now each thread has its own (`builtin thread static heap`, refilled 64 blocks at a time, handed back when the thread ends): 0.07 s on 11 cores. (2) Arenas were off inside tasks: now each thread has its own current arena. (3) A race: two threads sharing a list's buffer could both extend it in place into the same slot; the slot is now taken with an atomic add on the buffer's used count (copying unless the list was unique, tried first, made `push` quadratic). (4) With every worker reading the same tables, atomic `dup`/`release` were 56% of the workers' time and codegen took 4x the cycles of one thread: `strata_memory_keep_shared` (set by `parallel.map` while its workers run) keeps an object held more than once that a thread holds once more for good (a sticky count, never written again, never freed; not in check mode), and since each codegen instance runs in an arena, what a worker makes is never made sticky; a release at count 1 skips the atomic. After: codegen on 11 threads costs +14% cycles and +9% instructions over one thread. **Measured** (`build compiler --no-verify`, the fixed point against main's, a machine at load 13-20 from the other streams, so the totals are noise and the phases tell): cold, codegen IR 5.1 → 2.4 s, check (25 runs) 4.2 → 1.2 s, decode 0.51 → 0.36 s (instructions 363G → 396G); warm, codegen IR 3.8 → 1.8 s, check 0.45 → 0.23 s. `check` in compiler/: check 0.52 → 0.20 s, decode 0.74 → 0.55 s, 5.2 s in all either way (what is left: refinement probes 3 s, comptime's cache key 0.75 s, both one thread). Left: the rest of `build` is one thread (comptime, the cache key, untimed parts), and LLVM (stream parallel-llvm).
- **Arrays** (user): a built-in `Array` for core and code that must be fast, beside `List`. Lists stay as they are, vectors already (a header over a buffer with a capacity; `push` in place past the end, amortized O(1); slices share the buffer), but each element takes a 64-bit slot, so `List[U8]` is 8 bytes a byte. An array has a fixed size, its elements unboxed (`Array[U8]` is bytes; `I32`, `F64`), written in place (`a[i] = x` on a `mut` array), its indices proven in bounds by `verify` (so no check is left in compiled code). core does this today by hand with `Ptr`.
- **Dictionaries and sets as hash tables** (built, branch `speed-dict-hash`, the user's "`Dict` as a plain hash map"): a `Dict` or `Set` is a block laid out as the tuple `(List[E], List[Int])`: its entries (a dict's `(K, V)` pairs, a set's elements) in the order they came in, a removal closing the gap, and an index beside them (open addressing, linear probing, at most half full, each slot `tag << 32 | entry + 1` with 30 bits of the hash as a tag so most other keys are passed over without comparing; none for up to 8 entries, searched in order). The table code is C (runtime.c's `strata_tbl_…`: find, from, insert, remove, union / intersection / difference, in-place add and remove for `x = insert(x, …)`, equality, order, hash), called with functions codegen makes per key type on 64-bit slots: `@"tbl.hash:T"` over `@"hash:T"` (structural, agreeing with the order: values `cmp` finds equal hash the same; strings 8 bytes at a time, a union or a function 0) and `@"tbl.cmp:T"` over `cmp`. No order: `for`, `keys`, `items` and printing are the entries' order, which is the order things were inserted today but promised nowhere (docs say so); hashing is deterministic (no seed). `==` compares contents (same keys, equal entries), `<` / `sort` / a set of sets compare the entries sorted. `compiler/eval` keeps entries the same way (append, replace in place, remove closing the gap), so it prints what compiled code prints without hashing anything; its `==` compares the contents sorted. verify/smt models a dict or set by its size only, so nothing there assumed an order; a counterexample's empty dict is written `[]`, which codegen now turns into the empty table where a dict is wanted. **Set too, not a tree**: across `compiler/`, `std/`, the examples and every case, the only code that relied on a set's order was std.regex (three places: `to_list` of a set of class bounds or DFA states, now `sort(to_list(…))`); every other difference was printing order (goldens re-blessed, each diff checked to be a reordering). One table for both keeps one implementation, O(1) `insert` / `contains`; a sorted set would keep printing sorted but pay O(n) per insert, as before. **Measured** (one stage each, the same release build mode, `target/dev`, a busy machine): `strata build compiler --no-verify` 30-36 s → 24 s, `strata --file compiler check` (warm) 24-25 s → 18 s. The seed's codegen still calls the prelude's old sorted-list functions (`set_from`, `dict_get`, ...), so they stay in `runtime/prelude.strata` until the seed is a release with this change. Gotcha found (editing std, not fixed here): `strata --file std edit std.regex` (or from inside `std/`) auto-commits and rewrites all 16 std files with the module's name lost (`regex.Inst` in its definitions, programs then find no `std.regex`); `strata --file std/regex.strata edit` keeps the name but stores references that comptime's codegen can't compile (it falls back to the evaluator: `has_case_folding` isn't run by it); what worked: the file-mode edit, then `strata upgrade`, keeping only the std file it rewrote.
- **`verify`** (stream verify-speed, branch `speed-verify`; see "`verify`'s speed"): Z3 kept running, proofs kept by callees' interfaces, termination's loop findings cached, the encoder's string naming in one pass. `--no-cache`: std 46.6 s → 19.1 s CPU, `examples/tasks` 30.6 s → 15.2 s; cached: std 19.3 s → 2.6 s; by name in `compiler/` (19 functions), every verdict cached: 89 s → 27 s wall, 220 s → 27 s CPU (what is left is checking the program first).
- **Lean IR** (stream lean-ir, branch `speed2-lean-ir`; built): what `compiler/codegen` emits, the same program. A variable that isn't `mut`, a parameter, a pattern's name and a lambda's captured value are their SSA value (no `alloca`, store and load each); the result a `phi` at the exit (no slot); a block's slots loaded and stored as their type (`load ptr`, no `load i64` + `inttoptr`); a match arm's or a `for` element's names borrowed when the value outlives them unchanged; no field read for `_` or a name while testing a pattern; no fault block after an arm or pattern that can't fail, nor a test of a match's last constructor when the arms before took every other; a whole variable given to a `mut` parameter passed as its own slot (no copy in and back, no `dup`: the callee changes it in place, which also makes the compiled compiler faster); a bigger record's copy (`x.f = v` on a shared one) by a helper per type; `Int` `+ - * & | ^` and comparisons by a function per module (inlined at -O2; one `icmp` against a small constant); a list's element slot by `@"list.slot"`; `[]` is just the new list; `(a, b) := (x, y)` binds without a tuple; a variable's value leaving its scope or the function moved, not held and dropped; a `for` counter a `phi`; no `entry` block without slots; a cancelled task's drops (#428 made the compiler start tasks, so every call has a cancel point) by a chain of blocks, one a value, that the cancel points share. **Measured** on `compiler/`, same sources: IR 58.0 MB / 1.33 M instructions → 25.5 MB / 0.52 M before #428; after merging it, main's IR is 86.4 MB / 1.89 M (0.55 M of it cancel blocks) → 40.7 MB / 0.78 M. Before #425-#428: cold `build compiler --no-verify` 26.7 s / 326G instructions → 20.5 s / 253G (LLVM machine code 9.2 → 7.7 s, parse 2.2 → 1.0 s, codegen IR 2.6 → 1.4 s), warm 12.6 s / 150G → 8.0 s / 94G; after them (main's IR vs this one, both written by this branch's compiled codegen): cold 18.6 s wall, 38.3 s CPU, 370G → 17.3 s, 30.7 s CPU, 313G (LLVM's parts 1.7 → 0.9 s); `strata check` of `compiler/` with the compiler built by itself (warm): 67.2G → 56.5G instructions before #428, 78.8G → 64.7G after. Left: the cancel points (21k checks, 0.3 M instructions) are after every call in every function, although only a function that may wait (`await`, a channel, `sleep`…), directly or through what it calls, can be unwound: a call graph pass marking those would remove most of them; string interpolation as one runtime call instead of a chain of `strata_concat` (needs core.rt's twin too).
- **`verify`, round 2** (stream verify2, branch `speed2-verify`; see "`verify`'s speed"): every function encoded once, on worker threads, its solver questions asked as they come; termination's graph linear; std's termination findings kept by std's code. `--no-cache`: all of `compiler/` didn't finish in 300 s (killed at 2,000 of 3,100 functions) → 157-189 s (a loaded machine); std 13-16 s → 8.5 s; `examples/tasks` 9.8 → 5.6 s. The same verdicts everywhere (below).
- **Nothing cold** (user: what doesn't change is never redone; built, branch `speed2-nothing-cold`). (1) std's precompiled library is kept under the SHA-256 of its IR with LLVM's settings (`toolchain.object_settings`: LLVM's version, triple, CPU, -O level, frame pointers; `main.library_key`), not the compiler's identity, so any compiler that makes the same IR of std shares one library; which IR this compiler makes of std's files is kept as a pointer (`std-library-of/`, keyed by the compiler and std's and core's files hashed in C), so a warm build makes no IR at all and a new compiler (each bootstrap stage) makes std's IR (~1.5 s) but compiles it again only when it changed. core.rt the same (`main.core_rt_object`): it was compiled (codegen, then LLVM at -O2, ~1 s) on every build, warm ones too. (2) compile-time values are keyed by the code they run, not the compiler: a library's definitions by their stored code hashes (`lib_hashes`), the program's own as text, with core's files and the package's settings (`settings_stamp`); and a std module's `comptime` code is compiled with std and core only (`lib_world`), falling back to the whole program only when that doesn't compile. Before, each of the ~10 values of a build typed the whole program to learn its type and then compiled the whole program (25 runs of `check`): comptime 5.3 s → 1.0 s, its `check` 2.7 s → 0.4-0.75 s, the key 0.5 s → 0.04 s, cold. (3) Not built, measured not worth it now: check results stored per definition. `build --no-verify` type-checks nothing but codegen's `check.typed` (0.3-0.9 s for the whole compiler warm, in parallel on parallel-front's branch), and `strata --file compiler check` spends 0.7 s of its 10 s in type inference (refinement probes 2.6 s, decoding 1.1 s, the rest untimed); a stored inferred body would cost about what decoding it costs. (4) The untimed rest is timed (`build: …` phases while measuring): `codegen_errors` searched the 58 MB IR in Strata (0.55 s a build, now `strstr` in c/driver.c), `proofs_lines` hashed every closure of the program when nothing was proved (returns at once now), clang compiling `[native]` C and the C runtime each build (~0.8 s, kept by speed2-parallel-llvm). **Measured** (instructions retired, `build compiler --no-verify`, the machine shared with five other streams): cold 325G → 222G; warm 149G → 114G; a new compiler with a warm cache (what a developer sees after rebuilding `strata`) 327G (= cold: everything was keyed by the compiler) → 170G for an unoptimized new compiler (no LLVM for std or core.rt, no comptime). (5) A new compiler doesn't make std's IR again either (user, on #427): `strata build` links into each executable the hash of the sources it is built from (`main.sources_object`: the package's module files and its own C, std's files, core's, the prelude and the C runtime, hashed in C; a global `strata_build_sources` in an object of its own, kept by the hash: in the program's IR it would keep toolchain_objects from cutting it into parts; it replaces a weak empty one in c/cache.c), and what a compiler makes is kept under that hash instead of its identity (`main.maker_key`, `cache.build_sources`; `""`, the identity as before, in a compiler built by one that doesn't write it): the bootstrap's stages, an optimized and an unoptimized build of the same sources share std's library and core.rt with no IR made; any change to the compiler's code changes the hash. Measured: a new unoptimized compiler of the same sources with a warm cache 170G → 139G instructions, no `std library: IR` (1.5-1.9 s) nor core.rt's IR. Found while bootstrapping: the same compiler builds different bytes with a cold and a warm cache (one function of `eval` aligned differently in the -O2 object) when compile-time values are computed, i.e. run by the JIT in the same process, before the program is compiled; with a std library made cold it does not. It was hidden because every new compiler used to compute them again; now the bootstrap from main took 4 stages (seed, no hash, hash computed cold, warm = fixed point). Found and fixed (branch `fix-reproducible-builds`): LLVM 23 turns GlobalISel on for every AArch64 target machine, and the `-global-isel=0` the compiler parsed as a process-wide option (for FastISel at -O0) held only for the first module a process compiled; with GlobalISel, code made for the host's CPU (the JIT running compile-time values: apple-m3 here) leaves state behind, and the next `optnone` function (codegen marks functions over 1000 IR lines) compiled for apple-m1 got the host's alignments (`.p2align 6` for the function and its loops, not 2): `eval.builtin`. Shown outside strata by a small C program on LLVM's C API (an extracted function compiled alone, after a JIT, after an object for the host's CPU: only the last two differ). Now each target machine, the JIT's and the objects', is set to SelectionDAG (FastISel at -O0) by `LLVMSetTargetMachineGlobalISel` (`toolchain_selection`, c/jit.c), no command-line option; the objects' keys changed with it (`strata object 2`, `toolchain_settings` says `SelectionDAG`). Measured on compiler/ --release: a cold build and one with only its compile-time values, std's library and core.rt cached (every object compiled again, no JIT) are the same bytes (were 64 bytes apart in `__text`, 69 MB of the file shifted); the bootstrap from a seed of these sources is a fixed point at stage 2 (from v0.2.0: stage 3, its objects being made by the old selection). `tools/reproducible.sh [PACKAGE]` checks it (about a minute; it fails with v0.2.0). Also faster: -O0 objects were mostly made by GlobalISel (the option missed them): `LLVM: parts` for compiler/ cold 1.59 s -> 0.74 s, with objects alone rebuilt 1.03 s -> 0.84 s (43.0G -> 33.7G instructions for that build).
- **IR into LLVM** (stream llvm-api, branch `speed2-llvm-api`; built): `compiler/c/ir.c` reads the IR text codegen writes (the small, regular subset it uses: one instruction a line) straight into LLVM's C API (`LLVMAddFunction`, `LLVMBuild*`), values unnamed (the context discards names), and without the builder's constant folding (an operand stands in until the instruction is made), so the module is exactly LLParser's: `STRATA_IR_CHECK=1` builds both and compares them printed (equal for every module of a cold `build compiler`: program, std's library, core.rt, comptime), and the objects are byte-identical. Anything it doesn't know falls back to LLParser (`STRATA_DEBUG` says what). compiler/'s 58 MB: 2.0 s / 20G instructions (LLParser) -> 0.26-0.32 s / 3.9G. **FastISel at -O0**: LLVM picks GlobalISel for AArch64 at -O0; `-global-isel=0` (set once for the process: the JIT makes its own target machines) gives FastISel: machine code for compiler/ 4.5 s / 52G -> 2.5 s / 33G (the register allocator at -O0 was already the fast one). Whole warm `build compiler --no-verify`: 149G -> 113G instructions retired (-24%). *Measured and chosen*: codegen still writes text. A `sample` of `codegen IR` puts its string building (concatenation, copies, `emit`, `fresh`) at 15-20% of it (~0.3 s; half of `generate` is `new_gen`'s type checking), so calling the builder from each of codegen's ~660 emit sites, with operands as handles instead of text, would gain ~0.3 s more and touch every definition lean-ir changes: left for after lean-ir, with `ir.c`'s builder code as the C side of it.
- **The compiler's C, fast to build; the runtime never built by a release's user** (branch `c-glue-fast`; built). A first build (empty cache) spent 1.66 s in the C compiler, one file after another: `c/toolchain.cpp` 0.94 s at -O2 (802 lines, ~81,000 once preprocessed: not LLVM's headers, which it avoids, but the C++ standard library #425 brought in: `std::thread`, `vector`, `string`, `unordered_map`), runtime.c 0.53 s, solver.c 0.15 s, the rest under 0.1 s each. (1) `c/toolchain.cpp` is now C (`c/toolchain.c`: parts, their threads as pthreads, a hash table of names, hashing, caching, cloning) and the only C++ is `c/lld.cpp`, `toolchain_lld` wrapping `lld::macho::link` and `CommonLinkerContext::destroy` with hand-written declarations and no header at all: 0.75 s -> 0.20 s + 0.02 s. The same parts, objects and keys, checked by a harness running both on compiler/'s IR (185 parts at -O0, 65 at -O2: the same object lists and bytes; `files_key`, `link_key` equal). (2) `main.linked` gathers the C files not kept yet (runtime.c, a package's `[native]` sources) and compiles them at once, a C compiler process each (`toolchain.c_objects`: a task each waiting for its process), in a task started before LLVM compiles the program's parts and core.rt (`main.program_objects`); `--timings`' `C compiler: compile` is now the time waited for it after them. Cold `build compiler --no-verify`: that phase 1.66 s (7 files serially; 1.9-3.0 s at load 9) -> 0.38 s (at once, runtime.c the longest) -> 0.000 s (behind LLVM); 218G -> 203G instructions (clang no longer reads the C++ library; machine shared at load 9-11, so wall times 17.9-21.1 s before vs 12.7-21.4 s after say little); warm 1.10G -> 0.99G instructions, 0.08 s both. (3) A release ships the runtime made once: `tools/release.sh` compiles runtime.c to LTO bitcode (Homebrew's clang, `-O3 -flto -DSTRATA_CORE_RT`), links it with codegen's core.rt (`llvm-link`) and compiles the one module at -O3 with the compiler's own LLVM (`strata object -O 3`): `lib/strata/runtime-lto.o`, replacing `runtime.o` (unused), `runtime-core.o` and `core_rt.o` (-O2 each, calls between them never inlined). `linked` links a release's `runtime-lto.o` alone (`--profile` still compiles its own, with frame pointers). compiler/ linked with it: `check compiler` 52.1G -> 51.3G instructions (-1.4%), cycles the same. Checked: a release user's first build compiles nothing the release could ship (std's `std.o`, comptime values in the shipped cache, the runtime): `build wc --no-verify` with a fresh cache takes 0.11 s (its own IR, the sources object, lld); tools/release-check.sh now fails if a first build runs the C compiler, makes std's library or core.rt, or a runtime object. Not done: LTO of the runtime into the program (its parts are native objects; it would need bitcode parts and LTO in lld at each link, which is the compile this avoids). Found: `compile_part` (the parts) still reads IR with LLVM's own parser, not c/ir.c's (`toolchain_object` uses ir.c): kept as it was here (same output), worth switching (2.0 s vs 0.3 s on 58 MB, spread over the threads).
- **Cancellation checks only where a task can be cancelled** (stream cancel-points, branch `cancel-points`; built).
