Since #428 the compiler spawns tasks, so compiler/codegen put a cancellation check (`cancel_point_after`: an atomic load of `strata_cancel_any`, a branch, the way out dropping what the scopes hold) after every call in every function: 21.8k in compiler/, a third of its IR. Two steps, in `new_gen`: (1) a program's tasks can be cancelled only by a `race` (its losers) or a supervised task's panic, or inside `strata_test` (tests, fuzz cases, calls: a panic or a deadlock there cancels the run's tasks); any other program, the compiler included (`parallel.map`: `scope`, `spawn`, `await`), gets no checks at all (`cancels`). (2) Where tasks can be cancelled and panics don't leave functions (no `supervised spawn`, not std's library, not `--trace`), `waits_of` works out which calls may reach a wait, once per program: a graph of the calls by name (`f:K` a function, `i:m` every method `m`, `m:m` a call `x.m(…)`, which may also be a built-in calling what it is given, `closure` every lambda and function passed as a value, `i:next` from a `for` over what may be an iterator (a `Receiver` is one), `show` the `to_string`/`debug` methods, `implicit` refinements and defaults: if that waits, everything may), sorted into reverse edges and followed backwards from the waits (`await`, `scope`, `select`, `race`, a channel's `send`/`recv`, sleeps, reading input), each edge once. A call gets a check only when its callee may wait (`calls_wait`: by key; a method made later by its name; anything unknown may), a closure call when some lambda may, a trait value's method when some method of that name may. Measured (same sources, main's codegen vs this one): compiler/'s IR 41.5 MB / 797k instructions / 21.8k checks -> 26.9 MB / 535k / 0; cold `build compiler --no-verify` 203G -> 185G instructions (-9%; wall 14.4 -> 13.9 s on a loaded machine; the program's LLVM parts 1.09 -> 0.74 s); warm 1.05G -> 0.98G (0.09 s, all kept); `check compiler` by the self-built compiler unchanged (55.9G vs 56.3G instructions: its time is in core.rt and std's library, which keep theirs). Step (2) alone would leave compiler/ as it was: a lambda (`() -> typed(…)`) reaches `parallel.map`, and by name every `for` over an iterator may be over a `Receiver`; it pays off in programs that race (tested by `build-cancel-deep`: losers deep in calls, through a lambda, a function value, a list's `map`, a trait value and a bounded type parameter, every object freed; no check in a function that can't wait; none in a program that only spawns). Left: methods by name (`Fixed.take` checks because `Line.take` waits; per-type resolution needs the checker's types), and `supervised` programs keep every check (any panic leaves a function there).
- **Order** (proposed): (1) measure: frame pointers in the IR so profiles see Strata's frames, and per-phase timers; (2) desugar and derive once per definition, cached; (3) symbols; (4) immortal and arena objects, borrowed parameters; (5) the per-definition cache; (6) the concurrent pipeline and LLVM in parallel; (7) native SHA-256, no hashing on read. Steps 2-3 touch every module of `compiler/`: after the bootstrap work is merged.
- **Built: expand once** (branch `speed-expand-once`). The largest cost was not desugaring again but `comptime e`: `expanded` folded it in every round of loading std, and in the first round std.unicode's five tables could not be compiled (std.unicode_tables, which their code calls, was loaded only by the next round), so each fell back to `eval` (~1.5 s each, on every run: an error isn't cached), after type-checking the whole program for each value; `check` folded std's `comptime` again (by the JIT, then by `eval` in literal probes' compile) only to throw std's definitions away. Now `expanded` desugars each definition once (`desugar.desugar_more`: a round takes only the std definitions the last one's code named, with what was made before as context; `$where_k` numbered in the order first met, new ones appended), makes the `derive`s again while std grows (what they make reads std's code; cheap), and folds `comptime e` once std is loaded to a fixpoint (`derive.folded_in`; then the std its values name, the same way). `check`, `verify`, `holes` and the check summary expand only the package's own definitions (`derive.expand_in`); `std_reached` derives without folding. Node identity: `walk.expr_children_at` gives each child its place (its position in `kids`), so `check.tag`, `lam_marked` and `tag_fields` name a node by its path without finding the rebuilt children again by comparing whole trees (`check.replaced`, quadratic, is gone; it also gave two equal siblings the same tag). Measured with the bootstrap's fixed points before and after, back to back on the same (loaded) machine, warm caches: `strata --file compiler check` 34.6 s → 12.1 s (instructions 383 G → 161 G, cycles 98 G → 37 G); `strata build compiler --no-verify` 36.1 s → 20.5 s (instructions 376 G → 249 G, cycles 107 G → 66 G). Left: `reach_key` (the text of everything a `comptime` reaches, hashed in Strata: ~1.3 s once per run) wants the stored per-definition hashes; a persistent per-definition cache of this stage would key a definition by its `code_hash` with the `interface_hash`es of the record types whose defaults it fills, for a `derive` of the types and trait code it reads, for `comptime e` of what it reaches, and would want `$where_k` named by a hash of the refinement's text instead of a number.
- **Built: measuring, SHA-256 and floats in C** (branch `speed-measure-c`). `--profile` (or `STRATA_PROFILE=1`, inherited by what it starts) adds `"frame-pointer"="all"` to every function as LLVM parses the IR (c/jit.c's `toolchain_frame_pointers`, for objects and the JIT; the IR text is unchanged, and without the flag nothing differs), keys std's library apart and compiles runtime.c with frame pointers, so `sample` walks through Strata's frames: build a profiling compiler with `strata build compiler --no-verify --release --profile -o target/prof/strata`. `--timings` (or `STRATA_TIMINGS=1`) writes each phase's own time, time in all and count on stderr when the command ends (compiler/timings marks decode, load std, resolve, desugar, derive, comptime and its cache key, check, other checks, refinement probes, codegen IR; the C glue LLVM's parse, optimize and machine code, the JIT's, and lld). SHA-256 is CommonCrypto's (`CC_SHA256`, c/cache.c), the same values; reading a module file trusts the header's hashes, computed when the definition was stored (`binary.read_checked` checks them, for damaged files). Float show and parse are the C library's again with core.rt too (the shortest digits in at most three `snprintf`/`strtod` tries, not 17; output the same on 300,000 random doubles). The profile with Strata's frames, warm (`check` 16 s with a profiling compiler): `comptime` folding is two thirds of `check` (`derive.fold_comptime` 67% inclusive: each `comptime` program is type-checked and generated again, `check.typed` 44%), `reach_key` 20% (printing every reached definition as text to hash it: 10%), `binary.decode_module` 7% (5% hashing on read, now gone); `build`: LLVM 27% (machine code 17.5%, parsing the IR 7%), `codegen.generate` 27%. Measured on a machine shared with five other builds (noisy), second and third warm runs: `check` 33 s → 22 s, `build compiler --no-verify` 38 s → 28 s. Left: comptime cache keys from stored hashes instead of text (`reach_key` hashes the expanded program's text: derived definitions and settings read by `derive` aren't in any header, so this waits for per-definition expansion keys, the cache stream); writing a module re-encodes and hashes every definition of the package (`codebase.encode_files`), not only those that changed; core.float's `float_show`, `float_fixed` and `float_parse` are unused now.
- **Open**: whether record defaults are filled in when stored (call sites then keep the old value when a default changes, consistent with content addressing, as in Unison) or stay as written; an array's size known while compiling (`Array[U8, 64]`) or when made; its element types; `static`'s exact form.
- **Built: less counting (stream rc, branch `speed-rc`, 2026-11-24).** Measured with `/usr/bin/time -l` (instructions retired, cycles: steadier than wall time on a shared machine), `check` made 1.0e9 objects (73k live at its end). (1) core.rt's small-object allocator took a spinlock on every allocation and free even with no task running: now only while tasks run (check cycles -6%). (2) **Arenas**, with no new syntax: `std.memory`'s `arena(f)` and `arena_kept(f)`, whose bodies are `f()` (what a compiler without arenas, an older seed, runs); codegen intercepts the calls: `f` runs with every allocation bump-allocated in an arena (core.rt: 1 MB chunks, kept for reuse), the result copied out (`copy:T` helpers per type: what the arena made copied, the rest shared; a function, trait value, task or channel end refused), the arena freed; `arena_kept` never frees it. An arena's objects stay counted (so one held once is still changed in place: the arena's tag sits above the count bits, and `strata_unique` masks it), but the tag keeps the count from reaching 0: never freed one by one, never walked when dropped (drop helpers: 11.5% of check's samples -> 1.8%). Sound because objects from before an arena never point into it: a list is pushed in place only into the open arena's own buffer, arenas are off while tasks run (one open when they start is kept), a fault leaving a test run puts the arena back. Cost: objects from before that an arena's objects hold are never released (check's peak 252 -> 322 MB). Applied to `check.typed` and `check.type_errors_with`, per function (check cycles -9%, then -3% more once allocation stopped reading a thread-local). (3) `get(xs, i) ?? d` and `match get(xs, i) { Some(p) => …, None => … }` on a list make no `Option` (the element read where it is): check cycles -12%, heap objects 0.58e9 -> 0.38e9. Altogether `check` 356G -> 279G instructions (-22%), heap objects 1.0e9 -> 0.38e9; `build --no-verify` 356G -> 290G instructions, cycles about -4%. Tried and reverted: drops counting inline and calling `free:T` only for the last holder: check -4% instructions but the build's IR grows and LLVM pays more (+6% instructions, +16% peak memory). Not done: borrowed parameters exist already (a function borrows its arguments; `kept_by` finds those it keeps); what costs is results: the same as (3) for a `Dict`'s `get` (a lookup giving a slot, the value borrowed) waits for the hash-map Dict; arenas for decoding, `reach_key` and compile-time values (in `main`, which other streams are reshaping); immortal (count 0) values for types whose values are all constants (enums) need `counted` to know the program's types.
- **Built: `edit` does only what the edit needs (branch `edit-fast`, 2026-11-24).** Measured on `compiler/` (a copy, `edit codebase --no-commit`, one function `codebase.short_hex`), before: a no-op edit 3.7 s (10.5 s on a loaded machine; 88G instructions), a body change 5.9 s, an interface change 6.3 s, each rewriting 27 module files (every module's header `deps` came back in another order: `store.graph` listed a `Set`'s elements, which compiled code keeps in insertion order, not sorted as the doc said). The 2.7 s outside the timed phases was `write_modules` (encoding and hashing every definition 4 times, the whole graph, 0.85–1.9 s), `changes` (hashing every definition of both versions, 0.8–1.3 s) and `merge` (resolving the whole package again, 0.2–0.3 s); the check (resolve, desugar, all passes, refinement probes) another 3 s. Now: a no-op edit compares the merged definitions with the stored ones (`==` on the trees: the encoding is exact, so equal trees are equal bytes) and stops, nothing written, checked or logged: 0.10 s / 3.8G instructions. A body change: 0.52 s / 9.8G instructions, one file written, one definition checked; an interface change with 75 users in 6 modules: 1.4 s / 25G, one file written (the bootstrapped fixed point, with its identity file). How: `merge` resolves only the edits (`resolve.stored_from`); `write_modules` skips a module whose definitions are as they were, and in one it writes keeps each untouched definition's bytes and header entry (`binary.encode_module_kept`), the changed ones' deps found alone (`store.graph_of`); deps are sorted again. `main.edit_scope` finds what to check: the changed definitions, plus, when one's interface changed or one was removed (or added: a clash), the definitions whose header deps name it or whose bytes are written with one of its names (`binary.entry_strings`: the string tables, read without decoding; that finds a field read on a value whose type isn't named, or a method called on one, which headers don't list). `edit_errors` loads std as `check` does (its roots from the same string tables) and runs `check_errors_in`: the passes (`expression_errors_in`, `mut_errors_in`, `declaration_errors_in`, `contract_errors_in`) know the rest of the package as context, errors only for the checked set; holes of a pending change come from those errors as before. std, core and packages with dependencies are still checked whole. Left: (1) 0.3 s of every `check`/`edit`/`build` front end is `caching()` hashing the 100 MB executable when it has no `X.identity` beside it (any `strata build -o X` binary): with one, `shared_at` is 0.01 s; (2) `desugar(lib)` runs twice per check (check and literal probes), 0.13 s; (3) `--file std edit` stores std's modules under their local names (`json`, refs `json.JsonValue`), a bug older than this; (4) the open-holes count after an edit is of the changed definitions only (a full count walked the package).
- **Built: small toolchain fixes (branch `fix-toolchain-small`, 2026-11-24).** (1) The parallel IR parts (`compile_part`, c/toolchain.c) were still read by LLParser: they now go through c/ir.c's reader too (`ir_module`, `toolchain_module` without its timing, safe on many threads, each part a context of its own); the objects are byte-identical (all 148 parts of `compiler/`) and `STRATA_IR_CHECK=1` finds no difference. Rebuilding `compiler/`'s parts (the rest cached): 42.9G -> 34.4G instructions, user 6.9 -> 5.4 s, the parts' phase 0.89 -> 0.72 s wall. (2) Edit-fast's (1) above: a compiler built by `strata build -o X` (a program linking c/cache.c) gets `X.identity` beside it (`cache.write_identity`, in `main.linked`), as tools/bootstrap.sh writes one; and hashing, where there is no identity yet, is CommonCrypto's (`hash_file`): `check examples/math.strata` with a warm cache, identity unknown 0.36 s (old hash) -> 0.13 s (CommonCrypto) -> 0.06 s (identity file). (3) One version: `main.version` is the only place it is written; tools/release.sh asks the compiler (`--version`) and release-check.sh checks `lib/strata/VERSION` agrees.

### `std.regex.samples` matches its pattern, as a proof (asked by the coordinator 2026-11-24; started, not built)

The law: `samples(pattern, n, seed)` gives only texts its pattern matches whole, written as
`ensures match it { Ok(xs) => all(xs, t -> run_matcher(matcher(pattern), t)), Err(_) => true }`
with the loop invariant `all(out, t -> run_matcher(m, t))`. It holds by construction (a text is
kept only if `run_matcher(m, text)`), but the Strata verifier can't prove it yet. Proving it takes
four things in `compiler/smt` and std. The first two are written on branch `regex-samples-law`
(commit 714f5aa, WIP, not merged):

1. **Calls as uninterpreted functions.** Today each call's result is a fresh unknown ("the result
   of `f` (only its contract is known)"), so `if ok(x) { xs = push(xs, x) }` doesn't give
   `all(xs, ok)`: the two calls of `ok` aren't known to be equal. In the WIP, `call_term` makes a
   call an application `(|call:f| args)` when the function has no type parameters, no `mut` or raw
   memory parameters, and every argument and the result is one term (a scalar, or a datatype term:
   a structured result stays `VData`, so it can be passed on as it is). A call seen through in a
   contract is linked to the same application, and `why` reports the call's reason when its symbol
   is in the cone. Rust's verifier had part of this (the same call with the same arguments gave
   the same result, through a memo). With it, `kept(m, xs, text)` (one conditional push) is proven.
2. **Loop variables of a declared list type keep their element type.** `havoc_loop` refreshed a
   list to an untracked one, so an `all(out, ...)` invariant said nothing after a pass. In the WIP,
   a variable declared `List[T]` becomes a fresh `List[T]`, so the invariant carries. With 1 and 2,
   a `while` that pushes what `ok` accepts proves `all(out, ok)` when `ok`'s receiver is a scalar.
3. **Not done: the invariant when the predicate's receiver is a sum-typed value** (`run(m, t)`,
   with `m: Matcher`). The pass still fails with "an element of an empty list; the elements of a
   list are not tracked". In contracts the call is seen through (its body expanded per element),
   and the element machinery (`quantified facts`, regions) drops the fact. Next step: find where
   the element formula gives up for a datatype-receiver call, with `verify --smt` on a small copy
   (a two-variant `M`, `mk(p) -> M`, `run(m, t) -> Bool`, the same loop).
4. **Not done: termination.** `--require proven` also asks that `samples` ends, and it calls
   `matcher` (the DFA construction), which "may not terminate". The law can be proven while
   `samples` stays "not proven" until `matcher`'s and `run_matcher`'s loops get measures.

Before 1 is merged, run the std modules it can change (`verify 'std.MODULE.*' --require proven`)
and the `smt-*`, `cli-*verify*` and `cli-smt_*` cases: an application in place of a fresh unknown
can change which quantified facts are kept, and so the time of some proofs. A different law,
`run_matcher(matcher(p), t) == full_match(t, p)` (the DFA agrees with the backtracking matcher), is
a much larger proof and isn't needed for this one.

### Generated docs for std (user, 2026-11-23; not built)

The standard library gets a reference page per module on the docs site (`docs-site/`, Starlight), generated from its `///` doc comments instead of written by hand: the language guide only names the modules, and the hand-written table of what each has (docs/language-reference.md, "The standard library") goes once the pages exist. A `strata doc` command (or a step of `docs-site/sync.mjs`) would write Markdown per module: each public definition's interface (`render --level 1`) with its doc comment, grouped by kind, linked to the types it names. To settle: whether it is a `strata` command (usable on any package, as `cargo doc` is) or the site's script only, and how examples in doc comments are checked.

### Builtins viewable, with their costs (asked by the coordinator 2026-11-25 from agents' feedback; built, branch `builtin-docs`)

Agents benchmarked collections because `strata view Dict` said "names no definition" and Dict's doc had no costs. Built: `view NAME` and `find NAME` look a name the program doesn't define up in core (by its own name: `view insert` shows every overload) and std (qualified, `view std.json.parse`, or a public std name core doesn't have), also outside a package (views.select_library, finding.library_source); every builtin of core/builtins.strata and the List/Set/Dict/String types carry a last doc line `/// Cost: ...`, each checked against the code that runs (core.list, runtime.c's tables, codegen's `update_in_place`, the prelude's `str_*`, `sort_with`, `join`) and measured (doubling n, a benchmark of each operation: in-place `insert` and `push` flat, the others below quadratic in a loop). `edit` replaced a builtin's every overload with the last one given (merge and put_in keyed by name): now by name and parameters' types (codebase.edit_key), so core/builtins.strata can be edited as text. The skill points to `view NAME` for costs, `--no-verify` for throwaway builds, and piping `edit`'s input.

Slower than it should be, measured 2026-11-25 (documented as it is, not as wished):
- *Fixed by branch `copies`*: `xs + ys` copies both, so `xs = xs + [x]` and `xs += [x]` in a loop are quadratic (100k: 6.4 s; `push`: 0.02 s); when `xs` is held once (or its buffer's slot past the end is free, as `push` checks) it could append in place.
- *Fixed by branch `copies`*: `s = s + x` copies `s` every time: 400k one-byte appends 1.9 s, quadratic (`join`: linear). A string held once could grow in place (a capacity, as list buffers have).
- *Fixed by branch `copies`*: `Dict`/`Set` `remove` is O(n) even in place (the entries after it move down, the index is built again): 80k removals > 10 s. The copies stream makes it O(1) (no order promised); update the `Cost:` lines of both `remove`s when it lands.
- *Fixed by branch `copies`*: `insert` is in place only as `x = insert(x, ...)` / `x = x.insert(...)` on a variable or a field one level down (`r.d`); `e := insert(d, ...)`, a deeper field (`r.a.d`) or a `d` also held elsewhere copies the entries and the whole index (80k: 5 s). Even out of place, a new key only needs its slot: the index copy is what makes it O(n).
- `contains`/`index_of`/`split` on strings compare at every byte offset (O(n·m)); `reverse` and `repeat` go through `join`'s rounds (O(n log n)); `range(a, b)` outside a `for` makes the list.
- Found while benchmarking (a checker bug, not a cost): `mut console.print(n)` with `n: Int` (`print` takes a `String`) passes `strata check`, then crashes when run (signal 11 in `std.io.Terminal.print`); cause not looked into.
- `refs` doesn't yet find a program's uses of a builtin (`refs insert` says it names nothing here).

### Finding code without grep: `find`, `refs`, `search`, locations (designed with the user 2026-11-24; steps 1-2 built on branch `find-refs`, but for the runtime's side)

Agents grep for: where a name is defined, every use of it before a change, where an error message comes from, half-remembered names, code shapes (every `match` on a type, every construction of a record, writes to a field, `.unwrap()` calls), functions by type, who implements a trait, comments and TODOs. Grep is blind to shapes and types and noisy on names (comments, other modules' namesakes). Today `view 'core.*' --level 0` lists definitions, `view slice:core.f` mixes uses and used-by in one list, `interface` gives a module's API; short names (`slice:run_worker`) match nothing, and nothing says where in a body a use is. Three commands and a location form fix that; they are written in Strata (`compiler/`), on `compiler/binary`, `resolve`, `check`.

**Shared conventions.**
- Names: short (`run_worker`, any module), qualified (`core.run_worker`), glob (`*verdict*`), fuzzy (`~rnwrkr`), member (`Task.id`, `Worker.next`, `Shape.Circle`).
- **A short name that names several definitions shows them all** (user): first the candidates, with their signatures and number of matches each, then every result, grouped by definition. The agent narrows with a qualified name if it wants to; nothing stops to ask.
- Locations: `DEF@HASH/PATH`, the path made of the tree's field names and list positions (`core.get@3f2a91c0/body/0/index`). Every command prints and accepts them; the hash says when the definition has changed since (shown, with the same path in the current version).
- **Every result is shown as code in its definition** (user): the definition rendered by the printer, its signature line always, `⋮` for the lines left out, the enclosing blocks kept (`match step {`, closing braces), the match marked with `^^^`, the location as the block's header line. Several matches in one definition share one rendering. `--context N` (default 1), `--context all` the whole definition. `--list` (locations only) and `--json` for scripts.
- Exit codes as grep: 0 matched, 1 nothing, 2 error.

**`strata find NAME [--kind fn|type|trait|variant|field|method|impl|test|capability|extern] [--in SELECTOR] [--public] [--doc] [--limit N]`**: one line per definition, kind, qualified name, signature (a type's fields or variants; `--doc` the doc's first line instead). Reads headers and interface parts only.

**`strata refs NAME [--uses | --used-by] [--kind call|construct|pattern|read|write|type|impl|import|method] [--depth N] [--in SELECTOR] [--context N]`**: the two directions in separate sections ("used by — 3 uses in 2 definitions", "uses — 7 definitions"), each use labelled with its kind (a construction, a match pattern, a field read and a field write differ), shown as code as above. `refs Task.attempts --kind write`, `refs core.Worker --kind impl`. `--depth N` follows the graph and prints a tree (`core.same_ids └─ core.run_worker call body/4/cond ├─ core.drain …`). `slice:` stays as a `view` selector.

**`strata view LOCATION [--context N | --def]`**: a location as code (no new `show` command); `view --last-fault` the last run's fault.

**Errors carry locations.** A runtime fault prints each frame as a location shown as code (today: `stack: main -> get`); compiled code keeps a side table from each check and call to its location, like debug info, free until a fault. `--trace` says where each call came from (`→ core.get(xs: [1, 2], i: 5)  from main/body/0`). Checker errors and verifier refutations (with their counterexample) use the same form.

**`strata search PATTERN [--where C].. [--inside P] [--not-inside P] [--has P] [--not-has P] [--in SELECTOR] [--as expr|stmt|decl|type|pattern] [--rewrite T [--apply] [-m MSG]] [--count]`**, after ast-grep (https://ast-grep.github.io/) and comby (https://comby.dev/): a pattern is Strata code with holes, so an agent writes it without learning a syntax.
- `?x` one node (expression, pattern, type, name), bound; `?_` unbound; `...` zero or more items of a list (arguments, statements, fields, arms, parameters), `...?rest` bound; `(?x: T)` an expression whose inferred type matches `T` (holes allowed). ast-grep's `$X`, `$_`, `$$$`, `$$$REST` are accepted as the same (rewritten before parsing): `?x` is Strata's own hole syntax, the lexer already reads it.
- The same hole twice matches equal subtrees. A pattern is tried as an expression, a statement, a definition, a type, a match pattern (`--as` forces one).
- Names in a pattern mean what they resolve to (calls through an alias match, namesakes elsewhere don't); a name that resolves to several definitions matches them all, listed at the top with their counts.
- `--where`: `'?x: Dict[?k, ?v]'` (inferred type, cached by `code_hash`), `'?msg matches "time(out)?"'` (std.regex), `'?f in core.*'`, `'?n != 0'`.
- A definition pattern is type search: `search 'fn ?f(...) -> Result[?_, ParseError]'`.
- `--text TEXT [--strings | --comments | --docs]`: text in literals, comments, docs ("where does this message come from").
- `--rewrite '?r.expect("…")'` previews each change (`core.drain body/2/value  task_of(r).unwrap() → task_of(r).expect("…")`); `--apply` makes it an `edit` (checked, atomic, broken uses become `?pending_N`, one log entry).
- Speed: each definition starts with its own string table (docs/binary-format.md), so the names and literals a pattern mentions rule out most definitions before any decoding; only the rest are decoded and matched.

Later: saved rules in strata.toml (`[search.rules]`, in Strata) run by `check`, lint-style; search and refs panels in the viewer.

Order: (1) `find`, `refs`; (2) locations in errors, `view LOCATION`; (3) `search` with `--text` and plain patterns; (4) typed holes and `--where`; (5) `--rewrite`.

**Built (branch `find-refs`).** `compiler/locate` (locations: printed, read back, paths through a definition's tree with the names in scope at each node; code shown with `⋮`, enclosing blocks and `^^^`, through stand-in names the printer lays out like the nodes) and `compiler/finding` (`find`, `refs`, `view LOCATION`, check errors' places). Choices made while building:
- A path's steps: a block's statements and its result by position (an expression statement is its expression), field names elsewhere (`callee`, `args/N`, `arms/N/pat`, `params/N/ty`, `methods/N/body`, a record argument by its field: `args/attempts`), a one-value wrapper's value as `value` (`return x`, `assert x`). The hash shown is the first 4 bytes of the definition's identity hash (comments included, since paths count comment statements).
- `find` decodes the interfaces only (`binary.decode_interface`), every definition's, for members; signatures in stored form with the definition's own module's names short.
- `refs`: a field's reads are told apart by the checker's `mark_field_reads`, a type's own method's calls by `own_methods_resolved` (both only on the definitions that name the field or method); a trait's or capability's method by name (calls not of a type's own method). Uses in a root-module definition skip locals of the same name (paths carry the names in scope). The candidates are the definitions whose header lists the target's definition (or owner) as a dependency. A name defined outside the package (`std.list.map`) is found by name in every definition.
- Checker errors: the place is the first node written as code the message quotes (`` `y` ``, `` `+` ``, a field name), expressions before types; the checker doesn't track places itself. Printed as `--> LOCATION` and the code after `| ` (lines that start with two spaces are the checker's notes, which cases grep).

Left of steps 1-2: runtime faults with each frame a location (the side table from compiled checks and calls to locations: codegen works on desugared, instantiated trees whose paths differ from the stored ones, so the table needs paths carried through desugaring and instantiation), `view --last-fault` (the runtime, core.fault, would keep the last fault), `--trace`'s `from` locations, verifier refutations with their location (the place of the failing call inside `verify_file`'s report).

### Web UIs (direction agreed with the user 2026-11-20; steps 1-4 built, each step's "Not yet" below)

The second place Strata should be useful, after the command line: UIs in the
browser, written without JavaScript. The direction is the Elm architecture,
run on the server, in the way Phoenix LiveView and htmx work:

```rust
use std.web.{App, Html, div, button, text, on_click}

type Model = { count: Int where 0 <= it }

type Msg = | Increment | Decrement | Reset

impl App[Msg] for Model {
  fn update(mut self, msg: Msg) {
    match msg {
      Increment => self.count += 1
      Decrement => if self.count > 0 { self.count -= 1 }
      Reset     => self.count = 0
    }
  }

  fn view(self) -> Html[Msg] {
    div([], [
      button([on_click(Decrement)], [text("-")]),
      text(f"{self.count}"),
      button([on_click(Increment)], [text("+")]),
    ])
  }
}
```

(The syntax is a sketch; traits with a type parameter like `App[Msg]` may
need work.)

`strata serve` keeps each session's `Model` in memory and renders `view` to
HTML. A small, fixed JS file that ships with the runtime catches clicks and
form submits. `on_click(Increment)` puts the message's JSON in an attribute;
the JS posts it back, and the server reads it against `Msg` (refinements
checked, as for command-line options), runs `update` and `view`, and the JS
swaps the new HTML into the page. (As built, step 3 below: three functions instead of a trait, and messages as numbered handlers rather than JSON.)

Why it fits: users write no JS; no async is needed in user code; `update` and
`view` are pure, so they can be tested without a browser and the verifier can
prove invariants across every message (`count >= 0`); an unhandled `Msg` is a
non-exhaustive `match`. The compiler's output is LLVM IR, so a wasm build
could later run the same app in the browser, without changing user code.

Steps:
1. `std.web.Html` (**built**, branch `worktree-web-html`): `Html[M]` (element,
   text) and `Attr[M]` (attribute, flag, event), helpers (`div`, `a`,
   `input`, …, `class`, `href`, `on_click`, …), `render` and `document`.
   Text and attribute values are escaped; names are checked where they are
   written (`element(tag: TagName, …)`, `attr(name: AttrName, …)`; `AttrName`
   excludes `on…` so inline scripts can't be written), not in the type:
   `Html[M] = { node: Node[M] }` and `Attr[M]` wrap private sums, so only
   `std.web` makes them and the verifier has nothing to check in views
   (decided with the user: verifying markup is pointless; what matters is
   the Strata values put into it, which JSX will make explicit); events are `data-on-EVENT` attributes holding the message as JSON
   (new builtin `to_json`). Void elements can't have children (`element`'s
   `requires`). CSS goes in `style_sheet(css)` (not escaped, can't close the
   element) or `style_link(url)`. Everything in `std` is proven, and so is
   `examples/page`. While `Html`'s fields were refined, the prover needed two
   changes in `smt.rs`, kept since they help any recursive or generic type:
   `check_type` stops at the third nested level of the same named type (a
   recursive type like `Html` made it re-check every element of every list,
   level after level: minutes for a small page), with one unknown per (type,
   value) so an assumed result and a later check are the same atom; and
   validity is compared on types whose unrefined type arguments are erased
   (`erased`), so a generic helper's result `Html[M]` matches its use as
   `Html[Msg]`. Not yet: `javascript:` URLs in `href` are not rejected.
2. `strata serve` (**built**, branch `worktree-web-serve`): `std.http`
   (`Request`, `Response`, `Method` as a refined `String`, response helpers)
   and `fn handle(req: Request) -> Response`, called once per request
   (`src/serve.rs`, std-only, one request at a time; since `serve-compiled`,
   by the program compiled once when the server starts).
   Routes as in Hono (asked for by the user): `Router` with `app.get(pattern,
   handler)` etc., `:name` segments, `req.param(name)` an `Option`, HEAD via
   GET, 404 / 405. As with `Html`, the refinements (`Method`, `Pattern`) are
   on `Router.on`'s parameters and the stored `Route` is private and plain,
   so `Router` has nothing to prove (CI's older z3 gave up on regex
   refinements inside a list of routes). Building it fixed three things: a type's own method now
   comes before a function of another module with the same name when called
   as `x.m()` (`app.handle(req)` inside `fn handle`); termination no longer
   takes a local named like a function for a call of it (`let mut app` in
   `fn app`); the prover knows a function value's result is a valid value of
   its result type (`Val::FnOf`), and a lambda passed for a function-typed
   parameter gets valid arguments. Not yet: reading bodies and forms into
   records (step 4), static files, typed route parameters (a pattern's names
   checked against what the handler reads; `/users/:id` with `id: UserId`),
   `*` wildcards, several requests at once, URL-encoding helpers for links.
3. Apps (**built**, branch `worktree-web-app`): no `App` trait in the end,
   but three functions found by name, like `main` and `handle`: `fn init()
   -> Model`, `fn update(model: Model, msg: Msg) -> Model` (pure, returns the
   new model, as in Elm), `fn view(model: Model) -> Html[Msg]`. `strata
   serve` runs them when there is no `handle`: sessions in memory (a random
   id per page load, at most 1000), `/_strata/app.js` sends
   `data-on-{click,dblclick,submit,change}` messages one at a time and swaps
   the view in; messages are read against `Msg` (refinements checked);
   faults are 500s shown in a banner; an unknown session reloads the page.
   (Since `serve-compiled`, messages are numbered handlers, as forms' are, not
   JSON read back against `Msg`.)
   `examples/counter` is proven, `update` included. For the view to be proven
   when `Msg` has refined fields, the prover treats a type argument that is a
   record or sum without a refinement of its own as adding nothing *to
   prove* (its values are checked when made): `Attr[Msg]` from a generic
   `on_click`. Facts to assume keep the checks (skipping them there lost
   proofs in `examples/orders`). Not yet: an app and routes in one program; sessions are lost on restart.
   (The page re-rendered whole and input values in messages were done in
   step 4.)
4. Forms (**built**, branch `worktree-web-forms`; the user asked for the
   logic to be in Strata): handlers that make a message from what was
   entered, `on_input` / `on_change` (`(String) -> M`) and `on_submit_form`
   (`(Dict[String, String]) -> M`). Functions can't go into the page, so
   `std.web.render` numbers them in page order (a `mut` counter) and
   `std.web.dispatch(page, n, value, fields)` (Strata) makes the message;
   the server only re-runs `view` on the current model and calls it. The
   client patches the page instead of replacing it (a small morph): focus
   and scroll stay, and the field being typed in keeps its text (after a
   submit, the view's value wins). Validation is ordinary Strata in
   `update`; `examples/todos` shows errors next to the field and is proven
   (titles within `Title`, at most 50 to-dos). Prover fixes on the way: an
   empty list literal is valid at any element type, and an element of
   `[] ⊕ xs` (merged branches, concatenation) is an element of `xs`. Not yet:
   reading a form straight into a typed record (`decode[NewTodo](fields)`,
   with each refinement's error by field: needs a builtin that knows its
   type argument at runtime); a handler number can be stale if the page
   changes between an event and its turn in the queue; keyed lists (the
   morph patches children by position).
5. Later: commands for slow work in `update` (LLM calls, an HTTP client; see
   below), the wasm build. Capabilities in `init` / `update` (`Files`, the
   program's own), timers (`every`) and keys (`on_key`) are built (#145).

**JSX-like syntax** (user: wanted at some point, for simplicity). Markup
written directly in Strata expressions, compiled to the `Html` helpers, e.g.
`<button on_click={Increment}>-</button>`. Helper functions come first; the
syntax is added on top of them later. The parser checks the static markup (tag and
attribute names, no `on…` attributes, no children in void elements) as
syntax; only the `{…}` insertions are Strata code, and their types do the
rest: a `String` becomes escaped text, an `Html[M]` a child, an `M` an event's
message. The verifier has something to say only where an insertion's type is
refined (e.g. `href={url}` with a `Url` that excludes `javascript:`).

Questions to settle:
- **Async first, or web first?** (user, 2026-11-20; undecided.) The web design
  above doesn't need concurrency in user code: the runtime handles it, and each
  request is a plain call. Where it matters is `update` calling something slow
  (an LLM, an HTTP request): it would block the session, and with a sequential
  server, everything. Elm answers this with *commands*: `update` returns data
  describing what to do, the runtime performs it and feeds the results back as
  messages, so commands are easy to test, mock and verify. The concurrency model
  is now decided ("Concurrency: structured tasks without coloring": no `async`
  functions, blocking calls inside tasks), so it no longer changes how commands
  are written: the runtime can perform each command in a task. Claude's view:
  build the web first, with commands.
- Can `view` use capabilities? Pure is simpler and checkable.
- Send the whole page on every message, or a diff? The whole page to start.
- Sessions: kept in memory to start; later stored through the ORM.

### `strata repl` (asked by the user 2026-11-25; step 1 built, branch `repl`)

An interactive session to try small things with the current project loaded (docs/tools-reference.md, "The REPL"). Step 1 (`compiler/main`: `repl_command`, `repl_eval`, `repl_line`, `repl_load`): each line an expression, a binding or a definition, checked against the package plus the session (`check_errors_of` on a probe function `strata_repl` holding the earlier values and the line), expanded (`expanded`), then run as `comptime` runs (`computed_value`: only what the line reaches is compiled, by compiler/codegen, and run by the JIT under the fault trap: a fault is an error, the session goes on); `:type`, `:view`, `:reload`, `:quit`; piped input read line by line (exit 1 when an input failed). Piped, 16 lines on a small script take about 1.3 s in all.

Capabilities are the REPL's own (user, 2026-11-25): a line never gets the program's real ones. `console` is a `std.io.Recorder` (the language's stand-in `Console`), made for each line and read back with its value, so what a line prints is shown with that line's result and written nowhere else. No other capability is given yet (`disk`, `process` are unknown names), so evaluating a line can't write files or run processes.

Values are kept as the code that writes them (read back, as `run f` prints a result), not in compiled memory: each line binds them again. Simple, and correct for data; nothing is run twice.

Not yet:
- values that have no code (functions, closures, capabilities, tasks) can't be kept: keep them in compiled memory instead (a session module loaded once, as `serve` keeps a model, each line's function taking them `Given`/`Lent`), which also saves re-reading big values each line;
- each line compiles what it reaches again: compile the package once (its objects from the cache, std's precompiled library loaded) and each line only its own function, as `--precompiled` does for std;
- the other capabilities as the REPL's own: Files/Disk an in-memory folder by default (std's stand-ins), Process/Environment denied, the real ones by an explicit opt-in (`strata repl --allow files,process`); `console.read_line` reading from the REPL (it reads nothing now);
- `mut xs.push(7)` at the prompt gives the new list but leaves `xs` as it was (an assignment, `xs = …`, is kept): to look into;
- multi-line input (a definition over several lines, a block), line editing and history at a terminal;
- `:save`: a definition typed at the prompt written to the package (through `edit`: checked and logged);
- a fault is shown as an error without its kind and call stack (`computed_value` returns its message only).

## Design principles (decided)

- **No effects.** Capabilities (database, queue, console, clock) are traits; code takes an implementation as a parameter or holds it in a field. `mock T` generates an implementation that respects the trait's contracts. Iterators and concurrency are their own language features, **not** effects (concurrency: see "Concurrency: structured tasks without coloring"; it needs no capability, since tasks share nothing and `scope`/`spawn`/`await` are deterministic).
- **Immutable by default, mutable when declared**: `let mut`, field assignment, `mut` parameters and `mut self` with write-back to the caller's variable. Procedural code is normal, and there are no shared references.
- **Refinements are structural**; `type X = Y` with no `where` is an error, so use `distinct type` for a nominal type.
- **Modules meet only through their public (`pub`) contracts.** A folder of `.strata` files loads as modules, and `render --split` writes them back.
- **No backward compatibility.** Breaking changes are expected: never add shims so that older stored trees (`.strata/` objects) or old syntax keep working. Re-import from source instead.
- **Rendered code wraps at about 80 columns** by breaking lines, not soft-wrapping.
- **Viewer**: two levels (interface/source), the code view sliced around the selection, a type map as a separate view, and no Flow view.

## Decisions

### Verification: automatic theorem prover (SMT), with layered results
- Use an SMT solver (Z3 or cvc5) as the main verifier. Contracts (refinements, `requires` / `ensures`, trait laws) become proof obligations.
- Report one of four **layered results** per obligation:
  - `proven` (the solver proved it);
  - `fuzzed` (not proven, but no counterexample found);
  - `failed` (with the solver's or fuzzer's counterexample);
  - `assumed` (trusted: outside libraries, I/O).
  A project policy can require `proven` for chosen modules.
- **Loop invariants**: infer them where possible (e.g. bounds and accumulators). Explicit `invariant …` annotations only where they really help; agents may write them and the solver checks them. Plan and syntax: "Loops and invariants: what to build first" below.
- Callers are checked against a callee's **contract**, not its body, which fits modules meeting through contracts. Cache results by content hash, as `verify` already does.
- Suggested order:
  1. integer, length and string-format refinements;
  2. `requires` / `ensures` across calls;
  3. loop invariants;
  4. trait laws.
  Fuzzing remains the fallback for whatever the solver can't decide.
- Interactive proofs (Lean/Coq-style) come later, as a bigger separate project.

**Built (step 1 and most of 2)**: `src/smt.rs` and `verify::check_fn`. See docs/verification.md. Results: `Proven { checks }`, `Fuzzed { cases, discarded, proven, unproven }` (each unproven check with its reason), `Counterexample { .., found_by: solver|fuzzer }` (a solver model is replayed first, compiled, and counts only if it really fails), `Incomplete`. Z3 through SMT-LIB text over a pipe (`z3 -in`, `rlimit` for determinism, solver version in the cache key); `verify --smt` prints the queries (termination ones too), `--checks` lists proven checks. Cross-check: proven functions that terminate are fuzzed a little (`--cross-check`, step budget per run; past it, a run proves nothing); a failure there is reported as a prover bug, unless it is a fault in a function the checked one calls (that function's own verdict says so). Policies: `[verify]` in `strata.toml` (`proven`, `terminates`, `no_recursion`, each a list of modules) and `--require`. Calls already use the callee's contract (requires checked, ensures assumed), and the same call with the same arguments gets the same result (memo), so refinements re-evaluated in assumptions and goals line up. Impl methods are proven against their trait's contract; laws are still fuzzed only. Solvers: `smt::solver()` returns a `Solver { bin, version, kind }`. `kind` is `Cvc5` when `--version` mentions cvc5, else `Z3` (so a fake solver in the tests is run like Z3). `Solver::args` is `-in -T:20 rlimit=20000000` for Z3 and `--lang smt2 --produce-models --rlimit-per=5000000 --tlimit-per=20000` for cvc5. 5M cvc5 units is about 4 to 10 s of hard nonlinear arithmetic; typical queries use thousands. cvc5 1.4.2 needs no `--strings-exp` for `str.replace_all` / `str.to_int`. Its model is one line, with negatives as `(- 6)` and a backslash as `\u{5c}`. `parse_values` / `parse_str` read both formats (unit test `values`). The whole suite passes with `STRATA_SOLVER=cvc5`, and no test had to change. The examples get the same verdicts with both solvers; cvc5 is about 2x slower on `examples/tasks`. There is no portfolio yet (both solvers in parallel, first answer wins). cvc5 decides large Unicode regex classes exactly and quickly, where Z3 times out, so with cvc5 those classes need not be approximated (see the Unicode-regex change).
Unions (`Val::Union`): a tag plus one slot per member *shape* (`val_key` / `type_key`: `Int`, `rec:Square`, `sum:Shape`, …; members of the same shape share a slot, e.g. `Positive | Negative` or two variants of one sum, whose inner tag is then constrained). Since shapes are disjoint the tag is a function of the value, as in compiled code where a union value is the member's value. Validity is `in_type` (`shape_test` and `check_type`); merging values of different shapes makes a union; a `match` arm whose pattern can only match one slot rebinds the matched variable to that slot (exact under the arm's guard); a union used as an `Int`/`String`/`Bool` is `ite(tag = slot, member, fresh)`. Assigning a field of a sum or union value now makes it unknown (and the check unprovable) instead of keeping the old value. String builtins with an exact SMT-LIB counterpart are modelled (`slice` as `str.substr` with clamped bounds, `get`/`index_of` as `Option`s, `replace` as `str.replace_all` plus a not-empty-pattern check, `to_string` with a `-` for negatives, `ends_with`, `chars`, `is_digit`; `parse_int` exactly only for `-?[0-9]+` and digit-free strings, with the lemma `str.to_int x >= 0 <=> x in [0-9]+` that Z3 needs). `slice`, `get`, `take` and `drop` now accept big integers (saturated, so clamping is exact) instead of faulting. Z3 often gives up on `str.replace_all` with symbolic strings (those checks are fuzzed). Builtins that fault on some arguments are checks (`builtin_faults`, found 2026-11-21: before, an unmodelled builtin was a fresh value with no check, so `chr(n)` was "proven" and the cross-check caught `chr: -624 is not a character`): `chr` of a code point that is a character (its result `str.from_code` up to U+2FFFF), `ord` of one character (`str.to_code`), `split` with a separator that isn't empty, `repeat` with a count that isn't negative (its 100,000,000-character cap is gone from the runtime: a string too large is out of memory, as when built any other way), `float` of an Int below 10^308; `sqrt`, `floor`/`ceil`/`round` and float arithmetic (`+ - * / %`, faulting on a result that isn't finite) are checks never proven, floats not being modelled. So the prover can tell Ints from floats in lambdas, the checker records each lambda's type and writes concrete parameter types into the checked program (`annotate_lambdas`); `chars(s)`'s elements are one character each, and a list slice is a sublist (clamped). std needed: `max_code`'s value as its contract, `chr` guards in `class_of` and `parse_hex` and `json.read_string`, and `Float`'s decoder returns an error for an integer past 10^308 instead of faulting. Left unknown on purpose: `to_upper`/`to_lower`/`is_alpha` (Unicode tables), `trim` (Unicode whitespace at both ends), `split`, `lines`, `repeat`, `reverse`. Regexes (decided with the user: Strata regexes are modern Unicode regexes): `regex_model` is `std.regex.smt_model` (the tree translated in Strata, run as compile-time code; before, `regex-syntax`'s HIR in Rust); look-arounds only as `^`/`$` certain to be at the start/end; anything else is `None` (unknown). Z3 times out on unions of more than ~50-100 `re.range`s (cvc5 does not), so a class of more than `MAX_RANGES` (24) ranges makes `matches` a fresh bool between an under- and an over-approximation (`RegexModel::Between`); `regex_relations` adds exact inclusion/disjointness facts between patterns matched on the same string term, from `std.regex.relations`, a product of the two patterns' DFAs (bounded search; undecided means no fact). SMT strings stop at U+2FFFF (`MAX_CHAR`): classes are clipped there, and literals past it are unknown. Before, `str_lit` wrote them as `\u{e0041}`, which Z3 reads as 9 ASCII characters, so `len("\u{E0041}") == 9` was "proven" (the cross-check caught it). Proofs about strings hold for strings up to U+2FFFF; the cross-check is the backstop. The compiled fuzzer's strings for a pattern come from `std.regex.samples` (Unicode classes and word boundaries included).
Since then: `assumed` is built (capabilities, `extern c`, mocks); loops keep their invariants, inferred or written ("Loops and invariants"); lists are known by position (#190, below). List contents were first "every element satisfies P" facts (`Val::List { len, id }`; `Vc::lists[id]` has a `Gen`, how an element is made, and guarded preds; `every` proves a property of an arbitrary generated element with the quantifier on the skolem side, `all_of` / `know_all` give `all(xs, p)` both directions; elements are made under their existence condition, so an empty literal can't make a path contradictory). Not modelled: positions after `filter` and `sort`, permutations, `zip` / `enumerate`, dict and set contents; floats, dicts, sets; `sum` is modelled only as "≥ 0 for non-negative elements, 0 when empty", `all`/`any` only when empty; generic callees lose precision (a result `MemQueue[P, E]` doesn't satisfy a `MemQueue[Int, String]` check, because element types are compared syntactically); unions whose members can't be told apart by shape (`List[Int] | List[String]`, `Box[Int] | Box[String]`, a type parameter, a trait, `Float`, `Dict`/`Set`, a refined sub-union) are unknowns.

### `enumerate` on iterators; lambdas that take a tuple apart (reported by an agent writing Strata, 2026-11-25; built, branch `enumerate-tuples`)

`enumerate` is lazy on every iterator (core.iter's `Enumerated`, finite when its source is, `len` passed through), as `enumerate(it)` and `it.enumerate()`. A list's stays the eager built-in: `for (i, x) in enumerate(xs)` is unchanged, and a list's methods of a built-in's name (`xs.map(f)`) stay the built-ins even when core.iter, whose `Iterator` has methods of those names, is loaded.

The rule for tuples: a lambda of n ≥ 2 parameters written without types, where a function of ONE argument is expected and that argument is an n-tuple (or not known yet: a lambda of several parameters can't be a function of one anything else), binds the tuple's elements. Where a function of n arguments is expected (`fold`'s `(acc, x) -> …`) it stays a lambda of n arguments. `lambdas_typed` rewrites it to `($tuple) -> { (i, x) := $tuple; body }`, so the prover sees the destructuring; codegen takes it apart from the wanted type too, and compiler/eval from the one tuple it is given. Not built (each a syntax change, to ask): an explicit pattern parameter `((i, x)) -> …`, nested patterns `(acc, (k, v)) -> …`, and typed parameters (`(i: Int, x: Int) -> …` where a pair is expected is still a two-argument lambda). Still open: a lambda of two parameters where a function of one non-tuple argument is expected (`xs.map((a, b) -> a)` on `List[Int]`) isn't reported by the checker.

### `mut` arguments are taken after the other arguments (coordinator, 2026-11-24; built, branch `mut-order`)

`f(mut g, h(mut g))`: the call takes `g` once its other arguments have run, so what `h` did to `g` is what `f` is given, and `f`'s write-back keeps it. Before, the place was taken in argument order and `h`'s change was silently lost when `f` wrote its copy back (llvm.rs did the same; no test relied on it). #393 made that an error; now it is only an error to give the same variable `mut` twice in one call (`swap(mut g, mut g)`), where one write-back would undo the other. A receiver changed by the call (`mut g.add(bump(mut g))`) and an assignment inside a block argument follow the same rule.

### Values and copying: what is in place, `--copies` (decided with the user 2026-11-25; built, branch `copies`)

Agents writing Strata couldn't tell when a value is copied, and benchmarked instead (their loops "hung"). Decided: the rule is stated in docs/language.md ("Values and copying": an update of a value held by one thing is in place, a shared one is copied first, once) with exactly what holds a value, each form pinned by case `build-copies` (no copies at two sizes, 200,000 updates under a time limit, `STRATA_MEMORY=check`; the shared forms' copies counted and explained); and `--copies` (a flag on `run`, `test`, `build`) reports each copy made to change a value at the program's end: per location (`DEF@HASH/PATH`, `finding.copy_sites`), how many, how big, the largest, and why (the count: `shared: held 2 times`; a `for` holding the entries; `insert` not assigned back). Without the flag the IR is unchanged (codegen's `g.copies`; runtime.c's `strata_copied`, `strata_copies_push`, `strata_copies_append`, `strata_copies_start`). The patterns found quadratic, measured on main before (built, 20,000 → 80,000 updates; first-run time excluded), all linear after:
- a record inside a record, `bump(mut b.bids, i)` and `b.bids.words = insert(b.bids.words, …)` (10k 0.8 s → 40k 6-8 s): assignments in place along any path of records (`in_place_path`, `held_once_on`), `update_in_place` on any path, and a field given to a `mut` parameter **lent**: the field's count given up for the call when the records on its path are held once and no other argument reads the variable, put back after it (`call_fn_want`);
- a refined element type (`Dict[Int, Word]`, 10k 2.6 s → 40k >30 s: every assignment checked every element): `checked_update` checks what the update adds (the place's old value had its type already), with the same fault text (`: element -1`, `: key …`, `: [2]`);
- `remove` (10k 0.3 s → 40k 1.5 s; sets 0.5 → 3.7 s): O(1) — the last entry moves into the gap (dicts and sets have no order, user), linear probing's backward-shift deletion in the index, no tombstones; the copying `strata_tbl_remove` and compiler/eval's `remove` do the same, so the order is the same however the table was changed;
- a table copied to be changed shared its entries' buffer (pushed past the end) or its index with the old one, so it was never its own and every later update copied again (`keep = d` every 1,000 inserts: 40k 18 s): a copy is now the new table's alone;
- `xs += [x]` (40k 10 s) and `s += t` (400k 3.7 s): `strata_list_extend` (core.list `list_extend`: in place past the buffer's end as `push` is, else a buffer with room for as much again) and `strata_str_append` (core.str `str_append`: in place when held once and the block has room, `core.rt.block_room`; else a new block of twice the size);
- `insert`/`remove` on a temporary (`insert(insert(d, …), …)`) is in place, and `x = insert(x, k, f(x))` is too when `f(x)` is a value made from `x` (the count check sees one that holds it); only an argument that *is* the place stays excluded.
The agents' repros (strata-bench `repros/run.sh`, sizes as written: 1M / 200k / 20k; wall time with the first run's 0.25 s), main → this branch: p2 0.37 → 0.36 s, p4 (insert + remove) >60 s → 0.41 s, p5 0.39 → 0.40 s, p5b 0.41 → 0.37 s, p6 (`mut b.bids`) >60 s → 0.28 s, p7 (`b.bids.words = …`) >60 s → 0.29 s, p8 (refined values) 5.9 → 0.26 s. Left as the rule says (documented, and shown by `--copies`): a value read out of a container and updated back in the same step (`insert(get(outer, k) ?? …, …)`) copies the inner value each time (take it out first); `ys := insert(xs, …)` copies `xs`. Not done: moving a variable at its last use (`return insert(d, k, v)` with `d` local), which would need a liveness analysis.

Startup (asked): a trivial built executable takes 0.25-0.3 s the **first** time it runs and 0.00 s (15 M instructions) after: macOS assesses every newly written executable on its first exec (syspolicyd; a C `int main(){return 0;}` from clang takes the same 0.25 s; a copy of a Strata binary under a new name 0.16 s again). Not Strata's (not the 16 GB region reserve, which is untouched address space, nor std's init); nothing in the binary changes it. Benchmarks should time a second run, or the terminal can be listed under System Settings › Privacy & Security › Developer Tools.

### The cache is cleaned by hand, never evicted (decided with the user 2026-11-25; built, branch `cache-clean`)

- No size cap, no LRU, no automatic eviction: the own cache (`$STRATA_CACHE`, else `~/.cache/strata`) only grows, and `strata cache clean` deletes it, saying how much that freed. It never touches a release's shipped cache (`lib/strata/cache`) or std library, nor the verdicts kept in a git repository; it refuses a folder that isn't a cache of its own, and refuses while another running strata has scratch in it (`build/PID`, `run/PID`, `smt/PID` of a running process; `--force` cleans anyway).
- `strata cache` prints where the cache is and each top-level folder's size with what it holds (compiler/cache's `cache_folders`); a folder no code reads any more is marked as an older compiler's. Found on 2026-11-25: `std/`, `compiler/`, `scripts/`, `termination/` and `runtime/` (4.4 GB of a 20 GB cache), written by compilers before std-library/, objects/ and verify-strata/; `cache clean --obsolete` removes only those, and the scratch folders of runs that ended without removing theirs.

### Tool fixes: what agents ran into (decided with the user 2026-11-24; built, branch `tool-fixes`)

Bugs and rough edges reported while working through `strata`, each with a case:
- **Matches on another module's type are checked** (`check-matches_on_another_modules_type`). `compiler/exhaustive` read the program's sums by their own names, so a match in `main` on `ops.Op` (as stored) was never looked at: a variant added in `ops` was accepted and the program faulted at run time. Each module now sees the others' types by their full names. `verify`'s cross-check, which caught it, called it "a bug in the prover": a missing match arm is now said to be the code's. Left: a match on what the check can't type from the syntax (a field, `match p.op`) is still found only by `verify`.
- **A literal returned is checked against a refined return type**, as arguments and fields were (`check-returned_literals_meet_refinements`): `0` from `-> Positive`, the function's value or a `return`'s.
- **`edit` through `$EDITOR`**: its files are under `$TMPDIR`, not `.strata/edit` (git committed them, and the next commit deleted them); the commit never adds `.strata`. A text saved again that was rejected before (not only the last one: an editor alternating between two reopened it forever) gives up with its errors, exit 1; an error of several lines is marked on each line, so none is read back as code (`cli-editor_edits_stay_out_of_git_and_give_up`).
- **A module's top comment survives edits** (`cli-edit_keeps_a_modules_top_comment`). It is stored on the first definition (its comments end with a blank line); `codebase.merge` keeps the comments set apart above a replaced definition when the edit gives none of its own, and puts a removed definition's on the next of its module. No format change.
- **Tests move** (`strata move 'test "name"' MODULE`; `cli-move_a_test_to_another_module`).
- **A record field's type changed**: the reads of it that no longer check are holes of the pending change (`?pending_N`), as changed calls were; before, the whole edit was rejected (`cli-a_changed_field_type_makes_holes`).
- **Holes show the types the checker knows**: `strata holes` didn't know core's iterator methods (`xs.iter()` was `_`, so was each variable of a `for` over it), and a list's `Iterable` method didn't give its element type (`ListIter[_]`) (`cli-holes_show_the_types_of_iterators`).
- **Tests outside the root module run their own code** (branch `fix-cli-bugs`; `cli-tests_outside_the_root_module_run_their_own_code`). `strata test` asked compiler/codegen for each test by `test:N`, but a test in module `ops` is the function `ops.test:N`: the key named nothing, and `compiled_alone` falls back to `main`, so each test compiled `main`'s body under its name, called with no arguments (`strata --file compiler test` crashed, signal 11; a package without `main`, core and std's tests included, passed every test without running it). Keys are qualified now. Running them for real showed `assert xs == []` didn't compile (the empty list's elements of no known type, shown in the failure message: shown as the other side's type now). core.rt's tests (6) can't be compiled by the JIT (they call the runtime's own `strata_alloc`, `bump`, … as values of core.rt, which compiles only into the runtime): `strata --file core test` leaves them out and says so; they never ran with compiler/codegen. Left: running them (compiled bare, as `build-core` compiles core.rt, with their own names).
- **`strata --file std edit MODULE` keeps std's names** (`cli-std_edit_keeps_stds_names_and_stays_out_of_git`). std was opened with its modules named by their files (`json`), so an edited definition was stored with `json.UnknownKeys` for `std.json.UnknownKeys` (#438 limited the damage to the edited module). std's modules are named as stored now (`std.json`; `edit json`, `edit std.json` and `move f json` all mean it), its own context, and `--file std edit` commits nothing to git (`--file` is outside any log; `cd std && strata edit` still commits, as any package's edit).
- **`check` finds wrong calls in tests and methods** (`cli-check_finds_wrong_calls_in_tests_and_methods`). The checks without types (a call with the wrong number of arguments, an unknown name, a constructor's arity) ran on functions only; test blocks, `impl` methods and traits' default methods were seen by the type checker alone, which leaves those to them. They run on all four now (std's `host_…` names, the runtime's, count as known: resolve already keeps them to std).
- **A rejected edit is never lost** (branch `issues-editing`; `cli-edit_keeps_a_rejected_edits_text`). Its text is kept in the package's `.strata/rejected/edit-N.st` (several modules from the editor: a folder `edit-N/`), and the rejection says where: a stdin edit that doesn't check or doesn't parse, and the editor when it gives up (or fails after a rejection). In the package rather than the cache, so that it is found beside the code and stays until deleted; `.strata` is never committed (`git_commit` leaves it out, and the folder has its own `.gitignore` for a `git add` by hand).
- **Shape changes are pending changes** (`cli-edit_shape_changes_open_a_pending_change`). Arity, order and parameter-type changes already made holes when the edit gave only the changed function; they were rejected when the callers came in the same edit (a whole module given, which the editor always does), because an error in any definition the edit named rejected it. Holes are now made in every definition with errors, at the uses of what changed in its interface; what is left is checked again.
- **A definition that doesn't check in itself is still rejected**, not stored as a pending change with its errors (decided here; the issue asked for it). The package stays one that checks, during a pending change too (run, test and holes depend on it); a hole is a typed placeholder, and a body made one hole would hide the author's code in a `was` string and turn every typo into a pending change to fill and `done`. The editor reopens with the errors, and the text is kept, so nothing is lost. Revisit if agents still restart edits from scratch.
- **`strata edit --help` / `-h`** print edit's lines of `strata help` (`cli-edit_help`); **`--file F edit` that doesn't check** says `written: F doesn't check (N errors; --file edits are kept as given, unlike a package's edit, which is rejected):` with its errors (`cli-edit_file_that_doesnt_check_is_written`).

### Termination and helpers (decided with the user, session 3; built)
- Termination is reported for every function: terminates (with how) / may not terminate (with why) / assumed (`terminates` marker, after the contract). See docs/verification.md. The viewer shows "may not terminate" in orange; diffs show it in the verification word.
- **No `decreases` clauses** for now (user: a `for` loop covers the case where you can name the bound). Instead the bound is inferred. For `while a < b`, the solver tries `b - a` (and the other comparisons). For recursion, a smaller piece of a parameter is detected first. Otherwise candidate measures are tried (`termination::candidate_measures`): integer parameters, `len` of list or string parameters, both differences of each comparison of parameters in the body, then pairs compared in order (Ackermann). Not covered: mutual recursion, measures built from more than a difference (`n * n - k`), recursion through a lambda.
- **Helpers are seen through in contracts** when they terminate and cannot fail (`termination::analyze` → `transparent`): no `requires`, no refined parameters, no `mut`, no division by a non-literal, no `assert`, no hole, no refined constructors, and only such helpers or total builtins called. A helper whose termination is only assumed is still seen through, and the proof lists it. In function bodies, calls are still checked against contracts only.
- Policies: `--require terminates` accepts an explicit `terminates`; `--require no-recursion` rejects any recursion, even structural.

### `verify`'s speed (user, 2026-11-22; first part built, branch `verify-speed`)
- **Measured** (examples/tasks, 31 s): the solver 43%, one query at a time; compiling the fuzz drivers 27%; cache keys 22%; termination 5%; fuzzing itself 1%. LLVM took 0.1 s of the compile: the rest was regenerating the whole program's IR once per target whenever one target couldn't be fuzzed, to find which. `store::closure_hash` resolved the whole program once per definition.
- **Built**: a failing target is named in the error and left out (one more compile per failing target); `store::Closures` builds the graph and hashes once (same keys); functions on all cores (`par::map`), a function's obligations at once, termination's loop bounds and recursion measures in parallel, at most one solver process per core (`smt::solver_slot`); string lengths bounded by 2^64 in every query. All examples: 142 s → 59 s, the same verdicts.
- **Built** (branch `std-termination-cache`): std's own functions' loop bounds and recursion measures are kept between runs (`termination::StdCache`, one file per binary and solver): `hello` 1.25 s -> 0.68 s (`check` is 0.25 s).
- **Built** (branch `verify-cache-fix`): verdicts were kept only in a package's `.strata/verdicts.json` (and a script's cache folder), per worktree, keyed without the check's name (functions calling each other have one closure hash, so they shared a verdict: wrong ones in `std.ast.eval`), the seed, race, solver path or compiler, and written in place. Now one file per verdict (atomic), shared by a repository's worktrees (`.git/strata/verdicts`, else `~/.cache/strata/verdicts`), keyed by all of those and std's hash unless std is the program; solver give-ups aren't kept; fuzz drivers are compiled only for what runs; `--no-cache` replaces `--force`. std (`--cases 10`), CPU time: 720 s fresh, 1.2 s cached. Left: std's termination cache is keyed by name and binary, so std edited without a rebuild keeps old findings for the changed function; type checking and termination analysis still run every time.
- **Measured again** (2026-11-22, every example, empty cache, `--no-race`): 925 solver queries, 881 of them under 50 ms. Two queries of `memory.mem_record` (`examples/tasks`) reach the 20 s limit and are half of all solver time. The cross-check (proven functions run on 20 fuzzed inputs, a failure being a prover bug) costs at most 0.25 s of CPU per example: it has caught real prover bugs (#184's `break`, strings past U+2FFFF), so it stays. Each query is a Z3 process of its own.
- **The `mem_record` timeouts, explained and fixed** (branch `verify-solver-speed`): both are checks the prover can't prove (`find` and `contains` aren't modelled), so the answer is "sat" and Z3 must build a whole model: concrete strings for the 164 `TaskId`s the query mentions (each under the refinement `[A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,64}`), equal and distinct as the `find`s and `unique_ids` say. That is the string solver's weak spot: without the regexes, the same queries are sat in 0.7-3.5 s; with them, one is sat after 91 s and the other not within 120 s. Turning quantifier instantiation off doesn't help. Proofs don't need any of it: over std, the examples and `compiler` (1,968 queries), all 1,109 proofs took at most 422,229 Z3 resource units (0.5 s), while the 20M limit let counterexample searches run to 18M (16 s). Proof queries now get 2M (`Limit::Proof`); searches for models (loop invariants, which take up to 3.3M, and solver-made inputs) keep 20M. Past 2M a check is "the solver gave up" instead of a counterexample, which here was never a real one (replayed, it didn't fail); the fuzzer races it anyway.
- **One solver process per function** (built, same branch): a function's obligations go to one Z3 process, the sorts declared once, each query between `push` and `pop` with its own cone of facts (`Vc::solve_all`, `run_batch`; Z3's `rlimit` is per `check-sat`, `-t` per query; cvc5 with `--incremental`). The queries are written at once and the input closed, as for one query, so a solver that only answers a whole script (the tests' fakes) still works; answers missing when the process ends are asked again one process each. An interactive session (a query sent once the last is answered) hung on those fakes. On the 80 queries of `std.json`, one process took 0.03 s where 80 took 0.70 s: starting Z3 costs ~8 ms, more than most queries. The same 63 proofs of `examples/tasks` both ways.
- **Measured**: the same verdicts on every example, all of std and `compiler`; CPU time `examples/tasks` 32 s → 18 s, std 175 s → 143 s (a loaded machine: shares, not absolutes). Termination's measure queries still start a process each. Next: compiling the fuzz drivers, the largest cost outside the solver.
- **Measured again** (2026-11-24, the compiler in Strata, `STRATA_TIMINGS=1` now times verify's phases; CPU with Z3's from `/usr/bin/time`, a machine loaded by six other builds): `--no-cache` std 46.6 s CPU (44 s wall: Z3 24 s over 294 rounds, each a `sh` + `xargs` + a Z3 per script, 11 s of system time; the encoder 4 s; termination 2.6 s and 11 s of its Z3 searches), `examples/tasks` 30.6 s (the encoder 16.7 s of 25 s wall: `smt.body_with` replaced each string term naming a datatype read with `replace` over the whole script, a pass per term, and searched it once per term; Z3 2.5 s; fuzzing 2.3 s; the cross-check 1.7 s), traits 1.5 s, orders 1.0 s, math 2.9 s (the stall on traits and orders' 300 s are gone). By name in `compiler/` (10 functions of smt): 102 s wall, of which termination's analysis 45 s (Z3: every reached function's loops searched again), and still 69 s with every verdict cached (termination redone every run). The cross-check: std 0.76 s for 351 runs, tasks 1.3-1.7 s for 22: it stays.
- **Built** (branch `speed-verify`): (1) Z3 kept running (compiler/c/solver.c): one `z3 -in` per core, each script after `(reset)` with its limits set again (`:timeout`, `:rlimit`: they outlive a reset; `rlimit` counts per `check-sat`, so the answers are a process of its own's: compared on 600 scripts, and every script of std and tasks), error line numbers told from the script's first line, `-T` a kill (the process starts again); an in-process memo of answers (termination and the encoder ask the same loop searches: 199 of std's 945). Only a Z3 binary is kept running: scripts standing in for it (the tests' fakes, one claiming to be Z3) and a solver that doesn't answer a ready probe within 10 s get a process per script as before (the hang IDEAS warned about). `STRATA_SOLVER_POOL=0` turns it off; `STRATA_SOLVER_LOG=FILE` logs each script (time, flags, size, hash, first answer). (2) `body_with` names string terms in one pass when each is one parenthesized term (two such occurrences are apart or nested, so replacing where each starts, the longest first, is the same as one `replace` after another; any other term the old way): the same scripts byte for byte (sizes and hashes of every script compared). (3) Proofs kept by `interface_keys` (`verify-strata/proofs/`): the closure's definitions, a `ContractOnly` function by its interface hash, a transparent one by its code (and whether it is only assumed to finish); not by `--cases` (std's code in the key unless std is the program: then its own keys). A kept proof is cross-checked again, reported `(cached)`. Tests: `cli-verify_keeps_proofs_whose_callees_bodies_change` (a body changed: the caller's proof kept; a transparent helper's: re-proven; a contract: re-proven). (4) Termination's loop findings per function (`analyze_with`, `verify-strata/loops/`) by the same keys over the analysis's own view (smt's `transparency`), with the program's implementations' hash (which types are finite iterators). (5) Keys only for the checked definitions (and, for loops, what they reach), not the whole program's.
- **Measured after** (same machine, same verdicts on everything above, std modules `std.json`, `std.regex`, `std.toml` by name too): `--no-cache` std 46.6 s → 19.1 s CPU (44 → 17 s wall), tasks 30.6 → 15.2 s CPU (31 → 9.8 s wall; the encoder 16.7 → 2.3 s), math 2.9 → 0.9, traits 1.5 → 1.3, orders 1.0 → 0.9; `std.toml` 18.3 → 9.6 s, `std.regex` 7.9 → 4.4, `std.json` 3.5 → 2.7. Cached: std 19.3 → 2.6 s, tasks 25 → 14.8 s CPU (6 verdicts are never kept: "the solver gave up", though with `rlimit` that is mostly deterministic: telling it from a timeout, `(get-info :reason-unknown)`, would keep them); by name in `compiler/` with every verdict cached, 19 functions: 89 s → 27 s wall, 220 s → 27 s CPU (termination's analysis 39 s → 3 s; the rest is checking the program first: comptime, derive, check).
- **Measured** (2026-11-24, round 2, stream verify2, main 9ff6ad9's build): `verify --no-cache` of all of `compiler/` (3,100 functions) never finished: killed at 300 s, 131 s of CPU (verify ran on about one core of 11). The encoder's own work (Strata) came first: `smt.function` run again from scratch for every round of the loop-invariant searches' questions, each round waiting on its slowest question; `smt.refined` walked every type a type reaches on each call (the whole of `ast.Expr` for most of the compiler's values). Then the rounds themselves: `lex` alone, 18 functions, took 28 s, 12.8 s of it 35 rounds of questions one after another. Termination's graph work was quadratic (a walk from every node for what it reaches), and its loop bounds and measures were asked one query at a time.
- **Built** (branch `speed2-verify`): (1) Each function is encoded **once**, on worker threads (`parallel.map`, first `smt.counted_map`: each worker takes the next function as it finishes one, the results in order). Its questions go to the solver as it asks them (`Vc.live`: `ask` waits for the answer while the other workers go on), so every function's questions are in flight at once across the solver's processes. A question's answer doesn't depend on who asks it or when, so the encodings are the ones the rounds made. Without Z3 kept running (the tests' stand-in solvers), the rounds are kept (`encode_rounds`). (2) c/solver.c's pool is persistent worker threads around a job queue: `solver_pool_run` can be called from many threads at once; a finished function's obligations are queued ahead (`solver_pool_prefetch`, run when nothing else waits, only the answer kept), and a script already running is waited for, not run twice. (3) `smt.refined` uses the types that reach a refinement, found once per program (`Methods.refined_types`, backwards from the types that hold one). (4) Termination's analysis finds the strongly connected components once (Tarjan's algorithm, without recursion) and classifies every function in one pass. Only `while` loops and recursion go to the solver, each function's questions in the order it asks them, all functions' at once. The functions with `for` loops are encoded on the workers. (5) Termination's findings for std's and core's functions (loops and recursion measures, `verify-strata/loops|measures/`) are kept by std's code as a whole, the solver, the program's impls and package settings. A program's cold verify doesn't analyse std again once that's cached, and the release's warm-up programs ship them. (6) Chunks of 256 functions, not 32.
- **Measured after** (a machine loaded by five other builds; wall): `compiler/` 300+ s (killed) → 157-189 s (414 s of CPU, 3.3 GB); std 13-16 s → 8.5 s (14.8 s CPU); `examples/tasks` 9.8 → 5.6 s. The same verdicts on std, every example, and in `compiler/`: the 2,024 functions the old binary reached in 300 s have byte-identical verdict lines in the new binary's whole run. Each module was also verified on its own by both binaries, with the same solver answers; in that mode only, the old binary left 4 functions unfuzzed ("not compiled"), which its whole run fuzzed.
