Where `compiler/`'s time goes now: termination's encoding 72 s, verification's encoding 58 s, the cross-check 31 s (1,196 runs). Both encodings wait on the longest chain of one function's questions: loop-invariant searches at `rlimit` 20M that end `unknown` after 3-5 s each (51 queries over 1 s make 132 of the solver's 185 s). Next: that chain (fewer, smaller Houdini queries, or one function's loops searched in parallel); termination and verification encode the same `while` functions twice (different `Methods`), so they could share an encoding when no callee is seen through.
- **Fixed** (branch `fix-keep-shared`): `parallel.map`'s `driver_keep_shared` kept every object a worker dupped that was already held twice, for good; the encoder's workers run long and share their own data, so it leaked 33 GB on `compiler/`, and smt had its own `counted_map` without it. Now each `strata_memory_keep_shared` begins an **epoch** (1 to 255, going round; nested calls share one): what is made during it bears the epoch in its count word (bits 40 to 47; the count is now the low 40 bits, an arena's tag still bits 48 to 60) and is counted as usual (atomically when shared) and freed; only objects made before are kept. `strata_release` and the compiled code's inline release leave the epoch out (one `and`); `strata_alloc` stamps it only while tasks run (the tally written out, so the one thread tests that once). `parallel.map` takes elements as workers finish them (as `counted_map` did), and smt and termination use it again: `counted_map` is gone. **Measured** (against main built the same way, a machine at load 13-30): `verify` of all `compiler/` (`--no-cache`) 264 s, 461 s CPU, 660G cycles, 3.26 GB peak against main's 240-283 s, 457-484 s CPU, 697-711G cycles, 3.69-3.83 GB, the same verdicts; `verify 'smt.*'` 236 s CPU either way, 1.89 against 1.92 GB; `check` in `compiler/` one thread +0.3% instructions, 11 threads +1% instructions / +3% cycles (young objects now freed and counted atomically), 8% less memory; cold `build compiler --no-verify`: the same instructions (181.6G/185.5G on 1/11 threads against 181.9G/185.6G), cycles within 2%, peak memory 1.20 against 1.27 GB.
- **Round 3 (branch `verify-invariants`)**: invariants found by search written into the code, one search budget per run: see "Loops and invariants". On compiler/ it writes only two loops of `lex.lex` (about 1 s of search saved per uncached run): the expensive searches find invariants about values before the loop (`x₀`), which aren't code; making the candidates find their code forms is the next step. **Round 4 (branch `invariants-entry-values`)**: those are now written too (a value before the loop named by an expression in scope, else by a binding `start_x := x` made before the loop): compiler/'s search units 810M → 28M on the run after, `smt.str_len_terms`' 546M chain gone; see "Loops and invariants".
- **Next**: Compile-time results are keyed by the strata binary's time, so the first run after a rebuild pays ~5 s (`bank`'s literal probes). Position facts only for functions that index a list (the 7% of #190; tried on a branch, `lazy-positions`: 20% smaller queries but no solver time saved, not merged). Racing the fuzzer against the solver: decided by the user (2026-11-22), a full race, built (branch `verify-race`): each function's solver queries and fuzzer start together, the first failure stops the other (solver processes killed); the fuzzer waits up to 0.3 s and uses a cross-check's step budget until the solver answers (else it slowed quick proofs by ~7-10%); `--no-race` keeps the old order, for deterministic results and the prover-bug report (the tests that check the solver's own verdicts use it). Examples: same verdicts both ways, same time within noise; a 30 s query no longer holds up a bug the fuzzer finds (`the_fuzzer_stops_a_slow_solver`).

### Proven checks leave the compiled code (decided with the user 2026-11-22; built, branch `proven-checks`)
- **Principle** (user): what is proven at compile time is never checked again at run time; runtime checks stay only for what isn't proven, e.g. assumed code (outside code, capabilities), whose contracts can't be proven. Code that relies on assumptions but is itself proven needs no checks.
- **Where proofs come from**: `verify`'s cache. Then (user, 2026-11-22): `build` verifies first whatever changed since it was last verified (`store::unverified`: each definition's hash at its last verification, `ProofFile::verified`), quietly, with `strata verify`'s caches, and says how it went; a counterexample doesn't stop the build (its checks stay). `run` (the JIT) doesn't verify: it uses the cache; `build --no-verify` likewise. Scripts got a verdict cache too (with their proofs). `verify` writes `proofs.json` (package `.strata/`, or `~/.cache/strata/scripts/<path hash>` for a script): per function, the checks proven (with those true by the types alone, `Vc::given`) and those not (`open`: refuted, unknown, stopped), with the definition's closure hash; `build` / `run` use only entries whose hash is current (`store::current_proofs`).
- **Escape hatch** (user chose): `--all-checks` on `build` / `run`. Fuzzing, replay and the cross-check always compile every check: they are what catches a prover bug (#184's `break`).
- **How a compiled check meets its proof**: by name within the function, the text `verify --checks` prints (`assert e`, `ensures e`, `invariant \`e\` …`, `result of \`f\``, `assigning \`x\``, `` `let` annotated `T` ``, `constructing \`C\``, `index in range`; `llvm::same_check`). A check is left out only when every check of that name in the function is proven (at least one, none open). Sound as long as the prover sees every check site (it evaluates lambda bodies too); an unmatched name keeps the check.
- **Entry checks** (parameters' types, `requires`) are the callers' to prove: left out when every function referring to the callee proved the call, and nothing else refers to it (`llvm::Refs`: references from contracts, types, tests, impls, or a method call of the same short name keep them). `main` keeps its own.
- **`verify` before `build` had to be fast** (user: a first verify of a small program in seconds is unbearable). A script whose `main` ran a 7 s loop took 209 s to verify: the solver needed 0.1 s, the cross-check ran `main` 20 times with a step budget that a list's type check (in the runtime) never counted. Now a function without parameters is fuzzed and cross-checked once (every run is the same), and a list check counts a step per element when compiled for fuzzing: 0.5 s. A list check also shows the failing index only when an element fails (it made a string per element). Found on the examples: proofs were taken by a nested compilation made first (a pattern's matcher; only `Entry::Main` takes them now), and a record constructor's field checks didn't match its proof's name.
- **Measured**: a proven `List[Natural]` result checked on each call: 2.17 s → 0.04 s; a `requires` + `List[Natural]` argument per call: 6.7 s → 0.00 s; a plain bounds check in a hot loop: no difference (a predicted branch).
- **Not yet**: trait methods (their compiled names differ from `verify`'s labels; checks kept), std (precompiled once with its checks; its proofs could be kept with it), checks in the C runtime (division by zero, string indexes), the entry checks of a function `strata run f ARGS` calls (that probe isn't verified; it compiles without proofs).
- **Assumed code, only at the call** (user, 2026-11-22: what is assumed is the foreign code itself, not the code around it; built, branch `assume-foreign`): a function that calls C (`extern c`), lives in a module that declares externs, or takes a capability was reported as assumed as a whole (from the interpreter, which couldn't run C), so nothing in it was proven and its callees kept their entry checks (`fuzzy`'s `closest`). Now only the foreign functions and a capability's own methods (and mocks) are assumed: their contracts are taken at the call (and compiled code checks a C function's `ensures` there); the rest is proven and fuzzed as usual, C included, except that code taking a capability isn't fuzzed (nothing can make one: what the solver leaves is reported as skipped). `fuzzy`'s `main` and `wc`'s `wc` and `main` are proven; their builds leave out every check.

### Loops and invariants: what to build first (decided with the user 2026-11-22)
- **Studied on a real program.** An agent loop (the runner of an agent harness, in TypeScript) states its invariants in a comment: the conversation sent to the model starts with a user message of text only, and every tool result follows the tool use it answers; pruning keeps both. In Strata these are *data* invariants, not loop invariants: `type Conversation = List[Message] where …` (the comment's claim becomes `render`'s result type) and a `History` record refined over its messages and its two pruning indices. A declared type is already checked after every assignment and kept across every pass of a loop, so no loop needs an `invariant` clause. Stating "a tool result answers the message just before it" (local) rather than "some earlier message" makes pruning at a tool use obviously valid. Running the sketch: the fuzzer found that updating the two indices one field at a time breaks the record's refinement in between (assign the record whole); the scan loop's termination is proven; nothing else is, because list positions (`ms[i - 1]` next to `ms[i]`, slices, `any`, `all` over indices) are not modelled, and the fuzzer can't make a `History` (too narrow for random values).
- **Order** (user agreed): 1. a `break` leaves a `while` with its condition still true (unsound before: `ensures it >= 10` was "proven" for a loop that breaks at 3; only the cross-check caught it); built, branch `loop-break`. 2. list positions in the prover; built, branch `list-positions`. Not SMT sequences (they would need records and sums as SMT datatypes): an element of a list of a checked type is a `Template` whose unknowns are SMT functions of the position (`(v6 i)`), and of the outer positions for a list inside an element (`(v6 i j)`); `Shift` (slices, `take`, `drop`, `..rest`), `Concat` (`+`, `push`), `Rev`, literals, `range`, `map` and merged branches translate positions; `filter` / `sort` keep the old "some element" model. `all(xs, p)` known true is also a quantified fact over positions (`forall_positions`; unknowns made inside it are functions of its variable, and what it learns of lists stays inside), and `every` proves "all elements" by position where it can. `any` is `!all(!p)`. Left: a non-empty list as a counterexample (query the element functions), and nested quantified facts with strings (the agent loop's `answers`: Z3 times out on the 57 KB query; hand-picked instantiations at the positions the code takes, instead of MBQI, are the likely fix). 3. a fuzzer that can build values of narrow refinements (`History`); first part built (branch `solver-inputs`, user chose "ask the solver" over compiled generators): when random values can't be made, `Vc::inputs` asks Z3 for up to 20 different models of the parameters' types and `requires` (lists ≤ 6 elements, their first elements made by position, `input_elems`; each model blocked for the next; random seeds), run as one compiled module (`replay_all`). `render` and `shift` of the agent sketch are fuzzed now (were skipped). Few models come back once quantified facts are in the query (3-6 of 20); compiled generators for common patterns (sorted, consecutive, indices within a list) remain an option. Next: use `input_elems` for the solver's own counterexamples (it finds them but can't show a non-empty list). 4. inferred invariants (built, branch `loop-invariants`; `Vc::infer_invariants` for `while`, solved inline: `Vc::solver`; facts the declared types already give are left out; loops with `continue` since `loop-invariant-clause`; not inside a trial pass; examples: `isqrt` and tasks' `core.drain` proven, the agent sketch's scan in `shift` proves its indices, same CPU time): candidates from the loop condition and its non-strict forms, the `ensures` and later `assert`s (with `it` as the returned variable), `requires`, the body's `if`s and each changed variable against its entry value and the parameters; keep those true on entry, then drop those a pass doesn't keep until none is dropped (Houdini), so what remains is proven; reported as "inferred invariant: …", capped (~30 candidates).
- **Written invariants** (built, branch `loop-invariant-clause`): an `invariant e` clause between a loop's header and its body (one per line, like `requires` / `ensures`), for `while`, `for` and `loop`: proven on entry and after every pass and `continue`, assumed at the head (so after the loop with the condition false, not at a `break`), checked at run time at the loop head (on entry, after each pass, on leaving normally). In a `for`, it can't name the loop variable (user: a `for` rarely needs one). Stored as `Stmt::Invariant`, the first statements of the loop's body (a new tag, so no file conversion); `invariant` is a word only there. Inference treats written ones as given (joint induction: both are proven). Also built in the same change: `continue` keeps its state (`Vc::continues`, like `break`s), so loops with `continue` get inferred invariants and termination bounds (checked at every pass end).
- **Open**: `renderForModel`'s retry loop ends only because each `shift` moves `inner` forward or fails; saying so needs an `ensures` comparing a `mut` parameter with its value before the call, which can't be written yet.
- **Invariants found by search are written into the code; one search budget per run** (decided with the user 2026-11-24; built, branch `verify-invariants`). (1) Invariants found at once (templates and bounds, a few thousand resource units) stay implicit. (2) When finding a loop's invariants took a real search (more of Z3's resource units than a proof may use, 2M: `found_by_search`; each search query asks `(get-info :rlimit)`), `verify` writes them as `invariant` clauses on that loop, so later runs only prove them and readers see them, and says so (`edited lex.lex: added `invariant lx.at <= n` to `while …` (found by search in 0.7 s, 5M solver resource units)`); also when `build` verifies first. Only the program verified is written (its own definitions: never std or core as dependencies), only invariants that are code (no `x₀`) and that read back as the same tree (written, parsed, resolved again); the rest stays in the verdict cache. One edit per run, after the verdicts (coordinator's requirement: writing must not cost what the search saves): only the changed definitions' module files are encoded again, the package isn't checked again (an `invariant` clause changes no signature and verify has just proven it): 0.5 s on compiler/, against 38 s for a no-op `strata edit` of compiler/ (which re-checks the whole package). Logged in strata.log as verify's (`"by":"verify"`) and committed when the package was opened from its folder; with `--file`, written as `--file F edit` writes. `--no-write` turns it off. A loop with written invariants isn't searched: its inference only asks `probe`s at 0.5M units, giving up at the first unanswered (so a hand-written partial invariant still gets `i <= n` for free; strictly "no inference at all" would need `cli-written_loop_invariants_are_checked`'s `triangle` to write `i <= n` too). (3) All of a run's loop-invariant searches (termination's and verification's) draw on one pool of resource units (c/solver.c `solver_budget`: each script run is charged what it used, an answer from the run's memo nothing; deterministic units, though which loops are left when it runs out depends on the workers' order); once used up, a search is answered `budget` without running, the loops left are reported with how to go on (write an `invariant`, or `--search-budget N`), and those verdicts, proofs and termination findings aren't cached. Default 2,000M: measured with nothing cached, std's searches take 53M, every example's under 0.1M, compiler/'s 992M (smt.str_len_terms' inner loop alone 546M, 68 s). `--show-searches` lists every loop's search.
- **Measured** (compiler/, `verify --no-cache`, a machine loaded by other builds (load 8-13), so wall times are ±20 s): before 149-167 s wall (main's build; this branch with `--no-write`), 992M units of search; first run after (writes) 194 s, writing 0.53 s, two loops of `lex.lex` written (5M + 3M units, 1.1 s of search); second run after (uses them) 180 s, 987M units: those two loops now cost 0.5M each (probes). Verdicts: std and all 12 examples byte-identical to main's build (`--no-race`); compiler/ identical module by module except `lex.lex` proven with 5 checks instead of 3 (its two written invariants). So per run without a cached verdict about 1 s of solver is saved, against 0.5 s paid once.
- **Finding: compiler/'s hard loops can't be written as found.** Of the 16 loops whose search took over 2M units, 12 found invariants about a value before the loop (`i >= i₀`, `end <= end₀`): 789M units, 98% of those loops' and 80% of all search units (`str_len_terms` 546M, `locate.parse_location` 80M, `diff.fold_lines` 60M, `smt.str_len_terms`' outer loop 40M, `highlight.marks` 20M, `pending.unhex` 16M, ...; std: `std.toml.quote` 35M, `std.cli.parse` 6M). Their code forms usually exist (`end₀` is `n`: `mut end := n` just before; `i₀` is `k`), but no candidate states them: candidates compare a changed integer with the parameters, not with unchanged locals. Next (to decide): candidates against the unchanged integer locals in scope (`end <= n`, `i >= k`), so the search finds a writable form, or replace `x₀` by the expression the variable was set to just before the loop when that is made of unchanged variables; then these loops get written once and the 68 s chain goes. An `old(x)` in `invariant` clauses would be new syntax (ask).
- **Invariants about a value before the loop are written without new syntax** (decided with the user 2026-11-25; built, branch `invariants-entry-values`). For loop invariants there is no `old(x)` / `x₀` syntax: (1) when the value before the loop is an expression in scope and unchanged by the loop (the variable was set to `k` or `n` just before it, a parameter, a variable the loop never assigns), the invariant is written with it: `invariant i >= k`, `invariant end <= n` (`main.value_before`: the last statement before the loop that sets the variable, `x := e` / `mut x := e` / `x = e`, with `e` variables, literals, fields and `+ - *` of them, none of whose variables the statements since or the loop change or bind again); (2) otherwise `verify` makes an ordinary binding just before the loop, named apart from the function's text (`start_i := i`, else `start_i_2`, ...), writes `invariant i >= start_i`, and says so (``added `invariant i >= start_i` to `while …` and `start_i := i` before it``); both in the same batched edit, read back as the same tree as #437 requires, and proven, not searched, by the next run (`main.entry_forms`; `smt.LoopFound.code` keeps `$entry_x`, `LoopFound.changed` the loop's variables as the verifier saw them). Two found invariants that read the same once written (`i >= i₀` and `i >= k`) are written once.
- **Open (ask the user): `ensures` about a `mut` parameter's value before the call** gets a syntax of its own, different from the loops' answer above (a binding can't be made before a call from inside the callee's contract), still to be decided with the user; not built. (`renderForModel`'s retry loop above needs it.)
- **Measured** (compiler/, `verify 'MODULE.*' --no-cache --no-race --show-searches`, one run per module, 39 runs: a whole `verify --no-cache` of compiler/ with main's build hit the 300 s limit at load 29, after 1001 functions). Search units: before (main's build, `--no-write`) 810M; first run after (writes) 810M; second run after (uses them) **28M** (-97%). `smt.str_len_terms`' inner loop 546M units, 136-156 s of search → 0.5M, 0.1 s (probes); its outer loop 40M → 0.5M; `locate.parse_location` 80M → 0.6M, `diff.fold_lines` 60M → 0.5M, `highlight.marks` 20M, `pending.unhex` 16M, `smt.candidate_shown` 8M, `smt.parse_sx` 5M, `text.plain_float` 5M, `highlight.changed_words` 4M, `smt.replace_terms` 3M → each under 1M. Left over 2M: `lex.lex` 4M and `main.verify_file` 3M, which find nothing to write. Summed over the 39 runs: before 1324 s wall / 1296 s CPU (load 20-34), first after 932 s / 1076 s, second after 387 s / 591 s (load 4-12, so wall times compare only roughly; units are deterministic). Written: 14 loops in 11 definitions of 6 modules (own commits); in 9 the value before the loop is an expression in scope (`invariant i >= k`, `end <= n`, `i >= 0` for `mut i := 0`, `k <= len(parts) - 1`, `point <= byte_len(whole) + shift`), 5 got a binding (`start_i := i`, where the variable was set by `i += 1` or in an `if` just before, or from a variable the loop changes). Verdicts: std and all 12 examples byte-identical to main's build (`--no-write`); compiler/ identical module by module except the written definitions, proven with more checks (their invariants). Finding on the way: an expression naming a pattern-bound variable (`for (i, l) in enumerate(ls)`: `invariant j <= i - 1`) is equivalent but not proven by the verifier (it reads `i` again as part of a tuple of unknown shape), so pattern-bound names get a binding instead (`main.pattern_bound`).

### Values made as they are read, in the prover (decided with the user 2026-11-22; built, branch `smt-lazy-shapes`; its cells replaced by datatype terms, see "Values as SMT datatypes")
- **The problem.** `Vc::shape` made a value's symbolic form whole when it appeared (a parameter, a list element's template, a call's result, a variable after a loop): a record all its fields, a sum a tag plus the payload of *every* constructor, recursively to depth 3. For a wide recursive sum that is ~(fields × variants)^3 terms, cloned at every binding and branch: a loop over `List[Node]` with `Node` of 20 variants of `(Node, Node, Int)` took over 4 GB; `compiler/Expr` (35 variants) made a 30-line `merge_sort` over `List[(Expr, Expr)]` take 32 s and several GB.
- **Lazy parts.** A value's outer level is made at once (a record's fields that are plain values, a sum's tag); a field or payload that is itself structured (a record, sum, tuple or union) is a `Val::Lazy` cell, made the first time code reads it (`Vc::force`: a field read, a pattern looking inside, `is`, a refinement that reads it, a comparison with a made value). Cells are kept in `Vc::cells`, so a part is made once and every copy of the value sees the same terms. Each cell carries `valid`, "this part is a valid value of its type": `true` for a type without refinements, else an unknown defined (`valid = check_type(made part)`) when the part is made. Checking or assuming the validity of a whole value uses its parts' `valid`, so a parameter's guarantees reach a part when it is read, and a check never makes parts. The cost follows what the function reads; the depth cutoff is gone (a pattern five levels deep is proven with the payload's refinement).
- **Contexts.** A part is made in the context it was created in, not where it is read: its fresh unknowns are functions of the same positions (a list element's template: the cell keeps its facts with the placeholders, and each position's copy, `Make::View`, places them), with no path condition (they are facts about the value, true on every path), and its facts and unknowns go where its scope's are: a part of an outer value first read inside a quantified fact (`every`, `forall_positions`) is made as if outside it (`Vc::regions`), so it is not bound by that quantifier.
- **Merges and `==`.** Merging two branches merges made parts as before; two parts, one of them not made yet, become a `Make::Merge` cell (`valid` is `ite`), made when read. Comparing two such parts gives an unknown for their equality, defined (one level: tags equal, and the parts' parts compared the same way) once both are made. Making the other side instead would not end: a tree merged or compared with its own subtree (`if c { t } else { t.left }`, found on `std.regex.translate`, which took over 4 GB that way) pairs each subtree with the one below it. A part compared or merged with a value built by the code is made (one level per level of that value). So `a == b` costs nothing until the code looks inside, and then says exactly what it did before. Inside a template or a position-quantified fact, where such unknowns can't be kept, comparing makes both sides, to a depth of 4. Calls whose arguments have recursive types are now remembered too (the key no longer contains an `Opaque` cutoff).
- **Size budget.** At most `MAX_CELLS` (200,000) lazy parts per function; past it a part is `Opaque` (any value), with the reason. No proof of std or the examples comes near it. Refinements that read through a recursive type stay bounded by the existing contract nesting limit.
- **Counterexamples.** A part the solver's model doesn't cover (never read) is shown as the simplest value of its type (`some_literal`); the replay checks it, as for any model.
- **Termination analysis follows the selection.** `termination::analyze` takes the roots (the functions `verify NAME` checks) and runs the costly loop and measure proofs only for what they reach; others are left unknown (and not transparent). With no selection, all as before.
- **Left**: a refinement that calls a function with a `requires` is evaluated, when a part is made, without its call site's path condition (as a parameter's always was); `refresh` and `havoc` of a generic record still use the record's name without its type arguments.

### Exact rationals as the default number (`Real`; raised by the user 2026-11-22; open, not started)
The user's idea: decimal literals and default arithmetic as exact numbers that grow when they need to, as `Int` grows into a big integer, with `Float` kept as a type of its own, as sized integers (`I8`) are. In practice "real" means exact rationals (`p / q` of big integers): `+`, `-`, `*`, `/` and comparisons are exact (`0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3`); irrational results (`sqrt`, `exp`, `sin`, π) can't be.
- **Why**: the prover would model them exactly as SMT `Real` (linear real arithmetic is decidable and fast; nonlinear is decidable, slower, far better than bit-blasted floats), with division by zero the only fault (checked by `y != 0`, as for `Int`). Number code becomes verifiable as integer code is, and `0.1` means one tenth.
- **`Float` is not a refinement of `Real`**: `I8` is `Int` with a range check because its operations are `Int`'s; a float's are not (each rounds: `0.1` itself isn't a `Float`). So `Float` stays its own type, its operations defined as "the exact result, rounded to the nearest double, a fault past the largest", with an explicit conversion `to_float(r) -> Option[Float]` (`None` out of range); an evaluator could then run float arithmetic as exact arithmetic plus that one conversion.
- **To decide**: irrational functions (only on `Float`; with a precision, `sqrt(x, digits: 20)`; or, as Pyret, which has exact rationals by default, "rough" numbers whose `==` is refused); how `1/3` prints (`print`, f-strings, `{x:.2}`); JSON (exact decimals read exactly), FFI (`double` converted explicitly), the fuzzer's generators.
- **Costs**: small rationals are 10 to 100 times slower than hardware floats, and unlike `Int` they grow in ordinary use (100 compoundings of `x * 1.07`: a 200-digit denominator; Newton's method doubles the digits each step), so numeric code would say `Float`. Effort about that of big `Int`s: the type and literals, rationals over the runtime's big integers (normalized by their GCD) with a small-value fast path, code generation, the prover's mapping (the easy part, and the largest gain), std, examples and recorded outputs migrated.

### The verifier's gaps, closed (asked for by the user 2026-11-23, one PR: branch `verify-gaps`)
- **Trait laws proven**: a law's wrapper states the law as its `ensures`; a trait call on a value of a known type (or `Self.empty()`, the type's name passed along) uses that implementation, seen through in contracts when its method ends and can't fail (`termination::analyze` marks implementations' methods transparent, by `impl_label`); values of a type parameter are terms of an uninterpreted sort (equal or not); two lists known by position are equal when their lengths and items are, but only in a goal (the quantifier negated: one witness position), never as a fact (user, 2026-11-23: no quantifiers Z3 must instantiate). `examples/traits`: the four laws of `Money` and `List[T]` proven.
- **Counterexamples with non-empty lists**: the parameters' lists' first 6 elements (one nested level, at most 400 terms, only for `verify`'s proofs) are made when the function is, their facts kept out of the queries; a model that can't be shown is asked for again with them. Made at depth 3 for every termination query, they took 22 GB on `examples/orders` (found by another session; the memory guard now measures phys_footprint, not RSS).
- **Proven checks out of compiled code**: trait methods (looked up under `verify`'s label and name), a proven division (`strata_div_nz`), and std: all of std is proven (530 functions; user's call: assume it, keep it so with `std_is_proven` and `--require proven` on changed std modules), so its library leaves out the checks inside its functions; entry checks stay, `--all-checks` compiles std with the program.
- **Not done: nested quantified facts.** A conversation type whose every tool result answers some earlier message (`all(0..len, i -> ... any(0..i, j -> any(calls, c -> c == id))`): appending a call and its result is proven only with 12.8M Z3 units and 17,000 quantifier instantiations (3.5 s), past the 2M a proof may cost (user: proofs take milliseconds; a bigger budget is not the fix). Tried: stating each known range fact at each witness position of a goal: the query grew 4x and Z3 still searched (33,000 instantiations), reverted. What it needs is an encoding where the nested `any` is a witness function (a skolem per outer position) rather than a quantifier inside a quantifier, or a fact stated only at the positions the code builds (the appended ones), with the rest carried over from the parameter's validity unchanged.

### Values as SMT datatypes (discussed with the user 2026-11-22; built, branch `smt-datatypes`)
The prover built values out of plain solver variables, one per field: a record is its fields, a sum is a tag plus the payload of every variant, and since list positions (PR #190) a list element is a template of functions of its position, one per field. Lazy shapes (branch `smt-lazy-shapes`) built those parts only when the code read them, within a size budget. The user judged datatypes cleaner: records, sums and unions as Z3's algebraic datatypes (`declare-datatypes`), so a value is one term, never expanded.
- **Built** (`src/smt/data.rs`): a structured part (a field or payload that is a record, sum, tuple or union) is `Val::Data { ty, t }`, one term of its type's datatype sort; reading it unfolds one level through selectors (`Vc::unfold`), with no fresh unknowns, so cells, their contexts, `Make::View` / `Make::Merge`, the pending comparisons and the 200,000-part budget are gone. Merging two terms is `ite`; `==` on two terms of one sort is `(= a b)` (complete: equal values have equal parts, different constructors differ, and a value is never one of its parts, so `t != t.left` is proven, which lazy shapes couldn't). Sorts: one datatype per instantiated type (`T:Box[Int]`), declared in dependency order with mutually recursive ones together (Tarjan), a tag function per datatype, a union a datatype with a constructor per member shape, uninterpreted sorts for floats, functions, dicts, sets and type parameters; every name starts with `T:` (Z3 defines `Set`).
- **Refinements**: "`t` is a valid `T`" is an uninterpreted predicate per (erased type, sort), defined once for each term unfolded by the check of its level, whose parts are their own predicates: a recursive type is never expanded and nothing is quantified. Definitions hold on every path (`Vc::defs`, always in the cone); inside a quantified scope or a template they stay with what is made there.
- **Lists inside datatypes**: not SMT sequences, as planned. A sequence of datatypes inside a quantified fact gives Z3 no models (sat queries ran into the time limit). A list field is a term of a list sort (`T:List(X)`) with `len` and `at` functions (`Gen::Seq`); equal terms are equal lists, which is sound (a run gives a model where each list value is one term), only less complete. An item taken gets "the list valid ⇒ the item valid".
- **The outer level stays as before**: a parameter's (or a call result's) own fields are variables of their own, and its lists templates by position: with the whole value one datatype constant, its list fields became list-sort terms and quantified facts over them made Z3 slow on sat queries (62 s of CPU on `examples/tasks` against 21 s); structural at the top, the same 21-28 s.
- **Two things Z3 needed** (cvc5 needs neither): a string read from a datatype (`(sel t)`) under `str.in_re` makes Z3's string solver time out where a constant equal to it is instant, so each such term is named by a constant in the query (`str_terms`); and `every`'s element outside any scope is a witness constant instead of a bound variable (its formula is only ever a premise, `(=> q b)`, where that is skolemization), so its strings can be named too. cvc5 gets `--dt-nested-rec`.
- **Same verdicts** as lazy shapes on every example, on `compiler`'s `lex`, `exhaustive`, `resolve`, `parse`, `check`, and std's `json`, `toml`, `regex`, `web` (cvc5: the same verdicts, a few counterexample searches end in "unknown"). Counterexamples are read from models with an s-expression parser (Z3 prints `let`s); some now show other numbers.
- **Left**: values that keep their facts through loops and `mut` calls (still refreshed to their type's guarantees); a list inside a datatype value as a counterexample only when it is short and its items were read; the parameters' outer level as datatypes too, once Z3 copes with them in quantified facts (or with cvc5); `fresh_data`'s single-term values for top-level unions only.

### Integers and floats (decided, user, session 3)
- **`Int` is a big integer that is a machine integer while it fits** (like Python). Built: `Value::Int(i64)`, and `Value::Big(BigInt)` only for a value that doesn't fit (so equal numbers are equal values); arithmetic switches over on overflow, and a literal too large for 64 bits is `Expr::BigInt(digits)`. Integers beyond 64 bits are written to JSON as strings of digits (serde_json can't write larger numbers without `arbitrary_precision`, which breaks deserialising the internally tagged verdicts); they are read from a number up to 2^64 - 1 or a string. An integer pattern must fit in 64 bits.
- **Sized integers are types of their own** (decided by the user 2026-11-23, replacing "refinements of `Int`"; built, branch `fixed-ints`): `I8` … `I64`, `U8` … `U64` are core's `builtin type`s, machine integers whose arithmetic wraps. They are not aliases of `Int` (one is modulo 2^width, the other isn't), so nothing mixes them with `Int` or with each other: `x + n` with `x: U32`, `n: Int` is an error naming the conversions.
  - **Semantics**: an operation is `Int`'s, then the result modulo 2^width (two's complement for the signed ones). So `/` and `%` are Euclidean as on `Int`; `<<` by the width or more gives 0, `>>` gives 0 or -1; a signed type's shift by a negative amount is `Int`'s fault. A rotate is two shifts and an or (`x >> n | x << 32 - n`), which LLVM turns into one instruction.
  - **Conversions** (core builtins): `x.to_int()`; `n.to_u32()`, which requires `n` in range (proven by `verify`, else checked at run time: "`300` doesn't fit in `U8`"); `n.wrap_u32()`, `n` modulo 2^32. Between two sized types: through `Int` (`x.to_int().wrap_u8()`).
  - **Literals** (user: an integer literal is an `Int` unless its place says otherwise): a literal is of a sized type only where a type hint says so: an annotation, a parameter, a result, a field, an element of a list of them, or the other operand of an operator (`x + 1`, `x >> 3`: there it could only be an error otherwise). It must fit. The checker writes it as its conversion (`to_u32(5)`), so `verify` and the compilers see the type; the compilers fold it to a constant.
  - **Compiled** in its own width (`i8` … `i64`, LLVM's wrapping `add`/`mul`, `icmp ult` for unsigned order); in a block's or list's 8-byte slot, sign- or zero-extended. `extern c` passes them as they are. `compiler/binary`'s SHA-256 runs on `U32` words: 1.18 s against 1.69 s to hash 4 MB.
  - **Verified** as integers with their range as facts and every operation wrapped with `mod` (no bit-vectors: the rest of the model is integers); `&`, `|`, `^` keep their bounds only. So `ensures it > x` of `x + 1` on a `U8` fails at `x = 255`.
  - **Both compilers**: `compiler/check` types them as the Rust checker does (same errors), `compiler/eval` runs them as `U32(5)` values made at declared types and by the conversions, `compiler/codegen` compiles them as `llvm.rs` does.
  - **Left**: a literal in a `Set { … }` / `Dict { … }` literal or a generic call's argument without a hint stays an `Int` (`Set { 1.to_u16() }`): those literals are desugared into calls before checking. Patterns over sized integers check their literals' range in Rust only (`compiler/check` doesn't type patterns' literals yet). Skipping the hash check of files the compiler wrote itself would be a separate saving. `compiler/resolve` stores `[..].any(f)` differently from Rust (found while writing `compiler/codegen`, worked around there).
- **`Float` is `F64`**; `F32`, `BF16` and similar exist alongside, with their own LLVM representations. (Floats are not modelled by the prover yet; SMT-LIB has a floating-point theory for when they are.)

### Optimization only with `--release` (decided with the user 2026-11-23; built, branch `opt-levels`)
`strata build`, `strata run` (the JIT), `strata test` and `strata run f ARGS` compile unoptimized (-O0) by default; `--release` compiles with -O2 (and, for an executable, the runtime merged in with `-flto`). Measured on the compiler package: building took 30 s, 13 of them clang -O2 on 20 MB of IR (1.8 s at -O0); now 12 s by default, 30 s with `--release`. Compile-time code and probes were unoptimized already (they run once); `verify`'s fuzzing stays optimized (its cases run many times); std's precompiled library stays -O2 (built once, cached). Measured on the way, for what comes next (the user's goal: building the compiler in under a second): the IR is about 440 bytes per line of Strata, 78% of it the program's own functions (38% loads and stores of slots, 12% field addressing, 11% count updates, 7% `Int` small/big checks); `check` of the compiler package is 1.25 s, more than half of it compile-time code (each `comptime` copies the program, resolves it and hashes its dependencies again to find its cached value), 31% type checking, 21% refinement probes (decided: their results kept for `verify`).

### Compilation before transpilation
1. **Native compilation through LLVM** to machine code, with a garbage-collected runtime (no borrow checker in Strata). This comes before transpiling.
   - **Step 1 (built, branch `llvm-backend`)**: `strata build` (`src/llvm.rs`) writes LLVM IR as text from the elaborated program and compiles it with `clang` and a C runtime (`src/llvm/runtime.c`); no LLVM library is linked into `strata`. Scalars (`Int` as `i64`, `Float`, `Bool`, `String`, `()`), functions, control flow, `match` on literals / conditions / refinement types, f-strings, a few built-ins; contracts checked at run time with the interpreter's messages. `tests/build.rs` ran each program both ways and compared (now against recorded outputs, `tests/data/build/expected`). See docs/tools.md. Everything below up to "The interpreter deleted" was written while the interpreter existed; it was deleted in #192, so compiled code is the only way code runs.
   - **Parity with the interpreter, so the JIT can replace it** (user, 2026-11-21; one stacked PR per item; all built, and the interpreter is gone): (1) the small refusals; (2) unions beyond records and variants (`Int | String`), and branches whose values are laid out differently; (3) trait-value methods that mention `Self`, have type parameters of their own or a `mut` parameter, generic supertraits, returning a trait-typed value; (4) regexes (`matches`); (5) big integers; (6) `mock`; (7) `strata test` on the JIT (faults that return instead of exiting, captured output, compiled `test` blocks); (8) `strata verify` on the JIT (calling any compiled function with values, through JSON; a step budget). Later: `serve` (needs an FFI and a network library), `derive`'s compile-time evaluation (the same JSON bridge plus a step budget; the specialization is Rust already), a cache of compiled code (the JIT's `-O2` costs ~0.3 s on a medium program).
     - **(1) built** (branch `compile-small-gaps`): a field of a union of records at different places in each member (by the variant; one without it faults as the interpreter does), assigning a field of a union or a tuple (`pair.0 = …`, also added to the interpreter, which faulted `cannot assign`), any pattern in a `for` over a range, `==` on trait values (an equality entry in the table: the same implementation's, else unequal), the `stack:` line of a fault (`strata_enter` / `strata_leave` around the program's functions, not the compiled prelude's or lambdas, as the interpreter's), and running out of stack is `fault (Limit): stack overflow (recursion too deep)` instead of a crash.
     - **(2) built** (branch `compile-unions`): a union with members other than records and variants is a pointer: records' and variants' blocks as they are, any other member in a box `{ tag, value }` with a tag per member type, unique in the program (so a smaller union's values are a larger one's unchanged). Values are boxed where they flow into a union (`coerce`) and unboxed where the checker narrowed them (an arm's matched variable: `expr` reads the node's type). Type patterns and `is`, literal / list / tuple patterns (the member first), `==`, printing, dropping, refinement checks of a union-typed value (`-1 is not a `Positive | String``), and trait methods on a union (by the member; not a `mut self` one, which would write a member back where the union goes). Found in the checker, then fixed (branch `checker-union-fixes`): a declared type is the variable's, not its first value's (`mut u: Int | String = 3` made `u` an `Int`, so `u = "text"` was refused), and `List[Int]` is a type pattern (`CtorPat::Type`, the arguments in brackets, also what `match comptime` writes as `Option[x]`; it was parsed as a constructor's arguments and printed `List(Int)`).
     - **(3) built** (branch `compile-dyn-methods`): methods called on trait values that take or return `Self` as a whole type (the table's wrapper unwraps a `Self` argument, which must be of the same implementation, and wraps a `Self` result into a trait value), that take a `mut` parameter besides `self` (a slot through the table, written back), or that have type parameters of their own (a dispatcher per call's type arguments, switching on the pair's table among every table of the trait; dispatchers are made once the queue is empty, and again while new tables appear); methods of a generic supertrait (`super_sub` reads its arguments from `supers`); functions returning a trait-typed value. Still refused: `Self` inside another type (`List[Self]`, `Option[Self]`), and type parameters together with `Self` or `mut`. A `Self` argument on a trait value is refused by the checker (decided with the user 2026-11-21, branch `dyn-self-args`): `xs[0].bigger(xs[1])` could pass a `Ci` to `Sq`'s `bigger`, so it is an error with a `help:` (declare the parameter as the trait, `other: Shape`, to take any implementation; that is how the two meanings are told apart: `Self` is the receiver's own type, the trait's name any). Allowed: the receiver variable itself, or what methods returning `Self` make of it (`s.bigger(s.scaled(2))`). The table's check at run time stays (a `mock`).
     - **(4) built** (branch `compile-regex`): `matches(s, re)` with a literal pattern is compiled to a DFA when the program is: `regex-automata` (the engine under the interpreter's `regex`) builds `^(?:re)$`, and its states reachable from the start become tables (each byte's class, the next state of each (state, class), dead states, states that match at the end) that `strata_dfa_match` runs; Unicode classes work as in the interpreter. A bad pattern faults with the interpreter's message. Refused: a pattern built at run time (it would need a regex engine in the runtime; with a Rust runtime, `regex` itself), and a pattern whose DFA gives up (Unicode word boundaries) or passes 4M cells. Also: functions shown as values (`"<closure (x) -> …>"`, `"<fn twice>"`, as the interpreter), so `examples/page` compiles.
     - **(5) built** (branch `compile-bigint`): an `Int` in [-2^62, 2^62) is a machine integer as before; any other is a big integer, a pointer kept in the 64-bit values outside that range (above it for positive ones, below for negative ones), so indices, lengths and counts are unchanged, a big index is simply out of range, and comparing a small with a big integer needs no call. `+`, `-`, `*` stay inline when both operands and the result are small (their two top bits equal), else call the runtime; comparisons and `==` call it only for two big integers; `/` and `%` (Euclidean, as the interpreter's), printing, `float` (rounded to nearest, ties even; too large faults as the interpreter does), `floor` / `ceil` / `round` (exact), `parse_int`, literals and `Int` patterns handle big integers. The runtime's arithmetic (32-bit digits, schoolbook multiplication, bit-by-bit division) was checked against Python on 3,000 random cases. Big integers are counted and freed as any other object (decided with the user 2026-11-21, branch `free-bigints`): `counted` is true for `Int`, and each dup / drop of one is `@strata.int_dup` / `@strata.int_drop`, an inline test (`v + 2^62 < 0`) before calling the runtime, so a small one costs a compare and a branch (a loop of small arithmetic: about 3% slower). New `Int`s (arithmetic, `/`, `%`, big literals, `parse_int`, `floor`, generated ones) are temporaries; lists of `Int` copy their big elements as objects (`dup_slots` kind 2); a big integer's block comes from `strata_alloc`. A number literal is a constant (never counted).
     - **(6) built** (branch `compile-mock`): `mock T` is a trait value whose table's methods generate their results, as the interpreter's `mock_call`: `requires` checked (its fault is the interpreter's), then a value of the result's type generated until `ensures` holds (200 tries). A generator per written type and hints (`gen_fn`), as src/generate.rs: integers from the intervals the refinements allow (often their ends, or 0), lengths likewise, strings matching a refinement's regex (32 samples made when compiling, one picked at run time), unions by a member, variants by choice (the one with fewest fields past depth 3), trait types as mocks, and rejection until the refinement holds (300 tries). A mock's table is among its trait's tables, so dispatchers cover it. The random numbers are the runtime's (splitmix64 from 7).
     - **(7) built** (branch `jit-tests`): `strata test` compiles the tests with the JIT by default (`--interpret` to not; `--jit` says why when it can't, and they are then interpreted; `--emit-llvm` prints their IR). Each `test` block is a function (`strata.test.N`, no frame on the call stack); `strata_test` runs one with a trap armed, so a fault `longjmp`s back with its kind, message and stack instead of exiting, and what it prints is kept; each starts as the interpreter's (no stack, the generators' numbers from the start). The report is printed by Rust, the same for both. Found and fixed on the way (the `tasks` example had never been compiled, having no `main`): a parameter of a generic trait type (`mut queue: Queue[P, E]`) wasn't compiled per implementation as a plain trait's is (`is_trait` wanted no arguments), so a `mut` argument went in as the value where the callee wanted a trait value; and a constructor or type pattern on a trait value (`match worker.console { Log { lines } => … }`) compared the table with a tag, so it never matched (it now compares the pair's table with the implementation's, then matches the value). A crash now prints the program's call stack (`crash (signal 11) in:`).
     - **(8) built** (branch `jit-verify`): `strata verify --jit` fuzzes compiled. A driver per function (`compile_fuzz`: free functions, generic ones at `test_instance`, not methods nor what takes a function) generates the arguments with `mock`'s generators, records each as JSON, and calls the function; all drivers are one module, compiled once (`jit::fuzzers`; the engine lives as long as the process), and `fuzz_limited` runs a case through `strata_fuzz` (the test trap, seeded, a step budget) when the function has a driver, under a lock (the runtime's state is global; verify's jobs are threads). The verdicts are `fuzz_limited`'s: an unmet `requires` of the function itself is a discarded case, a generator that can't satisfy a type skips, a hole is incomplete, any other fault a counterexample. Compiled code counts steps only in this mode (each loop iteration and call; 50M without a budget, the cross-check's 200,000 with one). The SMT solver's counterexamples were then still replayed by the interpreter; since `verify-compiled` they are replayed compiled (below). Decided with the user (2026-11-21, branch `verify-jit-default`): compiled is the default (`--interpret` to not, `--jit` says why when it can't; the inputs are the runtime's random numbers, which is fine), and a runaway case stays `too many steps`: `verify` runs code on inputs it made up, so a budget is its own rule, unlike running a program.
     - **No limits on loops and recursion** (decided with the user 2026-11-21, branch `no-limits`): neither the interpreter nor compiled code stops a `while` / `loop` or a `range` at a count (the interpreter's 1,000,000) or a recursion at a depth (its 2000). Like Python's `RecursionError`, but where the memory ends rather than at a set depth: a recursion runs until the stack is nearly out, then faults `fault (Limit): stack overflow (recursion too deep)`. The interpreter checks the stack left at each call (`stacker::remaining_stack`, 256 KB kept; on its 1 GB thread about 95,000 calls deep); compiled code catches the guard page (SIGSEGV or SIGBUS, on the running thread's own stack, so the JIT on strata's 1 GB thread goes about 33M deep and a native binary's 8 MB main thread about 260,000), as `strata_fault`, so a test's trap catches it too. The `stack:` line shows a recursion as one entry: `main -> down (95846 times)` (four or more calls in a row; compiled code keeps runs, not frames). `verify` keeps its step budget (`too many steps`): it runs code it generated inputs for, not the program. Found on the way: the interpreter copied a variant's fields on every read (a chain of 8,000 links took 32 s to build; fields are now shared, `Rc`), and checked a declared variable's whole value on each assignment even when its type has no refinement anywhere (now skipped, `plain`). Lists are still copied when read. Open: a native binary could run its `main` on a larger stack, as the JIT does, so the depth doesn't depend on how the program is run.
     - **The call stack, cheaper** (branch `rc-frames`, 2026-11-23): `strata_enter` / `strata_leave` (the functions a fault's message shows, `down (95846 times)` for a recursion) were about 17% of the compiler's checker's time, mostly the calls and the thread-local lookups (on macOS, a call to find each). The frames, their counts and the depth are now one thread-local, found once per enter or leave; and a function whose code can't fault, in it or in what it calls (only count updates, allocation, drops, integer arithmetic and LLVM's intrinsics: `can_fault`), has no frame, since no message could show it. The checker: 8.5% fewer instructions. Considered and not done: rebuilding the stack from return addresses when a fault happens (free until then), because LLVM inlines the program's functions, whose frames would vanish from the messages without debug information read at the fault; and keeping the main thread's frames in globals while no task runs, because compiled code may run on several threads that aren't tasks (`verify` runs it next to the solver).
   - **Retiring the interpreter** (decided with the user 2026-11-21): LLVM is linked into strata statically, so compiled code can run anything the interpreter runs, compile-time code included; the interpreter goes, rather than being kept (or rewritten in Strata) for compile time. In order: (1) compile-time code without the interpreter (branches `comptime-data`, `comptime-jit`, built); (2) `verify` without it (literal refinements, the solver's counterexamples replayed, laws, fuzzing methods); (3) `run` / `test` without it, the JIT required; (4) `serve` (after the FFI), then `interp.rs` deleted. `Value` isn't kept (decided with the user 2026-11-22): outside the interpreter a value is a literal `Expr` (`P { x: 1 }`, `Some("a")`, `Set { 1, 2 }`), and shown as that code, never as JSON (JSON is only for the explicit JSON features: `std.json`, `derive Json`, `to_json`, `--json`). Built (branch `literal-values`): `native::read` reads what compiled code made into a literal; derive's compile-time values are literals (its maps keyed by the printed literal); the prover's counterexamples are literals; a fuzz driver hands each argument to strata as it is made (`strata_fuzz_keep`, read by its shape in jit.rs: the runtime's JSON argument buffer is gone); `verify` shows inputs as code (`input:   Square(0)`, `"EPVಀ", 3`, `printer::literal`: constructors by their own names); `strata run f ARGS` takes the arguments as code (`'"abc", 3'`, `'Circle { radius: 2 }'`), checked with the program before anything runs (a wrong value is a type error, a broken refinement a compile-time one), and prints the result as code; `fold_stack` / `show_stack` are in `names.rs` and `type_text` in `printer.rs`. `--trace` (branch `trace-literals`) is code too: each traced function instance has an id, codegen records its name and its arguments' and result's shapes (`llvm::Traced`), and the compiled code hands each value to strata (`strata_trace_arg`, `strata_trace_call`, `strata_trace_return`: hooks in jit.rs that read them by shape), which prints a line per call and return, indented by depth: `→ total([Line { sku: "tea", qty: 2, unit_price: 450 }])`, `← total: 2100` (`printer::literal`, one line however long). The runtime's trace functions and the generated `to_json` calls are gone. Values in compiled code's own messages are code too (branch `messages-as-code`, `Func::shown`: `debug`'s form, a native handle `<Db>`): `ensures … (it = Circle { radius: 1 })`, `Range { lo: 9, hi: 5 } does not satisfy …`, `with self = Graphemes { … }`; derived refinement checks' messages too. With it, no internal JSON is left: `to_string` of a record or variant with fields is its code (was its JSON), and the order on values (`compare`, sets, dicts, `sort`) is structural instead of by JSON text: a `distinct` value by what it is made of (`UserId(9) < UserId(10)`, was the reverse), variants in the order declared then field by field (`Red < Green < Blue`, was alphabetical by JSON), a union by member then value, a set or dict as its elements. Also: `Set {}` / `Dict {}` whose element type nothing fixes compile (a type without a value: helpers take nothing), and a method's frame is `std.io.Terminal.print` (was `std.io.Terminal.std.io.print`). Rules so a future compiler (self-hosted, or another backend) isn't tied to how it's done now: compile-time code is pure (no capabilities), deterministic and bounded by a step budget, so any engine gives the same result; its results must not depend on the machine compiling (sized types, when they come, computed at the target's widths); the code generator's output stays text IR, only the JIT needs LLVM's libraries (a self-hosted compiler would bind its C API, `llvm-c`, statically linked as now).
     - **Compile-time code, built**: most of it is data (all 161 evaluations of a six-field `derive CommandLine`: a description written out, a known name, a field of one), read directly (`Expander::data`). The rest (`comptime $Self.fields.map(f -> shout(f.name))`, a kind's refinement, a trait's `requires`) becomes a function `{ known values bound; _ := e; () }`, checked with the program as it is before its derived `impl`s (compile-time code can't use them), compiled (`Entry::Comptime`: its result type the checker's, steps counted), run on the JIT under the test trap with the budget (5M steps), and its value read back from memory by its shape (`llvm::Shape`, `llvm/native.rs`: what the FFI will use too). A fault or the budget's end is the `derive`'s error, showing the code as written. (The JIT is required since #113, so there is no interpreted fallback.) Found on the way: compiled `is` on a refined alias of a record (`x is Choice`) looked for the alias's own constructors and was always false.
     - **`verify` without the interpreter, built** (branch `verify-compiled`): literal refinement checks are probes (`let _: T = <literal>`, all in one module); the solver's counterexamples are replayed by a probe binding the values to the parameters' types (an invalid one discards it, as before) and calling the function; trait methods and laws are fuzzed through wrapper functions added to the program (`verify::wrappers`, `strata_verify_N_…`, their frames hidden from stacks; a law's wrapper ensures it holds), so `fuzz_limited` only ever runs compiled drivers, and what can't be compiled is skipped with the reason. Fuzzers are compiled from the elaborated program (checking it again needed one fix: a trait method named by an elaborated call, `Monoid::empty`). Probes and compile-time code are compiled unoptimized (O2 made `check` on `examples/page` 0.7 s; unoptimized, 0.04 s as before).
     - **`run` / `test` without the interpreter, built** (branch `run-compiled`): no fallback; what can't be compiled is an error naming it (`run`), or that test's failure (`test`, the others run). `run f ARGS` is compiled (a function binding the arguments, then calling `f`; its result read back, an argument breaking a refinement `invalid input: n: ..` from `f`'s own check), and `--trace` too (arguments and lines were JSON then; both are code since `literal-values` and `trace-literals`, above). Patterns built at run time match through Rust's `regex`, which strata gives the runtime as a function pointer (`strata_set_regex_engine`; a built program has none, and says so). `--interpret` stayed, only to compare with in the build tests, until `interp.rs` was deleted with `serve`. **No changes to `--interpret` from then on** (user, 2026-11-21) for anything compiled code (the JIT) already handles: a gap only the interpreter has is left as is, not fixed. Found on the way: compiling a resolved program resolved it again, which in a package qualifies an implementation's methods twice (`core.core.enqueue`, the method's code empty: invalid IR) — the interpreted fallback had hidden it; compile-time code, probes and fuzzers now compile the resolved program as it is (`Checked::compiled`, `elaborate_resolved`), only the compiler's prelude resolved. Fuzzers are compiled only for what `verify` checks, unoptimized (faster on every example than O2 or the interpreter).
     - **`serve` without the interpreter, built** (branch `serve-compiled`, after #145 and #155): the program is compiled once when the server starts, with a few functions of the server's written in Strata and checked with the program (`strata_serve_page`, `_message`, `_update`, and drops), and called through `llvm::compile_calls` / `jit::with_calls`: one engine for the server's life, each call under the test trap (a fault is a 500, the session unchanged). Each argument is `Given` (written by the host from a literal, checked all the way down, dropped), `Lent` (a session's model, a capability: borrowed) or `Moved` (handed back to be dropped). A session's model stays in compiled memory; a model that holds functions has no shape (`Shape::Opaque`), which is fine since the host never reads it. Decided on the way (Claude; the user can revisit): **messages are no longer JSON in the page**: `on_click`, `every` and `on_key` are numbered handlers like forms (`data-handle-click="3"`), and `std.web.dispatch` finds the message in the page the server served, so no JSON is read back against `Msg` and a page can only send its own messages (the test that sent forged `Set { n: 9 }` messages is gone with the possibility); **204 when the new view's HTML is the page the user has**, rather than comparing models (a model holding functions can't be compared). Found on the way: `x == None` didn't compile when the `Option` held functions; comparing with a constructor without fields is now a tag test (`x is None`). The viewer starts in ~10 s (check plus `-O2` of the viewer and the parts of std it uses) and answers a click in well under a second (the interpreter took seconds). (Then done, next item.)
     - **The interpreter deleted** (branch `delete-interp`, stacked on `serve-compiled`): `src/interp.rs` and `src/generate.rs` (its value generators; compiled fuzzing and mocks have their own) are gone, and so is `--interpret` (`run`, `test`); `cmd::capability` says whether the runtime gives a type, nothing more. `regex`, `regex-syntax` and `rand_regex` are dev-dependencies only (what `std.regex` is tested against), `stacker` is gone. One gap the interpreter covered, found by its last test: a trait method whose parameter is the trait itself (`fn bigger_shape(self, other: Shape)`) didn't compile; a method is compiled once, so such a parameter now takes a trait value, and a direct call (`Sq { .. }.bigger_shape(Ci { .. })`) passes one (made from the concrete value); a `mut` one in a method is still refused.
     - **Compile-time code and std at speed** (decided with the user 2026-11-21; std's own compile-time work, a regex library's for one, will be heavy): (1) **cache compile-time values** (built, branch `comptime-cache`): pure and deterministic, so a value is keyed by `closure_hash` of its function (the code and all it reaches) and the strata binary, kept as code in a cache shared by every program (`~/.cache/strata/comptime`, `STRATA_CACHE`; a two-type `derive Json` checks in 0.04 s warm, 0.11 s cold), read back as data (an entry that doesn't read is a miss); then batch a derive's evaluations into one module. (2) **stable constructor tags** (and box tags) (built, branch `stable-tags`): from the qualified name (40 bits of its SHA-256; a union box's from its member type's, above 2^40), not the program's numbering, so code compiled once reads values the same in every program (the FFI needs it too); a collision in one program is an error naming both; a match-heavy loop runs as fast as with dense tags. (3) **precompile std's non-generic code** (built, branch `std-precompiled`: the functions that aren't generic, take no trait value and don't read a package's setting (`setting("json.unknown_keys")` is written in per program, so they and their callers stay per program; 63 today), built the first time it is needed, about 9 s, with clang -O2 into `~/.cache/strata/std/<hash>/`; a program declares them instead of compiling them; under the JIT `dlopen` loads the shared library and the calls map to it, its own calls into the runtime resolving to strata's, exported; `strata build` links `std.o`; everything else the library compiles is internal to it. Not counted in steps (a runaway loop inside std isn't stopped by a budget); `--trace` compiles std with the program, so its calls are traced; `STRATA_NO_STD_LIBRARY` turns it off) at O2 into a library cached by strata's version and std's hash (generic code and copies made for a program's types stay per program; shared helpers `linkonce_odr`): under the JIT it is **dynamically linked** (loaded into the process, the program's calls mapped to it as the runtime's are), and an executable from `strata build` always links it **statically**.
   - **Next, roughly in order**: reuse when unique (step 3 of "Memory: reference counting with reuse"); `mut` arguments by reference (decided, "A `mut` argument is passed by reference"; today a slot of its own, copied in and out); skipping the runtime check of a contract `verify` proved. Done since: sized types (`I32`, `U8`, ...) in their own widths (types of their own, "Integers and floats"). Done since this list was written: reference counting, `main`'s capabilities and options, compiled `test` blocks.
   - **Both text IR and the LLVM libraries** (decided with the user 2026-11-20). The code generator writes IR as text, which a Strata compiler can do too (it won't have LLVM's libraries unless they are bound); the libraries are used to run that IR fast. **Step 2 (built, branch `llvm-jit`)**: `strata run --jit` (cargo feature `jit`, inkwell on LLVM 23) parses the IR, runs LLVM's O2 pipeline and runs it with MCJIT, calling the C runtime compiled into strata. The goal was a fast interpreter, as Python feels: the JIT is now the only way code runs (`run`, `test`, `verify`, `serve`, compile-time code).
   - **Step 3 (built, branch `compile-data`)**: the compiler works from the checker's types (`types::elaborate_typed`: each expression's type, by node), not its own guesses. Records, variants, tuples and `distinct` types (blocks of 8-byte slots behind a tag unique per constructor; a `distinct` value is its underlying one), generics by monomorphization, own methods, every pattern but lists, `!` / `?` / `except`, generated equality and printing per type. Refinements of a record's fields are checked when it is built; afterwards only fields of a type argument's type are (`Option[Positive]`).
   - **Step 4 (built, branch `compile-lists`)**: lists (a header of length, shared buffer and offset: `drop` and `[x, ..rest]` share the buffer, and `push` writes in place past the end when no other list has used that slot, so `out = push(out, x)` is amortized O(1) while values never change), list patterns, `for` over lists, closures (a block: the function's address, then the captured values, by value), named functions as values. The list built-ins are Strata (`src/llvm/prelude.strata`, compiled with the program as the module `strata.compiled`) over a few native ones (`len`, `get`, `push`, `take`, `drop`, `range`, `+`); `sort` is a stable merge sort given the interpreter's order, generated per type. `examples/math.strata` compiles whole; list-heavy code runs 15-20x faster than interpreted.
   - **Step 5 (built, branch `compile-traits`)**: traits, by static dispatch: every receiver's type is known once generics are monomorphized, so a call goes straight to one compiled instance per (trait, method, receiver type), with `Self`, the impl's and the trait's parameters filled in; default methods are compiled per type; a variant with an implementation of its own is chosen by its tag; static methods take their type from the call (the hidden type parameter `elaborate` adds carries nothing). `examples/traits.strata` compiles. Values whose type is a trait (`db: Db`) came in step 6b.
   - **Step 6 (built, branch `compile-capabilities`)**: `mut` parameters by reference: the callee gets the caller's slot (a field passed `mut` gets a slot of its own, written back after the call), then the variable's declared type is checked, as the interpreter's write-back does. Capabilities: `main`'s `Console`, `Files` and `Environment` are made natively (`Terminal`, `Disk` from `.`, `Process`), and std's `host_*` operations are in the C runtime with the interpreter's results and error messages (Rust's `io::Error` text). A trait-typed parameter is compiled per implementation passed (the language's own meaning of `db: Db`), so its calls still dispatch statically. Options from the command line came with `derive CommandLine` (`compiled-options`), and a trait as a field's or a list element's type in step 6b.
   - **Step 6b (built, branch `compile-dyn`)**: values of a trait type held anywhere but a parameter (a field `console: Console`, `List[Shape]`, a `let` with the trait as its type): a pair of the implementation's table (show as JSON, show, then each method of the trait and its supertraits, as small wrappers over the compiled instance) and the value. A concrete value is wrapped where it flows into such a place (a field, a list element, `push`, a `let`, an assignment, a branch, a return). A `mut self` call copies the pair, lets the method change the copy and stores it back, so values stay values. Methods that mention `Self` beyond `self`, have type parameters of their own, or a `mut` parameter besides `self`, and `==` on such values, came in parity steps (1) and (3). Also: the write-back check after a `mut` call is left out when the checker proved the parameter's type fits the variable's (`types::refinement_within`).
   - **Step 7 (built, branch `compile-collections`)**: the string built-ins, dictionaries and sets, `xs[i]` / slices, format specs. A `Set` is a list kept sorted in the interpreter's order (`compare`, the generated `cmp`) without duplicates, a `Dict` a list of `(key, value)` pairs sorted by key (a design choice to review: lookups are binary searches, but `insert` and `remove` copy the list, O(n), where a balanced tree or a hash table would be O(log n) / O(1); the interpreter copies too). So equal collections are equal lists (`==` is the list's), `for` and `items` are the list's, and the built-ins are Strata over lists in the prelude (`set_from`, `dict_insert`, `set_union`, ... given the element type's order as a closure). The order then followed the interpreter's (a `distinct` value by its JSON, so `UserId(10)` before `UserId(9)`); since `messages-as-code` it is structural (a `distinct` value by what it is made of, variants in the order declared), and floats by `total_cmp` (-0.0 before 0.0). Strings: native in the C runtime (by code point; `lines` is Strata over `split`); what needs Unicode's tables (`to_upper` / `to_lower`, `is_alpha`) gets tables the compiler makes from Rust's own (`char::to_uppercase`, `is_alphabetic`), emitted only when used, so both agree. Known differences from the interpreter, then: `to_lower`'s final sigma (fixed since: `ΑΣ'` is `ας'`); `parse_int` past 64 bits (fixed with big integers); `ord`'s message escapes only ASCII control characters as Rust's `{:?}` would; `-0.0` and `0.0` are equal to `==` but not to the order, and a set's `insert` / `contains` / a dictionary's lookups search by the order. Regexes (`matches`) came in parity step (4), then `std.regex`; at the time: the interpreter uses Rust's `regex` crate, and matching it exactly natively means either linking a regex engine with the same syntax and semantics into the runtime or compiling patterns (usually literals in refinements, such as `Sku`'s) to code with the compiler: compile constant patterns to DFAs in Rust (the `regex-automata` crate can build a dense DFA) and emit the tables, which keeps the runtime small; to decide.
   - **To fix after step 7** (user, 2026-11-21):
     - **`Dict` as a plain hash map** (user, 2026-11-23, replacing the earlier "print in sorted order"): no order at all, neither when iterating (`for`, `keys`, `values`, `items`) nor when printing: entries come out in the table's order, which looks random and may change whenever the hash function or the table's layout does ("nothing should assume that a dict has a constant order"). Kept: hashing is deterministic (no seed per run: compile-time code gives the same result everywhere, a failing test fails again), and equality is by content (`{a: 1, b: 2} == {b: 2, a: 1}`); where an ordering of dicts is needed (a set of dicts, `sort`), it compares their contents sorted, internally. The Strata evaluator (`compiler/eval`) implements the same hash and table, so it prints the same order as compiled code. Why now: a dict or set lookup was a binary search through a closure call per step, 15% of the Strata compiler's check of the compiler package. Built (branch `speed-dict-hash`): see "Dictionaries and sets as hash tables" in the speed section.
     - ~~**`Set` as a balanced tree**~~ (or another ordered structure), so `insert` and `remove` stop copying: decided otherwise (2026-11-24, branch `speed-dict-hash`): a `Set` is a hash table as a `Dict` is, with no order (see the speed section).
     - ~~The order on `distinct` values~~ (fixed: structural since `messages-as-code`).
     - ~~The final-sigma rule in `to_lower`~~ (fixed: `ΑΣ'` lowercases to `ας'`, as Rust's).
     - ~~First: iterators in std~~ (built: see "Iterators and `FiniteIterator`"). A way to walk a value once (a string's characters, a list, a dict's entries, a file's lines), lazily, in Strata. It comes before moving the string functions to Strata, and would also serve lazy `map` / `filter` chains and walking collections without copying them. The language docs already say iterators will be designed as their own feature.
     - **String functions in Strata, not C** (partly built: `contains`, `starts_with`, `ends_with`, `index_of`, `chars`, `reverse`, `split`, `replace`, `repeat` are the prelude's, over byte offsets; still C: `trim`, case mappings, the `is_` tests, `parse_int` / `parse_float`).
     - The rest of step 7's known differences: `-0.0` and `0.0` as different dict/set keys.
   - **Open**: the runtime is C for now, compiled with the program (no Rust toolchain needed to build one); the decision says the native core is Rust, so revisit when it grows (GC, I/O).
2. **A standard library** with batteries included. The user cited the V language (vlang) as inspiration (confirmed). Priorities:
   - basic I/O, files and the OS / processes, for CLIs;
   - networking (HTTP client and server);
   - web apps and views: HTML / CSS generation and serving, a first-class target because agents write web UIs for people;
   - agentic systems built in, e.g. LLM calls and tools as standard-library capabilities.
   These are capabilities (traits) implemented by the runtime, consistent with the decision that there are no effects; their contracts are `assumed`.
3. **Interop** with Python, TypeScript and Rust using **JSON as the boundary**: generate types and codecs (Pydantic models, zod / TS types, serde structs) from Strata types and contracts.
4. **Transpilation** to Python / TypeScript later. Rust transpilation is harder without a borrow checker (it would need a GC or reference counting in the generated code), so it has lower priority than LLVM.

### Foreign functions: `extern LANG` (decided with the user 2026-11-21; `extern c` built, steps 1-3 below; `std` SQLite and other languages not built)
One declaration form for every foreign language, `extern LANG fn ...`, with two mechanisms behind it:
- **`extern c`**: an in-process call through the C ABI. First, because the rest builds on it (SQLite, then the ORM).
- **`extern python` / `extern ts` / `extern rust`**: calls whose values cross as JSON, through the derived `Json` trait (PR #100, branch `json-trait`), not today's tagged debug form. That trait's mapping is what those ecosystems already produce: `Option` as the value or `null` (Pydantic `Optional`, TS `T | null`, serde `Option`), a sum's variants tagged with a `"type"` key (TS discriminated unions, Pydantic `Field(discriminator="type")`, serde `#[serde(tag = "type")]`), records as plain objects. So types and codecs on the other side can be generated (Pydantic models, TS types or zod, serde structs) and match exactly. A type crosses only if it is `Json`, as only FFI-safe types cross `extern c`: the derive is the check. Results are decoded against the declared type, refinements included, so a foreign function that breaks its contract is a fault at the boundary (a NaN from Python is a decode error, as `Float` is never NaN). `extern rust` can be a generated serde shim exported with the C ABI, i.e. `extern c` underneath. Python and TS run first as a subprocess speaking JSON lines (isolated, works without the JIT); embedding libpython / a JS engine later if speed asks for it. Open: large data (a NumPy array copied as JSON is slow): opaque handles to foreign objects (`extern python type Model`), or a binary path later; integers beyond 2^53 for TS (see the `Json` entry).

`extern c`:
- **Declarations, one at a time**: `extern c fn sqlite3_open(path: String, mut db: Sqlite3) -> I32`, `extern c type Sqlite3` (an opaque handle, only passed back). Each is its own definition (hashed, rendered, diffed like any other). Libraries are named in `strata.toml` (`[native] libs = ["sqlite3"]`, perhaps C files of the package to compile in as shims).
- **Types that cross**: the sized integers (`I8` … `U64`, as C's `int8_t` … `uint64_t`; plain `Int` doesn't, its size isn't fixed), `Float` (`double`), `Bool`, `String` (a NUL-terminated copy each way; a NUL inside is a fault), `()` (`void`), `extern c type` handles. A `mut` parameter is an out-pointer (`mut db: Sqlite3` is a `sqlite3**`), which is what `mut` already means: copy in, copy out. Not at first: structs by value, callbacks, buffers.
- **Contracts are assumed**: `requires` is checked before the call, the result's type and `ensures` after it (at run time: foreign code can lie). Never proven, never fuzzed: a function that reaches an extern is reported like one that reaches `host_*` ("it talks to the real system").
- **Capabilities (option b, user's choice)**: externs are called only from inside their own module, a module of extern declarations; that module turns them into a capability (a trait and its real implementation, as `std.fs` does with `Files` over `host_*`), and everything else works through the capability, so a signature still shows what code can touch. How a program gets the real implementation: see "Capabilities given to `main`" (revised with the user 2026-11-21).
- **Capabilities given to `main`** (decided with the user 2026-11-21; step 1 built, branch `capabilities`: `capability trait`, `capability Name { ... }` (expanded by `resolve` into a type with no constructor and an `impl` whose functions get an implicit `self`), `main` and tests asking for them (compiled: a null pointer, never counted; interpreted: an empty record), `verify` reporting their code and what takes one as assumed, the viewer's colour. Step 2 built, branch `capabilities-std`: std's `Console`, `Files`, `Environment` are capability traits and `Terminal`, `Disk`, `Process` capabilities; `main` (and `handle`, `init`, `update`) names `Disk` / `Process` and gets `Console` (the terminal) from the environment; the Rust table and `HOST_TYPES` are gone; a trait's method is found on a value of a type implementing it without the trait imported (`disk.read(path)` with only `Disk` imported).
