Step 3 built, branch `capabilities-env`: `[run] mode = true` in strata.toml lets `main` and tests ask for `RunMode` (std.process: `Running`, `Testing`, `Tracing`; hidden otherwise, with an error saying how to allow it); a test may ask for `Console` (the terminal, its output the test's own: the runtime's `host_write` keeps it while a test runs, as `print`'s); `--trace` already records the console's calls with their text and what `read_line` returned, so no separate recorder was added. Replaces the "private constructor" idea above.
  - **`capability trait`**: an interface to the outside world (`Console`, `Files`, `Database`). Anything may implement one (fakes for tests do); the marker says what it is for.
  - **A capability** (proposed syntax `pub capability Sqlite { pub fn open(path: String) -> Result[SqliteDb, SqlError] { ... } }`): a named set of functions that reach the outside world, with no data. Only the runtime makes one, only for an entry point; no constructor, no JSON, no `mock`. Its functions are called on the value (`sqlite.open(path)`), never on the name, so holding the value is the permission. It may implement capability traits (`pub capability Disk {}` with `impl Files for Disk`), which covers std's `Terminal`, `Disk`, `Process` and removes the table hard-coded in Rust (`capability()` in src/cmd/run.rs, the compiler's entry).
  - **`main` names concrete capabilities** (`fn main(opts: Options, sqlite: Sqlite, postgres: Postgres)`) and picks among them in ordinary code (an option chooses SQLite or Postgres), handing the rest of the program a capability trait value (`add_todo(mut db: Database, ...)`). The program's signature lists everything it can touch, used or not.
  - **Only `Console` is chosen by the environment**: `fn main(mut console: Console)` gets the terminal under `strata run`, a fixture in tests, a recorder under `--trace`. The run mode is an implicit parameter of `main`, not visible by default; a flag in `strata.toml` (name to settle) lets `main` ask for it to see or override the choice. No `default` implementations otherwise. `Files` and `Environment` are named concretely by `main` (breaking `examples/wc`, `tasks`, ...).
  - **Syntax** (decided with the user 2026-11-21): capabilities are ordinary parameters (`sqlite: Sqlite`), in `main` as anywhere; no effect lists and no separate `with` clause for entry points. The declaration has its own keyword (`capability Sqlite { ... }`), and the viewer colours capability names (and capability traits) differently from types, wherever they appear.
  - **Libraries**: a capability is only *made* at an entry point; a library with no `main` defines capabilities and code that takes them as parameters (`pub fn migrate(sqlite: Sqlite, ...)`), and the program passes its own on. A library's `[native]` C is linked into every program that depends on it (once `[deps]` are fetched). Tests may ask for real capabilities like `main` (decided with the user 2026-11-21): `test "opens a file" (sqlite: Sqlite) { ... }`, for a library's integration tests; a test that doesn't ask uses fakes through capability traits.
- **Only compiled code calls them** (user's choice: no libffi). `strata build` declares them in the IR and links the libraries; the JIT loads the libraries and resolves the symbols. (With the interpreter gone, compiled code is all there is.)
- **In the tree**: `DeclKind::Extern` added as the last tag, so existing files stay readable (no re-encoding).
- **Later**: the `host_*` layer as `extern c` declarations over `runtime.c`, instead of hand-written cases per builtin (`stdlib.rs`, `llvm.rs`, `jit.rs`).
- **Order**: (1) syntax, tree, binary format, checks (FFI-safe types, externs not `pub`, callers in the module only); **built** (branch `ffi`): `DeclKind::Extern`, the checks, and `verify` reporting anything that reaches a native module as assumed (step 3 done early, since generating a handle crashed the fuzzer); (2) compiled calls, `build` and the JIT, libraries from `strata.toml`: **built** (branch `ffi-calls`): `[native] sources / libs`, conversions in `llvm.rs` (`foreign_call`), the JIT compiling the sources into a library with clang and loading it, `Option` of a handle for NULL (needed for out-pointers: a `mut` argument must start as something), `examples/fuzzy`; (3) `verify` (built with (1)); (4) `std` SQLite behind a `Database` capability; (5) `extern python` over JSON, then TS, then Rust.

### ORM built into the language (decided)
- Records map directly to tables, building on the automatic JSON serialisation and on types being structural and validated. Refinements become column checks (`CHECK` constraints) and are validated on load. `distinct` ids map to keys.
- The first backend is SQLite. The database is a capability (a trait), so tests can use an in-memory implementation or `mock`.
- Queries should be typed (checked against the record's fields), not strings.

### `std.json`: a derived `Json` trait (user, 2026-11-21; built, branches `json-trait` and `json-more`)
The direction is in "JSON as a boundary format" (open questions). Built: `std/json.strata`, in Strata: `JsonValue`, `parse` / `stringify`, and `derivable(Record | Sum) trait Json` with `to_json`, `from_json`, `from_json_with(j, Ignore | Reject)`, `from_json_at(j, path, keys)`; records as objects with `"type"` (optional when read), `Option` as the value or `null`/left out, defaults for missing fields (a variant's too), sums tagged by `"type"` (one positional field as `"value"`), choices as strings, `List`, tuples (2 or 3 values), `Dict[String, V]`, `distinct` types as their value, unions whose members' JSON can't overlap (read: the first member the JSON fits), every refinement checked with a path in the message, integers within 64 bits both ways (`Int64`; writing a larger one is a fault). Unknown keys (user, 2026-11-21): ignored by default, refused with `[json] unknown_keys = "error"` in `strata.toml` (read at compile time through `std.typing.setting`), or per call with `from_json_with`. What has no JSON form is an error at the `derive`, including a field whose type doesn't derive `Json`. The derive machinery gained for it: kinds that are unions (`Record | Sum`), `match comptime $Self { Record => .., Choice => .., Sum => .. }`, `Union` and `Distinct[x]` type patterns, `t(v)` for a `distinct` `t`, a match comprehension over a union's members (an arm per member type), `if comptime`, `f.of(x)`, methods called on types known while compiling, `setting(key)`; fixes: the resolver resolves inside `comptime e` and a match comprehension's list, copies of `comptime` functions are named after the derived type, and the prover no longer takes every recursive type as refined (`smt::refined_in`). Not yet:
  - built (branch `runtime-types`): types are passed whole at runtime (`"List[Int]"`, built with the `$type` builtin from the caller's own type arguments where needed), so a method without `self` called on a type binds its impl's type parameters (`T.zero()` in `impl[T: Zero] Zero for List[T]`); `std.json` implements `Json` for `Int`, `Float`, `Bool`, `String`, `List[T]`, `Option[T]` and `Dict[String, V]` (`to_json`, `from_json` and `from_json_with` are default methods, `setting(..)` a compile-time constant everywhere). Since then (branch `json-generic`): methods with `self` call `T.m()` too: a call on a value of a generic type whose impl needs its type parameters when it runs (`types::heads_needing_types`) is given the receiver's static type whole (`$self_type(t, call)`, around the `mut` of a call that changes its receiver; transparent to the compiler and the prover); a trait-typed value doesn't carry it. `derive Json for Pair` derives `impl[T: Json] Json for Pair[T]` (a `Param` type pattern, copies generic in what they mention), a field of type `Pair[Int]` decodes (a static call's runtime type is made once the declaration is inferred), and the generic `Option` impl refuses `Some(None)` from `to_json`. Since then (branch `refined-types`): a refined alias without type parameters stays by name in the checker's types (`Ty::Con("Positive")`), expanded to its base by `resolve` (so every check is unchanged) and kept by `resolve_named` / unification for type variables: type arguments carry it when the code runs (`"List[Port]"`); the compiler gives an alias its base type's methods, and the compiler makes an instance per alias (`repr` maps it to its base). `T.check(x)` on any type (`$check`; the compiler's through `strata.compiled.check_result`), and std.json's generic impls check each element. Since then (branch `refined-more`): refined aliases with type parameters are kept too (`Many[Int]`, expanded with its arguments), and a refinement written as a type argument gets an alias of its own (`$where_0`, made in `desugar::inline_refinements`; messages and derive descriptions show it as written). The generic impls check their own type's refinement at the end of `from_json_at` (`Self.check`, where `Self` in an impl is the type it was called on). `verify` now checks and fuzzes a generic impl's methods at a whole type (branch `fuzz-whole-types`): each impl type parameter is a type meeting its bounds (`verify::impl_instance`: a built-in type first, then one of the program's), and a hidden type argument is generated whole (`List[String]`, not `List`);
  - integers (decided with the user 2026-11-21; built, branch `json-safe`): within ±(2^53 - 1) both ways (`SafeInt`), refused rather than written as strings, so the JSON's shape never depends on the value. Refused with an error, not a fault: `to_json` returns a `Result` (`to_json_at(path)` underneath), so nothing about integers needs proving by default, as JSON mostly crosses a network or an FFI where the data is assumed anyway. `[json] integers = "proven"` is the strict derivation: each integer field must be shown to be a `SafeInt`, and `verify` (which now checks the copies a derive makes, `std.json.encode_as.for.A.2`) fails on one that may not be;
  - unions now also refuse a choice (or a sum with variants without data) next to `String`, and two members with a `"type"` in common; a refined member (`Positive | String`) is matched by its base type;
  - the package setting is the first package a command loads (a process-wide value), which is the command's own package;
  - `Debug` / `Display` (built, branch `runtime-types`, as decided in the open question): prelude traits every type has, `print` / f-strings / `to_string` use `Display`, parts use `Debug`, an impl replaces either (compiled code and trait values' tables). `to_json` stays JSON; `strata run`'s arguments and results became code (`literal-values`).
  - found on the way (fixed, branch `json-generic`): a root-module function named like a builtin (`fn get(..)`) took the standard library's own calls of the builtin, since root names are bare. Resolved for checking and running, it is `main$get` (`resolve::root_fn`): the root module's uses mean it, every other module's `get` is the builtin; stored files keep `get`, and messages show `get`.

### Mocks: fakes for tests (decided with the user 2026-11-21; step 1 built, PR #153: `mock Name = { ... }` (a `mock` flag on `TypeDecl`, files re-encoded), test-only (the resolver), left-out methods generated (`resolve::mock_methods`: each answered by a generated `mock` of the trait), `verify` assumes them, hidden in `view` / the viewer. Not yet: recording calls, inline `mock Db { find(id) => None }`, std's other fakes as mocks (std.time's `FakeClock` is one, #258))
Today `mock T` (an expression) is an implementation of trait `T` with no code: each call checks the method's `requires` and returns a fresh random value within its result type and `ensures`. `verify` passes one for every trait-typed parameter it fuzzes, which is where it pays off. But a mock has no memory (a read after a write returns anything; nothing records what was printed), so tests of one scenario still need hand-written fakes: std's `MemFiles`, `recorder` / `printed`, `fake_environment`; `Log` in examples/tasks, `OneOrder` in examples/orders. Writing a whole fake first is the annoyance this removes.

```rust
/// An in-memory disk, for tests.
pub mock MemFiles = { files: Dict[String, String] = Dict {} }

impl Files for MemFiles {
  fn read(self, path: String) -> Result[String, FsError] { ... }
  fn write(mut self, path: String, text: String) -> Result[(), FsError] { ... }
  // exists, list, make_dir, remove: left out, generated
}

test "a refund for a missing order" {
  db := mock Db { find(id) => None }   // one-off: no declaration, the rest generated
  ...
}
```

- **`mock` declares a type** exactly as `type` does (form A, the user's choice): data separate from implementation, as in Rust; its trait implementations are ordinary `impl` blocks. Because it is a mock: it is for tests only (used in `test` blocks and other mocks; anywhere else is an error, so a fake never reaches a real run), its `impl`s may leave methods out (generated as `mock T` does), every call to it is recorded so a test can assert on them (`calls(m)`; a printed-lines helper for `Console`), and the viewer and `strata view` hide it unless asked (`--mocks`; selecting a test shows the mocks it uses). `render` and the editor show it, as stored. `pub mock` lets other packages' tests use it. A mock with no data: `pub mock FixedClock = {}`.
- **Inline**: `mock Db { find(id) => None }` as an expression, the listed methods answering as written, the rest generated; `mock Db` alone stays as it is.
- **`verify`** keeps fuzzing with generated mocks, never named ones (they cover every valid behaviour; a named one covers one).
- **Capabilities**: a `capability` (`Sqlite`) can't be mocked, only made by the runtime; its capability traits (`Database`) can (`mock MemDb`).
- **Replaces** std's `MemFiles`, `recorder` / `printed`, `fake_environment` (as `pub mock`s), and the examples' fakes.
- **Rejected** (user): fields and methods in one block, for mocks or for types (a type's own methods inside the type, sum types' variants in the block); `mock MemFiles for Files = {...}` (the trait written twice); `mock impl` on an ordinary type (nothing to hide or restrict).
- **In the tree**: a type declaration with a `mock` flag, or a new definition kind added last (no re-encoding); to settle when building.

### Derived traits, `comptime` and special methods (decided with the user 2026-11-21; steps 1-3 built; special methods not built)
Command-line options need code made from a record's fields; so will JSON codecs for compiled programs and the ORM. Instead of a hidden Rust generator (`src/cli.rs` today) or general metaprogramming over syntax trees, a trait can say how each of its methods is derived, as ordinary Strata code specialized per type at compile time (as Zig's `comptime` does). A derive returns a real function, never a syntax tree.

```rust
use std.typing.{Record, Field}

/// A record whose fields are a program's command-line options.
pub derivable(t: Record) trait CommandLine
  requires all(t.fields, f -> is_option_type(f.type)) {

  derived fn from_args(args: List[String]) -> Result[Self, CliError] {
    given := parse(comptime t.fields.map(spec), args)!
    t.try_make(f -> read(f, given))
  }

  derived fn help(program: String) -> String {
    help_text(program, comptime t.doc, comptime t.fields.map(spec))
  }
}

fn read(comptime f: Field, given: Given) -> Result[f.type, CliError] {
  match comptime f.type {
    Bool => Ok(given.has(f.name)),
    Int => parse_number(given.text(f.name)),
    ...
  }
}
```

- **`derivable(t: Kind)`**: parentheses, not brackets: `t` is an argument, a compile-time value describing the type derived for (`Self` is that type, `t` describes it). Its type is a kind from `std.typing` (`Record`, `Sum`, `Choice`: a sum whose variants carry nothing); deriving for another kind of type fails at the `derive` line. `requires` states finer conditions as a contract checked at compile time, naming the offending field.
- **`derived fn`**: a method with an ordinary body, specialized for each type it is derived for. A method without `derived` is a default method, or one the user must write.
- **Using it**: `derive CommandLine for Options`, or `derive CommandLine for Options { fn help(program: String) -> String { ... } }` to write some methods by hand (as overriding a default method). After specialization it is exactly an `impl` with those bodies: no inheritance, no relationship at run time. `impl CommandLine for Options { ... }` with every method written also works: it is an ordinary trait.
- **`std.typing`**: ordinary types describing types, imported when writing a derivable trait (like Python's `typing`): `Record` (`name`, `doc`, `fields`), `Sum` / `Choice` (`variants`), `Field` (`name`, `type`, `doc`, `default`), `Variant`. `t.make(f -> ...)` builds a `Self` from a function giving each field's value (applied once per field, at compile time; each result must have that field's type); `t.try_make(f -> ...)` does it with a `Result`, stopping at the first `Err`. The syntax tree is only read through these descriptions. Self-hosting (user, 2026-11-21): `std.typing` should be the type-declaration part of the eventual syntax-tree module (`std.ast`) the compiler in Strata needs, not a separate model, so the self-hosted frontend reuses `Record`, `Sum`, `Field`, `Variant` and a derive reads the same structures the compiler does.
- **`comptime`**: on expressions (`comptime t.fields.map(spec)`: evaluated at build time, the result put in the program), functions, and parameters (`comptime f: Field`: the function is compiled once per value, so `f.type` is a real type in each copy). `match comptime f.type { ... }` keeps only the matching branch. Compile-time code can't take capabilities (so it is deterministic, which the type system already shows) and must terminate (proven by `verify`, or within a step budget, else the build fails saying so). Not for generics: type parameters already do that.
- **Checking**: a derived body depends on the type, so it is checked when it is specialized, at each `derive ... for T` (as Zig and C++ templates are); errors point at that line and the field. The kind and `requires` catch most mistakes first. Less powerful than generating trees (a derive can't change a signature or add top-level definitions), which keeps derives tame.
- **Special methods, marked `@`**: value-level specials are methods with a reserved `@` name, declared in std traits and implemented like any trait method: `trait Add[Rhs, Out] { fn @add(self, other: Rhs) -> Out  law associative(...) }`, then `a + b` on such a type. Also `@mul`, `@neg`, `@index`, `@call`, `@iter`, ... For a numpy-like library: generic numeric code (`T: Add[T, T] + Mul[T, T]`) and laws `verify` checks. In the tree a special method is a function with a kind (an enum), not a magic name: the checker enforces each kind's shape, `a + b` and a call of `@add` are the same node, and the text is only one rendering of it.
- **Order of work**: (1) the syntax (parser, printer, binary format) and the checks; (2) the specialization: `std.typing`, `comptime` (run while checking: first by the interpreter, now compiled on the JIT), producing ordinary `impl`s that everything after it (checker, compiler, prover) sees unchanged; (3) `CommandLine` written this way in `std/cli.strata`, `main` calling `from_args`, and `src/cli.rs` removed (its messages and `--help` text kept exactly; tests compare the old and new on the same arguments). Then options compile. Special methods and operator overloading after.
- **Revised (decided with the user 2026-11-21; built, branch `comptime-for`)**, replacing what above differs:
  - `derivable(Record) trait X requires ...`: no argument; `$X` is a type's description (`$Self`, `$f.type`: `f.type` is a type, `$f.type` its description). `std.typing` only defines the description types (`Record`, `Sum`, `Choice`, `Field`, `Variant`, `Type`), to name them in signatures and kinds.
  - Descriptions are read with ordinary list functions. Building or taking apart a value needs its variant known at compile time (binding-time analysis: literals, `$X`, the variable of a `comptime for` or of a match comprehension, `comptime` parameters and `comptime e` are known, and what is made only of them). `make` is not a real method: on a description not known at compile time it is an error saying to use `comptime for`. Rejected: dispatching at run time on a description (a `Variant[T]` with an index), as implicit. Possible later: `match found { v => Some(v.make()) for v in $Self.variants, _ => None }`.
  - `comptime for v in $Self.variants { ... }`: the loop runs while compiling, its body written out once per element (Zig's `inline for`).
  - Match comprehensions, compile time only, arm first as list comprehensions: `match self { v => kebab(v.name) for v in $Self.variants }`; the point is the exhaustiveness check (at run time a loop does it). A general language feature, not only for derives.
  - `$Self.make(f -> e)` / `try_make` for records; `v.make()` (no fields) or `v.make(f -> e)` for a variant. `select` is gone. `T.check(x)` was added since (#140: a refinement checked into a `Result`).
- **Types as compile-time values (decided with the user 2026-11-21; replaces the description types above; built, PR #154)**. The old `std.typing` had two models of a type that didn't meet: descriptions of declarations (`Record`, `Sum`, and `Variant`, a copy of `Record`; a variant without fields was a `Variant` with no fields) and `Type`, a type as written (`Named`, `Refined`, `Function`, `Nothing`, `Union`), linked by `$`. Working back from what `std.cli` and `std.json` read, nothing needs a definition written out as data: every read starts at a type and goes one step (`.fields`, `f.type`, `.variants`), and code tells shapes apart with type patterns (`Option[x] =>`, `Choice =>`), not by matching the data. So:
  - **One `Type`**, a type known while compiling (as Zig's `type`), opaque: the compiler answers its properties when asked, so a recursive type (`Tree`) is no problem. No `$`: `f.type.fields`. A declaration only gives a type a name and a doc; the line is named vs. unnamed, not declaration vs. use (`Option[String]` is named `Option` with `args`; `Int where it > 0`, `A | B`, `(Int) -> Bool` have no name).
  - **Every type has** `name: Option[String]`, `doc: List[String]`, `args: List[Type]`, `written: String` (as written, for messages), `condition: Option[String]` (a refinement's, as written: text until `std.ast` has an `Expr`, then that; `check` evaluates it), and `t.check(x)`. Refinements and `distinct` are not a shape: a refined record still has fields.
  - **Its shape**, what code switches on (with the type patterns `Record`, `Tuple`, `Sum`, `Choice`, `Union`, `Atom`, `Int`, `Option[x]`, `Distinct[y]`, `Param`, ...): a record has `fields: List[Field]`; a tuple (`(Int, String)`, or a variant `Circle(Float, Float)`) has `elements: List[Type]` (user: a positional element isn't a field; not `items`, a dictionary function); a sum has `variants: List[Type]` (each a record, tuple or atom: variants are types); a union has `members: List[Type]`; a function type has `params` and `result`; a type with one value is `Unit` (`()`, was `Nothing`) or an atom (a variant without fields, a named unit, only defined in a sum); a built-in (`Int`, `String`, `List[x]`, ...) is told apart by name and `args`; a type parameter (`T`) is `Param`. (Later, with "Sums and unions are one thing": no `Sum` kind and no `variants`; a sum is a `Union` with `members`.)
  - **`Field`** stays a record: `name`, `doc`, `type: Type`, `default: Option[String]`, with `f.default_value` and `f.of(x)`; its `written` is `f.type.written`.
  - **Building**: `t.make(f -> e)` / `try_make` for a record or a variant with fields; `t.make((i, e) -> x)` for a tuple (index and element type); `v.make()` for an atom. A tuple's element is read with `x.0`.
  - Gone: `$`, `Definition` vs `Type`, `Variant`, `Named` / `Refined` / `Function` / `Nothing` as data, `Field.written`. Kinds in `derivable(Record | Sum)` are the shape patterns. Variants can't carry a `///` doc yet (the tree has none), so a variant's `doc` is empty.
  - **Built** (branch `typing-defs`): `Type` is a record (`name`, `doc`, `kind: Kind`, `args`, `written`, `condition`, `fields`, `elements`, `variants`, `members`, `params`, `result`), the lists a kind doesn't have empty; `Kind` is `Record | Tuple | Sum | Union | Function | Atom | Unit | Builtin | Param`, and `Choice` is `Type where it.kind is Sum && all(it.variants, v -> v.kind is Atom)`. `Self` is the type derived for, as a value (`Self.fields`, `match comptime Self`); `x.0` in a match comprehension's arm reads a tuple-shaped variant's element. Opaque in effect, not in the language: a named type's lists are written out except inside itself (a recursive type) or four named types deep, where they are empty; reading them from a type known while compiling (`f.type.fields`, `Self.variants`) always writes them out in full, so only compiled compile-time code walking deeper than that sees the empty lists. A refined type is named as its base (`Int where it > 0` is `Int`, with its `condition`); an alias or `distinct` type has its own name and the shape of what it stands for. `$` (`Expr::Describe`) is gone from the tree. Also fixed: the printer wrote `comptime (a ?? b)` as `comptime a ?? b`. Not yet: reading the `i`th element when `i` is only known while compiling (`x.0` must be written); `name` being an `Option` makes code write `v.name ?? ""` for variants, which always have one. (Later, with "Sums and unions are one thing": no `Sum` kind and no `variants`; a sum is a `Union` with `members`.)
- **Step 3 (built, branch `cli-derive`)**: `CommandLine` is a derivable trait written in Strata (`std/cli.strata`): `from_args(program, args)` gives the options, `ShowHelp` or `BadArgs`; `strata run` calls it, and `src/cli.rs` only says which types are options. Its messages and `--help` are the old `cli.rs`'s (compared on `examples/hello` and `examples/wc`). New for it: `$T.check(x)`, `comptime t: Type` parameters, `f.default_value`, `Option[x]` type patterns, kind patterns (`Choice`, `Sum`, `Record`) in `match comptime`, `std.typing.compile_error` and `Field.written`. Options in a compiled program: built (branch `compiled-options`): `main`'s entry calls the derived `from_args` with the program's name (argv[0] without its folder) and arguments, then prints the help (exit 0) or `error: <message>` (exit 2) as `strata run` does; `strata run --jit` too (the JIT now refuses IR that calls a runtime function its table lacks, instead of jumping to address 0). Not yet: "more than one `List` field" isn't reported; a record-level refinement on the options faults instead of a message. (The printer writing `Option[x]` patterns as `Option(x)` was fixed in #98.)
- **Step 1 (built, branch `derive-syntax`)**: The syntax is parsed, printed and stored (`derivable(t: Kind) trait ... requires ...`, `derived fn`, `derive Trait for Type` with or without a block, `comptime fn`, `comptime` parameters and expressions, a field named `type` after a dot); `derive`, `derived` and `derivable` are keywords only at the start of an item, so they stay ordinary names elsewhere; `comptime` is reserved. The checker refuses derivable traits, `derive` and `comptime` functions with a message saying they come next; a `comptime` expression is evaluated as written until then.
- **Step 2 (built, branch `derive-expand`)**: `src/derive.rs`, run right after resolution (and desugaring): each `derive X for T` becomes an ordinary `impl`. `T` is described as a value of `std.typing` (then `Record` or `Sum`; now a `Type`), checked against the kind (a refinement too: `Choice` is a refined `Type`) and `requires` (then in the interpreter, now compiled); each `derived fn` is specialized (`t` and `comptime` values written in, `comptime e` evaluated, `make` / `try_make` turned into the record's construction, a call of a function with `comptime` parameters turned into a call of a copy made for those values, with `f.type` and the types `match comptime` binds written in). The checker checks the results, not the generic bodies. Known limits: only a lambda's parameters hide a known name (not a `let`); functions with `comptime` parameters are specialized only from derived code; a failed `requires` names the condition, not the field; compile-time code taking a capability isn't refused yet (it can't get one: only `t` and `comptime` values are in scope); the budget is 5 million steps of compiled code.
- **In Strata (built, branch `derive`)**: `compiler/derive` ports `src/derive.rs` (`expand`), compile-time code run by `compiler/eval` (no step budget yet, no checking of the comptime expression first). `compiler/eval` runs derived programs: a function's type parameters are known while it runs, bound from the values it gets, the receiver's `impl`, and the result and receiver types `check.instantiated` writes around generic calls. `compiler check` writes each `derive` out first (the trait's whole std module loaded, core's types given to `derive`) and checks the result; derive's errors are reported as Rust reports them. Left: `setting(..)` reads no `strata.toml`; a lambda parameter of an overloaded built-in (`xs.map(x -> x.padded(n))`) stays untyped, so its generic `impl`'s parameters come from the value only.
- **Sums** (built, branch `variant-names`): `t.select(v -> test, v -> value)` gives `Some(value)` for the first variant whose test holds (both specialized with `v` known), else `None`; `v.make(f -> e)` / `v.try_make(f -> e)` build the variant `v` (nullary, positional or record fields).


### The `strata` command in Strata (asked by the user 2026-11-23; step 1 built, branch `cli-std`)
What has no Strata counterpart yet (about 9,000 lines of Rust): the codebase tool (store, diff, merge, history, pending, git, ast_value; the commands `edit`, `add`, `rename`, `move`, `status`, `done`, `abort`, `diff`, `merge-file`, `resolve`, `setup-git`, `sync`, `upgrade`, `log`, `view`, `interface`, `header`, `holes`, `pack`) and the driver (main.rs, cli.rs, package.rs, stdlib.rs, core.rs, bench.rs). The viewer and agent UI (ui.rs, serve.rs, ui/) are left out (user's call). Steps, one PR each:
1. **std for command-line tools** (built, `cli-std`). `std.cli`: commands as a union (`derive CommandLine for Command`, each member a command named as the member is, its `///` doc its help; needed `///` docs on union members, PR #340), a command field in an options record (the record's other fields are options given before it), an option given again and again (a `List[T]` field whose doc starts with its flag, `/// --rm …`, or a short name: `--rm a --rm b`; a `List` with a default stays the positional rest, as `examples/hello` has it), short names (a field doc starting `-m`), optional positional arguments (an `Option` field whose doc starts `[module]`; filled from the arguments before a `--`), `read_input(console, files, path)` (`-`: standard input). `std.io.Console`: `read_all` (the rest of standard input), `interactive` (whether it is a terminal). `std.fs.Files`: `write_bytes`, `is_dir`, `rename`, `remove_all`, `absolute` (with defaults built on the other methods, so `MemFiles` and mocks have them). `std.process`: `Environment.current_dir`, `Commands.run_with(program, args, dir, RunOptions { input, env, terminal })` (`run` is `run_with` with the defaults; `terminal`: the child uses this program's terminal, for an editor). Native: `host_read_stdin`, `host_stdin_terminal`, `host_is_dir`, `host_write_bytes`, `host_rename`, `host_remove_all`, `host_absolute`, `host_current_dir`, and `host_run` with input, environment and terminal, in runtime.c, llvm.rs and compiler/codegen.
1b. **Colors** (asked by the user 2026-11-23; built, branch `term-color`): `std.term` (`Paint` from `console.paint(Output | Errors)`, on for a terminal unless `NO_COLOR` or `TERM=dumb`; `FORCE_COLOR` forces it), `host_terminal(fd)` replacing `host_stdin_terminal`; `--help` headings bold and `error:` red, in the C runtime (compiled programs, `main`'s `Err` too) and in compiler/'s evaluator (`std.cli.styled_help`, `error_line`; `strata run` passes `STRATA_COLORS=out,err` since the evaluator's output reaches the terminal through it). The ported commands color their output with it.
2. **The store and the log, and pending changes** (merged with step 3 at the user's request; built, branches `store-port` (#346) and `edit-port`: `edit` (stdin, `$EDITOR`, `--file`), `add`, `status`, `done`, `abort`, `rename`, `move` in compiler/, each compared with strata's in tests/codebase.rs; a wall clock, `Clock.since_epoch` / `host_wall_now`. Found on the way, not fixed: compiler/'s checker misses some errors once an import fails (`unknown constructor in pattern`); a plain call named like a method of an imported module (`take`, `run`) resolves to that module's function, even a private one; compiler/'s evaluator lets a closure change a captured `mut` variable, which the checker refuses; `std.json.quote` writes `\r` unescaped. Before: built so far `store.graph` and `binary.encode_module`, byte for byte with strata's files on every package of the repository (`compiler store-check FILE...`), and the commands `init` and `log` in `compiler/codebase`, `main` now returning an exit code): a package's files read and written as module files (`compiler/binary` encodes whole modules, with the header's deps), strata.log entries, the git commit after a change. Commands: `edit` (stdin, or `$EDITOR` through `run_with` with `terminal` when standard input is `interactive`), `add`, `log`, `init`. Compared with the Rust ones on the test packages, as tests/parse.rs does.
3. **Diff, merging and the views** (built, branch `views-port`, one PR at the user's request; steps 3 to 6 of the first plan). `compiler/diff`: `diff` over the log's patches (replayed backwards from the package with each entry's texts, as `store::undo`) and with `--file` over git (each commit split into the log entries it added, then the working tree; old revisions read through an index of their own, `GIT_INDEX_FILE` + `checkout-index`, since a command's output comes back as text and module files are bytes), the verdicts before and after from `verify`'s store (closure hashes as strata makes them). `compiler/merge`: `merge-file`, `resolve`, `setup-git` (textconv `view --file`; the executable from `STRATA_EXE`), `sync` (renames either side logged followed in every package, each with its log entry, then every package and script checked), and `upgrade`. Views: `view` (with `render` folded in, in Strata only: a module named without `--level`, or a package of one module, is its file as text; `--split DIR`; `--members inline|separate`), `interface`, `header`, `pack` (`compiler/binary.encode_bundle`; `--prelude`), `holes` (compiler/check records each hole's goal, scope and obligations as it infers). Each compared with strata's in tests/codebase.rs (strata's `render` against Strata's `view`). Left out: `diff --json` and `holes --json` (the viewer reads them from strata); `sync`'s check of each package doesn't read its dependencies' code yet, nor check the package's settings; following a rename in `sync` doesn't yet confirm that every reference still means what it did (Rust's `resolve::follow`), it renames them as `rename` does. Found on the way: verifying `codegen.read_slot` (on main) crashes the process: the fuzzer gives its raw reads (`Raw.word`) arbitrary addresses, so any `strata build compiler` that verifies it again faults (`--no-verify` builds); holes in `test` blocks aren't reported by compiler/check (only functions' and methods')
   `render` goes into `view` (user, 2026-11-23): on a module, `view --level 2` already prints what `render` prints, so `view MODULE` (or a file) is the module as stored, round-tripping (mocks shown, since the module is named), `view --split DIR` replaces `render --split`, git's textconv calls `view`, and `render` is removed (tests, docs, CLAUDE.md moved to `view`). In the views step.
7. **The driver** (built, branch `cli-driver`, agent A of the v0.1 goal): the compiled compiler package is the `strata` command. `compiler/driver` reads the command line as Rust's `strata` reads it (`Args`: words, options with their values, what follows `--`; not a `derive CommandLine`, since compiler/'s checker runs no derive), the usage text, finding Strata's home (`STRATA_HOME`, else `<exe>/../lib/strata` (a release), else a folder above the executable or the current folder holding core/, std/ and the prelude). compiler/main dispatches: the steps the tests and Rust run (`lex FILE...`, `codegen FILE`, `check FILE`, `verify FILE`, `jit`, `object`, `link`, ...) keep their names and arguments when they come first; the codebase's commands get their words as before; `check`, `run` (and `run f ARGS`), `build`, `test`, `verify`, `build-core`, `lex`/`parse FILE`, `render` (= `view`), `builtins`, `--version` and `help` are the driver's, which enters the home (`driver_chdir`, c/driver.c; the paths given made absolute first; a program runs in the folder it was started in, `jit --dir`). `build`: verify what changed (`verify --quiet`), `proofs`, `codegen --proofs --precompiled`, then compiler/toolchain: objects in process (LLVM), core.rt and the C runtime (a release's objects, else codegen and the C compiler), lld against libSystem's stub; a package with C of its own is linked by the C compiler. std's precompiled library is a cache entry (`std-library/KEY`), or a release's `std/`. Every outside tool is in compiler/toolchain (`object`, `link`, `shared_library`, `solver`, `c_object`, `c_link`, `c_shared_library`). tools/compare-driver.sh compares it with Rust's `strata` command by command; tools/bootstrap.sh builds strata with itself and checks the fixed point (stage A = stage B, byte for byte). Gaps closed since (branches `cli-home`, `cli-gaps`, agent G): `check FILE`/`verify FILE` are the low-level steps only in the home (a user's `strata check math.strata` printed `unreadable`), and the driver's `check`/`verify` take the program's path first; a package's derived std trait loads that trait's module and derive's compile-time code sees the specialized copies (`std.cli.count_commands.for.Options.N`: examples/wc); compiler/codegen generates generic refined types (`NonEmpty[Line]`, orders' mock test); `view` is Rust's (default level 1) and `render` prints a module as text (setup-git's textconv `render --file`); `--json` for check, holes, builtins, verify (serde's bytes: compiler/driver's `pretty_json`) and log; `test --with-deps` (the std modules it loads and its dependencies, `std.cli: …`, before its own), `test` checks first, `test --emit-llvm`; `lex`/`parse` of a package; `--file core check` as Rust's (each module file read); `--release`: build/run/test unoptimized by default (the JIT's codegen at -O0: the orders tests spent >300 s in LLVM's DAG combiner at its default -O2), -O2 with `--release` (no LTO: the C runtime is a precompiled object); bootstrap and release build the compiler `--release`. tools/driver-cases.tsv: 65 of 68 cases the same (help and `ui`: dropped on purpose). Not yet: `diff --json`; `edit`/`rename`/`move --json`; `verify --no-race`/`--jit` accepted and ignored (compiler verify runs the solver then the fuzzer, as `--no-race`). Found: a bootstrap's fixed point holds only with no `verify` proofs recorded for the compiler package under the building compiler's identity (proofs are keyed by the executable's hash, so after `verify` on compiler/ with compiler X, X's build leaves checks out and the next stage's doesn't: a 2-cycle); Rust's `run` runs the program in the repository root, not the current folder.

### Long-term goal: self-hosting
The compiler should eventually be written in Strata. Gaps to close first:
- **Native compilation** (built: `strata build` through LLVM, and the JIT for everything that runs code; the interpreter, too slow to run a compiler, is gone).
- **Efficient mutation of collections**: in-place updates when a value has a single owner (as in Swift / Koka), instead of copying. Reference counting is built; reuse (Perceus) is not yet.
- **String and byte primitives** (built): a string's units (`s.bytes()`, `s.code_points()`, `s.graphemes()`), positions as byte offsets (`byte_len`, `byte_at`, `byte_slice`), bit operators (see "What iterating a string yields").
- **Early `return`, and `!` / `?`** to propagate errors (built).
- **A small native runtime**: GC (reference counting, built), I/O (the `host_*` layer, built), threads for tasks (built), and binding to the SMT solver (not built: the solver is still a process per query).

Bootstrap plan: write the frontend (lexer, parser, printer) in Strata and test it against the Rust one, then the checker, then compile the Strata compiler with itself and check that its output stops changing (a fixed point).

**The compiler in Strata (`compiler/`; first `std.ast`): the syntax tree in Strata, read and run by Strata code** (asked by the user 2026-11-21; step 1 built, branch `std-ast`). So parts of the compiler can move to Strata now: code in Strata reads `.strata` files, walks and builds trees, and runs them.
- **Not in std; a program, not a library** (user, 2026-11-22, branch `compiler-package`): the modules that were `std.ast*` are the `compiler/` package, an ordinary package (`name = "compiler"`, modules named by their paths: `ast`, `binary`, `text`, `lex`, `parse`, `resolve`, `check`, `exhaustive`, `eval`, `builtins`). It is not built into `strata` nor importable by other programs, so their check and verify don't pay for it. Its `main` runs one step of the front end on files (`strata --file compiler run -- lex FILE`, `parse`, `stored`, `written`, `check`, `eval`, `builtins`, …), and the tests (tests/ast.rs, lex.rs, parse.rs, resolve.rs, check.rs, eval.rs, builtins.rs) build it once and compare its output with the Rust front end's, instead of each storing its own driver script. Later `strata` runs it as its front end.
- **The tree**: `compiler/ast` mirrors `src/ast.rs` node for node, fields in the same order, variants in the order of their binary tags. Names differ where Strata needs it: variants are unique per module and don't reuse a type's name (`IntLit`, `StrLit`, `Var`; `VarDecl` for the statement; `IntPat`; `Equal`, `Less` for the operators; `Fallback` for `x ?? v`), and fields aren't keywords (`pre` / `post` for `requires` / `ensures`, `compile_time`, `terminating`, `mocked`, `conditions` for a derivable's `requires`). With unions-are-sums (merged), `Payload.MemberType` (tag 3) is a union member defined elsewhere; `Describe` went with `$` (the `std.typing` redesign).
- **One representation for values** (user, 2026-11-21, with the host-values work): values the host makes or reads (compile-time results, `run f ARGS`, verify's counterexamples, the Codebase snapshot) become Strata syntax, i.e. `compiler/ast` literals: what `Debug` prints, what `comptime e` writes back, what a compiler in Strata works on. `src/ast_value.rs` (Rust tree → the `compiler/ast` value, as `debug` writes it; `strata header FILE --tree`) is the first piece: the Codebase snapshot can be built with it. `std.typing` stays the checked description of types (what a derive reads); `compiler/TypeDecl` is how a type is written.
- **Step 1 (built)**: `compiler/ast` (the types); `compiler/binary`: `read` (module files and bundles), `read_header`, `decode_module`, `decode_definition`, `encode_definition`, `take_bodies` / `put_bodies`, refusing what `binary.rs` refuses (truncation, lengths past the end, non-canonical integers, unknown tags, nesting past 10,000, non-UTF-8 strings, a NaN); `std.utf8` (`encode`, `decode`, `decode_range`); `Files.read_bytes` (a default: the bytes of `read`; `Disk` reads them with `host_read_bytes`); built-ins `float_bits` / `float_from_bits` (compiled only). Tested (`tests/ast.rs`) against Rust on every module file in the repository, a program with every node, and a bundle: the same trees (`debug` vs `header --tree`), each definition re-encoded to the file's bytes, every prefix of a file refused. Reading all 39 module files (std included) and re-encoding them takes about a second. Found on the way: the verifier treated any type nesting more than 8 levels as refined (`refined_in`'s depth cut-off), so every `compiler/ast` value had validity checks it couldn't prove; it now visits each reachable type once instead.
- **Not yet in step 1**: the SHA-256 hashes (reading doesn't check them, so a flipped byte inside a definition can still decode; only definitions are written, not module files), and the header's names of `impl`s (they need the type printer). SHA-256 needs bit operations on integers: **decided** (user, 2026-11-21): operators `&`, `|`, `^`, `<<`, `>>` on `Int` (**built**, branch `bit-ops`): Python's meaning (two's complement infinitely sign-extended, `>>` rounding down, a negative shift a fault), Rust's precedence (tighter than comparisons: `|` < `^` < `&` < shifts < `+`), binary tags 14-18 (added at the end: old files read unchanged); `|` ends a refinement or a `where` pattern (the parser's `no_pipe`), so a bit-or is parenthesized there; the printer shows a bit operation compared in parentheses (`(x & 255) == x`, as the user wrote it, since C reads `x & 255 == x` the other way), which covers those places; compiled inline when both operands are small, in the runtime for big ones; the verifier models a shift by a literal and `x & (2^k - 1)` exactly (`*` / `div` / `mod` by 2^k), other uses as bounds only (one constant per operation and operands, so a refinement assumed and checked again is about one value). With them, **step 1b (built, branch `ast-hashes`)**: SHA-256 in Strata, **private to `compiler/binary`** (user, 2026-11-21: nobody should use it thinking it is a vetted, safe hash; it isn't constant-time and hasn't been reviewed as cryptography, so std gets a public hash only once one has been). Checked for what it does: FIPS 180's examples, the million `a`s included (a test in the module, run by `strata --file std test`); every length from 0 to 200 bytes, longer ones up to 20,000 bytes (from 8,192 bytes the length in bits takes three bytes, as in the million-`a` example, which doesn't fit on a command line) and constant inputs against the `sha2` crate (224 inputs); and all three hashes of every definition in the repository; about 100 KB hashes in well under a second. **A project tests only its own code** (user, 2026-11-22): `strata test` runs the tests of the project's own files (the package's, or the script's), not those of what it imports (std; dependencies, once fetched), which are compiled, verified and tested on their own: std's run when std itself is the program (`strata --file std test`). `strata test --with-deps` runs them too. (The program's own definitions come first in the resolved program, so the compiler takes the tests among those.) Seen on the way: `strata run f ARGS` took quadratic time in a list argument (50,000 elements 2.6 s), because compiling a list literal asked, for each element, whether a later one may assign (`Func::operands`); fixed separately (branch `operands-linear`: 200,000 elements in 0.2 s). `without_notes` in `compiler/ast`, `hash` / `interface_hash` / `code_hash` in `compiler/binary`, and `decode_module` checking each definition's hash, so a flipped byte never reads as another tree; `tests/ast.rs` checks all three hashes of every definition in the repository against the headers. Still not written by Strata: whole module files, whose entries list each definition's dependencies (that takes resolving names: the front end's resolver). Seen on the way, fixed since: `verify` reported a fault inside a callee (`main` calling `d(0)`, `d` dividing by its argument) as "a bug in the prover" from the cross-check; now it says the failure is in `d`, which has its own verdict.
- **Step 2: running a tree in Strata** (`compiler/eval`, built, branch `ast-eval`): **the start of the self-hosted front end** (user, 2026-11-21), not a second interpreter for running programs (the JIT does that). So it is built the way the front end will be: over stored trees, with its own name resolution, values as `compiler/ast` literals, tested against the compiled programs' recorded outputs (tests/data/build/expected). Front-end pieces next to it, each tested against the Rust one: the printer (tree to text), then the lexer and parser.
  - **What it does**: `run(decls, args)` runs `main` and gives a `Run`: stdout, stderr (with the fault or `main`'s error, as compiled code writes them) and the exit code. Faults are `Err` values (an `Escape`: a fault, or the `return` / `break` / `continue` a function or loop catches), never a crash of the evaluator; calls nested past 2,000 are a fault. The checks compiled code makes, with its messages word for word: argument and result types (refinements, every element of a list, set or dict, fields whose type is a type argument: `Some.0: -2 does not satisfy ...`, union membership), `requires` / `ensures` (a trait's too), `assert` with both sides, declared variables and assigned fields (and the record's own refinement), constructors, division by zero, non-finite floats, indexes, `chr` / `ord`. Values in messages are code, as compiled code shows them (`Range { lo: 9, hi: 5 } does not satisfy ...`); values are ordered as compiled code orders them (structurally: variants in the order declared, then field by field; a distinct value by what it is made of).
  - **Resolution**: references to definitions are stored qualified; methods are found from the receiver's type at run time: a variant's own `impl`, then its type's, then a trait implementation's (with the trait's contracts), then a trait's default method (supertraits' included); else a function of that name visible in the caller's module (its own, then its `use`s, then the prelude's), so `x.twice()` is `twice(x)`; else a built-in. `Item.new(..)` is a static method. A built-in's name called on an iterator (`len(it)`, `map`, `get`...) is its method, as compiled code does, and a trait method's name used as a value (`lines.map(summary)`) dispatches on its argument. A comprehension is the `filter` / `map` / `fold` calls compiled code makes of it, so it is lazy on an iterator; a `for` loop pulls an iterator's elements one at a time.
  - **Values**: `IntLit`, `FloatLit`, `StrLit`, `BoolLit`, `UnitExpr`, `TupleExpr`, `ListExpr`, `CtorExpr` (records with their fields in declared order, defaults filled in; a `distinct` value is its constructor around the value), `DictExpr` / `SetExpr` kept in order, a function as `Var(name)`, a closure as its lambda with the variables its body mentions bound before it (`Block { [x := 3], y -> x + y }`).
  - **Capabilities and the host**: `main` gets the capability its parameter names (`Process`), or the one that implements the capability trait it names (`Console`: `std.io.Terminal`); std's own code runs, and the host built-ins it reaches the system through are the evaluator's: `host_write` goes into the run's stdout / stderr, `host_args` gives the run's arguments. `matches` runs std.regex's engine, as compiled code does.
  - **The prelude**: `strata pack --prelude FILE` writes the prelude's definitions (types and code, under the names programs use, as parsed: storing would resolve them against the prelude itself) as a module file. A program runs with it, and with std's module files.
  - **`compiler/text`, the printer** (built, branch `ast-printer`): all of `src/printer.rs` in Strata, line breaking and definitions included (`module_text`, `decl_text`, ...), so the evaluator's messages break as compiled code's do. Tested against Rust on the stored trees, not the written form (which `render` shows, and which needs name resolution): `strata header FILE --text` is the Rust printer on a file's decoded definitions, and every module file of the repository (42) prints byte for byte the same (`tests/ast.rs`). Found on the way: a whole float of 10^15 or more was printed without a point (`1000000000000000`), which reads back as an `Int`; both printers now always write the point (`1000000000000000.0`). Also: a doc line is re-wrapped by the printer, but an edit doesn't store a change of docs alone (hashes leave notes out), so a std module stored from text with a doc line past 74 columns doesn't read back as shown (`resolve::tests::stored_and_written_forms_round_trip`) until its file is removed and stored again from `render`. The written form, the lexer and the parser followed (below).
  - **`compiler/lex`, the lexer** (built, branch `ast-lex`): `src/lexer.rs` in Strata, positions and error messages included (its quirks too: after a string literal the column moves by the string's bytes plus its quotes). Characters are classified as Rust's `char` does, with `std.unicode`'s `is_alphanumeric` / `is_alphabetic` / `is_white_space` (Strata's `is_digit` is ASCII only, so `x²` and `٣` would have differed). Tested (`tests/lex.rs`) against `strata lex FILE` (new: a text file's tokens and comments, a float by its bits, a string quoted as the printer quotes it) on the rendered text of every module file and on hand-written text with every error.
  - **`compiler/parse`, the parser** (built, branch `ast-parse`): all of `src/parser.rs` in Strata, comments placed as it places them, error messages word for word (the found token as Rust's `{:?}` writes it). Rust's generic `list(close, f)` became a loop per use, since a function type can't take a `mut` parameter. Tested (`tests/parse.rs`) against `strata parse FILE` (new: a text file parsed as a script, each definition as the `compiler/ast` value `debug` prints, as parsed, not stored) on the rendered text of every module file (44) and on hand-written text (comments everywhere, every shorthand, each kind of error). std, the parser included, verifies.
  - **Found on the way, not fixed**: the checker crashes (`types/exhaustive.rs`: index out of bounds) on a positional pattern of a record type, `Some(Comment(cs))` where the imported `Comment` was a record type of another module shadowing `compiler/ast`'s variant: it should be an error (a small package with that pattern didn't reproduce it on 2026-11-22). Two small differences remain possible, neither seen in the tests: a float in a parse error's message (`found Float(...)`) is written in Rust's `{:?}` form only approximately (Strata's `to_string` uses an exponent from 1e21, Rust's Debug from 1e16), and `is_upper` is "lowercasing changes it" where Rust's is Unicode's Uppercase property (they differ on titlecase letters such as `ǅ`).
  - **`compiler/resolve`, step 1** (built, branch `ast-resolve`; the user's choice between porting `src/resolve.rs` and a fresh design was deferred, so it is ported, tested tree for tree): `stored(decls, std)`, the stored form of a parsed program, std in scope: the table of definitions (types with their constructors, traits with their methods, a type's own methods), each module's scope (the root module, `use`s, its own definitions), and the walk that qualifies what names a definition (locals, type parameters, `it` and method names as written; `orders.ship(x)` the function `orders.ship`). Tested (`tests/resolve.rs`) against `strata parse FILE --stored` (new: Rust's `stored_form` on the parsed tree) on every script of the repository and hand-written ones. Packages too (`tests/resolve.rs`, `packages_store_as_in_rust`: each module's rendered text parsed as that module, all stored together, give the package's files' trees, the files being the stored form: std's 925 definitions and every example package). The written form too (`written(decls, std)`, `Store::Shorten`: each reference as short as the scope allows, a qualified function called as its module's method): every script and every std module, written back and printed, is `strata render`'s text (`written_as_rendered`). `union_members` too (a union's bare member is the type of that name when one is in scope, else an atom it declares; the prelude's names listed in the module). `named_unions` too (a union written as the named union with its members, `Shape` for `Circle | Square`, a generic one's instance, `Pair[Int, String]`, or the named parts of a larger one, `Shape | Dot`; done in the walker's types, bottom-up, on the stored names). `members_view` too (`inline_members`: what `render` adds, a type one union lists shown inside it when nothing is lost). The resolver's errors, first part: `errors(decls, std)`, a `use` of a module there is no module of, of what a module doesn't have, or of a private definition, as `strata check` words them (`use_errors_as_in_rust`). And of names used: a private definition (of a module imported, or any), std's host built-ins outside std, in the order `check` gives them. A mock outside tests and a foreign function called from another module too: the resolver's errors are all in Strata.
  - **`compiler/check`, the type checker in Strata, step 1** (built, branch `check-definitions`; ported from `src/types.rs` / `types/env.rs`, the same deferred choice as the resolver): `definition_errors(decls)`, the clashes of definitions (a name defined twice or the prelude's, two `impl Type` blocks, a trait implemented twice for a type), tested against `strata check` (`tests/check.rs`). A stored file can't hold two definitions of a name (an edit merges by name), so of these only a clash with the prelude reaches a stored program. A variant named like another type, trait or variant of its module too (`variant_errors`). A union with members of both kinds is split as the checker splits it (`mixed_unions`: `T$own`, the sum of the members it declares). Typing expressions, step 1: `expression_errors`, what the checker finds in a script's bodies without types: `unknown variable` (locals by scope; the program's functions and the built-ins are known; names imported by `use` not yet), and calls and constructors with the wrong number of arguments (a call's arguments are then skipped, a constructor's looked at up to its number of fields, as the checker does). Step 2: `type_errors`, inference (type variables, unification, the expected type passed down as the checker does) for `Int`, `Float`, `Bool`, `String`, `()`, lists and the program's functions: literals, operators, `if`, blocks, `let`/`mut` with annotations, assignments, `while`, `assert`, `return`, calls, list literals, `match` arms; mismatches worded as the checker words them (`if branch: expected `Int`, found `String``). Every other type is `TAny`, which fits anything: it can miss errors but not invent them (none on the repository's 46 module files). Step 3: the program's record and sum types without type parameters (constructor arguments, fields, missing and unknown fields, fields bound by a constructor's pattern). A variable or field of a sum type is `TAny`, because the checker narrows it (`match`, `is`, an early `return`) to the variants left, which is not modelled. Step 4: generics: a function's type parameters, fresh at each use and rigid in its own body (`found `T``); generic records and sums, whose constructors take their type arguments from the expected type; `Option` and `Result` (`Some`, `None`, `Ok`, `Err`, `??`). A message whose types aren't all known (the checker would write `?T0`) is left out. Step 5: methods. A method's body is checked (its errors are the `impl`'s: `impl Pt`, `impl Area for Pt`). A call `p.m(..)` is checked when `p`'s type is one of the program's types with that method, `self` being argument 1. A program function called on a value of a known type (`p.twice(2)`) is checked too, unless some `impl` or trait has a method of that name. Not yet: "unknown function" for a method no type has (std's traits may give the program's types methods), and generic `impl`s. Next: the built-in functions and methods (lists, strings, dicts), tuples, lambdas' parameter types, and narrowing. Step 6: a variable of a sum type (`Option` included) keeps its type unless its function narrows it (`match x`, `x is V`); a constructor's pattern takes the matched value's type arguments (`Some(n)` on an `Option[Int]`: `n: Int`). A place's type is its own or `TAny` (unchecked), never another: by construction (`if is_sum(..) { TAny } else { t }`). Stated as `ensures it == t || it == TAny` it is not proven: the verifier compares no values of a sum type and doesn't model `contains` (the SMT datatypes idea would let such contracts on the checker's own types be proven). Step 7: calls of built-in functions, typed from `compiler/builtins` (`len(xs)` and `xs.len()`); an overloaded one by the version its arguments fit (several fit: not checked; none: `no version of …`, for versions without type parameters); lambdas take their parameters' types from the function type expected (`xs.map(x -> x + 1)`); `Dict` and `Set` modelled. Step 8: tuples (`(Int, String)`, `p.1`, `(a, b) := p`, "no element") and the elements a `for` or a list comprehension goes through (a list's, a set's; `enumerate`'s pairs). Step 9: `r!` and `o?` (the value held), `except` (its fallback of the value's type, `e` the error), `xs[k]` (the built-in `at`), slices and ranges (`List[Int]`). Step 10: narrowing as the checker shows it. In a `match` arm naming variants of the matched variable (`Sq(_) =>`, `Sq(_) | Dot =>`), the variable is those variants (`Sq`, `Dot | Sq`), which fit where the sum is asked for; a field of one variant is checked (`variant `Rc` has no field `z``). `is` and early returns change no type the checker shows, so a variable tested with `is` is checked with its declared type. Step 11: traits and generic `impl`s: a generic `impl`'s methods take the receiver's type arguments (`b.get()` on a `Box[Int]`); on a value of a type parameter, the methods its bounds give (`x.area()` with `T: Area`); at a call, each bound of the program's own traits (not derivable, not implemented for every type) is checked against the type the parameter stands for (`calling `total`: `Pt` does not implement `Area``). Step 12: unions (`Int | String`: members sorted as the checker writes them; a member fits its union; `if` branches of known, different types give their union) and refinements (the prelude's `Natural`, `U8`, `Probability`, `NonEmpty[T]`…, and the program's aliases, refined or not, are the type they stand for). A union-typed variable its function narrows (`match`, `is`) and a field of union type are not checked. Step 13: methods called on a type (`Item.new(1)`: an `impl`'s method without `self`, `call to `Item.new``; `T.zero()` through `T`'s bounds), `has no method … called on the type`, and `unknown function` for a method no `impl`, trait, function or built-in has, on one of the program's types with no `impl` of a trait from outside the program. Step 14: a union-typed variable in a `match` arm naming its members (`Int =>`) is those members; `is` and early returns change no union's type the checker shows, so a variable tested with `is` is checked with its declared type. Step 16: traits as types (`List[Shape]`, `s: Shape`): a concrete type meeting one must implement it (`using it as a `Shape`: `Pt` does not implement `Shape``); a trait value's methods and a bound's supertraits' methods are checked. `compiler/declarations`: unknown types, constructors (in expressions and patterns) and traits, numbers of type arguments, `Self` outside a trait or an impl, `break`/`continue` outside a loop, an impl's methods against its trait's (missing, extra), methods defined twice, a pattern's number of fields and fields not all mentioned, alternatives binding different names; names a program imports by `use` keep these from being reported where they could be the imported module's. Step 17: imported names. `compiler check` reads the std modules a file imports (`--std DIR`, default `std`), and `type_errors_with` types the file against their functions and types (`call to `std.utf8.encode`, argument 1`); errors are the file's only, `unknown function` only on its own types (an imported type's methods may come from modules not read), and a `comptime` parameter is not modelled (`f.default_value`). The modules those import are read too, round by round (`std_imports`). Step 18: unreachable `match` arms (after a catch-all, a constructor or literal already covered whole, or every variant covered; `compiler/exhaustive`), and a function named like a trait's or a type's own method (`go` is both a function and a method of trait `T`). Not yet: an arm after an unknown constructor (the checker takes that one as a catch-all), and arms a refinement rules out. Step 19: values used as what they aren't: `!` and `?` on the other kind (`! needs a Result, found …`), `except` on a non-`Result` (an `Option`'s own message), `for` over a number, a Bool or `()`, a field of a basic type, calling a value that is no function, and f-string specs against their value's type (`.N` on a Float or a String, `+`/`0` on a number). Step 20: `r!` in a function returning a `Result` of another error type (`returns the error early, so the result type must be …`); and when the result is no `Result` (or, for `?`, no `Option`). A type not worked out yet is written `_` by both checkers (decided 2026-11-22, the user's open question defaulted: it was `?T2`, a counter that means nothing to the reader), so these messages can be reproduced. Step 21: task handles: `spawn e` is a `Task[T]`, `await` gives the task's value (of each, for a tuple or list of handles; anything else: `await takes a task's handle …`), and a `scope` whose result holds a handle: `a task's handle can't leave its scope`. A closure or `spawn` holding a handle from outside it, and a variable declared outside the `scope` holding or getting a handle, are reported too. Step 15: an arm binding the whole value (`other =>`) and an arm that assigns to the matched variable see the declared type, as the checker shows them. (`verify` with a selector that matched nothing, like a bare module name, verified nothing and exited 0; fixed, branch `verify-selector`: a module's name selects its definitions, and a selector part that names nothing is an error.). Found on the way, not fixed: `verify` crashes (a stack overflow in `smt::Vc::builtin`, signal 10, killing strata) on nested closures over lists, `xs.any(a -> xs.any(b -> to_list(a.1).any(y -> contains(b.1, y))))` in `named_type`: written as loops, it is proven (a standalone copy of the expression, over `List[(String, Set[String])]`, no longer crashes: checked 2026-11-22). Not yet either: `select` and `race` aren't looked inside. `compiler/builtins` (built, branch `ast-builtins`): the built-in functions' signatures as data (`Signature { name, type_params, params, ret }`, `signatures()`, `signatures_named(n)`), one per overload in `BUILTINS`' order, for the checker's built-in calls and methods; `tests/builtins.rs` checks them against `strata builtins`, and `ensures` clauses state that names are non-empty and `signatures_named(n)` gives only `n`'s.
  - **`compiler/contracts`** (built, branch `check-contracts`; ported from `src/types.rs`): `contract_errors(decls)`, the checker's rules on contracts and refinements without inference: a `requires`/`ensures` clause, a `where` (in a type, a parameter, a result, a field), a type's refinement and a trait's law that is surely not a `Bool` (literals, operators, parameters and `it` of `Int`/`Float`/`String`/`Bool` types, through plain aliases); `it` named in `requires`; a literal field default or assignment `x = lit` that breaks the declared refinement (conditions comparing `it` or a string's length with integers). Not yet: clauses whose type needs inference (`self.x + 1`), lambdas' parameter refinements, float refinements, and the literals `verify` checks at calls.
  - **`compiler/mutability`** (built, branch `check-mut`; ported from `src/types.rs`'s `check_place` / `check_mut_args`): `mut_errors(decls)`, part of the `check` command: assigning to a variable not declared `mut` (or from a closure), `mut` written where no argument is, a closure's call with `mut` arguments, and calls of the program's own functions (`mut` missing for a `mut` parameter, `mut` on one that isn't, an argument that can't change). Not yet: method calls and `mut` receivers (which method needs the receiver's type), calls of std's functions, a task's handle stored outside its `scope`; inside comprehensions nothing is reported.
  - **`compiler/capabilities`** (built, branch `check-capabilities`): `capability_errors(decls)`, the checker's rules on capabilities and concurrency that need no inference, worded as `strata check` (`tests/check.rs`): `spawn` outside a `scope` (a task opens none of its own; a closure keeps its enclosing one), `return`/`!`/`?` leaving a task, empty `select`/`race`, a `select` arm's shape and a `send`/`after` arm named with `as`, a sender from `(tx, rx) := channel(…)` never closed in its block, a task mentioning a sender (made so, or a `Sender[…]` parameter) without closing it, a capability used as a value, a test parameter that is a primitive or a root-module record or sum, a `pub extern c fn`. Not yet (need types): handles leaving a `scope` or held by a closure, a sender inside a tuple, `await` on a non-handle, `after` on a non-`Clock`, `RunMode` in tests (needs strata.toml), impl and capability methods' bodies. `main`'s parameters are checked by `run`, not `check`.
  - **`compiler/exhaustive`** (built, branch `ast-exhaustive`; ported from `src/types/exhaustive.rs`): `match_errors(decls)`, a script's non-exhaustive matches worded as the checker words them (`non-exhaustive match on `Sh`: `Rc` is not covered`), when the matched type is known from the code (a parameter not bound again, assigned, `is`-tested or narrowed by an enclosing match; a call to a non-generic function; a comparison) and is a sum of the program's, `Option`, `Result` or `Bool`, and the arms are `_`, names, its constructors or `true`/`false` (with `|`). Anything else (`where` arms, literals, lists, tuples, a constructor partly covered by nested patterns, types of other modules) reports nothing; `first_missing` states as `ensures` that a catch-all arm means nothing is reported and that a reported case is a variant of the type. Next: nested witnesses (`Some(Sq)`), tuples and lists, unreachable arms, and the types `type_errors` infers.
  - **Next**: the type checker in Strata, continued (built-in functions and methods, tuples, lambdas' parameter types, narrowing), which also lets `compiler/eval` run a type parameter's static methods (`T.zero()`). The resolver was ported from `src/resolve.rs` step by step (the user's choice between that and a fresh design was deferred, so it was ported, tested tree for tree), and so is the checker.
  - **Tested** (`tests/eval.rs`): the programs tests/build.rs compiles, now shared in `tests/common/programs.rs` (and `common/json.rs`): 61 give their recorded output exactly (faults, data, traits, dyn values, collections, strings, unions, iterators, capabilities, big integers, memory, examples/math and builtins).
  - **Floats, and std's `verify --require proven`**: the evaluator runs a program's float arithmetic, which may really overflow, so it can't do it with `*` (a fault in the evaluator itself) and no proof could help (the prover doesn't model floats anyway). Built for it (user, 2026-11-22): `checked_add` / `checked_sub` / `checked_mul` / `checked_div` / `checked_rem` / `checked_sqrt` (`Option[Float]`) and `checked_floor` / `checked_ceil` / `checked_round` (`Option[Int]`), total built-ins giving `None` where the operation faults; the evaluator turns `None` into the program's fault, worded as compiled code words it. `float(n)` is guarded by the exact bound (`|n| < 2^1024 - 2^970`, from which an Int rounds to infinity), which the prover now uses too (it had `10^308`). `{x:.N}` is computed exactly with Ints from the float's bits, a half to the even (as Rust's), at any precision (a float power of ten overflowed past `.308`, found by verify's fuzzer). With these, std, the evaluator included, passes `verify --cases 10 --require proven`. Seen on the way, not fixed: a list of `Option[Int]` and `Option[Float]` (`[a, b]` with `a: Option[Int]`, `b: Option[Float]`) makes LLVM refuse the IR (a `switch` with duplicate cases); and `float_from_bits` gives infinities (only a NaN is `None`), as a C function's `double` can, though the language says a `Float` is never infinite.
  - **Not yet**, each listed in `tests/eval.rs` with why: a type parameter's static methods (`T.zero()`, `T.empty()`): which type `T` is comes from the call's types, so it waits for the type checker in Strata; `derive` and `comptime` (made while compiling); files (`Disk`'s host built-ins); mocks; `extern` calls; `comptime for` and `for` arms. Open: whether the evaluator gets a fake file system for `Disk` (as tests do), or takes a `Files` capability from its caller.
- **`compiler/codegen`, the code generator in Strata** (asked by the user 2026-11-22; step 1 merged #263, step 2 merged #288, step 3 merged #294, step 4 built, branch `codegen-lists`): a checked program as LLVM IR text, for the C runtime `src/llvm.rs`'s IR is linked with (the same value layout, runtime functions and fault messages). Decided with the user (2026-11-22): **tested by what programs do, not by their IR**: each program of `tests/codegen.rs` is built by `strata build` and by `compiler/codegen` (its IR and `runtime.c` through clang), and both executables must give the same stdout, stderr and exit code (faults included). So its IR is its own: not `llvm.rs`'s text, quirks included (`llvm.rs` loads `a` and `b` twice for `a + b`). And **types come from `compiler/check`**, not from Rust: the checker writes what only inference knows into the tree (`check.typed`: each `x := e` / `mut x := e` gets the type inferred for it, when fully known; the inference records each declared variable's type in the order it meets them, and the tree is walked in the same order, checking each name), and the code generator works out the rest from what is declared (parameters, results), refusing what isn't typed. It grows as the checker does. **Step 1**: `Int` (small in 64 bits, big ones in the runtime, as `llvm.rs`), `Float` (checked finite), `Bool`, `String`, `()`; functions and calls (those `main` reaches); `if`, blocks, `let` / `mut` variables, assignment to a variable, `while`, `loop`, `break`, `continue`, `return`, `assert` (both sides shown); operators (bit operations and shifts included); f-strings without a spec; `print`; `main` returning `()` or an exit code. Anything else is refused, the construct and the function named (`in `main`: not compiled yet: ...`); `compiler run -- codegen FILE` prints the IR. **Step 2** (branch `codegen-data`): the program's records and sums, `Option` and `Result`, generic types (`Pair[A, B]`: one layout for all, so no copies per type), and tuples: built (by position, by field, a field's default, `P { ..p, x: 1 }`), a record's field or a tuple's element read, `x is C`, `match` (constructor, tuple, literal, `|` and `where` patterns, nested; `match e as x`; no arm matching is `llvm.rs`'s fault) and destructuring `let`; `==` and showing them (`print`, f-strings, `assert`'s sides) as code, by a helper function per type (`@"eq:T"`, `@"debug:T"`). Blocks are `llvm.rs`'s (a tag, then 8-byte slots); tags are numbered per program (`llvm.rs` hashes the constructor's name, so a value's tag is the same in every program: needed only when values cross programs, as compile-time values do). The checker hands over its model of the program's types (`check.types_of`: constructors, fields, type parameters), and `check.typed` now also reaches the variables declared inside `match` arms, constructors' arguments, tuples and fields. A constructor's type arguments come from its fields' values (`Some(1)` is `Option[Int]`), and a declared type completes what they leave open (`x: Option[Int] = None`). **Step 3** (branch `codegen-rc`): counting, with `llvm.rs`'s conventions: a function borrows its arguments and returns a value it owns; reading a variable or a field borrows; a value stored (a variable, a field, the result) is held once more, unless it is a temporary of the innermost scope, which moves; each statement, branch, arm and loop pass is a scope that drops its temporaries and the variables it owns when it ends, and `return`, `break` and `continue` drop what they leave. A temporary is never made where its scope's end isn't reached through it (a big literal in a pattern, `strata_int_cmp`'s result), and what a fault's message is made of isn't dropped (the program ends). A pattern is tested first and binds after, once nothing can fail, so a bound name holds its value (an arm that reassigns the matched variable keeps its fields alive) without undoing bindings on a failed test. A drop function per type (`@"drop:T"`: the block freed, then its counted fields dropped; a sum's by a `switch` on the tag; constructors without fields are constants, never freed). The counting functions are `llvm.rs`'s inline ones (`@strata.dup`, `@strata.release`, `@strata.int_dup`, `@strata.int_drop`). Tested by the runtime's memory check: each program of tests/codegen.rs that ends normally, built by `compiler/codegen`, frees everything it made under `STRATA_MEMORY=check` (which aborts on a freed object used). An operand that borrows is held until the statement ends when a later one may assign a variable (`f(x, { x = g(); 1 })`). Not yet `llvm.rs`'s optimisations: constant blocks, blocks on the stack (escape analysis), `strata_concat_owned`, reuse in place. Not yet: a sum's field outside a `match` (the checker refuses it too), refined and `distinct` types, unions, lists, generic functions, closures, methods and traits, std (capabilities), contracts, tasks. **Step 4** (branch `codegen-lists`): lists, `llvm.rs`'s layout (a header `{ len, buf, off }` over a shared buffer `{ cap, used, slots }`, runtime.c's `strata_list_*`): literals, `xs[i]` (out of range: `llvm.rs`'s fault), slices `xs[a..b]` / `xs[a..]` / `xs[..b]` / `..=` (clamped as in Python), ranges, `len`, `push`, `get`, `take`, `drop`, `at`, `slice`, `range` as calls or methods (`xs.len()`), `+`, `for` over a list (the loop holds it) or a range (a counter, no list), list patterns (`[a, b, ..rest]`, the rest a slice sharing the buffer), `==` and showing (`[1, 2]`), and counting (a list's drop releases the buffer, which drops its elements up to `used`). The stored tree has ranges, indexes and comprehensions as written (Rust desugars them in its resolver; the Strata compiler has no such pass), so codegen compiles them directly. `check.typed` now also reaches variables declared in `for` bodies, list literals, indexes and ranges. **Group A** (the rest of it, same branch and PR, as the user asked: all of A in one PR): (A2, A3) generic functions, one instance per set of type arguments (`@"strata:f[Int, String]"`), asked for by the calls (a queue, not a walk of the call graph). The type arguments are bound by codegen from the arguments' types, left to right, and what they leave open from the type wanted where the call is (`let xs: List[Int] = empty()`), instead of the checker writing them into the tree: the arguments' types come from what the checker wrote and what is declared, so no new syntax was needed; the plan's A2 is that. (A4) Closures: `llvm.rs`'s block `{ fn, drop, shown, captured... }` (a constant when nothing is captured), each lambda its own function taking the block first; a lambda's parameter types are written or come from where it is passed (`map(xs, x -> …)`, `search(xs, y -> y, …)`), its result from its body; named functions and built-ins as values (a lambda calling them); calls of function values (variables, fields, results).
