The Strata checker now reports, worded as `strata check` words them: `for` over a value that is no List, Set, Dict or `Iterator` (a basic type, one of the program's types with no `Iterator` impl, a type parameter with no bound; an `impl`'s bounds now hold in its methods); a trait's method used as a function (`price(x)`, `xs.map(price)`, `true.price()`) on a type that doesn't implement the trait or a type parameter no bound of which reaches it, through obligations solved when the function's body is checked (a script's own traits only: in a package the impl keys are qualified in ways the obligations don't follow yet); `mut` on the receiver of a method, missing or needless (a trait's or the type's own: the Rust checker skipped a type's own methods, which the language's rule (docs/language.md) covers too; fixed in both (branch `mut-own-methods`), which marked `std.http.Router`'s `mut self.on(…)` and examples/page's `mut app.get(…)`, and an edit whose errors include std's no longer panics in `holeify`); `mut` on an argument of a built-in; a `mut` parameter's exact type (`List[Circle]` is no `List[Circle | Square]` there; union members compared in any order); an `after` arm on what is no clock and with a time that is no `Duration`; `extern c fn` types that don't cross to C, type parameters and `strata_` names; a constructor of an `extern c type`; `mock` of a capability or a basic type; a capability made in another module (`clock.SystemClock {}`); `type Name = String` adding nothing. Also renamed `compiler/main`'s lazy loader's `take` to `take_entry`: as a root-module function it hid the built-in `take` from the package's other modules (98 errors in the repository sweep since #290). tests/check.rs's `iterables_bounds_receivers_and_externs_as_in_rust` holds these (18 errors). Of step 3's 52 lines only in Rust, 26 reproduced from the tests' programs (tests/cli.rs's traits, iterators, `mut` calls, clock arms, channels, variance, `mut` refinements, `extern c`, capabilities), 20 agree now; what is left is refinement-aware matches and compile-time refinements (the solver from Strata), `derive`, distinct types (`expected `UserId`, found `OrderId``), a sender inside another value (needs `channel`'s type, which core now declares), and `RunMode`/capabilities made by the program. `render --strata` now writes a module through `compiler written` (the resolver's shortening mode, which tests/resolve.rs's `written_as_rendered` already held to `strata render` on every repository file) with std's and the package's other modules for names, instead of `compiler text` on the stored tree: `render --compare` agrees on every example package and script, the tests/data programs, `compiler` (27088 lines) and `std` (18468; its modules are named `std.io`, … as Rust names them, which `lex` and `parse --compare` on std needed too). A folder of scripts without strata.toml (tests/data/build) reads as one module `main` in Rust, so `render --compare` says it isn't covered (exit 2). The repository sweep (`compiler`, `core`, each std module file, every example and tests/data program) stays clean. When each command's Strata side can be the default: `lex`, `parse` now (done); `check` once the refinement-aware gaps close; `render` now for whole scripts and modules (`--split`, `--level`, `--members` and chosen definitions aren't covered by `compiler written` yet); `run` once `compiler eval` gives `main` its `CommandLine` options; `build` once `compiler/codegen` covers `Result` values and packages.
- **Self-hosting, step 5: `run` gives `main` its options and real arguments** (asked by the user 2026-11-23; built, branch `run-options`, on #308's `compiler/derive`). `compiler eval` takes `--program NAME ARGS...` after its files (else each file's name and `one`, `two words`), and `strata run --strata/--compare` passes the arguments after `--` and the name `strata run` gives the program (its package's, or the file's), without making them absolute paths as it does the files it hands the compiler. `main`'s parameter of a type implementing `CommandLine` gets `T.from_args(name, args)` (the derived method `derive.expand` wrote): `Ok` is the options, `ShowHelp` prints the help on stdout and exits 0, `BadArgs` prints `error: MESSAGE` on stderr and exits 2, as `strata_options_error` does. The evaluator's state holds the `Files` the run was given (the compiler's own `Disk`; none for compile-time code), which run `host_read_file`, `host_read_bytes`, `host_exists` and `host_list_dir` (failures as the runtime's (kind, message), which `std.fs.fs_error` turns into an `FsError`); writing isn't run, since `--compare` would do it twice. `run --compare` agrees on every example package and script and tests/data program, and on hello and wc with options (`--help`, a bad option, `--times 0` against its refinement, a file read); tests/compare.rs's `compare_run_with_options` holds them. `run`'s Strata side can be the default for programs that don't write files or use the clock or other tasks (`select` and `race` aren't run).
- **Self-hosting, step 6: `render` and `run` default to the Strata side** (asked by the user 2026-11-23; built, branch `render-run-default`): `strata render` of a whole script (no view's options) and `strata run [PATH] [-- ARGS]` (no `--trace`) go through `compiler written` and `compiler eval`, the program's exit code kept; `--rust` gives Rust's. Anything else is Rust's, and so is a run the evaluator stops on (`isn't run by this evaluator`, `can't give`), which it detects before printing anything (it writes no files, so nothing happens twice). What's left (`check` with the solver, `build`) the user takes on.
- **Step 3: LLVM from Strata** through `extern c` and `llvm-c`: code generation stays text IR (a string, see the LLVM toolchain decision), so only the JIT needs the C API: `LLVMParseIRInContext`, the ORC `LLJIT` (`LLVMOrcCreateLLJIT`, `LLVMOrcLLJITAddLLVMIRModule`, `LLVMOrcLLJITLookup`). The FFI gaps that blocks: a `char *` that C still owns (an error message freed with `LLVMDisposeMessage`: read as a `String` and given back, not just copied), calling a function pointer (the address the JIT gives; a C shim in `[native] sources` works meanwhile), library paths and flags in `[native]` (LLVM's `-L` / `llvm-config --libs`), and arrays of handles (only for the builder API, which text IR avoids). `extern python` / `ts` / `rust` (FFI step 5) are not built; they're a separate step.

**`std.regex`: our own regex engine, in Strata** (decided with the user 2026-11-21; built, steps 1-5 below). A self-hosted compiler can't use Rust's regex crates, so regexes get a library written in Strata, used language-wide (programs call it like any std module) and by the compiler. It replaced, one use at a time: `regex` (the interpreter's `matches`), `regex-syntax` (parsing patterns for the prover, the generator and the code generator), `regex-automata` (DFAs for literal patterns in compiled code, and the inclusion / disjointness facts of `regex_relations`) and `rand_regex` (generating matching strings for the fuzzer and `mock`). It also gives compiled code patterns built at run time, which were refused because they needed an engine in the runtime; the runtime stays small (C), since the engine is ordinary compiled Strata.
- **The engine is the definition of Strata regexes**: compiled code, prover and generator all follow it. Rust's crates are only the reference it is tested against (`regex`, `regex-syntax`, `regex-automata` and `rand_regex` are dev-dependencies, used by `tests/regex.rs` only).
- **The full dialect stays**, word boundaries and anchors anywhere included (user: the library is for every program, not only for compiling). Parts that can't do a feature say so, as now: the DFA builder refuses Unicode `\b`, the prover leaves it unknown, the Pike VM runs everything.
- **Design**: a parser into a Strata syntax tree with the Unicode semantics (`\d`, `\w`, `\s`, `\p{..}`, `(?i)` folding); a Thompson NFA run by a Pike VM (linear time, no backtracking; captures, `find` and `replace` possible later, which a DFA can't give); a DFA builder (subset construction over character or byte ranges) for literal patterns and for products of two patterns; a generator; the SMT translation reads our tree instead of the HIR.
- **Unicode tables**: a `std.unicode` module generated once from the Unicode Character Database by a script and checked in, also meant for `to_upper` / `to_lower` / `is_alpha` (still made from Rust's `char`: the compiled prelude can't use std). Compiled programs keep only the tables they use.
- **Speed**: the compiler runs Strata code with the JIT (the interpreter is gone), so building DFAs for large Unicode classes at compile time is compiled code.
- **Step 1 (built, branch `regex-parse`)**: `std.regex.parse` reads a pattern into a `Regex` (`Empty`, `Literal(String)`, `Class(List[(Int, Int)])`, `Look(Assertion)`, `Repeat`, `Capture`, `Concat`, `Alternation`): the syntax first (an AST, as regex-syntax's, so nesting is limited the same way: 250), then its meaning with the flags threaded through (a flag set in a group lasts to its end, across `|`), and the tree built by constructors that simplify as `Hir`'s do (literals joined, `a|b` a class, a common prefix lifted, `a{1}` is `a`), so equal patterns give equal trees. `std.unicode` has sets of code points as ranges (union, intersection, difference and negation stepping over the surrogates as Rust's `char` does, so the ranges are regex-syntax's to the code point), property lookup with UAX #44 loose matching, simple case folding; `std.unicode_tables` (195 KB, Unicode 16.0.0) is generated by `tools/unicode_tables.py` from regex-syntax's tables (made by ucd-generate from the UCD), each set a string of numbers decoded on use. `tests/regex.rs` prints both trees the same way (`show`) and compares them, error or not, on ~280 curated patterns and 1000 random ones (`REGEX_SEED`, `REGEX_CASES`; 40,000 agreed before merging; in the interpreter, as CI runs it, a pattern takes ~50 ms). Not yet / found on the way:
  - speed: with std precompiled (PR #137), `std.regex` and `std.unicode` are in std's cached library, so a program calling `parse` starts in ~0.10 s (1.25 s compiling them with the program, `STRATA_NO_STD_LIBRARY`), and a pattern parses in ~0.4 ms. Literal patterns, nearly all of them, can still be parsed while compiling (`comptime parse(...)`, or the compiler for `matches` with a literal: step 2), their trees then constants;
  - the tables are data, not code: they were `match`es on names (a 1,700-arm `match` became a 9,000-line function, and a program using `property` took 1.2 s to start); they are now sorted constant lists of names and values, searched by halving (0.3 s), and case folding is two constant lists (`folding_keys`, `folding_others`) instead of a string decoded for each `(?i)` literal. For this the compiler makes a list or tuple literal whose elements are all constants a constant itself (see the memory entry);
  - error messages are our own (regex-syntax's show the pattern with a caret); only "error or not" is compared;
  - the Unicode data comes from regex-syntax for now; the script reads the UCD files once the crates go;
  - tooling (fixed by PR #127): `strata --file std edit std.M` accepted definitions that `strata --file std check` then rejects (a parameter named `min` hiding the function); and `strata edit M` run inside `std/` (as a package) names the module `M`, not `std.M`, and starts a `strata.log`, so std's modules must be edited with `--file std` and their `std.` names. Variants can't have `///` doc comments (the parser refuses them), so a sum's variants are documented on the type.
- **`comptime e` in ordinary code (built, branch `comptime-anywhere`)**: evaluated while compiling, as a derive's (on the JIT, values cached), and written in as a literal, so `comptime parse("…")` is a constant tree and the program never calls `parse`. `e` may use definitions only (a variable around it is an error that says so). Functions that are specialized where called (`comptime fn`, `comptime` parameters, derived ones) are left alone. Found on the way, and the cause of a run that took 22 GB: evaluating one resolves the program made for it (the cache key, `closure_hash`), and that program still held the `comptime` being evaluated, so resolving it evaluated it again, without end; the program made for compile-time code now leaves out ordinary code holding a `comptime`, and the pass doesn't run inside itself. Constructors of constants (`Some(1)`, a record, a `Regex`) are constant data too (branch `const-ctors`), so a `comptime parse(...)` tree allocates nothing. Not yet: `strata --file F edit` of a script accepts a `comptime` that `check` refuses.
- **Step 2 (built, branch `regex-pike`)**: `std.regex.compile` makes a Thompson NFA (`Inst`: `Chars(ranges)`, `Split`, `Jump`, `Save`, `Assert`, `Done`; repetitions unrolled, at most 250,000 steps), and `is_full_match` runs it as a Pike VM over the text's code points: the set of states, no backtracking, assertions evaluated between characters as regex-automata's `LookMatcher` does them (word characters `\w`'s, Unicode or ASCII). `full_match(text, pattern)` is what `matches` means. Compiled code matches a pattern built at run time with it (`std.regex.match_code`, then `pattern_error` for the fault), so built executables have them too, and the runtime no longer reaches Rust's `regex` (`strata_regex_match` is gone). `tests/regex.rs` compares it with `regex`'s `^(?:p)$` on curated patterns and texts and on random ones (texts made by `rand_regex` from the pattern, then changed a character); 12,000 random patterns agreed. Difference, on purpose: a pattern is a regex on its own, so `a)|(b`, which Rust's `regex` accepted inside its `^(?:…)$`, is an error. Not yet: captures and `find` (the VM tracks states only, `Save` is a no-op); a pattern built at run time is parsed and compiled at every call (10,000 calls of a short one: well under a second). Literal patterns got their DFA in step 3, and the prover its model in step 4.
- **Step 3 (built for compiled code, branch `regex-dfa`)**: `std.regex.automaton` builds a DFA from the NFA by subset construction: the code points split into classes at every range's ends (a `\w` pattern has ~1,600), `^` holding at the start only and `$` at the end (what follows it can't read a character); a pattern asserting anything else (lines, word boundaries), or making more than 4,000 states or 2M cells, keeps the Pike VM. `matcher(pattern)` is `Deterministic`, `Pike` or `NotARegex`. Compiled code makes each literal pattern's matcher while compiling (`literal_patterns` in llvm.rs: compile-time evaluation, cached, its value a function `strata.matcher.N` returning it as data) and calls `std.regex.run_matcher`; the program's code is left as written, so messages still show `matches(it, "…")`. regex-automata is gone from the code generator (and `strata_dfa_match` from the runtime); a program calling `matches` now includes std. Found on the way: a trait method named like a builtin (`find`) took the builtin's calls on every type (PR #161), and `STRATA_MEMORY=check` listed what compile-time code had made in the same process as the program's (its list now starts with the program). The generator and the SMT translation followed (step 4).
- **Step 3, the prover (built, branch `regex-relations`)**: `std.regex.relations(a, b)` is `(a <= b, b <= a, disjoint)` on the two patterns' languages, explored on the product of their DFAs (pairs of states reachable on the same text while one side is alive, at most 50,000; classes split wherever either DFA's do); `false` when it doesn't hold or can't be decided (a pattern without a DFA: lines, word boundaries, too many states). The prover's `regex_relations` calls it as compile-time code (cached on disk, and per pair in the process), so regex-automata is out of the compiler: it stays only as a dev-dependency, the reference `tests/regex.rs` compares `relations` with on curated pairs and random ones (each pattern, its union with another, `(?:p)+` within `(?:p)*`); 12,000 random pairs agreed: never a relation regex-automata disproves, and its answer whenever both patterns have a DFA. Less than before: a pattern with line anchors (`(?m)^a$`) gets no facts (regex-automata's DFA did them); the prover asks only when one of the two patterns has a large class, and the cross-check still runs.
- **Step 4, the generator (built, branch `regex-generate`)**: `std.regex.samples(pattern, n, seed)` makes `n` texts from the tree: a class picks printable ASCII three times in four when it has some, an unbounded repetition goes up to 8 past its minimum (as `rand_regex`), an alternation picks a branch, assertions make nothing; each text is kept only if the pattern's matcher accepts it, so word boundaries and lines work too (`a\bb`, which matches nothing, is an error). A linear congruential generator in Strata (the same texts for the same seed). The compiled generator's 32 samples per pattern (`regex_samples` in llvm.rs) come from it, as compile-time code (`stdlib::regex_value`, shared with the prover's relations); before, `rand_regex` read patterns without Unicode, so `\p{L}` couldn't be generated and the function was skipped. `tests/regex.rs` checks every sample against `regex` on curated and random patterns (10,000 random agreed), and that only a pattern matching nothing or asserting more than the text's ends has none. Found on the way: a `matches` in a parameter's own refinement (`s: String where matches(it, "…")`) wasn't seen by `with_std` or `literal_patterns` (they walked bodies, not written types), so fuzzing such a function panicked (`no entry found for key`); both now use `decl_all_exprs_mut`, and calling a function missing from the program is an error, not a panic. The SMT translation from our tree followed (next item).
- **Step 4, the SMT translation (built, branch `regex-smt`)**: `std.regex.smt_model(pattern)` is the prover's model, `ExactModel(r)` or `Between(under, over)` for a class past `max_smt_ranges()` (24) ranges, translated from our tree as `hir_to_smt` did from regex-syntax's (anchors only at the text's very start or end, classes clipped at U+2FFFF, `Under` keeping the ranges touching Latin-1 then the widest, `Over` filling the smallest gaps); `smt_exact` for the unit tests. `regex_model` and the tests' `regex_to_smt` call it through `stdlib::regex_value`, so the compiler no longer uses `regex-syntax`, `regex-automata` or `rand_regex`; the interpreter, their last user, was deleted with them (step 5, #192: they are dev-dependencies only). `tests/regex.rs` keeps the old Rust translation as the reference: 12,000 random patterns and the curated ones give the same text. Two differences, both the old one's: wrapped in `^(?:…)$` before parsing, a `(?x)` comment (`#`) swallowed the wrapper and the pattern had no model; and an ASCII class under `(?-u)` was a byte class it refused (the reference now reads both as std.regex does). A cold verify costs about the same; warm, each pattern's cache lookup adds a little (0.8 s on 14 patterns).
- **Steps** (one PR each, each tested against the crate it replaces): (1) parser and syntax tree, and `std.unicode` (built); (2) NFA and Pike VM: `matches` everywhere, run-time patterns in compiled code; (3) the DFA builder, replacing `regex-automata`; (4) the generator and the SMT translation from our tree, replacing `rand_regex` and `regex-syntax`; (5) remove the crates (done with the interpreter: dev-dependencies only). All built.

### Concurrency: structured tasks without coloring (decided with the user 2026-11-22; built: step 1 `scope`, `spawn`, `await` on OS threads, PR #200; step 2 channels and `select`, PR #203; step 3 `race` and cancellation, PR #229; step 4 supervised tasks, PR #235; step 5 `Clock` and timer arms, branch `tasks-clock`)

Settled before `std.net`, since it decides what a socket read looks like. **No coloring**: there is one kind of function, and any function may block (`net.read(…)` is a plain call). Colored `async`/`await` (Rust, JS, C#) marks functions that may suspend, and the mark spreads to every caller, which splits libraries in two; it exists to allow stackless coroutines (a state machine per `async fn`). Without it, a blocking-looking call is already the final API: it runs on OS threads first, on a scheduler with a small stack per task later (Go, Java's virtual threads), and user code doesn't change. Each task is plain sequential code, so `check`, `verify` and the prover keep reasoning one function at a time. The runtime's state is already per thread (#177).

**Syntax: keywords.**

```
fn fetch_both(net: Net) -> (Page, Page) {
  scope {
    a := spawn fetch(net, url1)
    b := spawn fetch(net, url2)
    await (a, b)
  }
}
```

- `scope { … }` is a block expression that ends only after every task spawned in it has finished, awaited or not.
- `spawn e` (or `spawn { … }`) runs `e` in a new task and gives a handle `Task[T]`. It belongs to the nearest enclosing `scope` in the code; a `spawn` outside one is an error, and a handle can't leave its scope (a helper that wants tasks opens its own `scope`). This is what makes borrowing safe without a borrow checker (below).
- `await h` (prefix, like `spawn`, `mut` and `comptime`) gives the task's result; on a tuple of handles a tuple of results (`await (a, b)`, the types may differ), on a list a list (`await [spawn fetch(net, u) for u in urls]`).
- `select { … }` waits on operations that already exist and commits exactly one, written like `match` with `as` naming the result:
  ```
  loop {
    select {
      commands.recv() as c => handle(c),
      await download as d => { show(d); break },
      clock.after(1.seconds) => flush(),
    }
  }
  ```
  An arm is an operation the runtime can commit atomically (`recv`, `send`, `await`, a timer): a losing `recv` took nothing, a losing `send` sent nothing (the value is still yours), a losing `await` leaves its task running. It starts no tasks.
- `race { … }` (built in step 3, with cancellation) has the same arms, but each arm's left side runs as its own task; the first to finish runs its right side and the others are cancelled. A loser may have done part of its work, which is why it is not `select` (Tokio's `select!` is really a race, and its docs need a section on "cancel safety"). `race` is `scope` + `spawn` + `select` on the handles + cancelling the losers, so `select` is the primitive.

**No `Tasks` capability.** `scope`/`spawn`/`await` are deterministic: tasks share nothing mutable and values cross by copy, so `scope { a := spawn f(); b := spawn g(); await (a, b) }` is exactly `(f(), g())`, and to `verify`, `await spawn e` is `e`. Spawning touches nothing outside the program, so there's nothing for a capability to stand for (a function that spawns is, from outside, like one that uses more memory: every task ends before it returns). Timing would go through a `Clock` capability (`clock.after`, `clock.sleep`; not built: std has no clock yet) and I/O through its capabilities, as now. Which `select` arm wins and the order of messages from several senders is nondeterministic: the prover treats every arm as possible, as with a `match` it can't decide. The runtime picks threads (decided: one worker per core by default; built so far: a thread per task); `strata test` is to run tasks on a deterministic scheduler with a seed, try several orders and replay a failing one (as Rust's loom and shuttle do), chosen by the test runner (`RunMode`, #152), not by a parameter (not built: today a task inherits the seeded generators, so `select` and generated values repeat, but threads interleave as the OS runs them). Rejected: `tasks.scope(s -> …)` with `Tasks` as a capability (the value was never used in the scope); Zig's `io.async`/`future.await` on a passed-in `Io` (uncolored too, but methods instead of keywords).

**Channels** are a std type: `(tx, rx) := channel()` is unbuffered, as Go's `make(chan T)` (a `send` waits for a receiver to take the value), and `channel[T](n)` has room for `n` values waiting (sends block only when it's full). The buffer absorbs bursts and lets the producer run ahead of slow or uneven consumers; the number of consumers is a separate choice (any number of tasks can receive from one channel, buffered or not). Every channel is bounded, so a fast producer is slowed to its consumers' pace (backpressure). An unbounded channel (Rust's `mpsc::channel()`, Erlang's mailboxes: `send` never blocks, the queue grows without limit) would get its own name, `unbounded_channel[T]()`, so the missing backpressure shows in the code; not planned until something needs it. `tx.send(v)` moves `v` in and blocks while the buffer is full; `rx.recv()` gives `Option[T]`, `None` once closed and empty; `for v in rx` is an `Iterator` that ends at close. Endpoints are runtime handles with their own synchronization (the borrowing rules for data don't apply), and can be copied: several senders, several receivers. **`close` is explicit** (`tx.close()`): each copy of a sender closes its own, the channel ends when every copy is closed, and the checker requires a sender to be closed before its scope ends (decided: on every path; built: somewhere in the block and in each `spawn`, see step 2). Rejected: closing at the last sender's last use (invisible in the code: mentioning `tx` once more would hang the program).

**How values cross tasks.** The meaning is fixed: a capture is a copy at `spawn` time, and there are no shared references, so how the copy is made is the implementation's choice and can change without changing a program. What the implementation must keep is "a count of 1 means nothing else can see it" (see "Memory: reference counting with reuse"): the data is immutable, but the count word isn't, so two threads may not change the same count with plain loads and stores.
- **Built (step 1): counts are atomic while a task is alive.** The runtime sets a flag before the first task's thread starts and clears it after the last one is joined (never while another thread could read it); `strata_dup`, `strata_release`, `strata_unique` and the allocation counters use atomic operations while it is set, plain ones otherwise. A spawned closure holds its captures (counted by the parent before the thread starts), so they outlive every use in the task; a count of 1 still means one holder, on one thread. Programs that never spawn pay one well-predicted branch per count change; code running while tasks are alive pays the atomics. Checked with a stress test (16 tasks dup-ing and dropping the same captured strings, 19 million objects, `STRATA_MEMORY=check`): clean on 20 of 20 runs, and with the counts forced non-atomic it crashes on 2 of 5.
- **The borrowing plan had a hole** (found while building step 1): "children borrow, never count; the scope holds one reference" assumed the parent leaves those objects' counts alone, but the parent keeps running during the scope and `dup`s and `drop`s the same objects (passing `xs` to a call, dropping a temporary), racing with nothing in the children only if they never count; children that keep or pass on a part must count too. Borrowing needs the shared objects to be frozen for the scope (no thread counts them: Lean 4's "marked shared" objects, or our constants' count of 0), which takes a walk of what was captured, or a copy of what escapes. Next, measured against step 1's atomic counts: mark what a `spawn` captures as shared (a walk at `spawn`; dup/drop of a marked object counted atomically, everything else plain), or freeze it for the scope; per-task tables of count changes (biased reference counting) stay a later option.
- **A task's result comes back by move; a `send` moves** when the value is uniquely owned all the way down and copies otherwise (as Erlang copies messages), or, with atomic counts, simply moves. None of these choices changes what a program means.

**`mut` across a spawn** can't happen: `spawn` takes an expression whose captures are copies, and closures can't change outer variables, so `spawn f(mut x)` is an error. A `mut` argument passed by reference (see "Mutation at the call site") stays safe inside a task: it lives on one task's stack, which no other task reaches, even while it blocks. Results come back as values (`x = await h`). Not planned unless needed: lending a `mut` to exactly one child of a scope.

**Step 7 as built** (branch `tasks-blocked`): the runtime knows when every thread of a run waits: deadlock detection, and virtual time.
- **Runs and waiters** (runtime.c): a run is the program, or one `strata test` / fuzz case (`strata_test` gives its thread a `Run` of its own); a task belongs to its spawner's run. Each thread has a `Waiter` in its run's list from its `spawn` (so a task not started yet counts as not waiting) to its end. A Strata wait says so under `chan_lock` (`wait_begin`: what it waits in, whether it ends by itself, a fake clock's deadline) and stops saying so on waking (`wait_end`). Every wake-up of `chan_cond` goes through `wake_all`, which moves `wake_gen` on: a waiter counts as waiting only if it began after the last wake-up, so a thread woken but not running again yet never makes a false deadlock. The waits: `recv`, `send` (full, or unbuffered until taken), `select`, `await`, the end of a `scope` (now a `chan_cond` wait until its tasks are done, then the joins), a fake clock's sleep and `after` arm. A real clock's sleep or `after`, and `select` asking a user-written clock again, are timed: they end by themselves. Host I/O isn't a wait (a thread in it can go on by itself).
- **Stuck** (`stuck`, run by the thread about to wait): every waiter of its run waits since the last wake-up and none is timed. Then a **virtual clock** waited on moves to its nearest deadline (the smallest time left among the waiters on virtual clocks) and wakes everyone; with none, it is a **deadlock**: a panic naming each wait and its stack ("deadlock: every task is waiting, so none can go on: main waits in the end of a `scope`, at main; a task waits in `recv`, at main -> spawn -> Receiver.recv; …"). In the program it ends it at once (exit 101; even inside a supervised task: a deadlock is the run's, not a task's). In a test it fails the test: recorded as the test's panic, every task of the run cancelled (they stop at their next wait), and `strata_test` reports it once the test's thread has left.
- **`VirtualClock`** (std.time; the user's choice on review of #258, instead of a `virtual_clock()` constructor): a capability, like `SystemClock`, that `main` and tests ask for by name (`test "times out" (clock: VirtualClock) { … }`), implementing `Clock`. It holds nothing: its time is its run's (`Run.vtime`, made at the first use, at 0, freed with a test's run), so each test has its own and its tasks share it; `now`/`sleep` are the builtins `host_virtual_now`/`host_virtual_sleep`, and its `after` arm is a timer (kind 5) on the run's time (`strata_virtual_timeline`).
- **No `FakeClock` in std** (the user's decision on review of #258): `VirtualClock` covers time that passes (timeouts, sleeps, timers, exactly and at once), and a test that needs a time standing still writes its own mock of `Clock` (`mock StoppedClock = { at: Instant }` with `now` returning it: docs/language.md). A clock the test moves by hand was rarely needed, so it was dropped, with what only it used: the `FakeClock` mock (briefly made a mock in this PR, `FakeClock {}`), `advance` / `set`, std.time's private `Timeline` type, the `host_fake_*` builtins, the counted runtime object (`runtime_object` in llvm.rs), and its tests (`std_fake_clocks_move_when_told`, `a_program_has_its_own_timeline`). The runtime's `Timeline` struct is now only a run's virtual time; its waking of sleepers and the fifth `select` arm kind stay, for `VirtualClock`.
- Cost: a wait looks at every waiter of its run once (O(threads) per blocking wait; only when it blocks), and each wake-up is one increment more. Nothing changes for code that doesn't wait.
- Tests: `waits_are_known` (a two-task deadlock reported natively and on the JIT; a real sleep and a long computation not reported; virtual time (`main` asking for a `VirtualClock`) settling a `race` timeout, three sleeps in deadline order and an `after` arm, everything freed, native and JIT alike) and `a_deadlocked_test_fails` (two deadlocked tests fail with the message; tests asking for a `VirtualClock` settle a timeout and each start at 0, all in one run).
- Not yet: the O(threads) look per blocking wait (a count would do for deadlock, the list is kept for the message); a deterministic scheduler for tests (trying several orders) can build on the same bookkeeping.

**Cancelling a wait for input, as built** (branch `io-cancel`): a task waiting in a host read now sees its cancellation, as at a `recv`.
- **Mechanism** (runtime.c, "waiting for input, cancellably"): a thread that can be cancelled (a task's, or a test's) waits for its descriptor with `poll`, together with a wake pipe of its own (made at its first such wait, closed when its task ends). While it waits it is on a list of readers (under `chan_lock`); every cancellation (`cancel_task`: a `race`'s losers, a cancelled ancestor; `record_panic`: a panic in a test or a supervised task) writes a byte to each reader's pipe, and the reader looks again: if it is cancelled, the read gives up and the compiled code leaves through `cancel_point` (checked once the read's value is held, so leaving drops it). Only threads in such a wait are woken; the main thread outside a test reads as before (nothing can cancel it), and code that doesn't wait for input pays nothing. `poll` and `pipe` are POSIX (macOS and Linux alike).
- **Covered**: `read_line` (stdin; `std.io.Terminal`). In a task it reads a character at a time from stdin's buffer and waits, cancellably, before each refill (stdio's buffer is looked into: `_r` on the BSDs and macOS, `_IO_read_ptr` on glibc; elsewhere a line already buffered waits for more input first). stdin isn't locked across the wait (another task reading it would wait on the lock, uncancellably). A cancelled `read_line` drops what it had read of its line. Also fixed: the old `read_line` leaked its line's C buffer.
- **Not covered, and why**: reading a file that is a FIFO or a device (`read_file`, `read_bytes`: `open` itself waits for a writer, and opening without waiting changes what reading an empty FIFO means; these belong to handles, like sockets, when files get them); writes that wait (`print` to a full pipe, `write_file` to a FIFO: the same mechanism with `POLLOUT` would cover them; rare enough to wait for std.net's writes); `extern c` calls (foreign code waits as it likes; a blocked call holds its thread, see "Open" below). `sleep` already was cancellable (it waits on `chan_lock`'s condition).
- **std.net, later** (for the user to confirm): every socket is non-blocking; `read`, `write`, `connect` and `accept` wait in `wait_readable` / a `wait_writable` (`POLLIN` / `POLLOUT` and the wake pipe), then do the call, which can't wait. A cancelled `read` has consumed nothing (it only reads once data is there). **A cancelled `write` or `connect` leaves the connection broken**: bytes already handed to the kernel are sent and the rest aren't, so the peer sees part of a message; the runtime marks the socket broken (later operations on any copy give `Err`) and the task that was cancelled drops it, closing it when it was the last holder. Rule proposed: no partial result is ever reported to the cancelled task (it is leaving), and a socket an operation was cancelled on is never reused.
- Tested by `reads_are_cancelled` (build tests): a `race` whose loser waits in `read_line` on a stdin held open, 20 times, and a supervised task's reader stopped when its sibling panics, end at once (before: they waited until stdin closed); a task's `read_line` reads the lines given, the second and third from what the first buffered; natively and on the JIT, everything freed.

**Step 6 as built** (branch `fake-clock`): std's `FakeClock`. **Removed in step 7** (#258, the user's decision: `VirtualClock` and tests' own mocks of `Clock` replace it); kept here as the record of what was built.
- `std.time.FakeClock = { time: Timeline }`, made by `fake_clock()` (at 0, user-confirmed; since step 7 a mock made with `FakeClock {}`); `advance(d)` (forward only) and `set(t)` (an earlier time does nothing, user-confirmed); `impl Clock` (`now`, `sleep`).
- **`Timeline` is private to std.time** (user's requirement, review of #245): declared there as `extern c type Timeline` (not `pub`: `extern c` items never are), so it is `std.time.Timeline`, can't be named or imported outside (`use std.time.Timeline`: "private to module"), and a program may define its own `Timeline`. Only std's `host_fake_*` builtins (src/stdlib.rs, internal to std) make or move one. Codegen treats this one std extern type as a **runtime object** (`Module::runtime_object`): counted like a value and freed by `strata_timeline_drop`, unlike C's handles, which are never counted. Copies share it, so a task can move the time for the others.
- **Not fully hidden yet**: records have no private fields, so `FakeClock`'s interface shows `time: Timeline`, and a program can read `clock.time` (a value it can't name or use) or build `FakeClock { time: other.time }` (a copy sharing the same time: harmless). Hiding it fully needs a new mechanism: an **opaque public type** (`pub` name, private body: outside its module no fields and no constructor, only its functions), which would also suit other std types holding runtime state. Not built.
- **No polling**: the time is read and moved under `chan_lock`, and moving it wakes every waiter (`pthread_cond_broadcast`): a `sleep` waits on `chan_cond` until the time reaches its deadline (or the task is cancelled), and a `select`'s `after` arm of a `FakeClock` is a fifth kind of arm (`target` the `Timeline`, `slot` the deadline in its time) that the select looks at whenever it wakes. Other user-written clocks keep #234's path (asked again about every millisecond).
- Tests: `a_program_has_its_own_timeline` (a program's `Timeline` next to `fake_clock()`; std's can't be named, imported or made), and `std_fake_clocks_move_when_told` (an hour's sleep, a `race` used as a timeout and an `after` arm, each ended by another task moving the time; under 20 s real time; everything freed; native and JIT alike, 20 native runs identical).
- **Virtual time: built in step 7** (above; the design below is what was built, with the scope's end and `await` as `chan_cond` waits). (The time jumps to the next deadline once every task of the run waits.) It needs the runtime to know that every thread of the run is blocked, and on what: today a thread can block in a channel wait (known), a scope's end (`pthread_join`), `await`, or blocking host I/O (not known), so a count of "threads waiting on `chan_cond`" would advance the time while a thread is merely reading a file. Design: count the run's live threads (main + tasks: `live_tasks` already counts tasks) and the ones waiting in a wait that only another task or the clock can end (channel waits, `select`, `await` turned into a `chan_cond` wait, a scope's end turned into one too, `sleep` / `after` of a fake clock); when the two are equal and some waiter is on a fake clock, move that clock to the earliest deadline and wake everyone; when they are equal and none is, every task waits on another: a **deadlock**, reported as a panic instead of a silent hang. Host I/O counts as running. That is the same machinery as deadlock detection, so they are one step.

**Step 5 as built** (branch `tasks-clock`): a `Clock` capability and timer arms.
- `std.time`: `capability trait Clock { now(self) -> Instant; sleep(self, d: Duration) }`, `capability SystemClock` (the system's monotonic clock; `main` and tests ask for it by name, as for `Disk` / `Process`), `distinct type Duration = Int` (nanoseconds; `nanos`, `micros`, `ms`, `seconds`; `.value`), `distinct type Instant = Int` (from the clock's own start), `elapsed(start, end)`. Native operations `host_now` and `host_sleep` (runtime `strata_host_now`, `strata_host_sleep`: `clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC)`).
- **Sleeping is a wait**: under `chan_lock`, on `chan_cond` with a deadline (`pthread_cond_timedwait`, which takes the wall clock: the deadline is recomputed from the monotonic one), so a cancellation wakes it and it gives up; the call is followed by `cancel_point`. `race { work() as r => Some(r), clock.sleep(ms(100)) => None }` is a timeout.
- **`clock.after(d)` in a `select`** (and so in a `race`'s arms): `after` follows the clock it is called on (user's requirement, 2026-11-22). Its deadline is `clock.now() + d`, in that clock's time, taken as the `select` begins. The system clock's (the clock's type in the compiled instance is `SystemClock`; a function taking a `Clock` is compiled per implementation, so passing the system clock as a `Clock` counts) is a timer the runtime waits for itself (arm kind 3 in `strata_select`: its absolute monotonic deadline; the wait is `pthread_cond_timedwait` to the first deadline). Another clock's (a fake, a mock) is asked again: the compiled code calls its `now` and tells `strata_select` whether the deadline has passed (kind 4); when no arm can happen, `strata_select` waits at most 1 ms and gives -2, and the code asks the clock again (slower, but right for any clock). The arm names nothing (`as` is an error), `d` is a `Duration`, and the receiver must implement `Clock`.
- Tests can now use a fake clock they control: any value implementing `Clock` drives both `sleep` (its own method) and `after` arms (see `fake_clocks_drive_after`: a clock that stands still, one 1000 times faster). Still open, after **step 6** below: a **virtual clock** whose time advances only when every task waits (deterministic timeouts without real waiting: it needs the runtime to know when all of a test's tasks are blocked, close to deadlock detection); tests get the real `SystemClock` when they ask for one. Also open: dates and wall-clock time (`std.time` has only the monotonic clock); arithmetic on `Duration` (it is `distinct`: write `ms(a.value + b.value)` or a helper).
- Tests: `clocks_time_out` (a `race` as a timeout whose loser waits on a channel deep in a call, a five-second sleep cancelled by a quicker arm, a `select` with an `after` arm, 20 timeouts in a loop; everything freed, native and JIT alike) and `clock_arms_are_checked`. Also `fake_clocks_drive_after`.

**Step 4 as built** (branch `tasks-supervised`): supervised tasks (see "Failures").
- **Syntax** (user's choice, 2026-11-22): `supervised spawn e`; `supervised` is a contextual keyword, only right before `spawn` on the same line (a name anywhere else), and per arm in a `race`: `race { supervised fetch(a) as r => …, … }` (held as `Expr::SupervisedSpawn`, binary tag 39, in `compiler/ast` too). Failure is handled on the `await`, not the spawn: `await h` of a supervised handle is a `Result[T, Panic]`, so `await h except p { cached(url) }` is plain `except`; a handler after `await h` is the awaited result's (`(await h) except …`, `(await h)!`), both parsers stopping the operand's postfix chain at a handler; the printer writes `await (a!)` for a handler inside. Rejected: `spawn supervised e` (the first try), a separate verb (`isolate e`), `spawn? e`, and `except` on the spawn (`spawn e except p { … }`: the handler belongs where the result is used). A supervised *scope* was the first idea; per task is finer, and a scope of supervised tasks is just that.
- **Semantics**: `Panic = { kind, message, stack }` (prelude). A supervised task is a boundary, like a test (`Contained` in runtime.c): its first panic is recorded, everything below it is cancelled (stops at its next wait), its sender copies close, and its result is `Err(Panic { … })` (built by a generated `task.sfail` function from the runtime's strings; `task.srun` wraps a normal result in `Ok`). A panic in an ordinary task below it is the supervised task's. Under `strata test`, a supervised task's panic is its result, as anywhere; an ordinary task's still fails the test.
- **Memory**: the region per task (option (b)) turned out unnecessary for compiled code and unsound alone: freeing a dead task's region would leave every count it took on objects outside (its captures, what it received) too high, so it needs full copying at the edges (captures in, sends and results out: Erlang's process heaps, with a copy function per type). Instead, a panic in compiled code unwinds the same way a cancelled task does: inside a supervised task `strata_panic` records the panic and returns, and the function leaves through its `unwound` block (dropping what its scopes hold; callers follow through `cancel_point`). So a dying task frees what it held, measured with `STRATA_MEMORY=check` (50 panics deep in calls, each frame holding lists: all freed). std's precompiled code is compiled with these checks too (the library is built as a program with tasks), so std called from a supervised task unwinds as well. **Panics found by the runtime's builtins return too** (built, branch `panics-unwind`): a division or remainder by zero, a negative or huge shift, a float that isn't finite, `floor`/`ceil`/`round` of one, an `Int` too large for a `Float`, `sqrt` of a negative number, `ord`, `chr`, `byte_at`, a string index out of range, `split`/`replace`/`repeat`'s bad arguments, a channel misused (`send` after the end, `close` twice, a negative buffer), a C function's NULL string. Inside a supervised task the builtin records the panic (`soft_fault` in runtime.c) and returns a harmless value (0, a constant empty string), and the compiled code checks right after the call (`after_builtin`: the `cancel_point` check, only in programs with a `supervised spawn` and in std's library, so other programs compile as before) and leaves through the `unwound` block, dropping what it held. Outside a supervised task the same panic ends the program at once, as before. Cost: none measured (150 million divisions and remainders: 0.62 s with the checks and without). The runtime's panic messages now consume their pieces (they leaked, harmlessly while a panic always ended the program). Tested: `supervised_tasks_keep_running` (each of those panics deep in calls, every frame holding lists, 50 times: everything freed, native and JIT alike). **Left as a jump** (`siglongjmp` to the task's trap: the task still dies alone with the right `Err`, but what its frames held stays allocated, tested as known): a stack overflow (detected in a signal handler on the guard page: there is no returning into the overflowing frame), running out of memory (an allocation that fails has no harmless value to give, and the code after it would need a check on every allocation), the step budget (`Limit`, tests and fuzzing only), and a panic in code the compiler generates without a way out (drop and show helpers: they continue with the harmless value until the next check). The fix for the first two is the memory budget below: a supervised task allocating from its own budget can be stopped before it runs out (a check at allocation, which already counts), and a deep recursion has a known stack per task.
- Not yet: memory budgets per supervised task (out of memory as that task's panic: needs per-task allocation counting, which the region would give), restart helpers in std (one-for-one, all-for-one, a restart budget: ordinary code over `supervised spawn`).

**Step 3 as built** (branch `tasks-race`): `race` and cancellation; a panic in a task ends the program (user's choices, 2026-11-22: a cancelled task stops at its next wait; a panic anywhere ends the whole program, see "Failures").
- `Expr::Race(Vec<SelectArm>)` (binary tag 38, in `compiler/ast` too; the evaluator refuses it, as `select`): `race { e as x => body, … }`, each `e` checked as a `spawn`'s body (in a scope of the race's own), the bodies as `select`'s arms. Codegen: a task scope, each `e` spawned into it, `strata_select` with an `await` arm per task, `strata_scope_cancel` on the others, the winner's result moved out of its task, the scope's end (waiting for the losers to stop), then the winner's arm. The prover treats it as an unknown value.
- **Cancellation**: a task has a `cancel` flag and its parent; it is cancelled when it or a task above it is. Waits (`send`, `recv`, `select`, `await`) give up when cancelled (`send` drops its value, `recv` takes nothing, `select` does nothing and drops its sends' values: -1, `await` gives NULL). The compiled code then leaves each function as `return` does (`cancel_point` in llvm.rs: after each call that may wait (since the stream cancel-points, only in a program whose tasks can be cancelled, and only after calls `waits_of` finds may reach a wait: see the speed section), and after each wait, a load of the global `strata_cancel_any` (cancellations pending) and, only when it isn't 0, a call to `strata_cancelled()`; the way out drops the scopes and returns a zero value through an `unwound` block, which callers check for before using it). A program without `spawn` or `race` gets no checks; with them, fib(35) runs in the same time (the branch is predicted). A cancelled task's result is never dropped (it has none), and its unclosed sender copies are closed without a panic.
- **A panic in a task ends the program** at once (printed with the stack from `main` through each `spawn`: `strata_spawn` keeps the spawning thread's stack), instead of being raised at `await`. Inside `strata_test` (a test, a fuzz case) it fails only that run: the run's threads all count as cancelled (`Contained`), so the test's thread stops at its next wait and the end of the task's scope raises the panic there; a panic in the test's own thread cancels its tasks before waiting for them.
- Checked with `races_cancel_the_losers` (losers stopped deep in calls at a `recv`, at a `send` to a full channel, at an `await` of their own task, in a `select`, in a race of their own, 100 races in a loop: every object freed, 40 runs alike) and `a_task_panic_fails_only_its_test`.
- Not yet: cancelling at blocking I/O (built for `read_line`: see "Cancelling a wait for input"), supervised scopes (see "Failures"), `compiler/check` doesn't look inside `select` / `race` (its `_` arm). Precompiled std code calling a closure that is cancelled doesn't check (std's non-generic functions are compiled without checks); generic ones, compiled per program, do.

**Step 2 as built** (branch `tasks-channels`): channels and `select`.
- `channel()` / `channel(n)` (builtins; the element type is inferred: calls take no type arguments, so not `channel[T](n)`), `Sender[T]` / `Receiver[T]` (built-in types), and their methods in the prelude (`Sending`: `send`, `close`; `Receiving`: `recv`; `Iterator` for `Receiver`, so `for v in rx`), over the builtins `channel_send`, `channel_recv`, `channel_close`. Not `select` a keyword: `select {` starts one, so std.web's `select(…)` stays a call.
- **A copy of a sender is one per task** (user's choice, 2026-11-22): the task that made the channel holds one, and so does each `spawn` that captures the sender (counted at `spawn`, only if the spawning task still holds its own). Each closes its own; the channel ends when all have. The checker requires `tx.close()` in the block that makes the channel and in each `spawn` that captures it (anywhere in it: not yet on every path), and refuses a `spawn` capturing a sender inside another value. At run time: a task ending with a copy still open has it closed for it and faults (`a task ended without closing its copy of a sender`); `close` of a copy the task doesn't hold (or twice) faults; `send` on an ended channel faults.
- `Expr::Select(Vec<SelectArm>)` (binary tag 37; `SelectArm { comments, op, name, body }`, in `compiler/ast` too; the evaluator refuses it). Arms: `rx.recv() as x` (`Option`), `tx.send(v)` (no name), `await h as r`. Codegen describes each arm to `strata_select` (kind, channel or task, slot), which commits the first that can happen, looking from a different arm each time (from the thread's generator: no starvation, and a run repeats); a send's value is dropped by the arm that won if it wasn't its own. The prover treats a `select` as an unknown value.
- Runtime: a channel is one counted object (both ends point to it; its last holder drops what is still in it); every channel and task end is under one lock with one condition variable (simple and correct, slow under contention: a lock per channel later). An unbuffered `send` waits until its value is taken; in a `select`, an unbuffered send arm is ready when a receiver is waiting.
- `scope { …; spawn e }` is `()` (its last task's handle isn't its value), since writing that came up at once.
- Not yet: timer arms (`clock.after(…)`: no `Clock` capability exists yet), detecting a deadlock (built in step 7), `close` on every path in the checker, a lock per channel.

**Step 1 as built** (branch `tasks-scope`): `Expr::Scope`, `Expr::Spawn`, `Expr::Await` (binary tags 34-36, in `compiler/ast` too). `spawn` takes the whole expression after it, as `return` does (`spawn len(xs) + fib(20)` is one task); `await` is prefix and takes what follows like `-` or `!`, so `await hs.fold(…)` awaits the fold's result: write `(await hs).fold(…)`. A scope whose last expression is a `spawn` gives back a handle, which is an error (end it with the value wanted). The checker: `Task[T]` (not writable in types yet); `spawn` outside a `scope` is an error, and so are `return`, `!` and `?` inside one, a closure or `spawn` capturing a handle, a handle in a scope's result, in a task's result, or assigned to a variable declared outside the scope. A lambda inside a `scope` may spawn into it (a comprehension's body is a lambda: `await [spawn f(x) for x in xs]`); a lambda that spawns can't leave the scope while its type mentions the handle, and the runtime faults if one is called after its scope ended (a generic function can hide the handle's type: a gap until `Task` is tracked through generics). The prover: `spawn e`, `await h` and `scope b` are `e`, `h` and `b`. Codegen: the spawned expression is a closure of no parameters; `strata_spawn` (closure, a `task.run` function that calls it and stores the result, a `task.drop` function for the result) starts a thread (64 MB stack) for the thread's innermost open scope; `strata_await` joins and gives a pointer to the result, which the task keeps (so it can be awaited twice) until `strata_scope_end`, which every way out of the scope calls (`return` and `break` too), joins every task, drops results and closures, then raises the first task's fault. A fault in a task (its own trap) is raised by `await` or the end of the scope, with the call stack `main -> spawn -> pick`; caught by a trap (a test), every open scope's tasks finish first. Tasks inherit the generators' state (split per spawn, so a run repeats), output capture (a test's), and the step budget; `print` locks stdout per line. Not yet (after step 3): the shared-marking optimisation above, `Task` in written types.

Open: blocking `extern c` calls on a scheduler (a blocked call holds a whole OS thread: Go hands that thread's other tasks to a new one); stack size and growth per task.

### Error handling, strings and mutation (decided)
- **Done**: `except` blocks (`e except { fallback }`, `e except err { ... }`, the fallback may `return`), `!` / `?` propagation, early `return`, f-strings, and string primitives (`chars`, `index_of`, `replace`, `lines`, `repeat`, `reverse`, case, `is_digit` / `is_alpha` / `is_space`, `ord` / `chr`). Strings are not indexed themselves: a unit is named (`s.bytes()`, `s.code_points()`, `s.graphemes()`), and positions are byte offsets (`byte_slice`). See docs/language.md, "Errors".
- **Mutation of a `mut` value happens in place**, with no copying, whenever nothing else holds the value. The hard part is knowing when that's the case: use the absence of shared references plus ownership or reference counting (as in Koka's Perceus) so updates through a mutable binding are real in-place updates.
- **Errors that say what was probably meant** (the user, 2026-11-21: as Rust's): an error names the problem in the program's terms, then `  help:` lines with what to write instead (`other: Shape` for a `Self` argument on a trait value). New errors should do this; older ones could be gone through.
- **Memory: reference counting with reuse, as in Koka's Perceus and Lean 4** (decided with the user, 2026-11-21; was: Boehm to start). Values never change once built and there are no shared references, so an object only points to older ones: the heap has no cycles and counting frees everything, with no cycle collector. The compiler inserts `dup` (one more owner: count + 1) and `drop` (one owner fewer: count - 1, freed with its contents at 0), moving a value at its last use instead of dup-ing it, and leaving read-only parameters borrowed (the caller keeps ownership; no counting in the callee). An update (`x.f = e`, a `mut` parameter, `push`, `insert`) happens in place when the count is 1 and copies first otherwise (copy-on-write), so copy-in/copy-out stays the meaning and in place is only the implementation. A count of 1 really means nothing else can see the value; while tasks run, counts change atomically to keep it so (see "Concurrency: structured tasks without coloring"). Rejected: Boehm (an outside library, and no in-place updates), precise tracing (stack maps from LLVM, and no in-place updates either). Steps: (1, built, branch `gc`) a count word before every object (so layouts are unchanged), constants with a count of 0 that are never freed (string literals, values of constructors without fields, and, since branch `regex-parse`, list and tuple literals and constructors (records, variants, `Some`, `Ok`) whose elements are all constants (a small `Int`, a `Float` or `Bool` literal, a string, another constant): one global each, equal ones shared, so a table of 1,700 strings is data instead of 1,700 stores, which took LLVM 3 to 8 s to compile; a push or an update copies them, as for any value held elsewhere), the runtime's `strata_dup` / `strata_release` / `strata_free` / `strata_unique`, and `STRATA_MEMORY` to report allocations at exit; (2, built, branch `gc-drop`) a drop function per type (`drop_fn`; a closure block carries its own, a trait value's table has drop and dup entries; the last field is dropped as a tail call, so a million-long chain doesn't overflow the stack), and counting in the code generator: functions borrow their arguments and return what they own, reads borrow, a stored value is moved (a temporary) or held once more, and every scope (block, branch, statement, loop iteration) drops its temporaries and variables, `return` / `break` / `continue` what they leave; a failed pattern drops what it bound; a `mut` argument always goes through a slot of its own (copy in, copy out); an assigned variable's old value is dropped at the end of the statement. `STRATA_MEMORY=check` keeps freed memory aside and stops on any use of it, and the build tests check every program frees everything (a loop of 28 million allocations: 1.4 GB before, 33 MB after). An operand that only borrows is held until the statement ends when a later operand may assign a variable (`f(x, { x = g(); 1 })`: `operands`). Count updates are inline (user, 2026-11-22; branch `rc-inline`): each module defines `strata.dup` / `strata.release` (`alwaysinline`), which add to or take from the count word themselves and call `strata_dup` / `strata_release` only when the runtime's `strata_slow_counts` is set (tasks running: atomic counts; `STRATA_MEMORY=check`); about 10% faster on an allocation-heavy loop, in the JIT and in executables. **The runtime is merged into executables only** (user, 2026-11-22): `strata build` links the program and `runtime.c` with `-flto`, so LLVM inlines the runtime's small functions into the program (about 5% more; without LTO in the linker, they're linked apart); the JIT keeps the runtime separate: it is precompiled into `strata`, and the JIT compiles only the program, its calls going to the runtime's functions in the process. **Values on the stack** (from the user's question, 2026-11-22: counts known while compiling; first part built, branch `rc-static`): measured first, on a loop making a record it only reads: skipping the count test saves a few instructions, but keeping the record off the heap was 3 to 4 times faster (no `malloc` / `free`), so that came first. `llvm/escape.rs`: functions borrow their arguments, so a function keeps a parameter only if its body lets it escape (`Kept`: per function and parameter, found for the whole program together, recursion alone keeping nothing). A variable escapes unless every use borrows it: a field read, an `is` test, a comparison, a `match` binding no name to the whole value, an argument to a parameter not kept; anything else (a copy, a method or trait call, a built-in, an f-string, a closure or task mentioning it, `mut`, a block's or branch's value) counts as escaping. `x := C { … }` (a record or variant with fields, not boxed in a union) whose `x` doesn't escape the rest of its block is built in a frame slot (`stack_block`) with a count of 0, like a constant, so any `dup` / `release` of it does nothing; where `x` is dropped (the block's end, `return`, `break`, `continue`), only its fields are (`stack_slots`). On that loop: 1.8 s to 0.5 s. Not yet: the uses above treated as escaping (built-ins that borrow, methods with a known receiver, f-strings), a constructor passed straight as an argument (`f(P { … })`), tuples, values made by a callee for its caller (it would need the caller's slot), freeing without the count test, and reuse in place. **Fields assigned in place** (branch `rc-escape`, 2026-11-22; step 3's first part): found while looking for what kept values off the stack: the compiler package had about a dozen candidates, while real code updates records all the time (`i.next += 1`, `i.errs = push(i.errs, m)`), and every such assignment copied the whole record. Now `x.f = v` on a record (one constructor, no refinement of its own) or a tuple, `x` not annotated, tests `strata_unique`: a count of 1 writes the field in place and drops its old value; otherwise (a copy in another variable, a list holding it, an operand reading it while a later one assigns, a task's capture, a constant, a value on the stack: count 0) it copies, as before. A 10-field record updated twice per iteration, 20 million times: 0.82 s to 0.13 s, 4 allocations instead of 40 million. A `mut` argument that is a whole variable no other argument of the call mentions is moved into the call (`Place`'s third part; the variable holds null meanwhile, which drops accept, and gets the result back without a drop), so the callee's assignments are in place too: the same loop through `fn step(mut s: State)`: 3.6 s to 0.8 s, 4 allocations instead of 40 million. Otherwise the call holds it once more, as before (copy in, copy out). Measured on the compiler package's type checker (`check` on its own modules, state in `mut i: Inf`): 4% fewer allocations (16.6 to 15.9 million) and no faster: its state's fields are dictionaries and lists, and `i.subst = insert(i.subst, k, v)` copies the whole dictionary (a sorted list) whatever happens to the record. **Insertions in place** (branch `rc-consume`, 2026-11-22): `x = insert(x, …)` / `x.f = insert(x.f, …)` (or `x.insert(…)`) on a dictionary or set, the values not mentioning `x`, leaves the old value dead, so when its list and buffer are held once (`strata_list_owned`; for a field, the record too) the element is put in place: the position from the prelude's `dict_slot` / `set_slot` (a binary search), then `strata_list_put` (a key already there: the old pair dropped) or `strata_list_insert` (shifting the rest, the buffer doubled when full); otherwise `insert` copies as before. 200,000 insertions into a dictionary: 70.9 s to 0.69 s (each copied the whole dictionary). The compiler package's checker: 6% fewer allocations, about 10% faster; most of its work is decoding module files and building trees. `remove` too (branch `rc-more`): `x = remove(x, k)` takes the element (or the key's pair) out in place (`strata_list_remove_at`), was a `filter` copying the rest. And a constructor passed straight to a function that doesn't keep that parameter, of the constructor's own type (`norm(P { … })`), goes on the stack, dropped with the call's other temporaries (`stack_temps`). Measured and left out (2026-11-23): freeing without the count test where the count is known (1.2% fewer instructions, no fewer cycles, on a loop making and dropping a record: the time is `malloc` and `free`, about 590 instructions an iteration, which the stack avoids altogether); a cache of std's decoded modules across the files of one `check` (about 20 million instructions per file, against about 760 million to check one of the compiler's modules: the checker is the cost). There is no operation replacing a list's element (no `set(xs, i, v)`; `xs[i] = v` isn't an assignment the language has), so none to do in place. Not yet: `push` (amortized already: a 24-byte header per call), fields deeper than one level. Not yet: paths of more than one field (`x.a.b = v`: in place only while every level is held once), sums with several variants, a `mut` field (`mut x.f`). **A small-object allocator** (branch `rc-alloc`, 2026-11-23): profiling the compiler package's checker (`compiler check`, self time by kind) put about 65% in memory management (`malloc`, `free`, zeroing, drop functions), 17% in the call stack kept for fault messages, 14% in SHA-256 (definitions hashed again when read; fixed-width types, being added by another session, make that a few instructions a step), the checker's own code a small part. Objects of at most 256 bytes with their count word (16-byte classes) now come from free lists carved from 64 KB chunks of one reserved range of addresses (`small_alloc`); `strata_free` knows them by address and each chunk's class from a table. The lists are shared by every thread behind a spinlock (one atomic exchange, uncontended unless tasks allocate at once). Bigger objects, and every object in `STRATA_MEMORY=check` (which keeps freed objects to catch uses), are malloc's. The compiler's checker on `check.strata`: 37% fewer instructions, 38% fewer cycles; a record-and-list benchmark: 2.1 times fewer cycles. Memory is never given back to the system (as most allocators of this kind). (3, rest not built) reuse when unique, replacing `push`'s in-place write past the end (the `used` trick), which can make a cycle through a recursive type (`Node(xs)` pushed into `xs`); (4) later: iterative drop for long chains, borrow inference, stack allocation of values that don't escape, big integers in `Int` slots, shared (marked) counts for what tasks capture (atomic counts while tasks run are built: see "Concurrency"). No `copy` / `clone` built-in (user, 2026-11-21): `ys := xs` already is a copy, so `copy(xs)` would only move when the work happens, and would suggest that plain assignment aliases. The runtime part (alloc, free, inc, dec) is small and native; most of the work is in the compiler, so it moves to Strata with it when self-hosting.
- **Self-hosting is a long-term goal**, after the language matures. The compiler would be written in Strata, target LLVM, and use the LLVM libraries.

### Failures: no exceptions, panics end the program, assurance levels (decided with the user 2026-11-22; not built)

Errors are values (`Result`, `Option`), passed and matched; nothing is thrown and caught, so no line of code can be left halfway by something it didn't ask for. What is left when an assumption breaks is a **panic**, which is not an exception: it can't be caught or handled, and it ends the whole program at once, whichever task or thread it happens in (not just the task and its siblings). A test that panics fails, and the other tests run. Today's "faults" become panics, and each kind is meant to shrink:
- **Contracts and refinements** (`requires`, `ensures`, refined types, `assert`): proven by `verify` where it can. An unproven one stays as a run-time check, and if it fails the program panics: an assumption we couldn't prove broke, so carrying on is wrong; the panic says which and where, so the cause can be found.
- **Operations that only work on some inputs** (`xs[i]`, `/`, overflow, `sqrt`, `ord`): give them preconditions the prover discharges at each use, as contracts. To do: measure what proving them everywhere costs `verify` (std and the examples); if it is cheap, they become preconditions; if it is expensive, find a way around it (total versions, `Option`-returning ones, cheaper facts).
  - **Measured 2026-11-22** (branch `partial-ops-cost`): `verify` already has these obligations: an index in range (`at` on a list or a string), no division by zero, `chr`, `ord`, `float`, `sqrt` / `floor` / `ceil` / `round`, finite float results, shifts by a non-negative count. `STRATA_PARTIAL_OPS=off` leaves them out, to measure them. **They are cheap: keep them as preconditions everywhere.** In std: 62 of 343 listed checks (34 functions: std.regex 17, std.ast.binary 4, std.unicode 4, ...), and all 887 functions not assumed are proven either way. In the solver they are 62 of 606 queries and 0.70 s of about 5 s of z3 time over all of std (each query timed alone: 11 ms each, against 9 ms for termination and 6 ms for other contracts), while a std `verify` takes 12 to 15 minutes: the time is elsewhere (fuzzing, compiling, cross-checks), not in proving these. Whole runs: 825 s and 878 MB peak with them, 907 s and 598 MB without, so too noisy to compare: other sessions' builds and `verify` runs shared the machine (load average about 35), and the run without them was the slower. On std.regex, std.unicode, std.utf8 and std.ast.binary, three rounds alternating: 154 queries with them against 107, CPU 6 to 8 s either way. The examples: 267 queries against 258 (`builtins` 6 more, `math` 3), and no verdict changes.
  - **Found on the way, fixed**: an index into a list the prover doesn't model as a list (the result of `??`, a closure's result) had no check at all, so `verify` called `(d.get(k) ?? [])[0]` proven and the cross-check reported "a bug in the prover". Every `at` now checks the index against the list's length (`list_len`, which handles unions and unknown lists), and a list read from a field is made before it is indexed (`every_index_is_checked`). It changes no result in std (887 proven, 343 checks, as before; 665 s). Left: such a list gets a new length at each use, so `if len(xs) > 0 { xs[0] }` on a list from a dict isn't proven yet (fuzzed instead); giving an unknown list one length per value would fix it. Also seen: the counterexample input for that dict prints as `[]`.
- **Can't happen once checked** (no match arm, a `let` pattern that doesn't match, no field): impossible by the checker; one that fires is a compiler bug. A refutable `let` must be refused when checking.
- **Running out** (memory, stack, the step budget): a panic; no language makes these values everywhere (every expression may allocate: a `Result` on each would be unusable).

**Assurance levels** (per package or module, like SPARK's levels; names to settle):
1. *Default*: unproven checks run, a broken one panics.
2. *Checked* (the user's idea): a function whose contracts aren't all proven gives its callers a `Result` with a contract error, which they must match, so an unproven assumption shows in the types instead of as a possible panic.
3. *Proven*: the build refuses any unproven contract or partial operation; nothing can panic but running out (SPARK's "silver": absence of run-time errors).
4. *Bounded*: resources proven too: termination (the checker exists), a computed stack depth (no unbounded recursion), memory within a computed bound or a budget given up front. The hard part, near research with immutable values and counting.

**Keeping running** (mission-critical code), as safety-critical systems do it: (1) make failures impossible and prove it (SPARK, Astrée on Airbus flight code; NASA JPL's "Power of Ten" / MISRA: no allocation after start-up, no recursion, bounded loops, so running out can't happen); (2) contain what still fails and restart it from a known state (ARINC 653 partitions with their own memory and time budgets; Erlang's supervisors, "let it crash"; watchdogs); (3) redundancy outside the program (the Shuttle's voting computers; dissimilar software, since Ariane 5's identical backup failed the same way). Strata's tasks share nothing (values are copies; channels are the only link), which is what makes Erlang's restarts sound: a dead task can't leave shared data half-changed. So a **supervised task** (opted into explicitly: `supervised spawn e`, built: see "Concurrency", step 4) contains a panic: the task dies, its channel ends close (its peers see the channel end), and its parent gets an `Err(Panic { … })` from `await` and can restart it. Everywhere else a panic still ends the program. Later: out-of-memory against the task's own budget as that task's panic.

### Memory in high-performance code: proven properties, not a second memory model (direction agreed with the user 2026-11-21; not built)
The user asked whether Strata needs manual memory management, an unsafe mode or a borrow checker for industrial / high-performance code, where both speed and proofs about memory matter (no leaks, memory used correctly). Agreed: no.
- **What the model already guarantees.** Values never change once built and there are no shared references, so the heap has no cycles and reference counting frees everything: no leaks, by construction (stronger than Rust, where an `Rc` cycle or `mem::forget` leaks in safe code). No use after free and no aliased mutation either, since a mutable reference exists only as a `mut` parameter (see "A `mut` argument is passed by reference"). A borrow checker protects against what shared mutable references make possible, and Strata has none; adding them would also make programs harder to verify (Rust's verifiers, Prusti and Creusot, lean on borrow information to cope), against the language's point.
- **Rejected**: a general unsafe mode or manual `free` (the escape hatch is `extern c`, with contracts at the boundary), a borrow checker with lifetimes, and V's autofree (incomplete, falling back to a GC).
- **What such code needs is predictability**: no hidden allocation, no counting in hot loops, bounded memory, in-place updates that are guaranteed rather than hoped for. Each becomes a property the checker or `verify` proves, in this order:
  1. **Fully in-place functions**, as Koka's `fip` / `fbip` (Lorenzen, Leijen, Swierstra, "FP²: Fully in-Place Functional Programming", ICFP 2023): a marked function is checked to run without allocating, each constructor reusing the memory of one its input gave up. Comes nearly for free once reuse (step 3 of "Memory: reference counting with reuse") is built. Marker and syntax not decided.
  2. **Allocation as a capability** (on top of the capabilities already built): a function without an `Alloc` capability provably allocates nothing, which is the real-time / MISRA rule "no allocation after initialization", enforced by types. To settle: whether `Alloc` is implied by default (and only refused where asked), how it interacts with copy-on-write (a copy is an allocation, so a no-`Alloc` function may only update values it can prove unique), and how faults that allocate their message are counted.
  3. **Arenas**: `with arena a { ... }` allocates in a region freed at once when the block ends, with no counting inside. The only check needed is that nothing allocated in `a` escapes the block, the same second-class rule as `mut` references, so no lifetime annotations. Syntax not decided.
  4. **Bounds checks removed by proof**: an index whose refinement `verify` proves within the length needs no check at run time (part of "skipping the runtime check of a contract `verify` proved"). Built (branch `proven-checks`): see "Proven checks leave the compiled code".
- **Self-hosting and the runtime.** Reference counting needs almost no runtime (no collector thread, no stack maps, no pauses: alloc, free, increment, decrement); most of `src/llvm/runtime.c` is printing, strings, faults, random numbers, contract tracing, fuzzing and host I/O, which a borrow checker wouldn't remove. To go without the C runtime, `std` gets a few raw-memory primitives (load, store, pointer offset, size of a type), usable only inside `std` (as Rust's `core`), so the allocator and the counting can be written in Strata over `extern c` `malloc` / `free`, or over `mmap` where there's no libc. Not decided: their names, and how "only inside `std`" is enforced.
- **`core`, below std** (decided with the user 2026-11-22; steps 1 and 2 built, branch `core`). A package `core/` that depends on nothing, imported into every module, with std depending on it: (1) the prelude moved out of Rust strings into module files, `core.types` (`Debug`, `Display`, `Option`, `Result`, the refinements, the sized integers) and `core.prelude` (was `std.prelude`: iterators, channels' methods, a string's units, `Panic`); (2) what the compiler provides declared there with `builtin`, a definition only `core` may have (`extern` with `Lang::Builtin` in the tree): `builtin type Dict[K, V]` (the types `BUILTIN_TYPES` listed) and `builtin fn` (the 97 signatures `BUILTINS` listed, overloads in the order tried). **Partial builtins state their preconditions as contracts** (user's choice, over a `partial` marker): `at`, `byte_at`, `chr`, `ord`, `float`, `floor` / `ceil` / `round` and `sqrt` (finite: `(float_bits(x) >> 52) & 2047 != 2047`), `channel(n)`; a builtin is total when it has no `requires`, so `ends_with`, `chars`, `lines`, `slice`, `range`, `index_of`, `to_upper` / `to_lower`, `is_*`, `parse_float` and `print`, marked not total before though they never fail, are total now. Both compilers read core: `types.rs` (`prelude()`, `prelude_code()`, `builtins()`, `builtin_types()`), and `compiler/` through `builtins.Core` (`core_of`, read from `core/` by `main` and passed to the resolver, the checker, the declarations' checks and codegen), replacing `compiler/builtins`' copied table and the prelude name lists in check, resolve and declarations. Core's names are global, so its folder isn't a package like the others: `strata --file core/ edit MODULE < text` stores a module as parsed, `--file core render` prints it, `--file core check` checks the core strata was built with (an empty program, and the builtins' contracts). Left:
  - **`verify` reads the builtins' `requires`** instead of its own copies (`builtin_faults` in smt.rs: `chr`, `ord`, `float`, `sqrt`, `floor` / `ceil` / `round`); then an obligation's message is `precondition of `chr``, as for any function.
  - **Failures not yet contracts**: `matches` with a pattern that isn't a regular expression, a `send` after a channel ended, a second `close` (they stay not total by name, `core::NOT_YET_CONTRACTS`). A regex pattern could be a refinement (`Pattern = String where is_regex(it)`); a channel's state isn't a value property.
  - **The compiled prelude** (`src/llvm/prelude.strata`, the builtins written in Strata: `split`, `sort`, sets and dicts as sorted lists) belongs in core too, as the bodies of the builtins it implements.
  - **std's host operations** (`host_*`, `stdlib::HOST`) as `builtin fn`s of a restricted module (`core.host`), visible to std only.
  - **The checker's knowledge of particular core types** (`Option`'s shape, `refined_int`, `Probability` as a `Float`) is still written into both checkers.
