  - **Step 3 built (branch `core-mem`)**: `core.mem`, raw memory over an untyped `builtin type Ptr` (user's choice: a block's layout is the compiler's, not a Strata type, so a typed `Ptr[T]` would mostly be cast; widths are named by the operation): `raw_alloc` (requires bytes > 0), `raw_free`, `ptr_offset`, `load_i64` / `store_i64`, `load_u8` / `store_u8`, `atomic_add_i64` (gives the word before). Compiled to plain LLVM loads, stores and `atomicrmw` (an `Int` crosses as its low 64 bits), `Ptr` never counted; never total (nothing checks an address). **Only core's own modules may use it** (user's choice, not even std): the checker refuses `Ptr` and its functions anywhere else, and a definition counts as core's only if core's files have it (`core::owns`: a package's module named `core.mem` isn't core). core's own tests run with `strata --file core test`. compiler/ gives the same errors (`capabilities.core_only_errors`, `Core.mem`).
  - **The prelude split by topic, each included only when used** (user, 2026-11-22): `core.iter`, `core.channel` (with the channel builtins and `Sender` / `Receiver`), `core.text`, `core.task` (`Panic`). A program gets a module when it names something the module defines (types, traits and their methods, functions, builtins), except names of builtins defined elsewhere (`map`, `len`, `get`: a list's `xs.map(f)` pulls in nothing), and what those modules use in turn (`core.text` and `core.channel` use `core.iter`); `supervised spawn` uses `core.task`. Compiled code only ever held what is reached; this saves checking, verifying and compiling the rest. Methods are named in their module (`core.iter.len`).
  - Found on the way, fixed: the printer wrote a called field `(b.f)(1)` as `b.f(1)`, a method call, so rendered code didn't read back (both printers).
  - **Step 4, the bootstrap (built, branch `core-rt`)**: decided with the user 2026-11-23: strata is built in two stages, not from a checked-in compiled file: stage 0 is `cargo build` with the C runtime's versions; that strata compiles core.rt (`strata build-core`, its `strata_...` functions exported under the C runtime's names, stamped with core/rt.strata's and core/mem.strata's hash) to `target/core_rt.ll`; stage 1 is `cargo build` again, build.rs linking it and runtime.c built without its versions (`STRATA_CORE_RT`). `tools/bootstrap.sh` runs both and checks stage 1 compiles core.rt as stage 0 did (a fixed point). Executables from `strata build` always link core.rt, compiled with the program. A plain `cargo build` keeps stage 1 while the stamp matches; after core/rt.strata changes it warns and falls back to C until the bootstrap runs again. First function moved: `strata_unique` (an acquiring load of the count, `atomic_load_i64`, new in core.mem): no measurable cost on a 20-million-push loop (JIT, stage 0 against stage 1).
  - **Step 5, the allocator in core.rt (built, branch `core-alloc`)**: `strata_alloc`, `strata_dup`, `strata_release`, `strata_free` are Strata, #307's design ported: objects of at most 256 bytes (count word included) from free lists, one per 16-byte class, carved from 64 KB chunks of a 16 GB reserved range (`raw_reserve`); a per-chunk class table (`builtin static chunk_class`, one `I64` word per chunk: a `U8` table would cost a conversion through `Int` per free) gives `strata_free` an object's class; one spinlock (`atomic_swap_i64` / `atomic_store_i64`) guards the lists; bigger objects and check mode use malloc. All state is in `builtin static rt_state` (threaded, check mode, tallies, lock words, region, free lists, the objects made in check mode). The C runtime keeps its own copies of `threaded` / `checking` (for `strata_slow_counts`, which the IR reads) and tells core.rt (`strata_memory_threaded`, `strata_memory_start`); `STRATA_MEMORY`'s report stays C, reading core.rt's tallies and made list (`strata_memory_allocated`, `_freed`, `_made`). A freed object's misuse stops the program through `raw_fail` (a constant message). The compiled module calls nothing but core.mem's C helpers: no frames, no big-integer code.
    - core.mem is on machine integers (`I64` offsets and words, `U8` bytes; `address` / `ptr_at` for address arithmetic) and gained `raw_reserve`, `raw_size`, `raw_fill`, `raw_fail`, `atomic_store_i64`, `atomic_swap_i64`.
    - **core.rt allocates nothing**, checked by both checkers (`core_rt_allocations` in src/types.rs, `capabilities.allocation_errors` over `walk.decl_exprs` plus `check`'s `arith` for the typed part): lists, tuples and constructors unless every part is a literal (one constant block), dicts, sets, closures, interpolation, ranges, comprehensions, tasks, `?`, `except`, big literals, calls to anything but core.rt's own functions, core.mem's and the integer conversions, and `+ - * / % <<` or negation on `Int` (a big integer past 2^62), `+` on strings and lists. Open: `_ := e` compiles through the refutable `let` (an `Int` drop on an `I64`): core.rt names what it discards.
  - **Step 6, the rest of runtime.c in core.rt (agreed with the user 2026-11-23; branch `rt-strings`)**. The goal is no Rust and as little C as possible: `src/llvm/runtime.c` (3,765 lines when this started) moves to core.rt a group at a time, each group's C versions kept for the first stage of the bootstrap behind `#ifndef STRATA_CORE_RT` (as the allocator's), both compilers (`llvm::compile_core_rt`, `compiler codegen --core-rt`) compiling it, behavior the same: output, faults and their messages, exit codes, and check mode's tallies (each counted object the C made is made too, a fault's message included).
    - **Why core.rt compiled in the root module (the open question of #355)**: core's names are global, so core's module files are stored as parsed (no package resolution): a call in core.rt is written `lock(32)`, not `core.rt.lock`. compiler/codegen doesn't resolve names: it keys each function by `module.name` and looks a call up by the name written. Under its real module the functions were `core.rt.lock` and the like, every call to one found nothing (`not compiled yet: the built-in \`checking\``), and library mode left out each `strata_` function reaching one. Nothing to fix in library mode: core.rt is compiled in the root module as core's other code is (`core/iter.strata` and the others, in `codegen_with`), which is what core's global names mean; the comment in `core_rt_text` now says so.
    - **Inventory of runtime.c by area** (lines, before this step): memory 320 (raw blocks for core.mem ~35, the allocator's C copy for stage 0 ~155, check mode's report ~35, string basics 48); faults and the call stack 82; tests 166; fuzzing 149; tasks, channels, the clock, virtual time, `select`, deadlocks 959; integers 474 (big integers, `/ %`, bits and shifts, showing, literals) and sized integers 52; foreign calls (`strata_cstr`, `strata_str_from_c`) 54; generated values (`mock`) ~75; floats (showing, `finite`, conversions, `sqrt`) ~127; lists 179; output, `strata_init`, signals, options errors ~145; host I/O (`host_*`: files, directories, processes, the environment, input) ~500; strings 397; f-string format specs 43.
    - **Plan, in this order**: (1) **strings** that need no lists: concatenation, `==`, `<` (`strata_str_cmp`), lengths, `byte_at`, `trim`, `byte_slice`, `at` / `slice` / `get`, `ord`, `chr`, `debug` (`strata_debug_str`), `to_upper` / `to_lower` (`strata_str_case`, over the compiler's tables), `is_digit` / `is_alpha` / `is_space` (`strata_str_is`); (2) **integers**: big integers (add, sub, mul, Euclidean `/ %`, comparison, `abs`), bits and shifts, `strata_int_lit`, `strata_show_int`, the conversions (`strata_int_from_i64` / `_u64`, `strata_int_bits`, `strata_int_fit`), sized integers' `/ %` and shift fault, `parse_int`; then, in later PRs: (3) lists (and `split` / `chars` / `lines` if they come back as runtime functions), (4) the f-string format specs and floats (`show_float` needs a shortest-digits algorithm in Strata: Ryu or Grisu), (5) faults and the call stack, tests, fuzzing, `mock`'s generators, (6) tasks and channels (threads stay system calls: what core.mem needs, `pthread_create`, futexes or `poll`, decided then), (7) host I/O as system calls through `extern c`. What stays C at the end is to be decided with the user (system calls and `setjmp` at least).
    - **core.mem grew what the strings and integers need**: `raw_copy` (LLVM's `memmove`), `raw_compare` (`memcmp`), `load_u32` / `store_u32` (a big integer's digits), `widen_u8` / `low_u8` (a byte as an `I64` and back: through `Int` a conversion is a call, `to_i64` a range check with big literals), `div_trunc` / `rem_trunc` / `div_unsigned` / `rem_unsigned` (LLVM's `sdiv` / `srem` / `udiv` / `urem`: `I64`'s own `/` is Euclidean, compiled as a call to `strata_word_div`, core.rt's own since the integers moved), `str_block` (a string's block, for literals: core.rt's strings are `Ptr`s, as C's `Str *`), and `raw_fault` (C's `soft_fault`, exported as `strata_soft_fault`, until faults move).
    - **Dead runtime functions deleted instead of ported**: `strata_str_contains`, `_starts_with`, `_ends_with`, `_index_of`, `_reverse`, `_replace`, `_repeat`, `_split`, `_chars` (and C's `find_from`, `push_str`): neither code generator calls them since they were written in Strata (`src/llvm/prelude.strata`, #180); only their declarations and the JIT's table named them.
    - **Strings, group 1, built**: the functions of the plan's first group are core.rt's (`str_alloc`, `str_part` and the UTF-8 helpers below them; a fault's message written in a raw block, then made a string once, as C's `snprintf` then `str_c` did). Test: tests/codegen.rs `core_rt_strings_and_integers_as_in_c`, the same output, error output, exit code and check mode's report with core.rt and with the C versions, faults in supervised tasks and faults ending the program included.
    - **Integers, group 2, built**: big integers on raw blocks laid out as a big integer's object (sign, count, 32-bit digits; `int_of` copies one into a counted object, as C did), `strata_int_add` / `_sub` / `_mul` / `_cmp` / `_eq` / `_abs`, `strata_div` / `_mod` and their `_nz`, `strata_int_and` / `_or` / `_xor`, `strata_shl` / `_shr`, `strata_int_lit`, `strata_show_int`, `strata_int_from_i64` / `_u64`, `strata_int_bits`, `strata_int_fit`, `strata_int_dup` / `_drop`, the sized integers' `strata_word_div` / `_mod`, `strata_uword_div` / `_mod` (a `U64`'s bits as an `I64`: the C ABI is the same `i64`) and `strata_word_shift_fault`, and `strata_parse_int`. A product of digits plus carries is up to 2^64 - 1: computed on wrapping `I64`s, its carry taken as `c >> 32 & (2^32 - 1)`. C keeps `num_of` / `int_of` for `float(n)` and `floor` and the like (`strata_int_to_float`, `strata_float_to_int`), which move with the floats. Test: the same `core_rt_strings_and_integers_as_in_c`, with big arithmetic, Euclidean division of every sign, bits, shifts, conversions and parsing, and their faults (in supervised tasks and ending the program).
    - **The runtime in topic modules (asked by the user 2026-11-24; branch `rt-modules`)**: not everything goes into core.rt. As core.mem holds raw memory, each topic of the runtime has its module, and core.rt keeps the runtime proper: the allocator, the runtime's state (`builtin static`), and every `strata_...` entry point (compiled code's and the C runtime's), each a one-line call into its topic module. Together they are the runtime, `core::RUNTIME` in `src/core.rs` (`runtime_module` in compiler/builtins): `core.rt`, `core.str` (strings: `str_concat`, `str_trim`, `str_case`, ...; a fault's message, `msg_*`), `core.int` (integers: `int_add`, `int_div`, `int_show`, the big integers' `num_*` / `mag_*`, the sized integers' `word_div` and the like), then `core.list`, `core.format`, `core.float` as they come. Names: `core.str` for strings next to `core.text` (a program's string units) because the runtime's names follow C's (`strata_str_...`); the topic modules' functions are named by their topic (`str_trim`, `int_parse`), never as a builtin (`trim`, `parse_int`) or as an entry point (`strata_...`), so nothing collides.
      - *Names*: core's names are global for what programs see (`core.types`, the builtins, the code modules), but the runtime's modules are never part of a program (not in `core::CODE`, so `needed` never adds them) and their definitions don't clash with a program's. Between themselves they use the module system, as any modules do: what one uses of another is `pub` there and named by a `from core.str use ...` (core.rt uses core.str and core.int; those use core.rt's `strata_alloc`, `strata_dup`, `strata_release`, `strata_free`; core.int uses core.str's messages). Rust's resolver needs it (a bare call to another module's function was left unresolved: "the built-in `str_concat` is not compiled yet"); compiler/codegen, which doesn't resolve, compiles the runtime in the root module (below), where the `use`s are inert.
      - *Allocating nothing*: the rule is the runtime's, not only core.rt's: a function of any of its modules may call only the runtime's functions, core.mem's and the integer conversions (`core::runtime_fn` in Rust's `core_rt_allocations` and in the checker's `Int` arithmetic check; `runtime_module` in compiler/capabilities' `allocation_errors` and compiler/check). It has to be: core.rt calls them from the allocator's callers, and what they make goes through `strata_alloc` explicitly.
      - *Compiling*: `llvm::compile_core_rt` reads every runtime file (`core::runtime_files`), resolves them together and exports the `strata_...` functions (only core.rt has them) under their C names; compiler/main's `core_rt_text` reads the same files in the root module, `bare`, `with_c_names` on the `strata_` roots. Calls from core.rt into a topic module are within one LLVM module (one object, -O2), so the one-line entry points are inlined. `-DSTRATA_CORE_RT` is unchanged: it leaves out the C versions of what the runtime defines, wherever in the runtime it is defined.
      - *Bootstrap*: the stamp of `target/core_rt.ll` (build.rs `RUNTIME_FILES`, `strata build-core`'s `core_stamp`) covers core.mem and every runtime file, so changing core.str makes build.rs fall back to C until `tools/bootstrap.sh` runs again. core's tests (`core::tests`) get the runtime modules' functions, as they got core.rt's.
    - **Lists, format specs and floats (branch `rt-lists`)**, each in its own module, the C versions behind `#ifndef STRATA_CORE_RT`:
      - *core.list*: `strata_list_new` / `_push` / `_owned` / `_put` / `_insert` / `_remove_at` / `_slice` / `_concat`, `strata_range` (each object made in the C's order, so check mode's lists of what is alive agree). C keeps `buf_new` and `append` for the host functions' lists (`host_args`, `list_dir`, `run`), which move with host I/O.
      - *core.fmt*: `strata_format` (padding, alignment, `+`, `0`). core.mem gained `widen_i32` (a C `int32_t` argument as an `I64`: through `Int`, `to_i64` is a range check with big literals). Nothing of the format specs was left in C. (The user, 2026-11-24: format specs are low priority; plain interpolation is what matters.)
      - *core.float*: `strata_show_float` (serde_json's layout), `strata_show_float_fixed` (`{x:.p}`), `strata_finite`, `strata_float_to_int`, `strata_int_to_float`, `strata_sqrt`, `strata_fmod`, `strata_parse_float`, and `strata_show_bool` (core.str's `str_bool`). What C took from the C library is computed exactly on core.int's numbers: the shortest digits as C found them (`%.Ne` for N = 1, 2, ... until `strtod` reads it back: here the correctly rounded N digits, ties to even, then whether they lie within half a gap of the float, a tie read back as the even one), `%.*f` (the exact value rounded to `p` decimals), `strtod` (the decimal's exact value rounded to the nearest float, subnormals and overflow included) and `ldexp` (`nat_to_bits`). core.mem gained `f64_bits` / `f64_from_bits` (bitcasts), `f64_of_i64` / `f64_to_i64` (`sitofp` / `fptosi`), `f64_sqrt` (`llvm.sqrt.f64`) and `f64_rem` (`frem`: C's `fmod`). With floats moved, C's integer code is all behind the guard too (its last users were `float(n)` and `floor`).
      - Tests: tests/codegen.rs `core_rt_lists_formats_and_floats_as_in_c` builds each program with both code generators (llvm.rs is the one whose in-place changes of lists call `strata_list_owned` / `_put` / `_insert` / `_remove_at`) and links each with core.rt and with the C versions: the same output, faults (supervised and not) and check mode's report; floats of every exponent (3000 bit patterns) are shown, read back and printed with 4 decimals as C does.
      - *Cost, measured*: showing and reading floats is about 15 times slower than C's (3000 floats shown and read back: 0.31 s against 0.02 s): each digit count tried computes with big numbers (powers of 2 and 10 up to 2^1074 and 10^340). A shortest-digits algorithm on machine words (Ryu, Grisu with a fallback) would remove most of it, if it matters.
      - Found: `strata build-core` doesn't run the checker on the runtime, so a function that allocates (`1023 + 62`: `Int` arithmetic on two literals) compiled; only `strata --file core test --rust` refused it. `build-core` could check the runtime first. *Fixed on the Strata side (branch `rt-faults`)*: `compiler codegen --core-rt` (what the default `strata build` links) checks the runtime's modules first, resolved (`resolve.stored`, their files being stored as written) in their own modules, with `check_errors_of`, and prints the errors instead of the IR (test: tests/codegen.rs `core_rt_that_doesnt_check_isnt_compiled`; 0.3 s). It caught, at once, a topic module missing from `runtime_module`. Rust's `strata build-core` (the bootstrap's) still doesn't check: `llvm::compile_core_rt` would run `types::check` on the resolved runtime (a Rust change, left).
    - **Faults, tests, fuzzing, generated values (branch `rt-faults`)**:
      - *Moved*: the generators (`strata_random`, `strata_random_unit`, `strata_gen_int` / `_float` / `_len` / `_string`) to **core.gen**, over core.mem's new `random_word` (C's splitmix64 step, `strata_next_random`: the state is per thread, set by `strata_test` / `strata_seed`); the text of a fault's call stack (`stack_from`: `down (101 times)`, ` -> ...` past 4096 runs) to **core.fault**'s `stack_text`, which C calls (`strata_stack_text`, a C string it frees). Test: tests/codegen.rs `core_rt_generators_and_stacks_as_in_c` (a test's mocked values run through `strata_test` from a C `main`; faults in recursions, by both code generators).
      - *Left in C, waiting on a decision (open question below)*: the call stack itself (`strata_enter` / `strata_leave`, per thread), raising and containing faults (`fault_at`, `strata_fault`, `strata_panic`, `soft_fault`, `record_panic`: `sigsetjmp` / `siglongjmp` back to a test's or a supervised task's boundary, `exit(101)`), the tests' and fuzz cases' runs (`strata_test`, `strata_fuzz`, `strata_step`'s budget, `strata_fuzz_arg`'s text, what a test printed), `--trace`'s lines (stderr), and `strata_overflow`.
      - **Per-thread state and leaving a run: decided by the user 2026-11-24** (it was this step's open question): (a) per-thread state is a new kind of definition, `builtin thread static NAME: BYTES`: one zeroed block per thread, thread-local, its name a `Ptr` to this thread's, as `builtin static` is one per program (over a core.mem block from C, or keeping C); (1) leaving a run keeps `sigsetjmp` / `siglongjmp` as a few lines of C behind core.mem builtins, everything around them in Strata (over compiled code returning an error flag everywhere).
      - **Built (branch `rt-stack`)**:
        - *`builtin thread static`*: compiler/ first (`ExternThreadStatic(String, Int)` in compiler/ast; lexed as words, parsed after `builtin thread`; printed; binary tag 4 of an extern item, so existing module files read as before; resolved, checked and compiled as a `builtin static`, but a `thread_local` global in compiler/codegen), and in Rust as much as the bootstrap needs (`ExternItem::ThreadStatic`, `Env::thread_statics`, a `thread_local` global in llvm.rs). Core's module files are now in tests/parse.rs and tests/ast.rs's parity runs, so `builtin thread static` is read, written and printed the same by both.
        - *core.mem*: `trap_size` / `trap_run(buf, f)` / `trap_leave(buf)` (C's `sigsetjmp` in `strata_trap_run`'s frame, which lives while `f` runs, and `siglongjmp`), and what stays C while tasks and output are its own: `run_begin` / `run_end` (a test's boundary and threads, `Contained` and `Run`), `task_origin`, `contain_fault` (a caught fault's effect on the run's tasks, then `finish_open_scopes`), `out_flush`, `fd_write`, `process_exit`, `thread_park`; and `narrow_i32`. Both code generators compile them from one table (`c_hook` in llvm.rs and compiler/codegen); `random_word` is gone (core.gen's own now).
        - *Moved*: **core.fault** has the call stack (`builtin thread static calls`, `strata_enter` / `strata_leave`), raising a fault (`strata_fault` / `strata_fault_at`: the stack with where a task was spawned, caught by a run's trap or ending the program with `fault (KIND): ...`, the stack, exit 101) and `--trace`'s lines; **core.run** (`test` is a keyword) has a thread's run state (`builtin thread static run_state`: the generators' state and seed, the trap, the last caught fault, what a test printed and whether it is kept, the fuzz budget, the fuzz arguments' text, what the host gave compiled code, compile-time code's value, `--trace`'s depth), `strata_test` (the caller's call stack kept aside and put back, `trap_run`), `strata_fuzz`, `strata_step`, `strata_fuzz_arg`, `strata_seed`, `strata_set_given` / `strata_given`, `strata_ct_keep` / `strata_ct_kept`, and `strata_keep_printed`; core.gen's generator (splitmix64) reads its state there. The C runtime's tasks read and write the same blocks (`strata_call_stack()`, `strata_run_state()`: `CallStack`, `RunState` in runtime.c, laid out as core.fault's and core.run's; the first stage has its own thread-locals of that layout).
        - *Still C*: the tasks (`Contained`, `Run`, `Waiter`, `task_main`, which still sets its own trap with `sigsetjmp`), `soft_fault` / `strata_panic` / `record_panic` (they call core.fault's `strata_fault_at`), the C-string accessors strata reads (`strata_test_kind` / `_message` / `_stack` / `_printed`, `strata_fuzz_args`), and `print` (it calls `strata_keep_printed`). They move with the tasks and output.
    - Found on the way, fixed in both versions: `strata_div_nz` of two small integers whose quotient isn't small (-2^62 / -1) read the quotient as a big integer's address and crashed (C's `int_of(num_of(q))`); it is `strata_int_from_i64(q)` now.
    - Seen, not fixed (not run by any command or test): `compiler check core/rt.strata` reports core.rt's statics as unknown variables (on main too), and, since these groups, type errors Rust's checker doesn't give, on `I64` arithmetic with a literal (`0 - big_base()` and `8 + an` with `an := str_size(a)` typed `Int`, then refused as allocating), though the allocator's `128 + k * 8` passes: the cause isn't investigated. compiler/codegen compiles the same code (it doesn't go through that path).
    - Found on the way, fixed in llvm.rs: an `assert` comparing `I64`s dropped, after it passed, the `Int`s made to show its sides, which only its failing path had made (undefined values: core.mem's test of `div_unsigned` crashed `strata --file core test --rust`).

### `std.collections`: an ordered map, an ordered set, a priority queue (asked by the coordinator 2026-11-25; built, branch `std-collections`)
Agents writing Strata each built their own sorted map and priority queue (one, a tree of dicts, was the source of a 115 s bug). Built:
- **`std.collections`**: `SortedDict[K, V]`, `SortedSet[T]` (an AVL tree, `Tree[K, V]`, by the keys' `compare` order: `get` / `contains` / `insert` / `remove` O(log n), `first` / `last` / `pop_first` / `pop_last`, `floor` / `ceiling` (at or below / above), `before` / `after` (strictly), `between(lo, hi)` O(log n + m), `items` / `keys` / `values` / `to_list` in order) and `Heap[T]` (a binary heap in a `List`: `push` / `pop` O(log n), `peek` O(1); smallest by `compare`, `Heap.by(key)` or `Heap.with(order)`), and `sorted_dict(pairs)`, `sorted_set(xs)`, `heap(xs)` (O(n)). Every operation's doc ends with a `Cost:` line. Names: `SortedDict` / `SortedSet` say what they add to the built-in `Dict` / `Set` (as Python's sortedcontainers); `Heap` says what the queue is (Python's `heapq`, Rust's `BinaryHeap`); `floor` / `ceiling` as Java's `NavigableMap`, `before` / `after` for its strict `lower` / `higher` (`lower` reads like `to_lower`). Updates are `mut self` methods (`mut d.insert(k, v)`, `mut h.pop()`), so a value nothing else holds changes in place: the heap's list through `set_at` / `take` (below), the tree by new nodes along the path (O(log n) allocations; reuse of the dead nodes, Perceus-style, is the compiler's to do).
- **What it needed from the compiler**: `compare(a, b)` (core/builtins; -1, 0, 1), the order `sort` already used (codegen's `cmp` helper per type, now callable), so generic code can order values of any type (`<` is only for numbers; there is no `Ord` trait); the verifier models it exactly on integers, else as -1..1, and eval runs it. `set_at` and its in-place update came with `xs[i] = v` (branch `index-assign`, merged while this was built; this branch had built the same built-in, and took main's). And `xs = take(xs, n)` (or `o.f = take(o.f, n)`) in place (codegen `take_in_place`, called from `update_in_place`): when nothing else holds the list, the elements past `n` are taken out from the end and dropped, so the buffer's used slots shrink and the next `push` goes in place too; before, `take` made a slice that left the buffer's slots used, so a `push` after it copied the whole list: a heap's pop then push was O(n). The count may read the list (`xs = take(xs, len(xs) - 1)`), unlike the dict case, since it is worked out first.
- **Measured** (`--release`, Apple M-series, keys `(i * 7919) % n`): `Heap[Int]`, n pushes then n pops: 125k 0.06 s, 250k 0.18 s, 500k 0.42 s, 1M 1.11 s user (21 MB); `SortedDict[Int, Int]`, n inserts, n lookups, n removes: 125k 0.51 s, 250k 1.24 s, 500k 2.93 s, 1M 6.33 s (66 MB): both O(n log n). `take` + `push` alternating is linear (before `take` in place: 20k, 40k, 80k alternations took 1.2, 4.9, 19 s). Every object freed (`STRATA_MEMORY=check`).
- **Proven**: the whole module is `--require proven,terminates` (64 functions; the heap's index checks proven, so they leave the compiled code). Not proven, so left out: sizes (`count(put(t, k, v).0) == count(t) + …`) and membership as contracts: `verify` doesn't see through a recursive helper (`count`) in a contract (only its contract is known), so they are fuzzed only, and std must stay proven. Sortedness and balance would need the same, plus `compare`'s laws (antisymmetry, transitivity) on any type. Next for proofs: unfolding a terminating recursive helper a few levels in contracts.
- **Found on the way** (not fixed here): (1) a lambda with untyped parameters inside a generic function, stored in a record inside an `Option` (`Box { order: Some((a, b) -> key(a) - key(b)) }` in `fn by[T](key: (T) -> Int)`), crashed when called (signal 11; the seed too): `Heap.by` types its lambda's parameters (`(a: T, b: T) -> …`) to avoid it. Fixed (branch `issues-crashes`): `codegen.construct_want` hands a field's type to its value when it holds a function's (`holds_fn`), so the lambda gets its parameters' types from `Option[(T, T) -> Int]`; it had none (want `TAny`), so it was compiled taking no parameters (case `build-lambdas_in_wanted_fields`). Left: with no type wanted at all (`r := R { f: Some(x -> x) }`, then `r.f` called in a generic function) the lambda's parameter is taken as `()` and LLVM rejects the IR (`phi i64 [ , …]`); codegen would need the checker's inferred types there. (2) `verify` of a `mut self` method that matches on a recursive field of `self` (`match self.root { Node { .. } => … }` in `SortedSet.pop_first`) ran out of memory (8.7 GB, killed): the match moved into a free function (`pop_first_entry`). Fixed in `issues-verify-crashes`: the branches merged a `Tree[K]` with a generic callee's `Tree[T]` (its result's type is not instantiated at the call, `smt.call_contract` shapes `f.ret` as written), two datatypes, and `smt.merge` took both apart level by level forever; now terms of one sort merge as a term, and of two sorts give an unknown value. Left: instantiate a generic callee's type parameters at the call (the merged value is then known). (3) An impl of a type with two parameters can't be selected by name (`verify 'std.collections.impl std.collections.SortedDict[K, V]'`: the selector splits at the comma); `module:std.collections` works. (4) Two `SortedDict`s with the same entries can differ in shape, so `==` on them compares shapes: compare `items()` (an `Eq` / custom `==` would fix it).

### Runtime and standard library (decided)
- **A small native core in Rust** (C is possible for the system-call layer; decide when we get there): the GC, the system-call / OS boundary, and bindings to LLVM and the SMT solver.
- **Most of the standard library in Strata itself**: collections, strings, the ORM, HTTP / web views, CLI helpers, and agent tooling. Only the thin OS / runtime layer is native.

### Sums and unions are one thing (decided with the user 2026-11-21; built, branch `unions-are-sums`)
A sum already is a union of the types its variants declare (`ShapeError` is `Empty | TooBig`), and matching, narrowing and coverage are the union machinery; what was left apart (where members are declared, which can have `impl`s) confused more than it helped. So there is one construct, the union:
- **Members declared inline or named**: `type Shape = Circle | Square { side: Float } | Dot` reuses `Circle` and declares `Square` and `Dot`. Today's sum is the case where every member is inline (`type Option[T] = Some(T) | None`). Declaring a member inline is declaring a type (a record, a tuple-shaped type or an atom), unique in its module as variants are now.
- **Only bare members flatten**: `type S = A | B; type S2 = S | C` is `A | B | C`; `type S3 = D(S) | E` is `D | E`, not `A | B | E`: a constructor wrapping something is a new type, and what it holds stays inside it (`x.0` of a `D` is an `S`).
- **A named union names a set of values, as an alias does**, under the alias rules: a `Circle` fits `Shape` without wrapping; the same set written out (`Circle | Square`) is the same type; a named union can have `impl`s, methods and `derive` (as a sum can today). A union that adds nothing is refused like `type P = List[Int]`: one that normalizes to one type (`type U = Int | Positive`), or a second name in the same module for a set already named (`type Figure = Circle | Square` next to `Shape`): "use `Shape`, or make it `distinct type Figure = ...`". Names are unique per module, not per program: `geo.Shape` and `ui.Figure` may name the same set, told apart by their module (user); which `impl` applies is the static type's (`geo.Shape`'s for a value typed `geo.Shape`); an anonymous union written inline has none of its own.
- **`distinct type Figure = Circle | Square`**: a separate nominal type, as `distinct` is today: built explicitly (`Figure(c)`), `f.value` is the `Circle | Square`, doesn't mix with `Shape` or its members, has its own `impl`s, and is written to JSON as its value.
- **`std.typing`**: `Sum` and `Union` become one kind with `members` (`variants` goes); `Choice` is a union whose members are all atoms. JSON's two paths (sums tagged by `"type"`, unions told apart by their JSON) merge: a member is tagged when it is a record, tuple-shaped or atom type, else told apart by its JSON kind, as unions are now.
- **Refined with the user (2026-11-21)**:
  - *A bare name refers to a type when one exists*, and declares a new one only when nothing has that name; with a payload too: `Some(Int) | None` is the prelude's `Some` (given `Int`) and `None`, i.e. `Option[Int]`, not new types. A member named like an existing type (`Circel` next to `Circle`) is a warning after the `edit` ("did you mean `Circle`?"), not an error: two types may have close names.
  - *Inline and separate are two views of the same thing*: `type Shape = Circle | Square { side: Float } | Dot`, or `type Square = { side: Float }`, `type Dot` and `type Shape = Circle | Square | Dot`, mean the same; `edit` takes either. So an atom can be declared on its own (`type Dot`), and a tuple-shaped type too (`type Moved(Int)`). Which one is shown is a setting of the renderer and viewer; by default a member is shown inline under its union when that is the only union using it, else on its own.
  - *The renderer uses names*: a union written out (`fn f(x: B | C)`) is rendered as its name (`x: A`) when the set has one in scope (the module's own first), and named subsets too when one name alone covers them (`B | C | D` with `A = B | C` is `A | D`); a `distinct` type is never used so. The checker agrees: a union equal to a named set in scope is that type (its `impl`s apply).
  - *The prelude is part of every module* (it is in scope unprefixed): `type Answer = Some(Int) | None` names a set the prelude already names (`Option[Int]`), so it is refused as adding nothing ("use `Option[Int]`, or make it `distinct`"). A module imported with a prefix (`std.json`, `geo`) is another module: naming the same set there is fine.
  - *A `distinct` union* is built and read as any `distinct` type: `distinct type Answer = Option[Int]` (or `= Some(Int) | None`), `Answer(Some(3))`, `Answer(None)`, `a.value` the `Option[Int]`.
  - *Later (viewer)*: holding a key shows hints: a type expanded into its members, the parameters' names at a call, and the like.
- **Built** (branch `unions-are-sums`, stacked on `typing-defs`):
  - *Stored form*: a union whose members are all defined elsewhere is an alias of their union; one declaring some is a `TypeBody::Sum` whose other members are `Payload::Type` (binary tag 3). The parser reads `A | B { .. } | C(Int)` and the bar-first form the same; whether a bare name is an existing type is decided when the code is stored (`resolve::union_members`: the module's types, earlier unions' declared members, the root module, the prelude and built-ins, what it imports; a second version of the same definition, as an edit has, doesn't count), so the decision is in the tree. A member with fields named like a prelude type (`Some(Int)`) is that type. One bare name with no bar is an alias (`type Port = Int where ..`); a union of one atom is `type X = | Dot`, an atom on its own `type Dot`. `Tuple` and `Unit` aren't built-in names for this (`(A, B)` and `()` are written so), which leaves `std.typing.Kind` its `Tuple` and `Unit`.
  - *Checking and running*: before checking, a union with members of both kinds becomes a sum of its own members (`Shape$own`) and the union of that with the others (`desugar::mixed_unions`), so the checker and backends see only sums and unions. A named union isn't nominal: a union type is found by its set (`Checker::union_name`, `Module::union_name`, a sum counting as its variants), and the named union's `impl`s and own methods apply to it, in `satisfies`, method calls, static calls (`Shape.from_json_at(..)`, passed by name) and the compiled dispatch (before each member's own). Two modules naming the same set may not both implement a trait for it (`Checker::union_conflicts`, below); otherwise the first name found (by name order) is used.
  - *Rules*: `Checker::union_adds_nothing` (one type once normalized; the same set as an earlier union or sum of the module, the root module or the prelude; the members a union declares, named apart, are fine); `distinct` on a union that only declares members adds nothing; `resolve::near_misses` warns after an edit (one change in a name of up to five letters, two beyond, or case).
  - *Rendering*: `written_form` names a union written out when its module (or the root module) names the set, and named subsets when they don't overlap, keeping the members' order (`resolve::named_unions`; not `distinct`, not generic). `render --members separate | inline` (`resolve::members_view`).
  - *`std.typing`*: `Kind` has no `Sum`, `Type` no `variants`; a union's `members` are as written, a named union one member, except the members a union declares itself, listed each. A named union can derive (`derivable(Record | Union)`); as a field it needs its own `derive`, as a record does. `std.json`: one path for unions, a record, atom or tuple-shaped member tagged by `"type"`, any other told apart by its JSON; messages for a union of tagged members as before.
  - *Default view (user, 2026-11-21)*: a type with a doc is shown on its own, else inside the one union listing it (`render` folds it unless it has a doc, an `impl` or `derive`, or type parameters); `--members separate` shows every member on its own.
  - *Type parameters are wrapped (user, 2026-11-21)*: a generic union's parameter is inside a member it declares (`Some(T)`) or inside a type (`List[T]`), never a member on its own (`T`), and no two members are alike when a parameter is in them (`List[A] | List[B]`): those couldn't be told apart. No `Either[A, B]`: `A | B` is written where it is used. (A bare parameter name in a union is that parameter, not a new atom.) So a union's set, its members paired by what they are and the arguments read off (`Checker::same_set`, `bind_targs` for unions), names one union: a generic one has `impl`s of its own, `Ints = List[Int] | Problem` next to `Many[T] = List[T] | Problem` adds nothing, and `List[Int] | Problem` renders as `Many[Int]` (whole unions only; a parameter only inside a declared member, `Value(T)`, can't be read off a written union). Also fixed: a bare `List =>` arm on a union member compiled to no match.
  - *Two modules naming one set (user, 2026-11-21)*: allowed, but not both implementing a trait (or having a method of one name) for it: `Checker::union_conflicts`, on the later one.
  - *Viewer*: `strata ui` has the setting (*union members: inline (switch)*, kept in the browser): each card carries its code in the other view, and a type the inline view folds has no card there.
### Unions, variants as types, and match patterns (decided 2026-11-19, built)
- **`Option` and `Result` stay as they are**, and so do `!` / `?` propagation. No `T?` / `T ! E` syntax.
- **Unions are sets of values**: `A | B` holds any value of `A` or of `B`. Unions normalize: `Int | Positive` is `Int`; `(Int where p) | (Int where q)` is `Int where p || q`; different bases stay a union. `A` fits `A | B`; `A | B` fits `C` when both do. Type parameters can be members (`T | E` with bounds); if they end up the same type, it collapses, harmlessly.
- **Variants are types.** `type ShapeError = | NonEmptyErr | TooBig { max: Int }` declares the types `NonEmptyErr` and `TooBig`, and `ShapeError` is `NonEmptyErr | TooBig`. So `Result[Circle, NonEmptyErr]` or a parameter of type `TooBig` are allowed.
- **Names are unique in their module** (changed with the user 2026-11-21; was: across the project): no variant may share its name with a type, a trait or another variant of its module. Another module's of the same name is told apart by its module (`geo.Circle`).
- **No automatic wrapping**: always write `Ok(…)`, `Err(…)`, `Some(…)` (`Result[Int, Int]` would be ambiguous otherwise).
- **Patterns are sets of values**: a type name (`Positive =>`, `Square =>`), a variant as today, a literal, a condition (`where 3 <= it && it <= 5 =>`, base type = the scrutinee's), `_`. An arm takes what no earlier arm took. Exhaustive = the scrutinee's (normalized) type is covered by the arms; an arm already covered by earlier ones is an error (unreachable).
- **Narrowing, no new name**: `match x { Positive => { x … } }` narrows `x` itself in the arm. For an expression: `match f(y) as x { … }`. Field paths (`o.status`) narrow too. Narrowing of a `let mut` lasts until it is assigned.
- **Coverage**: integer (and `len(it)`) comparisons with constants, combined with `&&` / `||`, are decided exactly as interval sets. Anything else waits for the SMT solver; until then such an arm needs a later `_`. This also lets a match on `NonEmpty` skip the `[]` arm.
- **Built** (branch `unions`): `Ty::Union` normalized by `Checker::union_of`; subsumption in `fits` (used by `expect`: union members, variants into their sum; type arguments stay invariant); `infer_at` passes the expected type through `if` / `match` / blocks / lists / constructors; `pattern` returns the narrowed type and a value set `P`; exhaustiveness and unreachable arms use atoms (`atoms`, `covers`, `missing`, `useful`) with integer intervals in `types::ints`. The interpreter (since deleted) had `value_in` / `shape_fits` for type patterns and `is`, unions in `check_type` and JSON input, impls on variants; compiled code does the same. Since then (decided with the user): or-patterns; unions inferred for alternatives when no expected type decides; type arguments fit member-wise (values are never shared) except for arguments to `mut` parameters, which must be exact; `!` passes errors into wider error types; length (`len(it)`) and `Float` conditions are decided (`types::reals` tests one point per region); the matched value's written refinement counts (`Coverage.excluded`), taken from variables' declared types (mutable ones too: every assignment and write-back is checked against the declared type), their fields and function results (`Checker::written`). Narrowing in an arm is skipped for a `mut` variable the arm assigns (`Checker::assigns_to`), so loops stay sound. A `mut` argument's declared refinement must contain the parameter's (`refinement_within`); assignments of literals are checked at compile time (`literal_outside`). Not yet: list lengths other than empty / not empty; conditions beyond constant comparisons (see "Match coverage with the SMT solver").

### Match coverage with the SMT solver (built, branch `verify-gaps`)
A match the checker can't decide because of a condition it can't evaluate (`where even(it)`, a refinement calling a function: `P::Cond` in `types/exhaustive.rs`) is no longer an error; the prover obliges "some arm of the `match` matches" (the `or` of the arms' conditions) for every match with such an arm, and the compiled code keeps its `no match arm` fault. Not yet: an obligation per arm that it can match (unreachable arms the checker can't see).

### Mutation at the call site, references, and unproven checks (decided 2026-11-19)
- **`mut` at the call site (done)**: an argument to a `mut` parameter is written `f(mut x)`; missing it, or writing it for a parameter that is not `mut` (or a builtin, or a closure), is an error. A `mut self` method's call is marked too, `mut queue.claim(2)` (since 2026-11-20; it was unmarked, as in Swift). `Expr::Mut`, checked in `check_mut_args`.
- **References**: immutable values can always be shared (no copying in the compiled form); a mutable reference only exists as a `mut` parameter. References stay second-class (never stored or returned), like Swift `inout` / Hylo, since first-class ones would need a borrow checker; a possible local form is `mut l := mut order.lines[0]`.
- **A `mut` argument is passed by reference, and may not overlap another argument** (decided with the user 2026-11-21; not built: today every `mut` argument goes through a slot of its own, copied in and out). Without threads or async, a `mut` parameter is the only way to the caller's variable while the call runs, and it can't outlive the call, so passing a pointer to the variable is safe with no borrow checker (Swift's "law of exclusivity"). Copy-in/copy-out stays the meaning, which is what the checker and the prover reason with; with a single reference the two can't be told apart. That holds only if the `mut` argument doesn't overlap any other argument of the same call, since read-only arguments are borrowed, not copied: `f(mut x, x)` would let the callee see its read-only parameter change, as would `swap(mut a, mut a)` and a part with its whole (`f(mut xs, xs[0])`, `g(mut r.lo, r)`). **Overlap is refused by the checker** (user's choice; rejected: allowing it and holding the overlapping argument once more, so the callee's write copies first), with a `help:` naming the copy to write (`y := x; f(mut x, y)`). Overlap means the same variable, or one path a prefix of another (`r` and `r.lo`; `xs[i]` and `xs[j]` count as overlapping unless the indices are different constants, or later proven different by `verify`). The same rule covers the open nested-block case: an argument block that assigns a variable used by another argument (`f(x, { x = g(); 1 })`) is overlap too, and can be refused the same way instead of held (`operands`). A field (`shift(mut r.lo)`) or an element is passed as a pointer into the record or list once it is made unique (copied first if its count is above 1). The reference makes the *variable* shared with the callee; the *value* in it is still updated in place only when its count is 1, and copied first otherwise (`ys := xs; f(mut xs)`): in-place mutation needs both this and reuse (step 3 of "Memory: reference counting with reuse"). An `extern c` `mut` parameter is already an out-pointer, so FFI is unchanged. Tasks keep this safe: a `mut` can't cross a `spawn`, and a `mut` reference on a task's stack is reached by that task only, even while it blocks (see "Concurrency: structured tasks without coloring").
- **Unproven checks are reported by default** (with the runtime check kept); a project policy can require `proven` for chosen modules. For the SMT work: an assignment to a refined variable is one more obligation (e.g. `x = f(x) - 3` with `x: Positive` needs `f`'s contract to imply it).

### Lambda syntax (done)
- Replace the Rust-style `|x| body` with a **bare arrow**: `x -> body` and `(a, b) -> body`, with optional parameter types such as `(s: String) -> …`. For a longer body, use a block: `x -> { ... }`.
- `->` is never an operator inside expressions, so there is no ambiguity; `=>` stays reserved for `match` arms.
- **No shorthand lambdas** (no `$`, `_` or implicit parameter) and **no separate anonymous-`fn` form**.
- **Refinements keep `it`** for "the value" (`where it > 0`).
- When implementing, update the parser, printer, examples, README and tests; `render` must still round-trip.

### Bindings and assignment (decided with the user 2026-11-21, built)
- `:=` declares and infers the type: `n := 0`, `(w, k) := pair()`, `mut x := 0`. With a type, the colon is already written, so the sign is `=`: `let x: Int = 5`, `mut x: Int = 0`. `let` only appears with a type; `let x = e` is a parse error pointing at `x := e`.
- `=` only reassigns a variable or field. `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `%=` parse to `x = x op e` (no new node), and the printer writes `x = x op e` back as `x op= e`.
- A function returning `()` leaves out `-> ()` (it was already optional when parsing; the printer now omits it). Function *types* keep it: `(A) -> ()`.
- A call's `(` must be written against the name: `f(x)` is a call, `f (x)` is `f` followed by `(x)`. So without `let`, `f() (a, b) := …` on one line is still a call then a binding (the printer puts them on separate lines anyway). Decided with the user 2026-11-21; the rendering is meant to be customizable per reader later (e.g. `let` everywhere, sums expanded or compressed), since the tree is the language.

### Requested cleanups (done)
- **String concatenation uses `+`**, not `++`: `"a" + "b"`. Lists use `+` too (`[1] + [2]`, decided), so `++` goes away entirely. Update the checker (`numeric` currently rejects `String` for `+`), the interpreter, the printer and all examples.
- **Use `len` everywhere, not `str_len`.** `len` is already overloaded for `String`, so remove `str_len` from the builtins and the prelude refinements, and update the examples.
- **Bring every example up to date with the current language**: `examples/*.strata` and `examples/tasks/`, plus the README snippets. Use the prelude types (`Natural`, `Positive`, `NonEmptyString`), inline refinements where a constraint is used once, `let mut` / loops where procedural code reads better, and (once implemented) arrow lambdas.
- **GitHub syntax highlighting**: label Strata code blocks in the README and docs as ```` ```rust ````. Rust is the closest match (`fn`, `trait`, `impl`, `let mut`, `match … =>`, `///`, `->`), and GitHub's highlighter for it is solid. Also add a `.gitattributes` with `*.strata linguist-language=Rust`, so `.strata` files are highlighted on GitHub and counted as code.

### A type's own methods, and shared method names (decided 2026-11-20, built)
- **`impl Item { ... }`** gives a type methods of its own, with no trait. A method with `self` is called on a value; one without `self` is called on the type (`Item.new(...)`), and is resolved into the function `Item.new` right after module resolution.
- **Traits as parameter types** are shorthand for a type parameter with that bound (`mut queue: Queue[P, E]` = `[Q: Queue[P, E]](mut queue: Q)`). Name the parameter only to tie types together.
- **Method names may be shared between traits**, each implementation with its own body (revised 2026-11-20 by the user). A call through a trait (`d: Describe`, `T: Describe`) runs that trait's implementation. On a known type, the type's own method wins, else the one trait of the type that declares the name. Anything else is a compile error: a type with two such traits and no own method; a receiver known only as `A | B` or `T: A + B` (even if the actual type has its own method: that doesn't say which trait's meaning the call wants).
- **Elaboration** (`types::check_and_elaborate`, done with the check): the checker records each such decision by call node, and the program that runs (compiled code, verify, SMT, termination) has it written in: `x.Describe::describe()` for a call through a trait, a direct call of the function `Item.describe` for a type's own method. A type's own methods are registered as functions (`Item.new`, `Item.describe`).
- **Trait methods without `self`** (built 2026-11-20): called on a named type (`Money.empty()`, `T.empty()`, `Self.empty()` in default methods and laws), elaborated to `Trait::empty(type, ..)`. The signature must mention `Self`. Generic functions whose type parameters are bounded by such a trait get hidden parameters `type:T` (of type `$Type[T]`, the type's name at runtime); each call passes the inferred type or forwards the caller's. The fuzzer generates them from its chosen instance. Since `runtime-types` (#122), types are passed whole at run time, which also covers methods with `self`.

## Git, diffs and review (decided)

- **Editing the standard library** (fixed, branch `std-edit`): `std/` is a package whose modules are `std.*`. `strata edit io` inside it names the module `std.io` (it named it `io`) and logs nothing (it started a `strata.log`; std's history is git's); `strata --file std edit` edits it as that package, checked like any package edit (it stored a definition that `check` then refused). `--file` on another folder or a file still stores what parses, checked afterwards.
- **Git stays the base** (built). A package's module files and its `strata.log` are committed; `.strata/` is only the local verification cache. `edit` / `add` / `rename` rewrite the files and append an entry (id, message, changes with each definition's text before and after, `rename`). The log merges by union. `strata init` writes `strata.toml` and an empty `strata.log`, so the log is tracked from the start (`git commit -am` would otherwise leave it behind).
- **Structural diff** between two git revisions: load both snapshots, pair definitions, compare trees. The log entries present at HEAD but not at the base are the atomic steps in between (for stepping through, renames, messages). A PR = merge-base..HEAD.
- **Change kinds**: added/removed; renamed/moved (one line, "N references updated", folded); signature change marked in place, with contract stronger/weaker; body change shown in place with unchanged statements folded, **falling back to a line diff of the canonical text when most of the body changed**; type field/variant changes (with "N matches must handle it"); trait changes; doc comments as a word diff; verification before → after. Ordered types → interfaces → implementations → tests.
- **`strata rename old new`**: done (`resolve::rename`). It reuses the resolver's scoping, so locals shadow and `use` imports work as the checker sees them, and it refuses a rename that would change what any other name refers to. Functions, types, traits, **variants, trait methods** (the trait, every impl's method and the calls) and **fields** (`strata rename Line.qty quantity`: declaration, accesses, record literals, updates, patterns). Variants and methods go through the resolver like definitions (`resolve::member` / `owner` / `defined`; an impl's methods are named in the trait's module, which rename mode used to get wrong). Field accesses are told apart by type, not by name: when another record (or the prelude, or `.value` of distinct types) has a field of that name, `resolve::rename_field` tags each access in a copy (`qty·d3k0`), and the checker (`types::field_owners`, a thread-local probe in `infer` for tagged `Expr::Field`) records the record type(s) of each receiver; only the owner's accesses are renamed, and an access on a union of the owner and another record is refused. Literals, updates and patterns are matched by their constructor. It rewrites the serialized tree in one fixed-order walk (tags and renames line up), and the command type-checks the result.
- **`strata move <name> <module>`** (`resolve::move_def`): a function, type or trait (with its variants or methods) changes module, keeping its short name. The resolver's write-back has a move mode (`run_with(.., moves)`): qualified references get the new module (`orders.charge(o)` → `billing.charge(o)`), short ones stay short and their module gets `use billing.{charge}`, `use` items that named it go (emptied lists are removed), and the moved definition's own short references get imports where it goes. Refused when something private would be out of reach (a private dependency, or a private definition used from its old module), or when any name would resolve differently (the same check as rename). Logged as `"renames": [{.., "kind": "move"}]`; diffs say "moved from `orders`"; `sync` follows moves (`resolve::follow_move`, compared by name since imports change the definitions' positions). Docs and comments aren't rewritten (the command lists the ones that mention the old name). Several renames are one step (`strata rename A.tag label B.tag label`, `resolve::rename_all`): fields are renamed together by `rename_fields` (an access on a union is renamed when every record it reads from is renamed to the same name), then the rest one after the other; one type-check, one log entry. The log entry has `"renames": [{"from", "to", "kind"}], "references"` (`store::renames_of` reads it; `strata move` writes `kind: "move"`). It works on the package's files and logs the rename.
- **Merge driver**: `strata merge-file %O %A %B %P` per definition, then type-check. GitHub won't run it, so: `strata sync main` merges main into the branch locally, and `strata ci` (all packages check, all modules canonical) was run before pushing, until it went (user, 2026-11-22: code is stored only in binary and a saved file checks by construction, so canonical-form and repository-wide checks were pointless; `strata upgrade` keeps the re-encoding, exports and `[verify]` rules are checked when a package loads). **No CI for now** (user, 2026-11-22): the compiler changes all the time and every agent runs the tests locally, so CI (installing LLVM and building strata on every run) only slowed merges; `cargo test` left CI in #185, then the workflow and the required checks went. To bring back once most of Strata is written in Strata and the compiler changes rarely; then cache the built `strata` (keyed by `src/`, `std/`, the Cargo files) so a change to `.strata` files alone doesn't rebuild it.
- **jj**: compatible (runs on git) but not a dependency.
- The tour is later. The user wants to settle the look first: offer a static mockup of the *Changes* view.

Order: ~~`strata rename`~~ → ~~diff core~~ → ~~`strata diff`~~ → ~~*Changes* view~~ → ~~merge driver~~.

**Built:** `src/diff.rs` (pairing through renames, words per change, line diff with in-place marks and folds that keep enclosing headers, line-diff fallback at ≥60% changed, contract notes, rename references, verification before/after), `src/history.rs` (a package's log replayed with `Codebase::states`, backwards from the files so hand-written code before the first entry is the start, one step per patch; or git: `BASE..HEAD` commits plus the working tree, each commit split into the log entries it added, replayed forwards with `store::apply`, plus a step "edits outside strata" for what the log doesn't explain), `strata diff [--from N] [--to N] [--step K] [--json]` / `strata --file DIR diff [BASE]`, and the viewer's *Changes* tab with its `/api/changes` (both since replaced by the activity and `/api/diff`: see "Viewer as a workspace"; the site's `build.mjs` still asks for `/api/changes`, so re-recording the site fails until it moves to `/api/diff`). The mockup is still in `design/changes-mockup.html`.
**Merge driver (built):** `src/merge.rs` and `strata merge-file` / `setup-git` / `sync` / `upgrade`, plus packages (`src/package.rs`, see below). `package::discover` finds every package (folder with a `strata.toml`) and script (a `.strata` file outside every package) with `git ls-files`; `sync` checks each one after a merge, and `strata upgrade` rewrites each in canonical form. (`strata ci` did both, plus `exports`, until 2026-11-22.)
How the driver works: definitions are paired by qualified name, and one-sided changes win. Two-sided changes go through `git merge-file` on the definition's canonical text, and the result is kept only if it parses back into that one definition; otherwise there are diff3 markers around it. Renames are detected without the log: a definition gone, and an added one with the same kind and module whose text is ≥60% similar to the base's after `resolve::rename`. The rename is applied to the base and to the other side. After the merge, the file is type-checked inside its package, with the package's other files read from disk (ours, because merge-ort runs drivers before it writes the worktree). Only errors that neither side has alone fail the merge. The whole-program check after all files are merged is `sync`'s check of every package and script.
`sync` passes the driver with `-c merge.strata.driver=...` and a temporary `core.attributesFile`, so it works without `setup-git`. It merges `origin/BRANCH` when there is a remote, commits only if everything checks, and otherwise leaves the merge staged.
CI: there is none for now (#186, see above): the workflow and the required checks are gone, and `strata ci` was run before every push (until it went, 2026-11-22). The repository is private.
**Renames across a merge (built):** `sync` runs the driver with `STRATA_MERGE_BASE` / `STRATA_MERGE_THEIRS`; then the driver skips its per-file type check (while git merges, the other files are still ours). After git is done, `merge::follow_renames` reads the renames both sides logged since the merge base and applies each to the merged package (`resolve::follow`): the renamed definition is put back under its old name (and `use` items naming the new name name the old one), every reference to the old name is renamed, and each definition must resolve as before (what the rename touched, with old → new) or exactly as merged (everything else), so a local that would capture the new name makes it refuse. The fix is a logged change (`"follows"`), staged into the merge commit; then `sync` checks everything.
Not yet: following renames under a plain `git merge` (only `sync` does it); a refused follow leaves every reference to the old name for `sync`'s check to report; moving several definitions at once (move a private helper with its user: make it `pub`, or move it first); a rename+edit is recognised only when it is ≥60% similar *and* callers switched to the new name (tiny functions look alike, so similarity alone gave false renames).

Diff: a renamed variant, method or field shows on its type or trait as "variant `Circle` renamed to `Round`" ("only a name changed · N references updated"), and code that only follows it is folded; other changes to the owner are computed against the old owner with the rename applied. `sync` follows member renames too (`resolve::set_member_name` puts the member back under its old name first). Diff, not yet: "N matches must handle the new variant"; moves between modules. Closure hashes (verification cache keys) now ignore docs and comments, and interface hashes ignore docs.

## Packages, modules and dependencies (decided)

- **`core` and `std` are reserved** (user, 2026-11-24): no package's module may be named `core` or `std` or below them (`core.text`), and no dependency may take either name; the Strata checker refuses it (`load_package`: "module `core` is named like the built-in package `core` …: rename the module"). Found because Rust's `strata edit` of module `main` in a package with a `core` module wrote into `core.strata`. Rust's `edit` still accepts such names (Rust-only, not fixed).

- **A package is a folder with a `strata.toml`**: `name` (required; `strata init` writes the folder's name, and it stays when the folder moves, e.g. in a worktree), `exports` (optional, modules or single definitions, the equivalent of `pub use`; default every `pub`), `[deps]`, `[verify]` (verifier policy, e.g. modules that must be `proven`). There is **no `lib.strata`**. **`main.strata`** is the root module and the default entry point. A `.strata` file outside every package is a script. A nested folder with its own `strata.toml` is a separate package.
  **Built:** `name`, `exports` (checked when the package loads), discovery, nested packages, `init`, `[verify]` (verify's policies), `[native]` (C for `extern c`), `[json]`, `[run]`, `[ui]`. `[deps]` by local path is read by the compiler in Strata (`compiler/deps`, below); the Rust tools parse it but don't load dependencies.
- **A file is a module; `module m { }` blocks are gone** (built). The parser has no `module` form, `printer::program` is one file's text, and `render` prints one module (`render MODULE`, `main` for the root) or `--split DIR`; on several modules without either it says so. A view across modules labels each group with a `// ── module m` comment line. Where a definition sits inside a file doesn't matter, and the standard rendering is one file per module. Folders organise modules freely; there are no submodules with special access. A module's name is its path inside the package (`shop/orders.strata` → `shop.orders`; confirmed by the user 2026-11-19), so names don't collide and moving a file is a module rename.
- **Imports, Python-like** (decided with the user 2026-11-22; built, branch `import-syntax`): `use std.time` binds the module to its last name (`time.now()`, `time.Clock`), `use std.time as t` to `t`, `from std.time use Clock, Timer as T` brings names in unqualified (a type with its constructors, a trait with its methods). Every other module comes in through a `use`, std's included: a name written in full (`std.json.parse`) needs a `use` of its module (or of one containing it: `use std` covers `std.json`), else the error says which to add; only the prelude needs none. The old forms (`use m.{x}`, `use m.X`, `use m.*`) are parse errors saying what to write. `UseDecl` has `alias` and its items `UseItem { name, alias }` (a format change; the repository's files were rewritten through their text). **Globs (`use m.*`) were dropped** (not decided either way): the migration replaced each with the names the module uses (`compiler/`'s `from ast use …` lists are long, printed filled to the width); they can come back on request. Only what is used is loaded, **in the Strata compiler only** (the user's call: lazy loading, path and git dependencies are new features, built in `compiler/`, not in `src/`): `compiler/main`'s `std_imports` (used by `check` and `package`) takes the std names the definitions are written with (`binary.decl_strings`: their string tables; stored code names everything in full) and decodes only the std definitions they reach through the header entries' dependencies (`binary.decode_entry`), instead of whole modules followed through their `use`s. Impls come once every std name in their header is in (nothing names an impl; one with no std name, `impl Show for Int`, once its module's file is read); a name that is no definition (a variant, a trait method) brings only the type, trait or capability it belongs to (since #290: the module's types, traits and capabilities are decoded once to find it, and only that one is kept; before, all of them came, with what they reach); a std module's own definitions aren't read again. Since #290 the same loader (`compiler/main`'s `load`, with `Lib`s: std, and each dependency) reads dependencies too (below). The Rust compiler still appends all of std (`stdlib::with_std`). Measured on the built `compiler`, `check FILE` (`/usr/bin/time -l`; user time is at the clock's resolution, so instructions retired too): `use std.unicode` + `use std.regex`, calling `max_code`: 2730 M instructions, 0.15 s user, 8.4 MB RSS before; 37 M, 0.00 s, 3.5 MB after. `from std.json use parse`: 370 M → 83 M, 4.1 → 3.8 MB. `use std.time` (Clock): 64 M → 47 M. `compiler/main.strata`: 346 M → 271 M. `std/toml.strata`: 1088 M → 1041 M. Slower: examples/tasks/core (no std) 93 M → 99 M and `std/web.strata` 286 M → 307 M (collecting the names re-encodes each definition's names). Not decided: the string tables as the source of roots (they over-approximate: a string literal `"std.x"` counts) rather than a walk over the definitions; they were the shortest correct way. Next (handoff order): local path dependencies with package-prefixed module names and no cycles between packages, then git URLs and `strata.lock`.
- **Dependencies from any git URL** (Go-like, not only GitHub), declared in `[deps]` (`json = "https://…"`, or `{ path = "../x" }` in the same repo), used as `use json.parse`. To be fetched into a global cache and run directly, with no install step (not built: `[deps]` is parsed, nothing is fetched). A generated `strata.lock` pins each dep to a commit + content hash (confirmed by the user 2026-11-19, not built), with `strata update`. Semver tags (explained to the user) can come later; Strata could compute the right bump from interface hashes and contract diffs. Capabilities-as-values mean a dependency can't do I/O unless handed a capability, which makes URL imports much safer than npm/Go.
- **Path dependencies and no package cycles** (decided 2026-11-22; built in the Strata compiler only, user's call: new features go in `compiler/`, not `src/`). `compiler/deps`: `manifest` reads `name` and `[deps]` (`json = { path = "../json" }`, the key must be the dependency's `name`); `resolve_deps` walks the dependencies depth first, each once and after what it depends on, and refuses a cycle (`dependency cycle: a -> b -> c -> a`) or a missing package; a dependency's modules are loaded under its name (root module `json`, module `text` -> `json.text`; first by `prefixed`, which wrote a dependency back as text and stored it again, replaced in #290 by renaming each definition as it is decoded, `resolve.relocated`). The compiler's `package [--std DIR] DIR` command loads a package with its dependencies and reports resolver errors in its own modules only (tests/resolve.rs `path_dependencies`).
  **Where git sources plug in (PR 4):** `deps.Source` (only `Path` today) and `deps.fetched`, which turns a source into a folder; everything after it is shared.
  **Built since (PR #290, all in `compiler/`):** the manifest is read with `std.toml` (`parse`, `lookup`), strata.lock too (written with `std.toml.quote`). A dependency's `exports` (modules or single definitions, as its own package names them: `text`, `text.twice`, a root definition as `parse` or `main.parse`, the root module as `main`) is applied: a `pub` definition it doesn't list is loaded as private, and the resolver's error for it says so (`` `parse` isn't exported by package `json` (only what its strata.toml's `exports` lists is part of its contract) ``). A module of the package named like a dependency (or below one: `json.x`) is refused (`` module `json` is named like the dependency `json`, whose modules are … ``). **Dependencies load lazily, per definition**, like std since #285: `compiler/main`'s `load` (what was `std_imports`, generalized to `Lib`s: std and each dependency) starts from the names the package's definitions reach (std's from their string tables; a dependency's through the package's `use`s, found by `resolve.relocated` walking the definitions with the `use` bindings, since the Rust tools store a package without its dependencies), reads only the module files those are in, and decodes only the definitions reached (through each definition's references and its header entry's dependencies). A dependency's definition is renamed as it is decoded (`resolve.relocated`: its stored names, `parse`, `text.twice`, and what it imports from other packages, become the program's, `json.parse`, `json.text.twice`), replacing `deps.prefixed`, which wrote a whole dependency back as text and stored it again. So a root module's variants (and every other root name) are short in the dependency's other modules, as in its own package. A dependency's `test` blocks are never loaded unless `check --with-deps`. `check` on a package with dependencies now checks its definitions stored with them (before, `toml.parse` through `use fmt.toml` was an unknown variable) and prints the resolver's errors first. Measured (`/usr/bin/time -l`, the built `compiler`) on a package whose dependency `fmt` has two modules (`fmt.toml` and `fmt.json`: std.toml's and std.json's text, 60 kB and 32 kB of module files) and that uses only `toml.parse`/`toml.lookup`: `check` 1924 M instructions retired, 7.8 MB RSS → 666 M, 5.0 MB; `package` 2501 M, 7.4 MB → 654 M, 4.4 MB (user time 0.10-0.14 s → 0.03 s); `fmt.json` isn't read. std only: a script using `std.json`'s `parse` and the variant `Null` 573 M → 401 M (the variant brings only `JsonValue`); `std/toml.strata` 1284 M → 1199 M; `std/web.strata` and `compiler/main.strata` unchanged.
  Still open: compiler/ has **no `test` or `verify` command** (and its checker checks only a test's parameters, not its body), so "a dependency's tests are left out unless `--with-deps`" is about loading and `check`; the Rust `strata test`/`verify` don't load dependencies at all until they move to the Strata front end. Exports are applied to the package being checked: a dependency using another dependency's unexported definition isn't refused (dependencies aren't resolved again), and an export naming nothing is reported when the dependency itself is checked (the Rust loader's `bad_exports`), not by the package using it. The root module of a dependency is read (its header, and its types and traits decoded once) as soon as one of its other modules' definitions is decoded, to tell its short names from the prelude's.
  **Git dependencies, design (handoff PR 4, 2026-11-22; built in `compiler/deps`, see "Built" below).** The user's call: built **only in the Strata compiler** (`compiler/`), not in `src/`; fetching, the cache and the lock are Strata code using std's capabilities. Defaults picked (change on request):
  - **Syntax:** a string is a git URL at the remote's default branch (`json = "https://github.com/x/json"`); a table is `{ path = "../x" }` (PR 3) or `{ git = "URL" }` with at most one of `rev = "…"` (a commit, or anything `git rev-parse` reads), `tag = "…"`, `branch = "…"`. Unknown keys, `path` with `git`, or `rev`/`tag`/`branch` with `path` are errors. Any URL git reads works (`https://`, `ssh://`, `file://`).
  - **`strata.lock`** (next to `strata.toml`, committed), TOML, written by strata in a fixed form: a comment header, then one `[[git]]` table per git dependency reached, transitively (a dependency's own git dependencies are pinned in the root's lock, as Cargo does), sorted by `url` then what is asked: `name` (the `[deps]` key, informative), `url`, one of `rev`/`tag`/`branch` (absent: the default branch), `commit` (the full hash). An entry is keyed by `url` + what is asked, so `tag = "v1"` and `branch = "main"` of one repository are two entries. A dependency found in the lock is used at its commit, with no network access when the cache has it; a new or changed one is resolved (fetched) and the lock rewritten; entries no longer reached are dropped; the file is written only when its text changes. The git commit hash is the content hash (the earlier "commit + content hash" collapses to it).
  - **`strata update [NAME…]`** resolves again every git dependency (or those named), fetching, and rewrites the lock.
  - **Cache** (`$STRATA_CACHE`, else `~/.cache/strata`): `deps/repos/<key>.git` is a bare clone per repository (`git clone --bare`, then `git fetch --force --prune --tags URL '+refs/heads/*:refs/heads/*'` to update; a `rev` on no branch is fetched by hash), `deps/<key>/<commit>/` the package at that commit (`git archive COMMIT | tar -x` into a temporary folder renamed into place, so concurrent runs are safe; never changed after). `<key>` is the URL without its scheme, characters other than `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` made `_`, plus 8 hex digits of its SHA-256. A locked commit missing from the cache is fetched again; one the remote no longer has is an error naming `strata update`.
  - A fetched package loads exactly like a path dependency (PR 3): its modules prefixed by its package name, the no-cycles check, its exports (applied since #290). Path dependencies inside a git dependency resolve inside its checkout (a path leaving it is an error).
  - **Running `git`: built** (`Commands` in `std.process`, PR A of this handoff): `run(mut self, program, args: List[String], dir: String) -> Result[Finished, String]`, `Finished = { code, out, err }`; the runtime's capability is `Launcher` (for `main`, or a test that names it), backed by `host_run` (`posix_spawnp` with pipes, no shell, stdin `/dev/null`). Defaults picked: `dir` is a `String` (`""` is the current folder) rather than an `Option`; `run` takes `mut self` (it changes the world, and a mock that records calls needs it); a killed program's code is 128 + the signal. No stand-in type in std: tests use `mock Commands` or a mock type answering the calls they expect. Not yet: standard input for the program, environment changes, streaming output, a timeout. Writing a commit's files can use `git --work-tree=DIR checkout COMMIT -- .` on the bare clone instead of `tar`.
  - **Built** (`compiler/deps` + `compiler/main`'s `package` and `update DIR [NAME...]` commands; test `git_dependencies` in tests/resolve.rs against a local bare repository): the syntax, strata.lock (read, kept, rewritten only when its text changes; an `update` re-resolves the named or all), the cache layout and key. Choices made: the SHA-256 for the key is written in Strata in `compiler/deps` (std has none; move it to std when something else wants it); a commit's files are written with `git --work-tree TMP checkout --force COMMIT -- .` on the bare clone (`rm`, `mkdir`, `mv` around it, through `Commands`), so no `tar`; the temporary folder is `COMMIT.tmp`, so two runs checking out the same commit at the same moment can still collide (a unique suffix needs a process id or randomness std doesn't give yet); a dependency present in the cache is used without checking its commit is still upstream. Not yet: the Rust `strata` doesn't fetch (only the Strata compiler does, as decided), so `strata update` is the compiler's `update` command until the Strata compiler is the front end; a path dependency inside a git checkout that leaves it isn't refused; no network test (file:// only).

## Standard library: I/O first (built)

The user asked for a standard library usable from any program, starting with I/O (print, exit codes), then strings, regex, a NumPy-like math library (later, bigger), and web UI. Built so far:
- **`std/` is a Strata package** (`name = "std"`), embedded in the binary (`src/stdlib.rs`), and precompiled into a cached library (#137). A program that has a `use std.…` gets std's definitions appended after its own at resolution (`stdlib::with_std` in `resolve::resolve`), so index `i` of a resolved program is still the program's `i`; code that zips resolved with original decls is unaffected, and `store::graph` truncates to the program's own. Its modules are `std.io`, `std.fs`, `std.process` and the rest (`std.cli`, `std.json`, `std.http`, `std.web`, `std.regex`, `std.unicode`, `std.utf8`, `std.typing`, `std.code`; the compiler in Strata, once `std.ast.*`, is the `compiler/` package now), also when `std/` itself is loaded (`package::program` prefixes a package named `std`; `store::module_path` maps back to files).
- **Capabilities, as decided**: `Console`, `Files`, `Environment` are capability traits. The real implementations (`Terminal`, `Disk`, `Process`) are capabilities (#150): only the runtime makes them, for `main` or a test that names one. Test stand-ins are public: `Recorder`, `MemFiles`, `FakeEnvironment` (plain types; making them `pub mock`s is a step of "Mocks" not built).
- **The native layer** is the `host_*` builtins (`stdlib::HOST`, implemented in `src/llvm/runtime.c`). The resolver rejects them outside `std` modules. `verify` and fuzzing never run code that reaches them (it is reported assumed); a test reaches them only through a capability it names.
- **`strata run [PATH] [-- ARGS]`** runs `main`: parameters are filled by type (Console/Files/Environment only), the return is `()` → 0, `ExitCode`/`Int` → that code, `Result` → `Ok` as before or `Err` printed to stderr (`error: …`) → 1; a fault → 101.
- Examples: `examples/hello`, `examples/wc` (with tests that use the stand-ins).
- Also fixed: the printer now escapes tabs and backslashes in string literals (it used to write a literal tab, and `"\\n"` came back as a newline).

### `std.toml` (asked for by the user 2026-11-22, for `strata.toml` files; built, branch `toml`)

TOML 1.1 (the version the `toml` crate implements) read and written in Strata, as `std.json` is: `parse(text)` gives the root table as `List[(String, TomlValue)]` (keys in the order written, as `JsonValue`'s objects), `TomlValue` = `Text | Integer | Number | Boolean | Moment(DateTime) | Array | Table`; `DateTime` keeps TOML's four kinds as optional `date`, `time`, `offset` (`Utc` for `Z`, `Minutes(n)`); a time without seconds reads as `:00`. The parser works on bytes (`byte_at`, `byte_slice`): all of TOML's syntax is ASCII. Tables are built as a tree of `Node`s (`Leaf` for values, inline tables and arrays, which are closed; `Branch` with how the table came to be: `Implicit`, `Header`, `Dotted`; `Tables` for arrays of tables), the rules as the `toml` crate applies them (a dotted key may extend a table made implicitly by a header path, which makes it dotted). Errors are `line:column: message`. `inf` and `nan` are errors (a `Float` is finite). `stringify` writes a table's values, then its tables as `[sections]` and arrays of tables as `[[sections]]`.
